MILDRED MILES DILLMAN 

Mildred Miles Dillman, author of the

Early History of Duchesne County and compiler of the community histories. 

Mrs. Dillman has spent thirty years in research and compiling material used in this book.

PREFACE 

It is hoped that this little volume will be read with the understanding that there has been no attempt to make of it a detailed, voluminous history, a Master's thesis. Rather has the data contained herein, including the personal stories, been gathered and written from original sources to be preserved for future reference. Much of it has been taken from the personal research files of Mildred Dillman. 

Since the material is an accumulation of statistical facts, incidents and stories taken from church history, diaries, and from conversations with the aged pioneers still living, it may not be in some cases entirely accurate. Some worthy of recognition as outstanding pioneers may unintentionally not be mentioned because they have kept no records. 

To each of the camps of the Daughters of the Pioneers in Duchesne County assignments were made to search and gather all possible history, and these descendants of the faithful and fearless pioneers did not fail the call. Much credit is due Mrs. Florence

Madsen, Centennial Historian of Duchesne County, for her support and contributions. 

Mrs. Margaret Maxfield, Captain of the Duchesne County

Camps, has been tireless in her efforts to encourage and assist the historians in each of her camps to gather the authentic story of the towns. She has been assisted by Mrs. Mae Lambert, of Altonah, Janette Beebee, Mrs. Maxine Burdick, Mrs. Lila Nickell, Olive

Harrison and the presidents of the various camps. 

This book is the work of research contributions from the Duchesne County Camp of the Daughters of the Pioneers, and compiled by Mildred M. Dillman and Donna V. Burgess.

The proof reading has been done by Mrs. M. Edwards. 

Appreciation is extended to Esther Lambert, Tina Wilkins, The 

Roosevelt Standard, the Duchesne Record, Alice Todd, and the many others who contributed to the book by loaning their valuable pictures and newspaper cuts. 

Dr. Levi Edgar Young once said: "Were it not for these courageous Daughters of the Pioneers gathering and preserving original stories, there would be little left for future generations to have these experiences first-hand. They are making a real contribution to the State of Utah. God bless them."

DEDICATION 

Dedicated to all of those

Valiant Pioneers that made possible our security and comforts of today.

FOREWORD 

The urge to explore and pioneer has been influential in changing the course of many lives. Throughout history the people of the entire world has come under its spell in some degree. 

Little difference is noted in the history of our own country, as we pay tribute in these pages to the pioneers of our own locality. 

We would like to give special recognition to Margarete Maxfield and the County Officers elected in 1945, who were in charge of the gathering of locality histories. 

To Mildred M. Dillman we express our most sincere gratitude and appreciation for the work she has done in compiling our material. In the forepart of the book she has given us thirty years of her research in Indian culture and archealogy. 

We hope that the Spirit of the Pioneers will carry on. May you find these pages most inspiring and enjoyable.

MAXINE W. BURDICK.

INTRODUCTION 

The State of Utah, in which the Uintah Basin is located in the eastern part, received its name from the Ute Indians, and is, as Dr. 

Levi Edgar Young states, "a corruption of the word 'Eutah,' the name of an Indian tribe who lived in these valleys and mountains long ago. 'Utah' means 'in the tops of the mountains, a land of peace and plenty." 

The word "Uintah" has been given several meanings. Dr. 

Francis Desmore states that it means "that at the foot of long-leaf, timber pines, clear streams are flowing." This is also the opinion of 

Dr. Alfred Reagan. However, most of the old Uintah Indians state that it means "Land high up where timber grows." It is more likely that Uimpah Ow-ump was the first name and the correct one. 

Uwim-pah) -Uim means “around," pah, water, and Ow-ump is the long-leaf pine tree. Because the white man was not able to catch the correct pronunciation of the word, he changed it to Uintah. 

Duchesne County is located in the western part of the Uintah Basin and was given the name of the river "Duchesne," which flows through the county. There have been many speculations as to the origin of the word "Duchesne." Since Father Escalante was Spanish, and according to his journal he gave the rivers and places he passed Spanish names, it is not logical that he used the word. However, there were traders and trappers, many of whom were French, through this country in early times, and the name may have originated from one of them. "Duchesne" is a French word commonly used in the early history in the United States. 

A more probable source is that given in honor of Sister Duchesne, a Catholic Mother, who came to this country from France in the early 1800's and settled in St. Louis. She established the 

Order of the Sacred Heart, and is reported to have had a mission as far west as Nebraska. A Duchesne College is still located there.

Because of her kindness to the Indians and trappers, the river may have received its name in her honor.

Many of the towns of Duchesne County are given Indian names. 

They will be discussed under separate headings.

CHAPTER I THE  

"Oh ye Mountains High

Where the Clear Blue Sky 

Arches over the Vales of the Free, Where the Pure Breezes blow 

And the Clear Streamlets flow, How I longed to your Bosom to flee."

CHARLES PENROSE. 

he Uintah BaSIN forms a huge bowl and lies within three

It is 300 miles long and a width of about 127 

states. 

miles. Approximately two-fifth lies within the State of 

Utah, two-fifths within Colorado, and the balance within Wyoming.

On the north rim of the Uintah Basin stretching east and west are the Uintah Mountains. This range is the loftiest in the State of 

Utah. There are eleven peaks that tower above timber-line to an elevation of over 13,000 feet. One of these peaks is known as King's 

Peak with an elevation of 13,498 feet. It is the highest peak in Utah.

At one time they were twenty thousand feet higher but have been weathered by the elements until the oldest form of rock is found on the summit of them. It is a thrill to stand in an electrical storm on top of King's Peak. Electricity buzzes through the strands of one's hair and makes it stand upright. Pointed fingers draw snapping sparks of electricity from the elements.

Some venturesome person has built a rough altar on top of the highest peak and placed in it a bottle and a blunt pencil to record the names of those who dared to climb and experience a few minutes of silent communion with Heaven. Among those of the few names recorded there are William H. Smart, to whom Duchesne 

County owes a deep debt of gratitude, Wm. Woolf, Ray E. Dillman, Jack McLea, George Walkup, Mr. and Mrs. Jess Allen, and Mr. and Mrs. Homer Edwards, entire families and Evelyn Ashton.

On the summit of the peaks, one can stand and view about 

550 lakes, and one asks the question, "How did they come into existence?" Geologists explain that ages ago one hundred twenty-one glaciers from five to twenty miles in length, resting on the south slope of the range, caused these lakes. Now they are fringed with many varieties of spruce, pines, and firs interspersed with the lovely 

Quaken Aspen. Surrounding the lakes are meadowlands dotted with bright colored wild flowers during the summer months. 

The Uintahs form the oldest range in the State of Utah. Within the Majestic Mountains are many caves. Pole Creek cave is 13

popular. One crawls through an opening until a large lake is reached, the beginning of which has not yet been described. In several places streams disappear into the mountainside, large springs suddenly bubble into the canyons. These are used for electrical power.

The western rim of the Basin is formed by the Wasatch Mountains, and on the eastern side of the Divide in this range lies the 

Scene at Moon Lake 

Altar on top of King's Peak

Ashley National Forest, Moon Lake, 1917 

Uintah River

Strawberry Valley, now converted into a reservoir for water used in Utah Valley.

On the south the Travaputs Plateau forms the boundary. To the east the Basin extends a short distance into Colorado and ends at the walls of the Rocky Mountains. 

From the center of the Uintah Basin, there is a gradual slope up to the rims. The elevation varies from 4000 feet to over 13,000 feet. Streams and depressions break the surface, making mesa and benches. On these much of the farming land is located. 

Uintah Basin is one of the most unique places in the world. 

It has within its borders a varied history of geology. Scientists have come from almost every corner of the world to study the famous exposures of strata and rock formations which are inlaid with rich deposits of fosils and matrixes from the various periods of the geological scale of time. Many universities and museums have preceded the Uintah Basin's interest and have carried away much of the wonders that should have been protected and left here for future generations to study in their natural surroundings. 

Ancient Lake Uinta has left many scars and riches to the Basin. It was here over a long period of time but finally existed no more. It has been estimated that the arm of the ocean extended a number of times into and over this area. 

Several places in the Basin expose within a few miles the whole story of Geology in reference to the forming of different periods. 

Among the wonders are the huge deposits of the ancient dinosaurs, and the families of the different animals that existed during the Mesozoic period, estimated by geologists as about 

200,000,000 years ago.

The dinosaurs varied greatly in size, from those about twenty inches to near one hundred and twenty-five feet, and there were many different species that were residents of the Uintah Basin about 140,000,000 years ago. 

Dr. O. A. Peterson, as early as 1892, found bones in the vicinity of the present Dinosaur National Monument. On August 

19, 1909, Dr. Earl Douglas visited this place and established a quarry to remove the bones.

The Dinosaur National Monument was created in 1915 by a proclamation of President Wilson. No attempt was made to preserve the rest of the fossils until 1923 under the direction of Dr. 

Boyle. This locality has produced more dinosaurs than any other place in the world.

The Basin is situated in what is generally known as the Colorado River Drainage area and is drained by mountain streams, the largest being Lake Fork, Duchesne, Uintah, Rock Creek, and the

Strawberry River. They all flow southward into the Green River

These furnish irrigation waters for the lands of Duchesne County. 

Concerning irrigation, thousands of years before the advent of the reservation homesteaders, the primitive peoples built reservoirs and made canals to irrigate their beans, squash, and corn. These canals are still discernible in different places. One of the plainest yet to be seen is on the W. K. Dye place in Neola. 

After the white men came, numerous irrigation companies were organized in sections of the county where river water was available. 

Some of the most important of Duchesne County are: The Dry 

Gulch Irrigation Co., Cedarview Irrigation Company, Lake Fork

Irrigation Company, Uteland Ditch Company Lake Fork Western 

Irrigation Company, Farnsworth Canal Company, Purdy Ditch Company, New Hope Irrigation Company, Anderson Ditch Company, Good Luck Ditch Company, Hartzell-O'Hagen, Mines Ditch, Knutson, McPhee, Uintah Independent Ditch Company, Big Six

Association, Whiterocks Irrigation Company, Colorado Park Irri gation Company, Ouray Valley Irrigation Company, Farmers Irrigation Company, and the Pioneer Canal Company, T. N. Dodd Irrigation Co., Blue Bench Irrigation Co., Boneta Irrigation Co., Myton 

Irrigation Co., later called Uintah Irrigation Co., Duchesne Irrigation Co., the Rhodes Ditch Company, and the Rocky-Point Ditch Company.

The picture of irrigation in Duchesne County can best be told by Byron O. Colton: 

"The factor that has held the Uintah Basin back much more than the absence of a railroad has been the unmerciful years of drouth. No section dependent for its well-being on agriculture can hope to progress without an assured water supply. There are more water sources and streams in the Uintah 

Basin, particularly in Duchesne County, than any other part of the state, but the construction of dams, reservoirs, canals and irrigation systems is an expensive process and requires time, effort, and money. "Slowly but surely the Uintah Basin has gone about conserving this life-giving water supply the past three years; since government funds have been available, greater advancement has been made."

Water Sources 

"A large portion of the Uintah Basin is watered by the Duchesne River, the Lake Fork and Uintah Rivers, including the Whiterocks 

River. The Duchesne River, including Rock Creek and the Strawberry River, supplies water to 8,500 acres of Indian land with primary water rights, and 25,500 acres of homestead land with secondary water rights, making a total of 34,000 acres of land under present cultivation. This total figure includes 6,000 acres of Indian

Building Kidney Lake Reservoir, 1917 Chain Lake Reservoir Early Canal Construction Chain Lakes

land under the Lake Fork River which are to be supplied water from Duchesne River by the Duchesne Feeder Canal and Midview

Reservoir when the Moon Lake Project is completed in exchange for Lake Fork waters to be used on homestead land under the 

Lake Fork and Uintah Rivers. 

The water required for full water rights under state regulations for 34,000 acres is 102,000 acre feet of water or three acre feet per acre. More than this figure or ample water is available from the direct flow of the sources of the Duchesne River with ordinarily a large surplus going to waste which may yet be used or conserved for Uintah Basin lands. As yet, no water storage development has been made on the Duchesne or Strawberry Rivers directly under these sources of supply. 

Blue Bench Development 

If the Blue Bench area is developed, that will require about 15,000 acre feet of impounded water to insure a low water supply and some conservation and storage of water will be necessary to irrigate other Duchesne River lands with this additional acreage load. In case of a period of drouth, whether Blue Bench is developed or not, some plans should be made on the Duchesne for water conservation. 

On the Lake Fork and Uintah Rivers side there is a total of 49,819 acres of Indian land under cultivation, excluding the 6,000 acres which will be transferred to the Duchesne supply and 67,745 acres of homestead land under cultivation at the present time. 

In addition to this more than 30,000 acres of homestead lands are under constructed canals and wholly without provision for a water supply. Water required for the homestead lands on the 

Lake Fork and Uintah Rivers now under cultivation would be about 

200,000 acre feet.

Supply Insufficient 

The dependable direct flow from these rivers is not sufficient for these lands and many storage projects along the Lake Fork and Uintah Rivers have been developed. Under the Moon Lake 

Water Users' Association eleven mountain reservoirs on the Uintah 

River, five on the Whiterocks River, and Montez Creek Reservoir have been constructed. Five mountain reservoirs on the Uintah river, five on the Whiterocks river and Montez Creek reservoir have been developed by the Dry Gulch and Whiterocks Irrigation Companies. Following is an outline of the reservoirs and their capacities:

This makes a total of 7,220 acre feet of water from the Uintah and Whiterock Rivers to which is added 46,200 acre feet from the Lake Fork River and the 12,000 acre feet from the direct flow of the Duchesne River through the Duchesne Feeder Canal. This makes a total of 65,420 acre feet of stored water for lands on the Lake Fork, Uintah and Whiterocks Rivers, in addition to the direct flow received in variable quantities as it is available. 

The cost of the storage reservoirs enumerated above is probably about $1,750,000, or somewhat less than $30.00 per acre foot of water.

Normally this amount of water will not be available, and the program of use must be so regulated as to leave part of the stored supply in the reservoirs each year for early use the succeeding spring.

Water is Security 

Due to the fact that allotted Indian lands hold the primary water rights, there has always been an element of great uncertainty in the operations of farmers on homestead lands in the Uintah

Basin. Lack of water to prepare the soil for early planting prior to high water period and for irrigation of crops so planted has handicapped the people on these lands and led them to defer planting until such time as the water supply was assured. Of course, this has been essentially a program doomed to failure. Not until the fall of 

1936, has there ever been any real assurance that water would be available for these homestead lands as early as the farmer might need it the following spring.

The conservation of water in storage reservoirs will thus result in a more definite program of work and planting on the part of the farmer on homestead land and replace failure with at least some degree of prosperity.

Progress Made 

The moderate amount of water in the small mountain reservoirs, while serving the purpose as far as it would go, was wholly insufficient to meet the demand on 67,795 acres of land. The additions provided by the Moon Lake and Midview Reservoir, together with the direct flow through the Duchesne Feeder Canal, brings the total available supplemental water supply to such proportions that all secondary users may feel, even in years of drouth, a measure of security. The Montez Creek Reservoir with its definite double use definitely adds to this measure of safety.

Future Possibilities 

The lands now under constructed canals which are not provided with storage water have in considerable measure been vacated or abandoned during the recent extended drouth period. Among these lands is some of the most fertile soil which is the basis of successful farming.

The conservation of our water supply should proceed to the point that these lands are assured of at least one acre foot of water in storage for each acre of land. Possibilities for this being successfully accomplished are existent, and the cost will be no greater than that which has already been done or is in course of being done. This applies somewhat to certain lands under the Dry Gulch 

Irrigation system and more particularly to lands east of the Uintah River under the Whiterocks Irrigation, Moffat, and Ouray Canals. 

These facts should be an incentive for our continued efforts." 

Before the opening of the Reservation, a company was organized by President William Smart, called the Wasatch Development 

Company. William Smart was president and Joseph R. Murdock, one of his counselors in the Wasatch Stake Presidency, was the business manager. Its purpose was to give to people reliable information and assistance and to inform them to the best ways to get good homesteads and water filings in the Basin. 

The company made several water filings on streams, not for

speculation, but to assist and prevent graft and land grabbing schemes common to all new countries. 

The Dry Gulch Commission grew out of the Wasatch Develop ment Company. It was incorporated in Vernal but moved to Roosevelt, and the first election was held March 15, 1906. President 

Smart made a trip on horseback from Heber to aid in the election of officers.

ORGANIZATION OF THE DRY GULCH IRRIGATION COMPANY 

From an application of the Dry Gulch Irrigation Company for the endorsement of Moon Lake Reservoir, written and presented by Ray E. Dillman, to Honorable Board of the State Water Storage Commission is quoted the following: 

"As soon as the lands thus embraced in the Indian Reservation were declared open to entry, several men made their applications to appropriate water from the streams tributary to the Duchesne River to irrigate these lands. While many of these filings were for large flows of water, it was not the intention of the men making them to commercialize the same to the injury of the homesteader. It was seen that the great extent of the territory to be developed required that it be handled on a basis of large co-operation, as the canals must of necessity be large and somewhat expensive." With this vision in view, on December 1, 1905, there was organized at 

Roosevelt, Utah, the Dry Gulch Irrigation Company by R. S. 

Collett, John Glenn, Ed Harmston, Hyrum Baird, George F. 

Merkley, N. J. Meagher, Herbert Tysack, John N. Davis, J. Garnett 

Holmes, Henry J. Harding, Wm. G. Fell and John W. Roseberry.

There were turned to the new company practically without cost seven water filings.

Since the organization of the Dry Gulch Irrigation Company in 1905, it has grown until it is now the largest co-operative irrigation company within the State of Utah. It has constructed 

509 miles of canal and laterals at a construction cost of $50,000.00. 

It now has 790 stockholders, and supplies water for 53,000 acres of fertile land. There is a population of 5,000 people living under and receiving their livelihood from the products of the land under this system. The following cities and towns are the direct result of the development of the lands either wholly or partly irrigated by waters from this company: Leeton, Bennett, Roosevelt, Cedarview, Montwell, Neola, Hayden, Bluebell, Mt. Emmons, Upalco, and Ioka."

After the establishing of the many canals, it was discovered that there was not sufficient water for all except by the impounding of the water that came from lofty mountain snows. President Smart in a letter written to Mildred Dillman, upon inquiring about the organization of the Dry Gulch and Moon Lake reservoir, said that

Moon Lake Reservoir Dedication Neilson, Horace Allred, Weston House, Will Moon Lake Officers: Arvil Dart, 

Hancock, Ray G. Labrum, Arthur Wiscombe, Leandrew Gilbert, Louis Galloway.

he came over horseback in 1905, and saw Moon Lake as a potential reservoir.

It was made into a reservoir very early by means of a dirt dam, which went out with the melting snows. Shortly after this, Ray E. Dillman drew up an application to the State Water Storage 

Commission for endorsement of Moon Lake reservoir. A survey was made for two years, and then the governor appointed Mr. 

Dillman to the State Water Storage Commission to help complete this reservoir. He served on this commission until released by his election to the State Senate. The project was assured during these years and was dedicated by Governor Blood in a ceremony starting work in April, 1935.

There were many who helped to make this a success. Among them there should be mentioned and credit given to B. O. Colton, Horace Allred, William Wolfe, Austin G. Burton, Arthur Wiscombe, Ray G. Labrum, W. K. Dye, O. A. Dart, L. G. Gilbert, Taylor 

Tidwell, Will Hancock, Louie Galloway, and the State Water

Storage Commission. Bruce Calder was the caretaker for many years.

MIDVIEW RESERVOIR 

The Duchesne Feeder canal, Midview reservoir, and Midview lateral were constructed as a part of the Moon Lake project in Eastern Utah by the 1968th company of C. C. C. enrollees from 

Camp BR-11, near Bridgeland. The work program on these features, constituted a water-conversion project of a "high order" and began on October 20, 1934.

There were 330,000 cubic yards of excavation, 130 bridges built, flumes, checks, turnouts, and the Midview Dam, with a capacity of 5,000 acre feet. The Bureau of Reclamation field engineers had charge of survey's inspection and other field engineering work.

It was dedicated with elaborate ceremony on September 10, 1937, and named Lake Borum, in honor of one of the employees killed during the construction. His wife and baby were honored by 

Congressman Abe Murdock at the dedication.

NATIONAL FORESTS 

There are 204,830 acres of National Forest in the Uintah

Basin, and sixty-seven percent of this is in usable timber. It is estimated that there is a supply of 1,334,839-M board feet. There are various types of forest trees among which are Douglas Fir, Ponderosa 

Pine, Alpine, Engleman Spruce, and Lodgepole Pine.

The timber has been used mostly for mine props, and the output has been a very small percent of what could have been

used if transportation had not been so difficult. At one time 33 sawmills were operating in the Basin. 

The beauty of the Timberlands has become famous. This is partly due to the myriads of clear, crystal lakes and ponds covered with water lillies and furnishing excellent fishing. The rough natural scenery has not been made over by man. The High Uintah's are rapidly becoming one of the choicest playgrounds of the Nation. 

In these forests live an abundance of wild life. Elk, deer, bear, wolf, coyote, beaver, mink, and muskrats are some of the common types, and it is rare that a pack trip by horseback into these high mountains does not afford an opportunity to see many of these animals. 

Birds of many varieties live in the Uintah Basin. Rare waterfowl are to be found in the marshes and swamps. On one short trip by motor a count was made of over seventy varieties of birds. 

Many game birds, such as the sage hen, are available to hunters in open seasons. In recent years the Chinese Pheasant was planted near Roosevelt and have spread over the entire county, affording both good shooting and excellent food.

The friendly little cottontail rabbit deserves mention also. Often it furnished a good meal for the starving Indian and the struggling pioneer. However, lately the cottontail has become infected with a disease which has almost exterminated it. 

The small, chubby, tan animal known as the Prairie Dog was considered a special diet by the Indians. Because it destroyed the gardens of the farmers, the Prairie Dog has almost been wiped out.

Duchesne County has an abundance of wild flowers of many varieties. The State Flower, the Sego Lily, grows here in profusion during the spring and early summer. It varies from white to a dark red color. On the hills, in the meadows, and along the banks of the streams grow Evening Primroses, Wild Daisies, Star Flowers,

Indian Paint Brushes, Dogtooth Violets, Wild Carnations, Larkspurs, Wild Roses, Sweet Williams, Bluebells, Lady's Slippers, Fox 

Gloves, Pinstamens, Wild Irises, and many other varieties. On the deserts the gorgeous cactus flower, almost translucent and ablaze with color, compensates the desert for its burning bareness.

MINES 

This Basin has been covered many times by an arm of the ocean. During one of the Geological periods an ancient lake came into its existence. This lake was known as Lake Uinta. It occupied a long shallow basin of the earth's crust about a hundred miles southward of Great Salt Lake. During the epoch of long ago this lake kept a sort of calendar and was estimated to have been in existence for 7,500,000 years.

During this time forests grew along its banks. Animal life was abundant, and insects resembled rather closely those now living. 

During a long period Lake Uinta had no outlet and its level was prevailingly low. The organisms lived in great profusion. Their remains accumulated on the bottom almost to the exclusion of all other material. They were not destroyed by a host of bacteria but suffered partial decay. The individual organisms finally turned into a great jelly-like mass and became a preservative to all organisms and plant life entombed. During this period thousands of layers were deposited and hardened. This mass became known in later years as Gilsonite, a crude form of oil. Oil shale was thus formed. So plentiful were the micro-organisms and so long did 

Lake Uinta persist that its deposits now contain locked up the equivalent of more than 70 billion barrels of crude oil. COAL Many undeveloped bituminous coal fields and one anthracite field exist in the Uintah Basin. According to government estimates there are 173,000,000,000 tons of coal. 

Several coal mines were opened early in Uintah County, some of which are still operating. A large vein lies north of Fruitland and was operated by Heber Hall for a few years. 

OIL SHALE 

The Uintah Basin contains the richest oil shale deposits known. 

In addition to all of the private holdings the United States Govern ment Naval Oil Shale Reserve No. 2 obtained vast holdings. These shales are particularly adapted to successful mining by steam shovels but, due to the high cost of transportation, have not been developed as any great industry. Ammonium sulphate will be a by-product from the production.

Asphalt sands mined here have been used for many years for street paving in almost every small town. There is a mountain of it. 

IRON AND COPPER 

There are three large deposits of high grade iron ore. None of them have been developed. Uintah Canyon has several filings on Ore Claims. Copper has been found in small quantities, but because of transportation there has been no development of this industry.

Phosphates have been located in vast quantities and it has been predicted that this will be one of the biggest industries in the Basin. 

Cinnabar and Ruby silver were discovered by W. M. Ryan and W. G. Reynolds in Uintah and was reported in the Vernal paper, May 17, 1891.

GILSONITE AND ELATERITE 

Uintah Basin contains large deposits of gilsonite and elaterite. 

United States Government reports estimate 150,000,000 tons of gilsonite in sight. The story of Gilsonite is a unique one. It has been told by many that they discovered it. The Indians knew about it very early. In fact they used it to water-proof their baskets long before the advent of the white man. As early as 1879, we have reports of its being heated as coal in government forges. S. P. 

Dillman reports that he took some in that year to Whiterocks. He was then the Government Farmer for the Indian Service. It melted, and they couldn't use it. Others have reported their finding in the old Strip, near Fort Duchesne. Sometime in the 1870's Sam Gilson investigated the mineral, and it was named after him. These veins were mnied in the early 1890's. 

Tom Taylor was one of the earliest operators of Gilsonite in the Uintah Basin; he was manager of the mine at the Strip Mr. and Mrs. Tom Taylor, who operated when the famous explosion octhe gilsonite mine at the Strip in 1890. curred.

Since that time there have been various companies operating and many mines have been opened in various parts of the Basin. 

The Raven Mining Company was among the first. The Pariette and Castle Peak are the best known in Duchesne County. The

Bonanza District contains six large veins of Gilsonite. Among those who have been most prominent in developing Gilsonite in Duchesne 

County are: Joseph Holder, Charles Neal, Fred Ferron, Heber 

Hall, and George Stewart.

Laterite mines were very early operated in Strawberry canyon. 

The old tunnels can still be entered through ancient timbered shafts.

Bentonite is a very valuable clay used in the manufacture of paper, paint, soap, and explosives. There is a large deposit in the 

Basin. 

MINES ROADS TO THE MINES IN 1891 

The road ran north on Vernal Street through Steinaker's gap to McKee ranch, across Big Brush Creek to Mines between Big and 

Little Brush Creek.

In the early development of the Basin the Victoria District was opened. The Rangley Test Well was sunk in 1891. W. R.

Greens operated a coal mine and sold coal at that time for $3.00 a ton.

THE DYER MINE 

Many old mines who history belong to Uintah County were the Dyer, the Bassett, the Enterprise Tunnel, and Old Dominion.

There seemed to have been a gold rush in the Basin in 1891. A city sprung up known as Parson City. Gold was reported found. 

Claims and new mines were located by the dozens. Only a few survived.

During the early history of the Basin there were several mine disasters. One of the coal veins was set on fire in the early 1880's and burned for twenty years. At Dragon a gilsonite mine caught on fire. In 1890 the mine at the Strip burned. The Pariette mine had a catastrophy in the early 1900's. An account was attempted to make a record of these disasters, but, upon interviewing thirty people who were living near the mines at the time, verifications from one to one hundred and thirty people were reported killed in a single explosion. There have been five men who stated that they were the first ones down the shaft after the disaster. Until more data has been compiled it is impossible to give an accurate account of these disasters. However, evidence gives Arch Jenkins 

Gilsonita Mines

MINE 

Gilsonite Mines

Parritte Mine in early 1900's. Arch Jenkins, in front row, contributed this picture.

the credit of being the first one down the Pariette mine shaft to rescue the three men entombed there. 

Remembered by all the early residents is the gallant gentleman, Colonel Timms of the Uteland Mining Company between 

Ouray and the Homer Edwards and Val Wightman homestead. 

Quite an extensive copper mining plant was installed by him with the aid of Val Wightman and the Colonel's colored valet. 

THE HISTORY OF TRANSPORTATION IN The Duchesne COUNTY 

The greatest contribution to the development of a virgin country is the construction of roads and highways. The story of transportation in Duchesne County is a long and interesting one. 

In the beginning Indians came on foot over the high passes of the mountains, possibly following the trails of the animals such as deer, elk, buffalo, and coyote into the Basin. Later with the coming of the Spanish into New Mexico the Indians traded furs and hides for horses. They even bartered their boys and girls in a slave trade for Spanish shawls, beads, and horses. 

Following the Indians came Escalante and his company, the explorers, traders and trappers, such as the Wm. Ashley party, the 

Reed party, the Robideaux brothers and their disreputable trader companions, and the missionaries on foot, mules, or horses. Finally the government officials for the agencies, the pioneer settlers, and the stockmen made their advent into the country. All used the 

Indian paths and were usually led by an Indian guide. 

The first of these old trails was known as the Eastern Part of the Old Spanish Trail. The first section of it lay between the Santa 

Fe, Toas, and Abiquin and the junction of the Uncompahgre

River with the Grand River. The next section was between the Uncompahgre River and Grand River junction and the confluence of the Green River, White River, and the Uinta River. The last section lay between these Rivers and the Utah Lake and the Salt Lake 

Region. 

Escalante's trail has been described. It is briefly from the 

Crossing of the Father's near Jensen, about four or five miles above, just near the Quarry, west up the Uintah and Duchesne Rivers, to where the Strawberry River enters the Duchesne River, and along Strawberry River ot the Divide and down Spanish Fork Canyon into 

Utah Valley. 

Another trail led westerly along the Duchesne River and up

Strawberry River through Strawberry Valley, thence northerly down 

Daniels Canyon to Heber Valley. 

Another trail followed Duchesne River over the Wolf Creek

Pass, and then down Lake Creek to the Heber Valley. 

There was still another up the Uinta River to its head, passing old Fort Wintey. or Robideaux and thence over the top of the

Uinta Mountains, passing Fox Lake and down along Smith's Fork of the Green River to the Fort Bridger Country. 

Over the divide into Wyoming from the Uintah Canyon a trail 

Shale Dugway (Uintah Canyon) 

Roberts Pass (High Uintas)

Early Ox Team 

Freighters on Price-Myton Road

passes by a series of beautiful lakes. Fox Lake is the largest and is now one of the reservoirs for the Dry Gulch. It was named by 

Ray G. Labrum, who was one of the first men to go over the trail before the opening of the reservation. Just as he passed, a fox ran out from the brush along the shore, and he spoke of it as Fox Lake. 

Another trail into the High Uintahs leads from Uintah Canyon to Chain Lakes and over Robert Pass into Atwood Basin, making a loop through Painter Basin and down Uintah Canyon past Fox

Lake over the Divide into Wyoming. Chain Lakes are named because there are several lakes that form a chain. In the early 

1920's, Wm. Woolfe, employed by the Dry Gulch Irrigation Co., started a series of dams to impound the waters. Lake Atwood was also started at this time and as yet hasn't been finished completely. 

Louie Galloway spent some time supervising their construction. 

Robert's Pass was named in honor of George Roberts, who used this section as a sheep range and helped to make the trail. The trail down the Roberts Pass is very rugged. It is made through a slide of large boulders. The trail has been hammered through the rocks. Once a horse slipped, and it was impossible to get him back. 

His bones are still bleaching where he fell. One forgets the hazardous risk because the scenery is absorbing. Nowhere in the world can more beautiful views be found than looking from the Roberts Pass in the High Uintahs.

There are no records available as to the very first roads, Robidoux mentions a wagon in his accounts, but whether he brought one into the Uintah Basin isn't definitely proved. The first establishing of a road from the Eastern part of the Basin is given in the 

E. D. Berthoud's journal of his trip from Denver to Utah Lake, by J. D. Doty: 

"In the month of July, 1861, the Central Overland Mail

Company fitted out an expedition under the command of Mr. 

Berthoud, known as one of the best engineers in the Pike's Peak gold region, with the old mountaineer, Major Bridger, as guide, for the purpose of exploring a new route for a road from Denver City to Salt Lake City. This route lay westward by the sources of the Blue and Yampah river, and through the Colorado and Uintah valleys."

They made a raft and ferried their provisions across. They drowned two mules here. This is the first record of a ferry on 

Green River. If the other traders and trappers of the 1830's had one, the account hasn't been discovered to the present date.

From the Journal of George W. Bean, pioneer of 1847, copied for Mrs. Dillman by Flora Bean Horne.

"In February, 1852, Indian Agent Stephen B. Rose decided to make a trip into Uintah Valley to see and get acquainted with the Indians and the country they inhabited and for the purpose of doing some trading, at least those in company. The party consisted of Major S. B. Rose, myself as Interpreter, E. W. Van Etten, William D. Hendricks and Farley, and an 

Indian of Wanship's band, Salt Lake City, as Guide. "We traveled with pack animals and went by way of Spanish Fork Canyon, and when we reached what is now known as Soldier Summit we encountered deep snow."

After telling of the experience with Chief Tabby's band and hunting antelope, "After resting a day or two at the Indian Camp, we went on over to the North Fork of Uinta river where Louis Roubidous' Trading Post formally stood for twenty-five years. The old Frenchman had left and gone to California where I saw him in 1855 living on the Santa Anna river."

They didn't get much trading done with the Indians. 

One of the earliest reports on the possibility of roads into Uintah Basin is quoted from a letter written by Amos Reed, a government employee of the Indian Service: 

Sir: 

Great Salt Lake City, Utah Territory 

September 12, 1862 

On the 20th of August, I left this city to execute your orders previously received, to make an examination of the valleys of the Uintah river and its tributaries, with a view to their adaptability for a settlement therein of the Utah Indians. I returned yesterday, having been as far east as Colorado, or Green river, at the mouth of the Uintah. Accompanying me were C. A. Huntington, an Indian interpreter, S. M. Johnson, as guide, Caleb Rhodes and Richard

Pangborn, as guides. These men were previously engaged for expedition by your direction. Most of them were somewhat acquainted with the country we went to examine, having hunted and trapped therein.

I took the ambulance to Rhodes prairie, a valley in the Wasatch-Wausatch, (This is spelled as it is in the report-Md) mountains, forty miles southeast of this city and containing good road. From thence we rode horseback and used pack animals. We traveled east from Rhodes prairie, up Beaver creek, a tributary of

Weber river, for ten miles. Here we crossed the Divide over slight elevation between the waters of Weber and Timpanogos or Provo River. Four miles southeast brought us to Provo river, up which we passed for five miles to the summit of the Wausatch Mountainside, the "divide" between the waters of the Great Salt Lake and

those flowing into the Gulf of California, through Green and Colorado rivers. On this summit is a beautiful valley, in size about one mile or two miles, finely watered by springs and creeks, and surrounded with timber. To this point a good wagon road is practicable. We descended from this summit southeast, through a steep canyon, the best pass we could find, but entirely too steep for a road to be built, for six miles, and reached a branch of the northern and principal fork of the Duchesne river, along which is a valley of excellent land, very suitable for cultivation, about two miles wide by four miles long. This branch empties into the main stream, ten miles northeast, from which points the river runs a little south of east. From this junction, extending down the river about sixteen miles, is a valley, varying from two to six miles in width, of the very best quality of land, extremely well watered and timbered. On the bluffs on either side are broad table lands of fine stock on the grass. Everything indicates for this place a warm climate and mild winters. It is by far the finest section of country that we have seen on the trip. In the bluffs along the north side of this valley are vast quantities of red sandstone. A specimen which I hand you. From this, to designate the valley we called it the "Red Butte Valley." Below this valley for fifteen miles, the bottom is narrow, and can only be cultivated in small places. Here is a large fine stream that comes in from the north, on which its branches the men with me say are small but good valleys, fit for cultivation, from this stream, down the river 18 miles to where this fork empties into Duchesne river, the valley is from one and one-half to two miles wide, generally good soil, and portions of it can be irrigated and cultivated. From this junction to the junction of the Duchesne river with Uintah river, the bottom continues from one to two miles wide. Some portions of it can be cultivated; but alkali begins to make its appearance in the soil. The bottoms of the two rivers unite three or four miles from the junction, and some portions of them have been overflowed the past season, which, however has been an extraordinary season for snow and rain, and it may not overflow in ordinary seasons. About the junctions of the 

Duchesne and Uintah rivers is a strip of timber say three miles long by one mile wide, of cottonwood, quaking aspens & c. The rivers also have trees along their banks. The Uintah, which here comes in from the north, has a wide strip of timber; and for fourteen miles above the junction, has a bottom from six to eight miles wide of good land, which could be irrigated by damming the river above and turning a portion of its water at the same time making a good water-power. This is a fine location, and is the one designated to you by Antero, one of the Utah Chiefs, as the place where he wished a house built for himself. Above this valley comes through a canyon three or four miles long, above which the bottom

widens again to five or six miles; some of it good for cultivation. 

Here the Uintah is formed by the junction of three forks coming in, respectively, from the northwest, north and northeast, on which the men with me say are good valleys, and excellent grazing, and considerable tillable land. It is at the junction of these streams that 

Robideaux, years ago, had a post for trading with the Indians. From the mouth of the Duchesne the Uintah runs nearly due east to Green river, fourteen miles, the bottom from one to two miles wide till near its mouth, where they unite with those of Green river. 

Green river is well skirted with timber, has a bottom, including both sides of the river, of ten or twelve miles wide, presenting a very fine appearance. The soil, however is by no means as good as that we have passed over, containing too much alkali; but still no more, I judge, than some lands in this Salt Lake Valley, which grow fair crops. I did not cross Green river, there being no good ford; but the lands on the east side had the same general appearance as those on the west.

In returning we followed back our own trail to where we first reached the Duchesne river when going out. We then followed up the Duchesne to where the Strawberry river joins it from the west, the Duchesne coming in from the southwest; then up the Strawberry to a point about thirty-five miles from the mouth of the Duchesne. Thus far these rivers have no bottoms worth mentioning, running generally through deep gorges in the mountains. Finding the ascent of the Strawberry difficult, we left it entirely, crossing over the mountains to the northwest about ten miles, where we came into a fine open country on a tributary of the Strawberry, having small bottoms suitable for cultivation, and on either side a wide scope of excellent grass and grazing land, extending north nearly to Red Butte Valley, which we considered about ten miles distant. 

Through here the Overland Mail Company laid out their new road from Denver City to Utah Lake last year. We passed up the 

Strawberry, leaving it to the south for some distance to avoid the hills, and crossing several of its tributaries with small but good bottoms, till we entered Strawberry valley on its southeast side. This valley lies from northwest to southeast, is about twenty-five miles. long and varying from six to twelve miles wide. It is intersected by numerous small spring creeks, which unite, forming the Strawberry river, flowing out of its southeast corner. This is a beautiful valley of very superior grass and grazing land, but is supposed to be altogether too high to be useful for cultivation. Its altitude may be judged from the fact that, on coming out at its northwest end, we had only to make a gentle ascent-by no means difficult for a wagon for thirty or forty rods, and we were again on the summit of the Wasatch mountains, the divide between the waters of Great Salt Lake and the Gulf of California. This is also the western

boundary of the Uintah reservation. It is a gradual rise from Green river to this summit, and a good road is not only practicable, but of easy and comparatively cheap construction. From this summit we descended to Round Valley on the Provo river, through Daniels' Creek Canyon, sixteen miles, which has a very gradual descent, and admits of a good road being built up it. It is now filled with a thick growth of timber and small brush. I am informed there are good passes for roads out of the south west side of Strawberry valley, down streams entering Utah Lake at or near Spanish Fork and Springville, thus affording a nearly straight road from Utah Lake to the mouth of the Uintah river, and indeed, to Denver City and Leavenworth · on the Missouri river.

The Uintah Indian reservation is entirely free from white settlers. It may be considered-the bottoms, the table land and even the mountains- -as a fine grazing country. The valleys are as I have described. It is well watered and abundantly timbered. The climate is warm and from all accounts the winters are so light as not to prevent stock living on the range and doing well all winter. I consider it most admirably adopted for a permanent location of the Indians in small settlements on its various streams. Of course an agent would be required to reside in their midst, whose proper location would unquestionably be on the Uintah river, the place designated by Chief Antero for his residence. From this place he could with facility visit and superintend all settlements into the reservation. 

Very respectively your obedient servant, Amos Reed, Hon. James Duane Doty, Clerk of the Superintendency 

Superintendent of Indian affairs, Utah Territory

It has already been mentioned that Brigham Young sent a road making party and a survey party into the Basin in August, 1861. The following was printed in the Deseret News of September 15, 1861. "Levi Stewart and E. W. Van Elten arrived in Great Salt Lake City with an express from the Uintah County saying that they had not found a good country as anticipated. The President gave orders to 

John Lytle to call back the road makers."

The following description of one of the roads that came into the Basin before 1897, and traveled by many of the settlers of Old Ashley, is quoted from an account given by S. P. Dillman, who arrived in 1877, with William and Finn Britt to Ashley Valley. "It took twenty one days to come from Heber to Ashley Valley. 

We came up Daniels Canyon and crossed the Creek thirty-six times. 

We traveled through Strawberry on the right hand side of the valley. 

The road goes on the left side now. We continued up Coal Canyon and down by Soldier Spring up to the head of little Deep Creek.

We then cut to the left of the mountain over to Currant Creek, then about three or four miles down Currant Creek, pulled over to Red Creek, thence over a cedar ridge to Duchesne river close to

Arnold Reef once said, "They are good roads under two feet of mud." 

First stage from Price to Fort Duchesne

First automobile stage from Price to Vernal

the Tabby Mountains. We then followed the Duchesne river down to a point of rocks, near Rock Creek, then three or four miles and climbed out on to Blue Bench. We crossed Blue Bench to a point of rocks over to Lake Fork, near Red Cap. "It had been a week or more since we left Heber and we had seen only one human being. We met John Bennett who drove his team and wagon so far out on the side of the road that we didn't stop nor talk to him. He just waited for us to pass. We did meet old 

Billy Gibson, and he pulled out for us to pass. We both stopped our wagons and waited for each other to break the silence. Finally, old 

Billy, said, "Well, Gentlemen, let's all pray." They all laughed and chatted about the country and roads that were ahead of them, one reporting on eastbound and the other on the westbound. "From Lake Fork we took across the country to the Uintah River. It was too early to make camp so we went on to where Deep 

Creek flows into the Uintah river and turned off the road that leads to Whiterocks. We thought that we could save time and distance by following along the road that lead to where Lapoint now stands. We thought that we could cross a stream and go directly into Ashley Valley, but there was no road there at all. We simply had to follow the creek and on up to the LaPoint country. When we reached the crossing there was no food for the mules. We had to camp but went on the next day to greener pastures near the Dodd's Twist. We had no water here and I went on to get a drink of water. I had to have one. I finally, after walking over hill and down hill, and over another hill, for twenty miles almost, came to Ashley Creek. It was here that 

I later staked by first homestead." 

He said that there was no road that led to Duchesne. Nigger

Heaven was on the cedar ridge that he describes— This was the old agency road that he followed until he turned off on the Uintah River where Deep Creek enters. The road continued up the Uintah River to the Whiterocks agency.

Dewey Merriweather described the old road as follows: "The road came through Strawberry, thence to Deep Creek, north to 

Fruitland, over Nigger Heaven, and the Dead Ox to Meeks Valley.

It then folowed up Rabbit Gulch, West Branch, to the Hill by John Madson's ranch. From here the road divided. One went to the 

Headquarters of the Government Station, and it didn't hit Duchesne.

It followed the rim of Blue Bench, crossed Northeast to Cedar Hills, Boneta Bench to Ed Cox Place. It continued on to a parting at 

Ed McConkie's at Boneta. One road went East across Lake Fork on a line. The other north through the west part of Boneta Flat to 

Jesson's place, crossed the river and on to Dry Gulch No. 1, up the river to east side of Lake Fork to Forks Yellowstone and Lake Fork to Whiterocks. In 1906, two men were crossing Lake Fork and one man drowned. At the Ford at Lake Fork where one road parted

west to Mt. Sheep Cabins on Pigeon Water and down the east side of Lake Fork northwest to Moon Lake. This was before 1906. "On Rock Creek nearly east from the present Indian Ranger

Station the road parted and one went down to the old Rock Creek Agency, and on up to Still Water. The other went over the west side of Farm Creek Pass to Stockmore."

With the advent of a stage line many stations sprung up along the road. The early travelers remember the Rockhill's on Soldier 

Creek. "Liz" and her cooking was popular with thousands who stopped both on Soldier Creek and after she moved to Fruitland where she lived many years. She seldom left her little store, but she always managed to find time to attend a Republican Convention. 

Bether's station at the top of Daniels canyon was a popular stopping place for many freighters.

The Price-Myton roads were made mostly by the soldiers from 

Fort Duchesne. From Price the road leads Northeast up Soldier

Canyon. Tall tales are told of this road, especially when it was muddy. One officer from Fort Duchesne relates about a trip where the horses or mules even had to be held on the road, a part of which had slipped off during the storm. His wife was panic stricken. Soldier 

Station operated during the entire occupancy of Fort Duchesne.

Crossing over the divide it dropped into the Minnie Mand Creek, known as Nine Mile.

There were many stops in Nine Mile. The Brock place, where the Nutter ranch is located, was the last stop out of Nine Mile and perhaps one of the earliest. Ed Lee ran the station where mules and horses were changed, and often passengers stayed over night. Mr. 

Lee came from Payson to Nine Mile about 1888. There are records of its being in operation and used by the Soldiers and freighters before 1890.

MAIL 

The first contract for mail from Price to Vernal was let July 

1, 1886 to Tom Miles and Joe Black from Huntington, Utah, with

Tom Miles as the driver. The first day they went as far as Ed and 

Tom Taylor's ranch in Nine Mile, a distance of about forty miles. 

Next morning they went as far as the Well's Station, which was run by Owen Smith. There they met another driver who took the mail to Vernal, changing horses at Ft. Duchesne, and sometimes at the Bridge (now called Myton). Later on a contract was let to Tom Taylor and Ed Lee, for a daily mail from Price to Vernal. 

Coaches with four horses were used. Horses were changed at 

Soldier's Canyon and at Taylor's and Lee's ranch, the Wells Station and Ft. Duchesne. A Post Office was established at Lee's Ranch in Harper, to accommodate the people in Nine Mile Canyon. I was one of the first passengers to go over the route with Tom Miles.

Many men from Price were drivers on that stage line, among them 

Arthur Burch, Oswald Barlow, and Miles Black. The last contractor was Tom Whitmore, whose line was managed by his son, June Whitmore. When the mail route was purchased by the Uintah 

Gilsonite Company, the run was from Mack, Colorado to Vernal and Ft. Duchesne, Utah.-MRS. TOM TAYLOR. 

Down the canyon a few miles is the Egan ranch. It was used for a while, and sleeping quarters were started but never finished. 

The story of Nine Mile stage road wouldn't be complete without Frank Alger. He drove the stage for years from Price to Myton. 

Mrs. Neal Hanks, who at that time was Mrs. Hank Stewart, cooked for the passengers at the Alger ranch. 

The old rock house, built by the Harmon family, also had its share of helping to take care of the stage passengers. 

On this road the first telephone to Price had its headquarters, and the telegraph office was built of sawed logs and is still standing on the Nutter ranch. The poles were all iron. The freighters and 

Indians burned the wooden poles that were first used.

Many old freighter talks of a wonderful cook by the name of Martha, an Indian woman who lived at the home of Don C. Johnson (or G. C.) They always tried to plan their trips so that when night would overtake them they would camp at the Johnson ranch in order to eat Martha's good homemade bread and pies. As old timer said, "And could she cook meat!""

From Nine Mile the road went up Trail Canyon and entered 

Gate Canyon. Travel was never safe during July or August. Rains brought flood water pouring down in torrents. It was not uncommon to have the creek swell fifteen feet in fifteen minutes. The barren cliffs didn't hold back the rains. Many cars have been caught and overturned. Cattle have drowned before they could climb to safety. After one of these storms, most of the road would be washed out, and a new road built before the journey could be resumed. 

There was no water between Gate Canyon and Myton most of the year. In 1890, a Mr. Smith conceived the idea that a well in the draw over the divide from Gate canyon would be a blessing and a good place for a station watering place for cattle and freight teams. The well was dug about two hundred feet and did prove a blessing. The canyon was known as the Well's Draw. 

From the Well's Draw the route took over the Bench into 

Myton. From Myton it left the Duchesne River and followed a draw just south of Roosevelt, through Independence Country following up Bottle Hollow into Fort Duchesne. After the founding of Roosevelt, the road came over the Bench past Theo Phillip's place and north into Roosevelt.

In 1915, Superintendent Kneale made a road that was known as the Kneale Highway. It was built from Myton to Fort Duchesne.

The road followed the Duchesne River until it reached a point just south of the Fort. Here the road crossed the river and continued north to Fort Duchesne. 

One of the early roads came via Colton, through Whitmore

Park up Willow Creek, over the Divide and down into Indian 

Canyon following Indian Canyon to Duchesne. This road was used as a stage line in the very early history of the Basin. There were several stops for the stage along the way. In winter it was very difficult to cross over the divide because of heavy snows, and a few times men carried the mail on their backs from one station in Willow Creek over the divide to the Station in Indian Canyon. 

A road came from Castle Gate up Spring Canyon and joined the Colton road in Whitmore Park. This was built by prison labor during the administration of Governor Bamberger. A prisoner built a monument honoring Governor Bamberger at this time and placed it in Whitmore Park at a point where the two roads meet into 

Duchesne County. 

Before 1909 A. M. Murdock established a stage line from 

Colton to Duchesne which was in operation for many years. He also organized the Duchesne Stage and Transportation Company about 1918. In the early history of the Basin many privately owned stage lines were operated.

The story of the road coming from the East belongs to Uintah County history, but the swinging bridge over White river, the toll bridge and ferry at Ouray, the Corduroy road over the Carter 

Dugway and Taylor Mountain belong to the unique history of

Uintah Basin. 

ROADS 

The Gilson Asphaltum Company is supposed to have organized the Uintah Railroad Company. They constructed a narrow guage railroad from Mack Colorado, on the D. & R. G. Railroad to

Dragon, Utah, a distance of about 65 miles. This is one of the most unique roads of the United States for it ascends the Book Cliffs at a maximum grade of seven and one half percent and a maximum curviture of 67%. The road ascends to an elevation of 8700 feet at Baxter Pass. From the end of the railroad at Dragon a road was built to Vernal and Fort Duchesne. 

Permission was granted this company on June 1, June 5, and

July 11, 1905 to survey and locate a line of railroad to construct a toll road and to operate a ferry in the Uintah Reservation in Utah. The authority was granted in deference to the wishes of the War Department since the immediate construction of the toll road, and operation of the ferry would greatly facilitate the handling of supplies for that department. The company's employees were permitted to enter the reservation under restrictions intended to safe-

Wagon Bridge 

Duchesne City.

Top, left-Fort Duchesne, built in 1882 under the supervision of S. P. Dillman. He paid 50c a day for labor; $1.50 for man and team. The bridge was still standing in 1939.

Top, right-Old swinging bridge over White River 

Early Bridge at Duchesne 

Myton Bridge in 1910

guard the interests of the Indians and the War Department would cooperate with the Indian agent to prevent any illegitimate use being made of the authority granted.

There have been many attempts and organization to bring a railroad into the basin. March 3, 1887, an act granting the Utah

Midland Railway Company the right of way through the Uncompaghre and Uintah reservations, in the Territory of Utah, passed the Forty-ninth Congress. It was to be routed as follows: "Said railway shall enter said Uncompahgre Reservation at a point East boundary line of Utah at or near the place where the White river crosses said boundary line, running thence by the most feasible point to Salt Lake City."

RAILROADS 

Printed in the Vernal Express of August, 1891. "The route of the Utah Central Railroad as specified in the documents recorded in the County is from Salt Lake City to 

Park City now finished, thence via Wolf Creek Pass to Duchesne river and Lake Fork then via Uintah river and Ashley river to the Colorado State line at a point near Popper's ranch 

(Ouray) in Snake Valley.

Maps showing the definite location of this company's proposed line of railroad through the Uintah Reservation, in Utah were transmitted to this office by the Commissioner of the General Land Office on Nov. 4, 1904, and were forwarded by this Office on 

Nov. 11th to the director of the Geological Survey for information as to whether the director of the line as proposed would interfere with the plans of the reclamation service. On December 20th the 

Geological Survey advised the Office that the railway location between railroad stations 450 and 730 appeared to be partly below the level of the water line of a proposed reservoir site in the Strawberry Valley and was recommended that the maps be approved only upon the acceptance by the railroad company of the following stipulations: 

"That the right of way is accepted subject to the right of the United States to cross the same and the works constructed thereon with any canals or water conduits of any kind or with telephone or electric transmission lines which may in the future be built by the United States, or to flood the lands in the Strawberry Valley below elevation 7,538.5 feet above the United States Geological

Survey datum, and the United States is hereby released from all damages which may result there from.

The company through its attorneys in the city, on March 7, 1905 declined to accept the stipulations suggested by the Geological Survey contending that it had prior vested rights to a right of way over the proposed reservoir site. Further report was made by the 

Geological Survey on March 30th, adhering to its former recommendation on the ground that the approval of the maps without such imposed conditions would result in the abandonment of the proposed irrigation projects, there being no other available site for the water storage for irrigation of these lands. The reports of the 

Geological Survey maps, and other correspondence were forwarded to the Department with office reports of March 31. No further action has been taken by this office."

In 1918, Governor Bamberger tried to bring a branch of his rail line into the Basin. He was not successful. 

At a hearing before the Interstate Commerce Commission, July 

27th to July 31st, 1925, before the Honorable Commissioner Henry

C. Hall, the Salt Lake and Denver Railroad Company applied for the right to construct a railroad extending from Provo, Utah, through the Uintah Basin, to Craig, Colorado. This was commonly known as the Moffit line as it would be routed through the famous Moffit Tunnel. Up to the present date the nearest sound to a train whistle that the children of the Basin have heard is the steam whistle of the threshing machine as it announces dinner to a hungry crew of harvesting men.

CHAPTER II 

EARLY CIVILIZATIONS UCHESNE COUNTY is the Treasure House of old civiliza tions. Very little research has been done within its orders. There are still many acres where dwellings once stood, where ancient feet have trod, and where the peoples of different cultures have fought with nature and foes for a bare existence.

In Pleasant Valley a Folsom Complex has been isolated. Some of these ancient people lived over 10,000 years ago. Their chipped artifacts have also been found in other parts of the United States along with the bones of the prehistoric bison, mastodons, sloths, camels and other animals. In Pleasant Valley artifacts have been found that are very similar to those isolated by Dr. Frank H. 

Robers of the Smithsonian Institution on his Lindenmeirer site in Colorado. Doubtless, other places in Duchesne County contain evidences of ancient human culture. 

This primitive man practiced the arts of making fire, sewing skins together with sinew, and using bone awls, chipping rocks to be used as scrapper, gravers, and coup-de-poings. They perhaps had the 

Atlatl also. They lived around water holes where the animals came to drink and could easily be trapped. 

There must have been many civilizations coming between this old Folsom man and the next group of people who have been isolated. These are known as the Basket Makers. They are found in 

Duchesne County and they can be studied best in Nine Mile. Uintah 

County is rich in archaelogy for the study of Basket Makers. 

Ruins of these are found on Farm Creek, Brush Creek, and near Jensen, Florence Creek, and Hill Creek. They have left much to tell of their habits and of their culture. They lived in caves and excelled in basketry. They buried their dead in huge baskets. It has been thought that yet they still used the Ataltl, and that the bow and arrow had not been developed. It was sort of a notched bow or stick which was hurled. The Basket Maker did not engage in very extensive agriculture but depended mostly on seed and wild fruit and game. They did not make pottery, unless it was a crude unbaked type in the later period of their occupation. They made coiled baskets and bags. They wove a snare for catching small game, such as rabbits and birds from grasses. Sandals and perhaps clothing from bushes and bark were also woven. 

There is some disagreement as to the division between the Basket Makers and the Puebloes that followed. However, as one group followed so closely upon the other and as the men were all 44

EARLY CIVILIZATIONS 

45 killed and the women who understood the arts and practiced them would carry them over into the conquerors culture, it is difficult to say just what belonged to Basket Makers and what to the Puebloes. 

It is understood that they were a very different type of people, the Top, left-Folsom Complex in Pleasant Valley. Top, right-Folsom Complex. Center left-Baskets from ancient dwellers. Center, right-Fremont Ruins on ledges in Nine 

Mile. Bottom-Pit Dwellers Fire Pot (Jim Reed and Mary Harris, above Ft. Duchesne)

Basket Makers being a long narrow headed type and the Puebloes being rather broad and short in stature. 

QUOTING FROM THE BOOK "UTAH", (Page 32) 

"The Storage cists were enlarged and improved until they took the form of semi-subterranean dwelling places. These pit houses and slab houses, as they are called, were constructed in excavations from one to five feet in depth. Sometimes four posts were set up at some distance inside the pit and their tops connected with horizontal beams. Slanting walls of poles and adobe construction connected the beams with the periphery of the pit. The flat roof of the same construction was provided with a smoke hole which, having perhaps also a side entrance, must have looked somewhat like a small mound of earth. In other types of houses the roof was a simple cone of pole and adobe construction with the base extending to the edge of the pit. The other important development in this period designated as Basket Makers III, or as modified Basketmaker, was the Ceramic Arts. These were the invention of fired pottery sometimes painted with crude black designs on a gray background." 

These are the people who lived here in great numbers. Their villages can be traced over many acres. In Arcadia one can walk for ten miles and still find the ruins of these old Pit houses. They are scattered all over the Basin. Some archaelogists have called them Puebloes.

There still may be a possibility that these people might have been related in some way to the Fremont people who are found in Nine Mile. There has been no extensive research upon this subject, but their culture was somewhat similar. Clay figurines have been found in these Pit dwellings, in addition to game rocks, pottery disks, pipes, and other artifacts known to both cultures. They are thought by the best archaelogists to have lived here about the same time.

The next groups of people who intruded the Basin were short, broad headed groups of people. They developed the bow and arrow and used a groove in their axes. Many fine axes of this type have been picked up in the Basin. There are some of the highly decorated pots and the black and white shards picked up in different complexes, but the most of it is just plain gray. They left their record upon the rocks in many places. However, there was a group of people who did develop agriculture to quite an extent. Whether or not it was Basket Makers III, Puebloes, or the Fremont Peoples, has not been definitely proved. In fact, there has been little extensive study in the Basin, and the ideas of Archaelogists are changing. rapidly as new discoveries are brought to light by added research. 

Just how many and what cultures came can only be drawn by the studies made in others parts of the country. There is one fact

EARLY CIVILIZATIONS 

47 comparatively certain that at one time a great civilization inhabited the Basin. They lived in Pit houses; they were not nomadic in their habits and engaged in agriculture. They made canals and reservoirs, planted corn, beans, and squash. They used huge metates and small ones possibly for seed. They lived in villages. They made pottery of the crude gray kind and chipped rocks for various uses. 

It does not take a scientific person to establish these facts. Many a farmer has overturned these Metates and have taken them home to use as dishes for chickens, dogs, or cats. Many a beautiful specimen has been destroyed by water left in them in the winter to freeze and crack. Thousands of arrow points and broken pieces of pottery have been gathered in great numbers. HP 

Pictographs of Duchesne County

A canal of great interest and wonder may be best seen at the W. K. Dye ranch in Neola. It is comparable to the Whiteman's constructed just below it. It is easily traceable to the Uintah River. 

Another one is found on a bench in Nine Mile just below and opposite Bull's Canyon. The furrows may still be traced where corn and squash residue can be dug. 

Wonder and speculation with many an added theory of just who and when these people came and how they built and lived has been indulged in by the early homesteader. How right they have guessed has been due to the findings of these ruins in their virgin state. There are a few left in this virgin state, and it is hoped that these remaining few can be preserved for future generations to study. 

The most challenging civilization is that of the Fremont Culture isolated in Nine Mile by Dr. Donald Scott. Quoting from the papers of the Peabody Museum of American Archaelogy and Ethnology,

Harvard University: 

THE ANCIENT CULTURE OF THE FREMONT RIVER IN UTAH 

"During the spring of 1928, Mr. Donald Scott, now Assistant 

Director of the Peabody Museum, and Mrs. Scott took a trip along the Colorado from the Escalante to the Fremont and thence into the country near Torrey in the Fremont Valley, making careful notes of any evidences of prehistoric remains. (Dr. Noell Morss made an expedition under the Claflin Emerson project because of interest from Dr. Scott's notes.) "The work of July and August, 1928, revealed an unexpected and interesting situation. To the southeast of the district under examination, across the Colorado river in San Juan County, a rich series of remains had long been known, ranging from the classic 

Basket Makers III sites of Grand Gulch to ruins in the Mesa Verde period. To the west, the explorations of Judd had also established the presence of a definitely Puebloan culture while Nusbaum as well as Judd had found typical Basket Maker II remains. Consequently, we had every reason to believe that any remains found by us would fall readily into the established sequence of Southwestern cultures with only minor local variations. Quite to the contrary, the 

Fremont drainage proved to be the seat of a distinctive culture to which nearly all the local remains are to be assigned. This culture was characterized by cave sites with a slab cist architecture similar to that of the Basket Maker and Pueblo periods; by a distinctive unpainted black or gray pottery; by the exclusive use of a unique type of moccasin; by a cult of unbaked clay figurines obviously related to, but more elaborate than Basket Maker III figurines; by abundant pictographs of distinctive types; and by a number of minor features which tend to identify it as a Southwestern Culture of approximately a Basket-Maker III level, but showed consistently a

EARLY CIVILIZATIONS

Culture). Bottom, left-Walter Atwood studying Petroglyphs. Bottom, right-Interesting 

Petroglyphs.

rugated pottery with other evidence, showed that this complex was contemporary with Pueblo III in other regions. "During a short field season in 1929, the writer confirmed the
