Mr. and Mrs. Lee Snyder and Mrs. Dillman have studied many hours this culture and, upon finding a clay figurine of unbaked origin, wrote to Dr. Scott. His reply was that it was of the 

Fremont Culture and definitely of Aztec origin. 

Many noted archaelogists have made trips to the canyon to study them. Among them was Dr. John Gillin, now of Duke university. 

Dr. Cummings many years ago came into Nine Mine when he was first starting his interest in archaelogy. Dr. Ralph Richards and Rulon Wells spent several days in the early 1900's on the Wm. A. Miles ranch in the interest of the archaelogical findings that were there. The artifacts were taken to the University of Utah. 

Several boxes of findings were gathered by Mrs. Miles and her daughters and sent to the University of Utah at that time. Since then many university professors and individuals have gleaned the ledges and carried away the evidences that would have made 

Nine Mile Canyon a store house of wonder and delight.

The Basin owes a debt of gratitude to Mr. Leo Thorne of 

Vernal, who for years preserved at his private museum much of the early artifacts of the Basin. He kept the history with his file of thousands of pictures that he carefully took over a long period of years.

Dr. A. Reagan, an employee of the Indian service at Ouray, Utah, compiled several scientific studies upon the ethnology of the Basin; he was the pioneer in this field. 

Clair Brown of Montwell has carefully preserved hundreds of ancient artifacts found in this vicinity. 

Lee and Dora Snyder have done much to preserve ancient cultures in the Basin; they have spent many years with various expeditions.

Marion Penfield tried for many years to awaken the residents to the consciousness of the rich storehouse of primitive material that is in the Basin; he was especially interested in paleonthology; he strived to have fossils left here "in suti".

THE UTE PEOPLE 

Early in the history of the Uintah Basin the Shoshoean People came. They were the ancestors of our modern Ute. 

The Indian is a very misunderstood race. They are not lazy nor savage in their original heritage. Their worship of God is very inspiring. They did not have any word in the Indian language that designated a Diety nor any profane language. They thought that the

Top, left-Ben Wero; Top, right-Tecumseh or Nauhaunan, son of old Chief Tabby. 

Center, left-James Wammeo who knitted over one hundred sweaters for the Red 

Cross during the World War No. 2; Center, right-Mrs. Albert Harris and Mary

Shavenaugh with sagebrush clothing worn by the early Ute people. Bottom, left-Lorena 

Denver, a prominent Indian woman; Bottom, right-A corner in Mildred Dillman's museum.- -Courtesy of the Deseret News.

EARLY CIVILIZATIONS 

53 word "God" was too sacred to be uttered by mere words, as "the Worship of the Great Mystery" was silent, solitary, free from all self seeking. It was silent because all speech is of necessity feeble and imperfect; therefore, the souls of the Indian ascended to their 

God in wordless adoration. "The Soul of the Indian."

They do not worship the sun any more than the Christian worships the cross. It is an emblem to them, just a continuation of their love for their own parent. The Earth represented the mother, and all creation came from her with a union of Father Sun. Their religion was deep. Their family altar was sacred, and honor cherished. It was not until the advent of the whiteman's practices that they grew corrupted.

Perhaps their culture can be told by part of a pageant written and produced by Mildred Dillman at the Uintah Basin Industrial 

Convention in 1937. 

Harken, Spirit of Uinta. 'Tis a people calling clearly, Weary footed, traveling nearer,

Ever seeking hunting grounds, Ever roaming o'er the prairie, Climbing plateaus lined with cedars, Gathering herbs in the high mountains; 

Heard they far, your invitation, Came they drifting o'er the mountains. 

Over Kaib high and rocky,

From the land of Tabby Moushy, 

From the land of rising sunshine, From the West they came from Provo,

From the land of Tabby Yaikway, 

Hunters fleet with bows and arrows, Roamed thy basin seeking foods.

Peaceful tribes were Ute Shoshones 

Only asking for a living,

Thus to keep from famine loved ones, Thus to make from skins their clothing. 

See them hiding in a dug out, On the trail of the fleet red deer, On the path of Teah, Kone-unts; 

Waited for him passing downward, Shot the deer from hiding places,

While he sought for pah the water, Hunted mountain sheep in like ways, Sought the Na-gadz on the ledges,

Drove in herds the Indian Quintsin, Many thousand buffalo shot they. 

Gave a dance while hunting buffalo, Put on horns to go among them, Made a chief to honor buffalo, Shot him with their bows and arrows,

Skinned his tough hide with a stone knife. 

Dried the meat for the long winters,

Smoked it well o're smoldering fires, Used him horns for cups to drink from.

Many legends lives among us of the hunts and tales of wonder: 

How Quintsin was killed by blizzards 

In a Basin to the Northland;

How Shinob, the great chief Spirit 

Angry took away the buffalo. 

Left the Indian here without him; 

For he killed Quintsin for white man.

Many thousand skins were traded, Much good meat was left untasted.

Thus was Shinob made to punish 

Peoples for their wontan wasting.

Hunted they the rabbit Tab-boots, Drove him into thick sage brushes, Shot him with a bow and arrow. 

Killed him with a heavy brush club; 

Had a chief that rose each morning,

While the Indians hunted rabbits. 

Talked to hunters of the Pershi,

Pleaded with the great chief Spirit 

For success each day in hunting.

Skinned the rabbit for their warm robes, Made a blanket soft and furry,

Twisted they each string long cut, From the ears in round thin strips.

Sewed together they with buckskin, Thus to make a warm soft blanket, Thus to fashion them the Mayrup.

Ground his bones to make a soup from, Used the meat baked in the ashes, Carried they the feet for good luck.

Could it be from ancient Indian 

That the superstitious negro 

Made the rabbit foot for luck charm?

Snared him on the snow in winter,

Thus the rabbit was important To these Utes here, happy dwelling. 

Many other animals Used they for their skins and good meat, Such as Gam-boods, the wood chuck;

Parree, the tall handsome elk; Owump-itts the sly badger; 

The fat lazy prairie dogs; 

Called him Tun-uch, Prairie dog, Called the wild pig by Kacheena. 

Made a bait of birch bark hollow

To catch Paw-inch, the beaver. 

Fine for meat was busy Paw-inch,

Was Paw-inch the busy beaver, From his tail a pickled dainty Served in winter meat from beaver. 

Used his soft skins for a blanket, Sewed together for a warm robe.

Like the porcupine, Yer-umpitiz, Easy killed was slow Yer-umpittz, Used his quills for decorations.

Used his tail for combing long hair, Cooked the meat as they did rabbit, In the hot red aspen ashes. 

Made they a robe of wolf and coyote.

Used the weasels skins for shamons, Braided in their hair in worship.

Pavich-ete, long and swift and vicious, Sought, they to be swift as weasels;

Worshiped they the soaring eagle, Thought he came from the great Spirit.

Built a trap to catch Quinance, Dug a hole to hide from eagles, Made a covering over heads 

Of long poles and brush on top part, Baited it with fresh red meats, That the eagle might be caught, When he lighted on the brush trap

Those who hid beneath would catch him. 

Used his feathers for their head dress,

In the dances used his Pee-ab. 

Made a necklace of his sharp claws, Tied on abolone sea-shells, Took the bones in wings for whistles, For the Sundance used these whistles. 

Killed they many Prairie chicken 

With their snares and traps and arrows. 

Brought their families here o'er trails,

Over high green mountain passes. 

Carried household goods on long poles,

Drawn by squaws and half grown children.

Built their houses first of tough reeds Woven over twelve high tent poles, Tied together with the rushes. 

Made a door of woven rushes 

To keep out the wind and sunshine, Always to the east they faced them, Thus to welcome the first warm sunshine. 

For protection from the west winds, To keep out the snow in winter. 

Made their tepis, too of cedar,

Splitting fine the cedar brown bark, Tied it well with fresh green willows. 

Used they buckskin strips for tieing, More secure the long stripped cedar. 

Used they later tall skin tepis Made of elk and oftimes buffalo, Added poles to guide the fire smoke, Stretched them tight like drum in summer.

From the Indians of the great plains. 

Copied they their fine skin tepis.

Summer houses built of willows, Sometimes made of cotton green trees. 

Made in many shapes and sizes, For their eating place in summer, Thus to have some cool protection 

From the winds and sun of summer. 

Made they fire of Sawb, the sage brush, Shredded fine and placed on Cedar wood,

In hole, a stick of greasewood, Turned it back and forth for friction,

Rolled between the palms of strong hands 

Thus a spark to kindle brightly,

Thus did Ute man make the fire. 

Made these old Ute people pottery;

Found a fine clay in the Basin, Dried and ground the bridght red flower 

Growing on the lonely prairie;

Used it wet for glue in pottery, Added sand in a small portion, Wove a basket tight to hold well This finely kneaded red clay; 

Patted on the inside of a basket,

Baked it in a kiln of ashes, Made a fine manure burning, Filled the Basket with manure, To burn well the new made pottery, For three days they burned this pottery. 

Thus a fine design of baskets

Was imprinted in their pottery. 

From the knots on pine trees growing, From the ow-ump in the mountains, Made they cups, ow-oats to drink from, Made they bowls to keep their food in: 

From the horns of many beasts here, Made they cups for Pah, the water.

Made We-itch with fine chipped edges;

Thus to save the skins from red deer, Thus to take the hair from buffalo. 

From the prairie, seeds they gathered, Using them for thickening gravies, Gathering them to make a cereal.

Small black seed from common grasses. 

Used they sunflowers much in winter,

Ground and stored in earth for winter. 

Boiled and dried the seeds of sunflowers. 

Gathered many mountain berries, Dried with pits and made a small cake;

Thus their foods, they store for winter. 

Used they many plants for healing, Mother nature gave them wisdom, From wild flags to make a poultice.

From the flowers on the ditch bank, Of the sweet green fragrant mild mist;

From the sage and rabbit bushes, From the mountain tiny green leaves, Made they tea to cure their sickness, Dug the roots of many wild plants,

Found that they were all quite useful. 

Used the yucca dried for fine soap.

Found a root that made a good dye, Dug from mountains wild potatoes, From the Baldy's dug potatoes, Stored them here in pits for winter; 

Gathered sego roots and ate them,

Gathered pinenuts too, from Pinyon 

Called the useful pinenut-Tewb. 

From the Spanish got they horses; 

Thus they learned to make a saddle, Used a wood for making saddles 

Made the front and back the same height, Covered them with strongest buckskin,

Sewed with sinew tough and sturdy, Decorated them with long fringe. 

Made the stirrup wide of strong wood, Holding firm, the small squaw's wide foot, Made the horn of joined antlers, From the deer that roamed Uinta, Drilled the holes into the deer horns,

Sewed them on with toughest sinews, Padded well the tree with raw hide, Made from thickest hide of buffalo. 

Made a rope by braiding tough bark,

Of the bush that grew by rivers. 

Wove these Ute many baskets, Of the limbs of many willows,

Gathered them and tied in bundles, Put them in the swamp to soften, Much as later Europeans 

Retted Flax in running water.

Used the tow-er-ish, squaw bush, Gathered too, Kan-ab the willow,

Split the wood of softened willows, With tarnup, their strong white teeth, Wove the baskets with a bone awl,

Many shapes for varied uses. 

Many sizes wove these Ute squaws. 

Waterbottles lined, with pitchgum; 

Made a woven lid for bottles. 

Made they, baskets for their pottery, gathered they, their seed in baskets, Corn, Komm-mee and many berries,

Stored in baskets in the tall trees, From the damp for the cold winters. 

Used they baskets small for playing.

Many games with other Indians, Used a basket resonator

For the morache to be played on, Baskets shallow served to winnow

Many seeds and grains for storage, Oftimes colored red and purple, Fine designs were interwoven.

In the baskets by these Indians, Still we find among the old squaws 

Basket weaving is continued;

May they guard well in there keeping 

This old art of basket making.

For their babies to be near them, Always for the papoose safety 

Made they cradles to be carried, On their back, koo-nutz the cradle. 

Shaped them as the pear in Autumn, As the leaf of prickly pears,

Covered them with softened buckskin 

Tanned with brains of deer, this buckskin.

Made a covering of small willows 

Bent and arched to shade the papoose, Often white man came among them, Beaded they with rare designs

Sewed the beads with toughest sinew, Thus to decorate their cradles.

Made sacklike buckskin covering, Thus to strap the papoose safely To this well made baby, koo-nutz 

As the papoose grew in action

On a shawl, it is still carried 

Strapped on back of attentive mother. 

Guarded she from harm her loved one.

In the days of earliest culture 

Skins were rare and often lacking, As the deer was hard to capture

And the elk was high in mountains;

Thus the thrifty Indian women 

Made their cloths of Wap, the cedar, Made their cloths of Suwab, the sage bark. 

Made their sandles soft of sage bark 

Woven loose for meager comfort; 

Later made their clothes of elk skin. 

For their dances, feasts, and weddings,

Used they skin with hair upon it, Thus to line the mocassins 

So their feet would not be bruised 

And the cold of snow be conquered. 

Beaded collars on their dresses 

First with shells from the far ocean,

Often with the teeth of tall elk, Made a cape like shoulder covering, When the Spanish came to southlands

Shaws were traded for the fine furs; 

Thus the customs of the squaw shawl 

Took the place of beaded buckskin. 

When the white men came among them, Manufactured cloth was chosen

Thus to make the sacklike dresses 

That are worn now by the women.

Decorated were these Old Utes 

With the porcupine quilled vests, With a vest of polished bones, Gay in head dress made of feathers. 

From the eagle made they headgear, With their head dress made from badger, From the hair of the grey badger.

Much they painted up their bodies 

With the paint from old Uinta, With the earth they made their colors, Like the sunset made their bodies 

So that Shinob please would see them, So that enemies would fear them. 

Chipped they from the hard flint 

Arrows fine to kill their game fleet.

Many size and shape and color 

Made old Utes their fleet arrows.

Many games were played by Indians 

And were copied by the white men; 

Such as football played by kicking 

Balls of buckskin with a bladder 

Over goals of arched young willows. 

Naked played these ancient warriors 

This game now a national favorite. 

Too-rock i nup was played for pastime

On a painted piece of buckskin; 

Wis-sock played in basket plates, These and many others played they, To relate would take an evening. 

Played a game with bone of guessing, Sang they while they played this bone game.

Many dances had these old Utes. 

Danced they to give vent to feelings. 

Chanted songs to every dancing. 

In the Spring they danced the bear dance. 

For the mating danced the bear dance 

As did those that came before them. 

Danced they telling of brave warriors 

Of the treatment of the sick, Told of scalps and scouts returning 

By their dances in Uinta. 

Often squaws enjoyed dancing: 

When alone they often danced.

Many songs were learned from dreaming. 

Of the Bears they learned their songs. 

Thus the song of Nu-sina, Thus the song of Yellow Hair,

Thus the Dust of the Red Wagon, All were taught by dreams to Indians. 

Many legends told these Indians 

Of the frogs and wolves and coyotes, Of the stars and things of nature;

Thus is handed down to children Still the legends of the old Utes. 

Old Uinta in your keeping 

Still these Utes are happy dwelling. 

Still your promise lingers with them 

That no king has e're ruled o'er them.

Many forts have been erected 

In Uinta for protection 

Not necessary to the white men 

As the Indians in Uinta 

Have been ever peaceful people. 

Thus to white man's history 

Leave we thus the Old Forts story.

Schools were early made for children 

Dwelling on this reservation. 

At the Whiterocks, a Miss Ayers 

Taught the school in 1875.

At Randlett, a school in 1895, Taught by Mrs. Kittie Owens. 

Many years she taught these children: 

Loved was she by all these children. 

Very early came the missions, To the Indian taught religion, Of the white man's faith and teachings.

Many departments were too added 

For advancement of the Indian 

To improve their ways of living: 

Forestry, administration,

Medical care for years was given 

To these natives of Uinta;

In connection with the early events of the Indian, history would not be complete without mentioning Miss Canfield and Miss Carter, lady missionaries, sent to the Indians who loved and revered them; when they returned to their homes, they took a small Indian boy and gave him many opportunities. 

Also Dr. Rose, a lady, helped many, even performing operations on kitchen tables. The Reverend Hershey and Father Talbot served many years in bringing Christianity to the Indians. The founding of the Saint Elizabeth Mission very early has also been a definite help in this direction. Nor should be forgotten, the many merchants at Whiterocks, Randlett, Ouray, Myton, and Wong Sing who extended credit when they needed it. Outstanding in their service are William Colthrap, W. E. Waugh, Robert Marimon, the Curry

Brothers, and Mr. Adams at Randlett. 

Their dealings with white people are included in the two following chapters. 

Some very fine and famous Indian people have been dwellers of Uintah Basin. Among them is Chief Sowiette. He is mentioned many times by Brigham Young in his journals as a Chief of Peace.

EARLY CIVILIZATIONS 

61 He lived just below the old Fort Robideau. In his old age he assigned his chieftianship to Tabby, who carried on the reputation of Sowiette. His son, Nauhannan, still lives at Whiterocks. 

Ouray never came to Uintah to live but his honored mate Chipeta brought her people from Colorado, giving up a lovely home and meadow lands to keep peace and do the bidding of her government who promised her that which she never did receive. The events in the life of Chipeta are typical of the Indian history of our nation. 

She died in poverty, blind, hungry, and suffering from the effects

She has been called "The 

Queen of the Utes." 

Henry Harris was among the Ute Indians of Uintah Basin, whose keen mind and fair attitude keeps alive the old virtues that have been attributed to the Indians of the past. He served as Interpreter for the government and as Property Clerk at old Fort 

Duchesne for years. It was Henry Harris who with Ray E. Dillman ferrited out several treaties and grazing injustices that gave the annuity money that has benefited the Indians so much in the past few years.

Captain and Mrs. Bonin have been nationally honored. For a long period of time they served their people. 

Red Cap, Yellowstone, Wanroads, Unca Sam, Colorow, Dun can, Andrew Frank, Chapoose, LaRose, Cesspooch, Pawwinnee, Reed, Santio, Wash, Shavenaugh, Natches, Harris, Denvers, Wapsock, are a few of the most prominent families of the Utes.

CHAPTER III EARLY EXPLORERS, TRADERS, AND TRAPPERS AND TRADING POSTS 

HE OLD SPANISH TRAIL played a very important part in the opening of history of the Ute Indian. In the South 

West many Franciscian Priests were building missions, trying to Christianize Indians and establishing trails Westward, mainly from Santa Fe to Monterey, in California. Among the most important to us was the expedition of Father Garces and Juan Diaz, who with an Indian guide named Bebastion accompanied Captain Juan Bautisak Anza from New Mexico to Santa Fe. He corresponded with Father Escalante, and compared notes on the country and the Indians. He states that there were Indians on the north of the 

Colorado river-the Yutas. This was in 1775. 

Don Juan Maria de Rivera in 1761 in one of his expeditions with Father Garces, carved his initials on a cottonwood tree at the junction of the Grand and Colorado.

FRAY ALONZO DE POSADO 

The Spaniards now had control of New Mexico, and time could be given to the exploration of the country farther North or the land of our Yutas. Under the administration of Governor Veles 

Cachupin an exploring party was sent out in 1763, to examine the country north of New Mexico which is now the State of Colorado. 

This expedition was accompanied by Father Alonso Posado, who had for fourteen years held a position of ecclesiastical importance in New Mexico.

Father Posado afterwards wrote the "Informe," a history of this expedition, and it is to this "Informe," Escalante refers to in the Diarie of his journey from Santa Fe to Utah Lake. Posado named 

Green River after Father San Beunventure, and Escalante also continued to call it by the same name in his diary. Both encountered 

Yutas and wrote about them in their journals. 

EXPLORERS The Escalante Expedition 

To Eastern Utah belongs the story of the entrance into the state of the first white people who left any written records. 

It is still a disputed question whether Fray Alonso de la Posada came into Utah or only into Colorado. Father Escalante makes reference to the fact that he studied very carefully the travel notes of Fray Alonzo, who had come into Northern Colorado as early as 63

1763. He mentions the Green River, and Father Escalante writes the following in his journal: "This river of San Buenaventure is the largest that we have crossed, and is the same one that Fray Alonzo de Posada alente Crossing on Green River

Father Silvestre Velez de Escalante was a Franciscan Friar of great ability. He had a keen mind and a dynamical personality. 

He was a Spaniard by birth and came from Spain to New Mexico in 1768. During his travels he became obsessed with the idea that a northern route from Sante Fe to the missions in Monterey would be much safer than any other because he thought the Yutas were a less warlike people and the forbidding chasms of the Colorado 

River could be avoided.

Posada records were destroyed by a revolt of the Indians so that to Father Escalante and his party go the credit of being the first white men to enter Utah and cross its lands and keep a record. 

Herbert S. Auerbach established the fact, however, that Fray Francisco Atanasia Dominquez was in charge of the expedition, and that Father Escalante was the scribe. It is recorded, though, that Escalante initiated the project and finally persuaded the civil and church authorities to send the party for the purpose of finding a safer and easier route between the missions. Another noted member of the expedition was Don Bernardo de Mierra Y Pacheco, a famous surveyor and chartographer.

The party entered Utah, September 13, 1776, about seven miles north of Jensen and crossed the Green River, the 16th of September.

They followed the west bank of the river for a short distance, then crossed the tributary streams which they called Rio de San Simon, and Rio Ladeo. On the west bank of the latter river they camped at a place east of the present town of Randlett. The camp was named Magas de S. Franco. 

From there they journeyed west and camped near the Duchesne River, which they called Rio San Damean. About where Myton now is situated was their next stop "A land white and barren, with thinly scattered sagebrush, greasewood, and salt grass." The party then continued west along the Duchesne River, crossing a small stream and camping at the junction of the Duchesne and Strawberry Rivers near the site of Duchesne. Escalante called this place, Camp Saint Catarino. 

On September 19, they followed the Strawberry River and camped on the banks of it where a small stream flowing south from Fruitland, a present "village of ranchers," joins it. They gave this place the name of S. Eustaquio. Escalante described the country as "rolling, barren hills strewn with big boulders of pink sandstone.

Against this pinkish background grew the dwarfed and twisted junipers." They also passed "grey hills and thin grassy plains."

Continuing up Strawberry river they finally came to its head-

waters and rested at Camp Saint Lucia. Before crossing the divide into Diamond Creek, the party camped twice. The divide was named 

S. Lino. Their entrance was made on the 13th of September, 1776, and they left the Basin on September the 24th or 25th, 1776.

TRADERS AND TRAPPERS 

During the age of Traders and Trappers in the history of our West, many of them wandered into the Uintah Basin, but we have no record of them except the stories told by the Indians. Several traders and trappers were here as early as 1820 and named the Green River, Rio Colorado of the West. Others called it Spanish 

River, Seekeedee, Skunden, and Prairie Hen, but Wm. Ashley, who came in 1824, renamed it Green River in honor of his friend who lived in St. Louis.

According to the journal of General William Ashley, a company of well known traders and trappers enlisted by him, among whom were Thomas Fitz Patrick, Robert Campbell, and James P.

Beckwourth, left St. Louis in the fall of 1824, and came west. They embarked upon a perilous voyage down Green River in rawhide boats. After reaching Henry's Fork, just below the Utah-Wyoming boundary line in April, 1825, they established a sort of rendezvous for traders and trappers. Cashing most of their supplies, they then continued their adventure! Many times they escaped death, braving the rocks, rapids, and whirlpools of that treacherous river.

On the rocks near Ashley Falls is inscribed, "Ashley-1825.” 

The men finally reached "Tewinty" (Uintah river) where kind Indians gave them food. The party went on down the river as far as Green River City. Apparently they had had enough thrills on 

Green River, for they came back to the Uintah River via land. From here the party followed the stream up to the junction of the Uintah and Duchesne Rivers and then crossed the Uintah Mountains just above where Stockmore now is located. Then they descended into 

Heber Valley.

FIRST TRADING POST Indians Say Reed Enterprise Preceded Fort Robidoux Near White Rocks Village By MILT JACOBS 

The old Indian, aged and sick, pushed himself up to a sitting position on his bunk and repeated himself. "How can you find something that has never been lost?" 

He was logical.

Our interpreter kept the interview moving. 

We had asked old Nauhnan (son of Chief Tabby), who said he is 106, about the location of Fort Robidoux, the fort which con-

Top, left-Rodidoux at Whiterocks; Top, right-Old Fort in Nine Mile: Center, left-Spanish writing in the Old Fort; Center, right-Close view; Bottom, left-1839, on a rock in Nine Mile, possibly one of Robidoux party. Bottom, right-M. Dillman,

Mrs. Mary Harris and Dorothy on Robidoux's Fort, 1934.

temporary historians of the state have set up as the first year-round trading post ever to be established in Utah.

He had replied to a question of ours regarding the "discovery" of the location of the old fort in October of 1934 by an eastern 

Ph. D fellow of the American Association for the Advancement of Science and the "rediscovery" of the fort site in 1936 by a Utah historian who reported his "findings" in a Utah State Historical 

Society publication. 

Indians Knew of Fort

We sat on a bare wooden bench in the one-room log cabin in which Nauhnan keeps alive on a $10-a-month government pension and the goodness of a few whites.

His cabin is but a few miles from the site of old Fort Robidoux, near White Rocks, Uintah Basin. "Then, you Indians knew where the fort was all the time," 

I said. 

He nodded. "We knew about an earlier trading post. There was a white man the Indians called "Sambo" and the other one called 'Julie'." 

That was what I had been waiting to hear.

Source Ignored 

A trading post before Fort Robidoux! And who should know better than the Indians themselves? Historians have ignored the

Indians, or at least after they have taken the information they need from them have failed to give them credit for their "findings."

We'll skip the rest of the interview with Nauhnan and get down to cases.

From all information available, there was a year-round trading post before Fort Robidoux, and oddly enough it was located just 

20 yards from the site of Robidoux.

Credit for establishing the trading post, which should be called the Reed Trading Post, first of all should go to the Indians, who have known about it all the time, and secondly to Mrs. Ray E. Dillman of Roosevelt, a woman whom all the Indians of the Basin have learned to love and trust.

Mrs. Dillman Shown Site 

As early as 1925, and a picture on this page proves this point, the Indians showed Mrs. Dillman the site of the Reed Trading Post. 

And most important among those who went over the ground of the old Reed site with Mrs. Dillman was Mrs. Mary Reed Harris, now 

88, whose father, Jim Reed, then 13 years of age, was one of four white men who established the trading post.

Mrs. Harris, whose mother was an Indian (half Shoshone and half Ute), is a delight in interview.

Her father used to tell the story of the Reed Trading Post as they rode by the old fire places which stood in ruins for years along side the old road which ran through the present White Rocks area linking Roosevelt country with points north and east. 

The story ran as follows: 

In the year 1828, four white men-Toopeechee Reed, young 

Jim Reed, a nephew of the older Reed; Denis Julien and Augustus

Archambeaux, French traders from Kentucky-entered the Uintah country and set up a trading post near a spring of water just south and east of the present settlement of White Rocks. They brought in the first butcher knives, coffee beans, and other articles ever traded to the Indians for furs. The Indians called Julien, "Julie," and Archambeaux, "Sambo."

The post flourished. 

Reeds Were Preceded

The Reed post personnel were not the first white men to travel through the Basin, though possibly they established the first year-round settlement in the area and the state. Father Escalante traversed the route in 1776. William Ashley, a fur trader, came through there in 1824, and it is believed Antoine Robidoux, who established Fort Robidoux, was through the area as early as 1824. 

Historians have set the date for Fort Robidoux as 1832. 

During the interview with Mrs. Harris we drove to the site where the old spring, now dry, was located. The Reed Trading Post, say the Indians, was located on the west side of the spring, Fort Robidoux on the east. The old road runs near the location of the spring separating the two historic sites. 

When Robidoux, according to Indian reports, decided to build his fort, the Reed post was already established. Mrs. Harris said her father told her that he and the other men sold out to Robidoux. 

Julien Remained 

The elder Mr. Reed returned to Kentucky, where a number of years later he died. Young Jim Reed, Mrs. Harris' father, went on to California. Archambeaux joined the Fremont party, and Julien stayed on at Fort Robidoux until 1836. 

Fort Robidoux, a center for fur traders, had a life of 12 years.

It became a den of iniquity and in 1844 was burned to the ground by revengeful Indians. Every white man at the post was massacred. 

Antoine Robidoux, the founder, and a guide were away from the fort at the time and escaped. Travelers have recorded how Indian women were bought and sold by the whites of Fort Robidoux, how

young Indian children were tied to trees and used as targets, how the whites gambled and drank. 

Years later, young Jim Reed drifted from California back into

Wyoming and married the half-Shoshone, half-Ute Indian girl, and in 1878 brought her into the Uintah Basin, back into the country he had pioneered as a white boy at the Reed post. Mr. Reed lived to be 117. 

Mother Was Mormon 

Mrs. Harris was born in Wyoming. Her Indian mother, she said, was a member of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints, having been baptized as a girl. Brigham Young, it was reported, baptized her Indian grandparents.

After inspecting the historic old sites of the Reed Trading Post and Fort Robidoux with Mrs. Harris and Mrs. Dillman, we drove south several miles to a sandstone cliff upon which Denis Julien scratched his name and the date 1831. There were many names of recent vintage which almost blotted out that of Julien. Early settlers have mentioned finding the name and date on the stone. 

We photographed Mrs. Harris, with one of her granddaughters, at the site. Julien then undoubtedly was in this area before Fort 

Robidoux was established in 1832.

Testimonies of several Indians, now dead, have been recorded as to the authenticity of the Reed Trading Post. These are the possession of Mrs. Dillman, who, with the Indians, established the site of the post.

THE FIRST TRADING POST IN UINTAH BASIN 

Careful research verifies the fact that the first trading post in the Uintah Basin was established between 1826 and 1830, and it was called the Reed Trading Post.

Just across a spring ditch from the Areep's and Pagett's cabins and directly south from Unca Sam's, Mary Van's, and Ivan Duncan's old cabins was a road leading from White Rocks to Vernal. On one side of this road stood the Reed Trading Post. Chimneys of the crude houses in the settlement were standing until the early 1900's, and remains of the post can still be seen. The founders were Wm. 

Reed, his small nephew, Jim Reed, Denis Julien, and Augustus Archambeaux. 

These traders sold the first coffee beans to the Redskins who boiled them for days, thinking they were ordinary beans. They brought the first knives, cloth, and other articles for barter. They measured cloth by laying it on a beaver hide and cutting it the length of the hide.

The Reeds finally left the post and went to Wyoming. William later on went to Kentucky, but Jim stayed and afterwards rode for

the Pony Express under Ben Halliday. When Jim began to age, he yearned to return to the home of his youth. He had married a 

Shoshonian squaw in Wyoming. He brought his wife and big family back to the Basin, where he lived to be a very old man. He built the first two-story house in Ashley Valley in 1878. 

One of his two living children is Mrs. Mary Harris, now nearly ninety years old. Her home is in the vicinity of the old Reed Trading 

Post, and it is she who first told the story and showed the remains of the Post. Many old Indians have also verified the existence of such a place and have told incidents that have happened there as told by their grandfathers.

The name, Denis Julien, is engraved in several places upon the rocks of the Basin (Denis Julien-1831). The Indians called him "Julie." A granddaughter of his by an Indian squaw died recently. 

She was called "Julie" after her grandfather.

Albert B. Reagan, in an article written for the Deseret News, October 6, 1934, says: "In the same year (1831), probably in the month of December, Denis Julien carved his name on "Picture 

Rock," east of Mrs. Daniels' place, east of the Uintah river, about half way between Fort Duchesne and Whiterocks, about six miles southwest of Fort Robidoux. This same Denis Julien, who was a native of St. Louis, carved his name on the rock walls of the Green 

River-Colorado Canyons as "D. Julien, 1836." The last inscription of these is dated "16th of May, 1836," in the upper end of Cataract Canyon, and it is supposed that he lost his life in these swirling waters.

AUGUSTUS ARCHAMBEAU 

Augustus Archambeau evidently was not a member of the 

Robidoux party, although Old Fort Robidoux was built near the old Reed Trading Post, and it was at the fort that he joined Colonel 

John C. Fremont's company. In his diary Colonel Fremont writes: "I strengthened my party at this place (Fort Robidoux) by the addition of Augustus Archambeau, an excellent voyageur and hunter, belonging to the class of Carson and Godey." The Indians affectionately called Archambeau, "Sambo." J. Cecil Alder writes: "The 'Sambo' undoubtedly refers to Archambeau, who went to South Pass, or Pacific Springs, Wyoming, in the late 1840's and was there until about 1860." This coincides with the Indian's account of 

Jim Reed's going to Wyoming and staying there until 1878. 

About 1825, the Robidoux brothers traveled northward along the old Spanish Trail from Toas or Santa Fe to establish a trading post. They called it Fort Uncompahgre after the tribe of Indians of that name in that vicinity. It was also known as Fort Robidoux.

This fort was located on the Grand River near Delta, Colorado. 

From this fort a trail followed along the Green River to Brown's

Hole, to Fort Bridger, to Fort Hall, and to a northwest trading post. 

Another trail led to Fort Wintey, or Uintah, or Robidoux in the

Uintah Basin near Whiterocks. 

This Fort Whitney, or Robidoux, was located not far from Whiterocks, Utah, on the old Whiterocks-Vernal road from the Reed Trading Post. Since its establishment the Indians have shown the site of the old fort to several white people, among them Mildred 

Dillman. Mrs. Mary Harris, Nauhnan, Charley Reed, Provo Nellie, Julie Servee, Unca Sam, "Watermelon" Jane, Henry Harris, Blackhawk, Pete Arkansas, John Duncan, Peter Sacknacket, and Jim Reed are some of the Indians who have been on the fort grounds and related stories about it.

The history of Fort Robidoux is typical of that recorded of early western trading posts. The Indians bartered beaver hides, buffalo skins, and many other articles to traders for horses, guns, materials, and food supplies. From the diaries kept by visitors to the fort come horrible tales. In 1919, Charles P. Everett found the journal of Joseph Williams in an old bookstore. Included in the journal was "Narrative of a Tour from the State of Indiana to the Oregon Territory in the years 1841-42." The book was published at 

Cincinnati in 1843. Williams writes the following of his stop at Fort

Robideau: 

"We had to wait there for Mr. Robideau about eighteen days till he and his company and horse-drivers were ready to start with us to the United States. This delay was very disagreeable to me on account of the wickedness of the people and the drunkenness and swearing, and the debauchery of the men among the Indian women. 

They would buy and sell the squaws to one another. One morning 

I heard a terrible fuss because two of their women had run away the night before. I tried several times to preach to them, but with little or any effect. "I was told here of a Frenchman, who lived with an Indian woman, and when one of his children became burdensome, he dug a grave and buried it alive. At another time he took one of his children and tied it to a tree, and called it a target, then shot at it and killed it.

Robideau had collected several of the Indian women to take to New Mexico to keep for his own use. Some of the Spaniards would buy them for wives. This place is equal to any I ever saw for wickedness and idleness. The French and Spaniards are all Roman

Catholics, but are as wicked men, I think, as ever lived. No one who has not, like me, witnessed it, can have any idea of their wickedness. Some of these people at the Fort are fat, and dirty, and idle, and greasy." 99

Rufus Sage Mentions the Type of Trading 

"A small business is carried on also with the Snakes and Ute Indians, living in the neighborhood of this establishment. The common articles of dealing are beaver, otter, deer, sheep, and elk skins in barter for ammunition, firearms, knives, tobacco, beads, awls, etc." 

The Indians claim that Kit Carson was at the fort in 1833-1834. 

They point to a tree where the Indians traded with him, and on a ledge are the letters K. C. engraved. They also state that Jim 

Bridger came across the trail by LaPoint about this time. 

Myron Eells in his book, "Marcus Whitman, Pathfinder and 

Patriot," relates that Marcus A. Whitman and Dr. A. L. Lovejoy,

Protestant missionaries from Oregon, visited the Fort on October 20, 1842. 

In Nine Mile Canyon, at the mouth of Dry Canyon is a rock inscribed with the date 1839. Spanish carvings are to be found in the old Fort on the Miles ranch indicating that Robidoux men might have ventured early into Nine Mile Canyon.

Brevett Captain J. G. Fremont visited Fort Robidoux in 1844. 

It was shortly after this that the Indians burned the Fort, because of the suffering they had to endure, and the loss of their young people as slaves to the Spaniards. 

Across Green River from Ouray, Utah, in 1833, another trading post was established and used as headquarters by Kit Carson. It was known as Fort Kit Carson. Indians have taken the author to these old ruins, and old Spanish guns have been found by Walter 

Atwood while excavating.

Another trapper, W. A. Ferris, spent October and November of 1833, at Kit Carson. In his book he describes it and gives its exact location. He also tells of pasturing his horses on White Rocks and Altonah bench lands, and wandering over the Brush Creek country. He relates some valuable Indian experiences in his book. 

General Rose and George Bean with Indian guides also visited the Basin in 1852, and stood on the ruins of old Fort Robidoux. He tells of his visit in his diary. 

Extracts from E. D. Berthoud's journal of his trip from Denver to Utah Lake, by J. D. Doty: "In the month of July, 1861, the Central Overland Mail Company fitted out an expedition under the command of Mr. Berthoud, known as one of the best engineers in the Pike's Peak gold region, with the old mountaineer, Major Bridger, as guide, for the purpose of exploring a new route from Denver City to Salt Lake City. This route lay westward by the sources of the Blue and Yampah Rivers, and through the Colorado and Uintah Valleys.

We followed up the valley of the Uintah and the Duchesne Fork of the Uintah, gradually ascending to the Wausatch range,

which we crossed through a very good pass to a stream, which we followed down to its junction with the Timpanogos river." 

The next party of record were the surveyors and those sent out by Brigham Young in August of 1861, to prepare the way for about 200 Saints to colonize the Basin. Their report is given in a later account. Supplies had to be brought in by wagon or packed on mules and horses. 

The expedition of Major John Wesley Powell should be mentioned. He brought a party down the Green River into the Basin in 

1869, and walked from Green River up to the Indian Agency at Whiterocks. He records, "Toward evening we crossed several beautiful streams, which were tributaries of the Uintah, and we passed through pine groves and meadows, arriving just at dusk at the reservation. Captain Dodds, the agent, is away, having gone to

Salt Lake City, but his assistants treated us very kindly. It is rather pleasant to see a house once more and some evidences of civilization, even if it is on an Indian reservation several days ride from the nearest home of the white man." While here he tells of his visit to an old Indian chief, probably Sowiet, and his wife. 

Some historians record that Major Powell and Captain Dodds dug up the remains of Blackhawk and sent them to the Smithsonian Institution. This is not the fact. Upon investigation only ancient skeletons were taken back from the lower Colorado Canyon. Pardon 

Dodds heard of this report while he was still living and was very resentful that such a report has been circulated. He denied the story to many old pioneers.

Uintah Basin was settled very early by the whiteman in 1873, by Pardon Dodds and M. R. Evans, who had been employees of the government and left the service to raise cattle. They selected Ashley 

Valley for their home. During the next few years Al Westover, John 

Kelley, James Rynaman, Robert Turner, Charles Bentley, James 

Barker, Ben Doke, Charley Jones, Bill Hayden, Pat Lynch, Pete 

Dillman, Finn Britt, and Will Britt, all bachelors proved up on claims. They were Squatter's claim because of lack of a government survey.

The Robert Snyders were the only Homesteaders in the Valley and Mrs. Snyder was the only woman. Many came as early as 

1877, but their story belongs to the Uintah County History. 

There were a few white men that had married Indian women, living on the reservation with their families. Mr. Daniels moved a few miles above Fort Duchesne as early as 1882. His place was a resting place to many freighters on their way to Vernal.

CHAPTER IV. 

THE  UINTAH BASIN The Home of the White Man 

INTAH BASIN is a specious paradise of fertile valleys, flowing streams, mountains covered with timber, walled canyons grand with scenery! This was what came to the ears of Brigham Young from the stories of the early traders and trappers. More and more Saints were coming to Zion and President 

Young needed added lands for farms and homes. Why not colonize the Uintah Basin?

Furthermore, Indian conditions seemed favorable for the project. Sowiette, an old Uintah Ute, was in command of the Basin Utes, and he remained their chief until May, 1888. Because of old age and infirmities, he turned the control of his tribes over to the peaceful Chief Tabby. "This band is composed of the Timpanogos, Uintahs, and the Sanpitches, and number about 4,000. The other 

Utes are controlled by Kanash and number 1,500. It was also reported that Blackhawk and his outlaws caused most of the troubles." 

Perhaps, Blackhawk considered himself justified in defending his heritage. 

On the 25th of August, 1861, Sunday, in the Tabernacle,

President Wells read a list of names called to make a settlement in Uintah Valley. (Church Journal History). 

On the 31st of August, 1861, the following notice was printed in the Deseret News: 

"The Uintah Missionaries met at the President's school house at 6 p.m. President Brigham Young, Daniel Wells, Elder Wilford 

Woodruff, and a few more names were added. Several persons volunteered to start with Surveyor General Fox on Monday, the second, and on Monday, 9th, six volunteers would follow to make a road into Uintah, and the majority considered they would be ready to start in two or three weeks with the main body or when the President would designate. The President said he considered 

Monday, 23rd, the time when the general camp would start.”

A notice was inserted on September 2nd, issuing a call to the 

Uintah Basin, then again on September 11th, 1861, the following notice appeared in the Deseret News: "In view of the great increase of population in these valleys, it has been deemed expedient to form a settlement in Uintah this fall, and a company left this city on the 2nd instant for the purpose of selecting a suitable location and making surveys before the main 75

company which has been selected to go thither and which will start according to present arrangements or on about the 23rd of this month shall arrive. "A company started on Monday, this 9th instant to open a road through the hills from Parley's Park by way of Rhodes Ranch, Kamas Prairie to that William Lytle, Mosiah L. Hancock, Joseph

McRae and John L. Fry, the party who had started to make roads to Uintah Valley returned at 2 p.m. Surveyor General Fox returned. 

He had been engaged in exploring the Uintah Valley." 

On September 25, 1861, a notice was printed in the Deseret News reporting that the "Uinta not what was represented. "The exploring and surveying party that started for Uintah Valley on the 2nd, also the road makers who followed them on the ninth have returned with a very unfavorable report in relation to that part of the territory. The fertile vales, extensive meadows, and wide pasture ranges so often reported to exist in that region were not to be found and the country, according to the statements of those sent thither to select a location for a settlement is entirely unsuitable for farming purposes, and the amount of land suitable for cultivation extremely limited. "The explorers having upon their arrival there found things so very different from what had been anticipated and after searching up and down the streams flowing eastward from the Wasatch range to the Colorado without finding an oasis, sent back two of their number with a report of what discoveries they had made up to that time, which report was read from the stand at the Bowery of Sunday week. "The balance of the party continued their explorations several days longer, but without discovering the land of the walnut and the vine. "After becoming thoroughly satisfied that all that section of country lying between the Wasatch Mountain and the Eastern boundary of the territory and south of Green river country was one vast, continuity of waste and measurably valueless, except for nomadic purposes, hunting grounds for Indians and to hold the world together, the party returned home by different routes, some arriving on Wednesday, others on Thursday last, unanimously reporting that there is no such country there as had been described by hunters, trappers and other wanderers, who have unreservedly asserted that it was a beautiful valley and more to be desired than they had seen in the great Basin, not excepting that of Great Salt Lake. "Why men who have lived for years in this country, have roamed in this country, have roamed over the mountains and sterile plains, and have witnessed the experiments that have been made in tilling the soil, where any exists, have not by observation learned what portions of the desolate wastes can and cannot be cultivated,

we are unable to say, but such is the case as had been demonstrated more than once. The men who were sent out in this instance to view Uinta, and select a location for a settlement if one could be

Following this on the 15th of October, 1861, from the Journal 

History of Brigham Young the following is quoted in part: 

"Brigham Young has lost Uintah Valley, one of the most fertile in Utah, after having announced his intentions to settle it with the 

Saints. The President (Lincoln) by order dated October has di rected an Indian Reservation to be made there."

Superintendent Martin, who was then Superintendent of Indian Affairs for the Territory of Utah, on hearing of the proposed enterprise, immediately wrote the Commissioners of Indian Affairs, informing him of the same and recommending the settling apart of the valley and vicinity for an Indian reservation in order to prevent the Mormons from settling there."

It is reported that these families selected to go into Uintah were sent down into Southern Utah.

The Basin was set apart as a reservation by President Lincoln by the following order:

Department of the Interior, Washington, October 3, 1861. 

Sir: 

I have the honor herewith to submit for your consideration the recommendation of the Acting Commissioner of Indian Affairs that the Uintah Valley, in the Territory of Utah, be set apart and reserved for the use and occupancy of Indian Tribes.

In the absence of an authorized survey, (the valley and surrounding country being as yet unoccupied by settlements of our citizens), I respectfully recommend that you order the entire valley of the Uintah river within Utah Territory, extending on both sides of said river to the crest of the first range of contiguous mountains on each side to be reserved to the United States and set apart as an 

Indian reservation. Very respectfully, your obedient servant, Caleb B. Smith Secretary.

Executive Office, October 3, 1861 

Let the reservation be established as recommended by the 

Secretary of the Interior. 

A. Lincoln 

President

The Indians took their time in coming into the new reservation. 

Treaties had to be made before their removal here.

The first agency was built at the head of Daniels Canyon about 1864. Thomas Carter was their Superintendent and D. W. 

Top, left-S. P. Dillman on Second Old Fort Thornburg (Old Ashley. Top, right- 

Blackhawk, who went with 'Pete' Dillman to rescue the white women at Meeker, 1879.

Center-Fort Duchesne, established in 1886. Bottom-Meeker Massacre Indian Camp, 1879.

Rhodes, agent. Because of the severe cold and deep snows it existed for only part of one year.

The agency was then moved into the Basin near Hanna at the foot of Mount Tabby on the Upper Duchesne. Pardon Dodds was appointed agent. Quoting from his diary, the following dates, so much disputed, can be cleared up: "I enlisted October 9, 1862, in the Tenth Ohio Volunteer 

Cavalry with Lieutenant A. L. Jones and went to Camp Cleveland.

I received an honorable discharge from the army by Governor of the State, John Brough, on May 31, 1865. Later I opened up and operated for a short time an oyster parlor at Alliance Ohio. Here 

I met with an army comrade by the name of Harwood Head. We decided at once to come West arriving in Salt Lake City September 7, 1866. In the spring of 1867 we went to Ruby Valley, Nevada, and put up a saw mill. That fall I came to the Uintah Agency and took charge as agent. The agency then being located on the upper 

Duchesne. In the spring of 1868 I received by commission as agent from President of the United States, Andrew Johnson, and also moved the agency to Rock Creek. In the fall of 1868 I moved the agency to Whiterocks, arriving there on Christmas day with a crew of seven men. We constructed buildings and prepared for farming the coming season. The reason for recounting the agents record is this: Some people doubt and ask if I ever had an appointment, I certainly did and had the paper signed by the President who made the appointment, and I was under $20,000 bond. I remained here until my successor, John J. Critchlow, arrived. Then in company with Morris Evans, I came to Ashley Valley to locate, arriving 

February 14, 1873. We engaged in the horse and cattle business."

It was during this time that many cattle men came into the Uintah Basin. A. M. Murdock was among the first, and he relates many thrilling experiences of the early days. He mentions that the 

Indians were self supporting then and spent much of their summer time in killing and drying meat and berries for the winter food supply. He said in one camp of just a few Indians that over five hundred pounds of deer meat were dried and placed in buckskin bags.

The Indians resented the removal of the agency because they felt that their homes and lands had been confiscated. Pardon Dodds operated it for two years, and then it was burned. The agency was then built at the junction of Rock Creek and Duchesne River. The pasture and farming lands were so limited that it was deemed advisable to make a permanent agency where conditions were more favorable.

At that time Amos Reed, a government clerk in the employment of the Indian Service, made a trip to White Rocks. His

Indian guide, Chief Antero, said that since he was to be moved to

the Uintah Reservation he would choose White Rocks for his home, and he suggested to Mr. Reed that the Indian Agency be established at that place. His reason for his choice was the fact that White 

Rocks was located in the center of the Basin. Here the local Indians were used to staying. Here traders and trappers had established posts and headquarters. Many trails led to and from it, and here the site had a lovely natural setting. 

As has been stated, it was moved to White Rocks on Christmas 

Day, 1868. 

Lieutenant George W. Graffam became the next Superintendent, and in 1871, J. J. Critchlow followed him. During the superintendency of Mr. Critchlow, the Meeker Massacre occurred in 

Colorado in the fall of 1879.

The account of Meeker Massacre, a blot upon the history of the United States, does not belong in this book, except that present White River Utes dwelling in the Uintah Basin, some in Duchesne County, are here because of a misunderstanding between the Indians and an agent who knew nothing of the habits, customs, and heritage of the Redskins.

A casual remark by him caused a tragedy. The agent was determined to "civilize" the Indians by forcing them to adopt the white man's ways and form. He apparently had a worthy objective, but the means he used to accomplish it failed. 

Agent Meeker had no information of the rumor spread among the Indians that Major Thornburg and his troops were on their way to Meeker. They asked him if the news was true, and he answered partly in jest and partly to scare them into working, "Yes, the troops are coming if you lazy Indians don't get to work."

Then the Indians held a council and decided to raid the commissary, steal supplies of flour and blankets, and flee to the mountains. The report is that they found liquor, and all became intoxicated. They killed all of the employees of the Agency, except Mrs. 

Meeker, her daughter, Mrs. Price and her children, and one employee who escaped. They also ambushed Major Thornburg and his troops, killing him and many of his soldiers.

Because of the Meeker trouble the White River Utes were sent to the Uintah Reservation. The Uncompahgre Utes were also transferred by their own consent.

Following the Meeker affair, several enclosures and stockades were built in the Uintah Basin. One stockade was erected on Green 

River at the Isaac Burton ranch, where over eight-five stockmen from Blue Mountain gathered to defend themselves from the Indian raids. When they returned home, they found their cabins burned. 

At the same time Vernal was also turned into a stockade built

82 

where Penney's Store now stands. However, the Indians did not molest the whites here.

Across the Green River from the Charley Popper Place, where Ouray now stands, was a fort and trading post at which the Indians received their issue including their money. It was known as Fort 

Thornburg after Major Thornburg, who was killed in the Meeker

Massacre. It was built under the supervision of Ike Burton and 

George Merkley. In the spring of 1882, however, Fort Thornburg was moved to the homestead of Peter Dillman, located near the settlement of Ashley, Uintah County. The reason for its removal was due to the fact that it was not only difficult to get supplies to the old Fort across the Green River, but that the settlers in Ashley needed more protection.

Thinking that trouble was over with the Indians, the Fort was vacated, and the troops returned to Fort Bridger. Again an incident occurred that was an outrage to the Indians. The Uncompahgres, along with the White River Indians, had returned to Colorado to gather their cattle, horses, and camps and bring them to the Uintah Chief Ouray and Chipeta who gave up their home to help the white people have peace

reservation. The militia and homesteaders of Colorado followed the 

Indians and, after a few words council with the leaders, shook hands with the Indians, telling them that it was fine for them to camp on an open flat for the night. The Indians thought that they were on their own reservation. Why General Adams encouraged them to camp here was demonstrated the next morning at daybreak when the militia and their escorts opened fire, killing and wounding several of the Indians. They also took all of their possessions, including about five thousand pounds of dried meat, twenty-five hundred goats and sheep, several hundred head of horses and cattle, in fact, all of their lifetime possessions. The Indians had little way of defending themselves. Their horses were pastured some distance away. Atchee and Colorow were wounded trying to make peace long enough for the women and children to be removed to safety. 

Among this group were Chipeta and her friends who had given their homes to the government for the sake of peace. These Indians have never been reimbursed for this offense. 

Now to protect the Indian from the White Man, troops were again sent into the center of the Uintah Reservation. Thus Fort 

Thornburg was replaced by Fort Duchesne, established in 1886 and approved August 31, 1887.

To protect the settlers, keep peace and care for the Indians, Companies I. K. F., and C, 21st U. S. Infantry troops at Fort Fred

Steele, Wyoming were sent to Fort Bridger, Wyoming, and there awaited the arrival of Brigadier General Crook. 

The General met them at Fort Bridger and ordered Captain Duncan to take command and proceed with the soldiers to the 

Uintah River by following the road to Fort Thornburg and old Ashley.

From Ashley to the Uintah River they could take a choice of two roads, one by way of Deep Creek, and the other, the regular traveled road though much longer. "So our commander, always looking for short cuts decided to take the short cut. However, 'Captain Billy', Indian Police, came into camp and warned us that 300 Ute braves were ambushed and ready for another 'Custer Affair'. 

We took the longer road." 

Henry Fiack in his account of Fort Duchesne: "Beginning, states that on reaching Uintah river the troops, having marched about thirty miles without water or anything to eat, confronted 700 Ute and Ouray Indians in full war dress and paint, and hostile. General Crook also arrived in an army ambulance. 

Thinking he had reached the Duchesne river, he raised the U. S. flag and said, "This is the future Fort Duchesne." Picket lines were thrown up and trenches dug. The troops stayed in them for three weeks, short on ammunition and provisions. "Two companies of colored cavalry, B. and E., 9th U. S. Cav-

alry, Fort Washakie, Wyoming, under the command of Lieutenant Colonel Benteen, re-enforced the beleaguered companies. "During the winter Major Bentine had quarters on a hill opposite the bridge. He lived in a tent, while many of his soldiers occupied dugouts on the side of the hill. These companies and troops were relieved June 20, 1888, by companies A, C, K, and F, Sixteenth Infantry, and Troops C and M, Ninth Cavalry. "During the building of this post under the command of General Hatch, a steam sawmill was in operation in the timber 40 miles to the northwest. Its capacity was about 6,000 feet per day, and there was also a good planer, shingle machine, and lath machine. 

A detail of one commissioned officer, one non-commissioned officer, and 30 enlisted men and 1 civilian engineer was constantly at the mill preparing material while the building operations were in progress. "Six mule and horse teams hauled the lumber to the fort. Work

On August 19, 1912, by Executive Order, the fort was abandoned for military purposes, and it was placed under the jurisdiction and control of the Department of the Interior.

THE OPENING OF THE UINTAH RESERVATION TO HOMESTEADERS 

Superintendent Forrest Stone, who has so kindly brought co-operation between the Indians and White Settlers. 

The order was as follows: 

When the Indians were moved from Spanish Fork and gave up their Reservation there, a treaty was made between them and the Government that if they would move peacefully to the Uintah Reservation the Uintah lands would not be opened to settlement of the White People unless two-thirds of all of the male Indians voted in favor of it.

At the Uintah Agency, a Council was held from May 18, to May 23, 1903. It was known as the James McLaughlin Council because he was sent by the government to obtain consent by signers of the Indians.

To one who has studied Indian history for years, it is one of the most unfair acts that has ever been committed in United States 

History. 

The Indians never did consent. They never had a chance. The 

Act was passed by Congress a year before they were ever given
