THE LOST RHOADES MINE 

Rohan's mine

Presented by Robert A. Powell, son of John A. Powell 

(Caleb Baldwin Rhoades was a Pioneer of 1846, as he camped in this valley in the summer of 1846 on his way to California). 

This is the story of a very rich gold deposit supposed to be in the Uintah Mountains. Its whereabouts was known only to 

CALEB B. RHOADES, who was my uncle by marriage to father's sister, MALINDA POWELL. 

Believe it or not, but here is the story as I have heard it from my father and uncle Caleb's own lips.

First, I will relate some of the words which my father, John 

A. Powell, has related to me. My father and Caleb Rhoades were great hunters and were out in the mountains together very often in their early days, where upon many occasions, Uncle Caleb told my father much in regards to the gold he knew of, but he would never show him where it was located. Although my father had seen much of the ore and said it was very rich in gold, he could not understand why, if Caleb knew where there was so much gold, he did not locate it or at least more of it than he did and not talk so much about it. 

One time in the early days of Kamas, Caleb and my father, being the first two settlers in the Kamas Valley, went on a hunting trip away back in the mountains, west of Kamas. While on this trip they killed a large buck deer too large to carry to camp, so they cleaned it and hung it on a tree till the next day a horse could be brought in to pack it out. Nothing was said about the gold at this time but a number of years later when they had moved from 

Kamas to Price, Caleb asked my father if he remembered the time they killed the deer and father said that he did. Then Caleb told father that not very far from that place where the deer had been killed, was the place where lay the rich gold ore. He would not tell in which direction or how far he meant by saying 'not very far'. 

So father was at a loss to know whether it was one mile or five. 

Uncle Caleb never talked of locating his mine until a few years before the Indian Reservation was to be thrown open for settlers to file claim on the land. Then he talked about locating the mine. He told father "If I can live till the reservation is thrown open, you will never need for anything as far as finances are concerned." Another time earlier, Uncle Caleb had been gone from home for a month. Father had a pretty good idea where he had gone, but soon after he returned, they met in Price and had a long talk. "John, come up, I have something to show you." Father knew what he meant; so next day he went to Caleb's place and spent the greater part of the day. Caleb showed father two pack bags 133

of very rich ore of gold that he had brought back from his mysterious trip.

Now I could tell you a lot of these stories related also by many reliable people. I would like to tell a few things I heard from 

Uncle Caleb which he related at a party given in the Price Town Hall.

It was the year of 1898 or '99, a party in honor of the old folks of Price of which group Caleb Rhoades was one of the earliest pioneers. He was asked to talk. He started by telling of coming to 

Utah with his father Thomas Rhoades and others. They arrived in Utah in 1846 a year ahead of Brigham Young and his followers. 

They were called the 'trail blazers'. Caleb was ten years old, born in Illinois in 1836. Brigham Young, gaining confidence of the

Indians by a policy of feeding them and not fighting them was told by the Redskins where a rich deposit of gold was and permitted him to send scouts to get some of it. So Thomas Rhoades being able to talk the Indian language was chosen and set apart for that mission. He had faithfully performed his work until stricken with illness. Brigham chose Caleb, son of Thomas, now 19 years of age. It was now 1855.

By choosing Caleb to go now, made it so that he could accompany his father as soon as he was able to go, but before Caleb was permitted to go, he said he had to make a covenant to God, before Brigham Young and Indian Chief Walker, that he would never tell or show anyone where this gold was. Then Brigham

Young laid his hands on him and blessed him and set him apart, the same as his father had been set apart before he was sent after gold. Then the next thing was a guide to show him where the gold was. Indian Chief Walker then picked a young Indian as a guide and Chief Walker told the young Indian that if anything happened to the young man, meaning Caleb, that the guide was to protect Caleb from all other Indians, the guide would explain their mission and they went unmolested. The guide showed Caleb the place and they got what ore they could carry and returned unmolested back to Salt Lake City.

Caleb said when his father was well, they together made a number of trips for gold and as long as Brigham Young lived, the Indians, although they were great friends, forbid them taking any more gold, and what he had been able to get since Brigham Young died he had slipped out and sold it un-be-known to the Indians. 

Although he said many other things in his remarks, which lasted about one hour and a half, the thing that impressed me most of all was in his finishing remarks, in which he stated, “Until this day I have kept my covenant that I made with God, before 

Brigham Young and Indian Chief Walker. I have never showed

LOST RHOADES MINE 

135 any man or told any man, and I never intend to do so, as long as I live." 

How these words were to me a testimony! I never heard a person bear a testimony in any more earnestness than he did this one. While he explained it also to why he never would show any man where it was, but as soon as he thought the reservation was to be thrown open for settlement he had a very strong desire to locate it which would have broken his covenant which he said he had made. He even went so far as to send some of the ore back to 

Washington, D. C. and offered to pay the national debt, which was pretty large at the time, if they would fix it in Washington so he could locate the mine, but they turned him down and tried to find it themselves by organizing the Florence and Raving Mining Company and although prospecting on the reservation was prohibited, this company prospected and located all the latterite, gilsonite and other minerals they could find, but they never found the Rhoades mine and I know there have been not hundreds, but thousands of men hunting for it, but no one at present has found it; and when the Florence and Raving Mining Company was prospecting the country, Caleb was asked if he was not afraid they would find it. 

He said no; they will never find it because it is where they will least look for it.

There have been many fine specimens of ore found, but where they came from nobody knows. The strange thing about it is that after Caleb claimed to have made such a covenant, he could hardly wait for the opening of the reservation, so that he could locate it. 

He talked about it a great deal with my father. There is one thing that has made me feel that there was something in the covenant he made, because just a short time before the opening of the reservation, he took sick and only lived a few days. While he was sick, my father visited him often and was there to help wait upon him as much as possible, and father said all Caleb wanted to talk about was his mine and if he could only live to locate it. When he realized he might die, he tried to draw a map of it so that his wife, then an old woman, could find it after he was dead. She spent two summers searching, but failed to locate the mine. (It is also rumored that while searching for his mine he was killed by Indians. His body was never located).

Now I want to say before closing that I personally have met a few of the old Indians that knew Caleb Rhoades and have talked with them about Caleb Rhoades and the money rock as the Indians called it. Bridger Jim, Mountain Sheep, Happy Jack, and Blackhawk are four of the Indians that I talked with. They all said 

Caleb Rhoades was their Heap Winno friend. (Heap, meaning great friend; Winno, meaning good friend, or great good friend). 

Brigham Young was a Heap Winno Chief, and they told me

about him sending flour out there for the Indians; how they in turn let Brigham have Money Rock. Blackhawk even told me the canyon

Caleb went up when after the Money Rock, and said his brother was the first Indian to find the money rock or gold. This brother fell from a load of hay and broke his neck. He told many more interesting stories that would take a long time to tell, so I cannot help but believe there is some truth in this Caleb Rhoades' gold story, even if it is never found.

TRANSPORTATION Presented by Mrs. Maxine Burdick THE WELL IN WELLS DRAW 

Long before the Indian Reservation was opened for filing a road was established from Price to Vernal, Price being the nearest railroad. This was a long distance, as Vernal is the farthest city from a railroad in the United States.

Several stops had to be made with freight wagons, and there were quite a few stations established along the route. 

The road from Price went east, then up Soldier Canyon, down Nine Mile Canyon, thence up Trail across the Benches dropping into Gate Canyon.

After reaching the Summit of Gate, they followed a long Draw, which became known as Well's Draw, then on across the desert north to the Duchesne River at Myton.

A man by the name of Smith dug a deep well in this Draw, about midway from the river and Nine Miles, and got water, and established a station. This well was 180 feet deep and well timbered. 

The water was drawn by a horse, in a huge wooden bucket. As it was the only water between Nine Miles and the Duchesne river, people were glad to pay for water for horses and cattle. It cost a dollar for each team, and 30c a head for cattle. Neal Hanks, an old stockman and settler from Nine Mile has said that he saw as many as 500 head of cattle watered there in a single day. The buildings were all made of rock and were welcomed by many people and travelers for many years.

ALTONAH PIONEER HISTORY OF ALTONAH, UTAH 

By Margaret Maxfield

LTONAH, Utah, is a town with a population of 320 people. 

It is located in Northern Duchesne County. It has an altitude of 6,000 feet; plenty of wood and timber is available. It is an ideal country. Altonah is about fifteen miles from the famous fishing resort of Moon Lake. 

The supply of water for the raising of stock is received from Lake Fork and Yellowstone rivers. These streams come from beautiful snow-capped peaks, Mt. Emmons has an altitude of 13,428 feet, Kings Peak, the loftiest mountain in the State of Utah, with an altitude of 13,498 feet.

During the fall of August 1905, the upper Lake Fork Bench was opened to the white people for settlement. John Glenn of 

Vernal was put in charge of the filing for any who wanted to homestead. The first man to file was James B. Murray of Vernal. 

On the same day Wm. Ashby, David J. Thompson, J. B. Payne, and Ed. Wilderman, and Wm. McKune also filed. Wm. Bowden filed for the place now owned by Mary Bowden and Louie. A. W. 

Thompson, father of David J. Thompson, filed on the place now owned by Heber Carroll. 

In 1906, Thomas Holdway, surveyor from Vernal, was hired to survey the canal now called the Payne Canal, and at the same time the corner stones of the homesteaders were surveyed. He was assisted by D. J. Thompson, Wm. McKune, J. B. Payne, and James 

B. Murray. 

In the year 1907, Sept. 15, Lott Powell filed on his homestead, and six months later came out and built a claim shack, cleared off the land, and planted a garden. In the spring of 1908, Lott Powell, James C. Burgess and Raymond Burgess came. They moved their families out to Alexander, the name of the first post office, which now is called Altonah. 

Some of the first homesteaders were Robert Alexander, Milton 

Alexander and Bird Alexander. The Burgess families filed on their homesteads in the spring of 1908; Earl Burgess, the son of Raymond and Esther Burgess, born May 1, 1909, was the first white boy born in Alexander; Mary L. Powell, the daughter of Lott and Mary 

Jane Powell, was the first white girl born there. Bird Alexander was the first school teacher.

The conditions of the country when first settled were very hard. Water was scarce and it was hauled from Chidester Spring. 140

ALTONAH 

141 

1-Lake Fork Bridge; 2-Charles Anderson; 3-James E. Burgess; 4-Pioneer Picnic on the River; 5-Annie Johansen; 6-Construction of Payne Canal; 7-Mr. and Mrs. 

C. R. Shiner; Mr. and Mrs. J. M. Millard; Brighton Timothy.

Duchesne, Myton, and Roosevelt were the nearest stores and post offices. 

In the fall of 1907, the first work was started on the canal. In 

1908 a small stream of water was brought through the canal. 

Work on the U. S. Lake Fork Canal started in 1907; W. B. 

Fhurman was construction foreman; Clair Preece worked with the surveying party, and W. D. Kohler was the time-keeper.

After the canal was completed, it was run by the Dry Gulch Irrigation Company and the homesteaders who had located on o near the canal. The Government allowed the white people to use the water from the Indian canal free of charge while they were building homes and constructing their own irrigation ditches. A charge of 50c per acre for use of the Government canal was required in 1912.

In June 1911, W. R. Preece was transferred from Myton to the Lake Fork canal, with headquarters at the main head as the first ditch rider. A house at the headgate was built by the agency for the Indian Farmer. The first man who held that job was Mr. 

G. A. Dennis. Mr. Preece lived with Mr. Dennis and his wife when he first came here. Mr. Wm. Preece was ditch rider on the government canal until about October 1920; then he was transferred to 

Myton as supervisor of construction ditch rider.

In 1915 construction work was started on a telephone line under the direction of J. M. Millard. The line was started from 

Altonah through Boneta, Mountain Home and Talmage to Duchesne, connecting this line with that of the Dry Gulch Irrigation Company at Roosevelt. He also ran this line to Kamas. 

SCHOOLS 

The first school teacher was James Bird Alexander. He taught school for two years at Clay Basin from 1910 to 1911. The building was a small log cabin with a dirt floor and one window; there were thirteen pupils attending. The first school house was built south of where the church now stands; H. Y. Mitchell was the teacher. The next building for a school was built east of the present school house; it had two rooms and a bell was installed. In 1914 the present school house was started. Altonah had a high school until 1923.

The first Board of Trustees were: Milton Alexander, H. D. 

Allred, and Hans Jacobson.

At this time the precinct was called Alexander. President Smart wanted part of Hans Jacobson's place for a town site, but Mr. 

Jacobson did not think it was the right place. Mr. Millard consented. to let him have forty acres and President Smart changed the name from Alexander to Altonah.

ALTONAH 

143 

TOFFICE ALTONAH SCHOOL 1913 School Board Members: J. M. Millard, Hans Jacobson, Elmer Gale. Altonta 

School. Lott Powell, James B. Murray, Clyde R. Lambert; Mr. and Mrs. Jeremiah 

H. Clark; Moon Lake; Mrs. Lott Powell; First Bishop of Altonah, Aklu on right.

POST OFFICE 

In 1910 the first post office was established in Clay Basin at Robert Alexander's place. It was called the Alexander post office and Nannie Alexander was the first post mistress. The post office was moved from there to Samuel Miller's place, and Margaret Miller became the post mistress. From there it was moved to the present town and Herman Smellow was post master, later J. M. Millard.

Next Charles E. Anderson was appointed postmaster and he served for a number of years. Clair Preece, Charles Anderson, Elizabeth

Barney, Caroline Thompson, Howard Dunn served as post mistresses and post masters. Mrs. Elva Snyder is serving at present.

Robert Alexander carried the mail the first three months without pay from the Roosevelt post office. He later turned the mail route over to Dave Thompson, who was the government paid mail carrier. The mail was then brought from Duchesne. 

CHURCH HISTORY 

Heber Bowden was the first Branch President (then called Alexander). He was appointed by President Smart; his counselors were Lott Powell and Hans Jacobson. Later Lott Powell was appointed Branch President of the Altonah ward until Owen Bennion was appointed as bishop. The Young Ladies M. I. A. was organized 

August 3, 1913 and was held in a large log house on the south of Main Street, with Mrs. Dorthella Dastrup as president. Alta 

Murray was appointed the first Primary president of Altonah Branch. of Bluebell on August 3, 1913, with Cleo Caldwell and Ellen Burgess as counselors. Almira Caldwell was the first Relief Society president with Mary J. Powell as second counselor on August 3, 1913. On Sept. 17, 1916, Bishop Owen Bennion was released fro:n the Altonah branch and transferred to Mt. Emmons as Bishop and 

Peter I. Akelund was sustained as Bishop of Altonah Ward. At this time, Mt. Emmons and Altonah were made two separate wards.

STORES 

The first store in Altonah was established by J. M. Millard in 1912, on the corner where the service station now stands. In the winter of 1915, Clair Preece put up a building and started a store the next spring. Frank Maxwell came from Myton and established a store about 1915. At the present time this store is now owned by Glen Mohlman, who bought the Maxwell store about 1939. 

Jesse A. Fowler bought Clair Preece's store about 1919.

In 1920, a commercial club was organized with J. M. Millard as president and D. J. Thompson, secretary and treasurer.

ALTONAH 

145 We also had a newspaper printed here in 1915 and 1916 called "Reservation News." It was edited by Mr. Deubendroff and was a 

Socialist paper. In 1914, Albert Dastrup built a blacksmith shop and dwelling located south of the Fowler corner. This same year 

George Petty built a planning mill which was later sold to Prows and used as a Pool Hall.

M. J. Millard came from Price in 1911 and bought forty acres from the Indian Department, plotted it in thirty-six lots and sold the lots for forty dollars each. He built the first store and amusement hall in 1917 which later was sold to the Alexander Brothers and burned down. School was held in this hall for some time. 

J. M. Millard was postmaster, store proprietor and an untiring booster for Altonah, Queen City of the Reservation, as he called it. 

J. B. Payne and family came to the Altonah bench in 1906; he stayed through the winter and the next summer he raised the first crop which consisted of ten acres of potatoes which he hauled with team and wagon to Myton to sell. He later sold his place and left.

J. B. Murray and family came here in 1906 but moved out during the first two winters. The summer of 1907 he raised the first wheat on the bench. They lived in a one room dirt roof and floor, one half of the room was made into a large bin where the wheat was stored. At that time the thresher was run by horse power and many weeks were spent in threshing at one place with a large crew of men. His wife, Atta Murray, endured many hardships, living quarters none too good, raising a family of eight children and lending a helping hand to anyone in need. 

S. W. Ashby and family, William Ashby and family, and John

Ashby all brothers lived here a short time, later moving to Vernal. 

John Ashby stayed here and was a great cattle man. He later moved and at the present time resides at Murray, Utah. 

Mr. and Mrs. Heber Bowden and family say: "We left Randolph July 24, 1910, by wagon, spent twelve days on the trip with a baby two months old. As we were coming down Taylor Mountain, a wheel broke, turning all four wheels up in the air, as luck was with us, I and the children were walking. We lived in Measer until 

February 12, then started for Alexander, and took three days to drive the cattle and horses to Roosevelt. When we came here, the snow was about three feet deep. We lived in a cotton pole room 

8x10 until we could build our house. The place was 160 acres of cedars and sagebrush. Plenty of hardships faced us without any money. Hay was hauled by Heber and the boys from Hancock 

Cove. They cleaned quite a few Indian leases to get money to make

a living. We saw many hardships for five years. The first ladies we met were Ellen Burgess and Mame Powell."

Robert Alexander and family were among the first to settle in Clay Basin, also his brothers Milton and Bird. They erected a tall pole with a sack on the end and would put this up to show the other pioneers that came later where they lived. 

J. D. McKune moved from Price in November 1913 and camped on the Yellowstone river about two miles from the mouth. 

They stayed at their camp until July next year. Then they moved on the sawmill site which was about three miles west from the famous Yellowstone rocks. While they were camped in the vicinity of the river, they built the first bridge in that part of the country across the Yellowstone river. Mr. McKune started to operate the sawmill in October, 1914.

Mr. and Mrs. Ralphs and family moved from Ferron in 1908 and homesteaded on the place they now live on. Their first house was a one room log house with one window and dirt floor and roof. They have raised a family of eight children. Mrs. Ralphs has been one to lend a helping hand to sickness or to anyone in need. 

Presented by Mrs. Virginia Smith 

"My father Lafayette Chidester, was born September 28, 1872 at Bloomington, Bear Lake County, Idaho, and my mother, Elsie 

C. Hicks Chidester, was born July 28, 1883 at Elsmoore, Utah.

They heard of the opening of the reservation for homesteading. 

They were living at Bingham, Utah, and at the time were contented where they were. Later they moved to Huntington, Utah for a few years. While here father's brother, Joe Chidester and his wife came on a visit to Huntington from the reservation, and his description of the development of the settlement persuaded them to sell and try for a new home. They then sent their first load of furniture by his brother, and my sister Luella came with them. 

When she arrived in the Basin, she wrote her parents not to sell, but they did not receive the letter in time. They had sold the home, farm land, stock, and had started with their family of eight children and some more of their furniture and belongings. 

When my parents arrived in the Basin in June, 1917, my mother then realized her first great disappointment. Seeing strange towns and homes so far apart and having to travel horseback or by team and wagon looked very discouraging. We arrived at Uncle Joe 

Chidester's home about five miles from Altonah. We visited a day, then moved into two rooms of aunt Elthier Chidester's home until my parents could locate a home for sale. Not ever seeing the Basin before, my mother was broken hearted and gave way to tears.

She had never considered coming out so far in so much unsettled country, but unable to return, she decided to make the best of it.

ALTONAH 

147 My parents bought a nice home where we moved to town the last of August. This home was bought from Herman Smellow. It was a large five room frame house. The place had a young orchard and a good garden spot all fenced in, a large hay barn and other buildings. My father then took a lease and tried farming, but found it difficult being a cripple. He then tried freighting in the fall of 

1918 and continued off and on hauling for A. F. Maxwell's cash store. He traveled by team and wagon via Myton through Nine

Mile, Gate Canyon to Price and it took nine days to make the round trip. My father and Enoch Chidester took a contract to drive a tunnel at the Farmers Lake for a reservoir, off to the east of the Yellowstone river which he finished the fall of 1920. He then returned to freighting." 

Mr. and Mrs. Ervin Caldwell filed on the place where Lott 

Powell now lives. They later built a hotel east of the Fowler store but some time later it was burned down. 

Mr. and Mrs. Hans Jacobson and family came to Altonah in 

1909 from Emery. The bowery was built on their place where they held the first Sunday School and the first 24th of July celebration. 

The floor was 30x40 feet and for some time all recreation was held there. S. W. Ashby and Joe Thompson hauled the first load of logs used to build the bowery.

The winter of 1914, a group of men went to the mill and purchased lumber for the present ward hall. Ed and D. J. Thompson built a butcher shop and general store east of Millard's. In 

1913 William Bowden built a barber shop east of the store.

Brig Timothy and family were the early pioneers of Altonah, and at the present time he is the oldest pioneer living here in the ward. 

Peter I. Akelund and family came from Castle Gate, March 

1914, with Wm. Maxfield and family. On September 17, 1916,

Bishop Owen Bennion was released and Pater I. Akelund was put in as the Bishop of Altonah Ward. 

Dr. Shipp was the only doctor around Duchesne County for a number of years. Mrs. Wamsley and Mrs. Redding were the most faithful and two of the best midwives any town could ever wish to have in their midst. Many babies were ushered into the world by them, and they were always on hand when sickness was around. 

We can place these two women on the honor roll. 

This history has been gathered and compiled by the Daughters of the Utah Pioneers of the Altonah camp. The camp was first organized January 28, 1936, at the home of Dorthella Dastrup. 

County President Mattie Edwards from Roosevelt was officiating. 

Dorthella P.

Dastrup was nominated as Captain and was put in by aclamation.

Next meeting was held in February 1936, at the home of Elsie Chidester and the following officers were chosen: May R. 

Lambert, first vice captain; Atta A. Murray, second captain; Margaret M. Maxfield as secretary and treasurer; Edith M. Anderson, recording secretary; Lola C. Jones, historian; Elsie H. Chidester, recorder; Mary J. Young, organist; Ada Smith, custodian of relics, and Olive F. Beebe, chaplin. 

Daughters who have served as Camp Captains: Darthella 

Dastrup, May R. Lambert, Atta A. Murray, Margaret Maxfield,

Bertha Ralphs, Janett Beebe, Mary Carroll. 

THE HISTORY OF HEBER CARROLL AND ELIZABETH JANE BILLS CARROLL Heber Carroll and Elizabeth Jane Bills were married March 

20, 1900, by Bishop S. D. Colton in Vernal, Utah. Heber was born 

June 10, 1875 at Heber City, Utah, he was the son of Patrick 

Carroll and Margaret Euphemia Robinson.

They moved to Vernal in July 1879. Elizabeth Jane, daughter of David L. Bills and Sarah Catherine Searle, was born November 

18, 1878 at Clover, Lincoln County, Nevada, moved to Vernal in 1881. Heber and Elizabeth both grew up in Maeser, Uintah County. 

They went to school in Vernal. They received their Endowments in the Salt Lake Temple May 30, 1900. 

Their children were: Fern Elizabeth, born May 28, 1901; 

George Franklin, born Nov. 16, 1902; Robt. Aldon, born Feb. 29, 1904, Lola, born March 4, 1906 and David Heber, born July 24, 1910, all were born in Vernal, and Don Herald, born Sept. 4, 1917 at Altonah, Utah.

Their parents were some of the earliest pioneers in Vernal. 

Patrick Carroll owned and operated the first saw mill and planer in that part of the country, and David L. Bills owned and operated a blacksmith shop in Maeser then known as Millward. They lived in the old Carroll home until 1913 and were among the first homesteaders in Altonah. They came here in the Spring of 1908 and 

1909 and then moved back to Vernal for the winter. Finally, on 

May 19, 1913, they sold the home in Vernal and moved to Altonah to stay. Their home was built of logs sawed by the mill owned by 

Patrick Carroll. They were some of the first settlers in Altonah. 

When they came here there was a store owned by J. M. Millard. 

The post office was also owned by J. M. Millard and was located in part of the store. There was a little old one room log building used as a Church house. The school was two rooms. Altonah was a part of the Bluebell ward. "When we came here, Lott Powell was 

Presiding Elder. It was part of Roosevelt Stake and we used to go to Roosevelt for our Church convention and conferences. Many times Mrs. Carroll and Mrs. James Murray have gone to Roosevelt

ALTONAH 

149 on horseback to their meetings. They were Primary and Relief 

Society officers. We children rode horseback to school, but in 

November 1913, we bought a lot in town and built a home on it. 

We moved to town each fall when it got so cold that we could not ride horseback and stayed until spring and time for cropping. 

Then we would move back to the farm and have to ride to school again. Father bought a little Indian pony from Long John (an 

Indian), for two dollars for Aldon for his birthday and he traded a sack of oats to a man in Vernal for another horse; we girls rode one horse, and George and Aldon rode the other. In the fall of 

1918 we rented a house in Maeser, and we moved there for Fern and George to go to high school. That fall was the first year of the 

Influenza and school closed down, so in November we moved back here.

When Don was 16 months old, he had pneumonia and almost died. After that he was sick most of the time. He finally developed heart trouble and he was under the doctor's care most of his life until March 15, 1933 when he died and was buried in the Altonah cemetery. Heber Carroll was sick quite a lot after that. He had high blood pressure and in September he had a stroke from which he partially recovered; two more strokes followed which resulted in his death and he was buried in Altonah cemetery. Mother then stayed in town where she lived until 1935 where in May she developed pneumonia and on May 20, 1935, she died and was also buried in the Altonah cemetery beside her husband and son. 

It has been impossible to get the entire history, many have moved away. Following are some of the names of the families that have lived in Altonah:

Heber Carroll MISCELLANEOUS EFFORTS AND EVENTS Calders Creamery: During the fall of 1933 Calder Brothers from Vernal, erected the building where the Creamery now stands.

Altonah Cheese Factory

ALTONAH 

151 Until that time Altonah had two or three cream stations where on the first day of every week, people brought their cream. This was always considered the biggest day of the week. The creamery has made the dairy industry the greatest boon that Altonah ever had. 

People have realized more good from this industry than from any other source. 

Rodney McKune sold the McKune saw mill in 1945 to Chase

Watterson and son. It is now known as the Salt Creek Saw Mill. 

Altonah is considered one of the best locations for pastures. 

American Legion Auxiliary: This post of the American Legion 

Auxiliary was organized in 1934. Soon after our organization was perfected we began our activities. 

Our first venture was selling home made ice cream. On June 

14 and 25, ice cream was sold at Fowler's store by members of the organization. The net proceeds amount to eight or nine dollars. 

The next effort to raise money for our organization, was the fourth of July. A concession was sponsored by the organization, and the net proceeds amounted to $27.00.

Our first officers were only temporarily elected. In September we met at Rowley's ranch and elected permanent officers. We also had an elaborate roast lamb dinner. Our next meeting was held at 

Yellowstone. At this meeting we enjoyed a fish fry. 

About October 21, we sold pie and coffee at a dance given by the American Legion. The members donated pie and we added nine dollars to our treasury. On October 26, members made doughnuts at Mrs. Fowler's store and Mrs. Lambert's and sold them along with coffee and cocoa at a dance given by the Legion. 

In the meantime we were planning other projects at our regular meetings. Etta Smith made a beautiful door stop, which she raffled off bringing us five dollars. Another interesting project was the making of a quilt, each member furnishing material for blocks and helping to complete the quilt. When finished it was raffled at an 

Armistice dance. The proceeds were around $17.50. A pillow was also made and donated by Mrs. Lambert, also a doll dressed by 

Mrs. Dunn. 

During the time these projects were being completed, we had some very enjoyable and interesting work meetings. We took turns entertaining at our homes.

We have observed each poppy date, tried to do our best to help the cause. Each memorial day has been observed by some members of both the Legion and Auxiliary. 

The Boy Scouts feel grateful to the Auxiliary for their support in helping them obtain their baseball equipment. The Auxiliary sold pie, coffee and cocoa at a boy scout dance and gave them around $12.00 to apply on this equipment.

Each Christmas the needy children in the community have been remembered. Orphans of men who were in the service have also been cared for. 

FOREST HISTORY 

The Ashley National Forest as we know it now was once part of the Uintah National Forest which was set aside in 1901 by 

President Theodore Roosevelt. The Uintah Forest included all of the timber country in the north side of the Uintah Mountains and the 

Weber and Provo River watersheds. 

In 1905, the Ute Indian reservation was thrown open for settlement. In 1906 the timber country on the south side of the mountains and around south to Currant Creek and east to White Rocks canyon, country within the Indian reservation was added to the National Forest. The supervisor was Ives Pack and the head quarters was Kamas, Utah.

A few years later the Uintah Forest was divided into the three forests: Ashley, Wasatch and Uintah. The headquarters for the 

Ashley was established at Vernal, Utah. The headquarters for the 

Wasatch was set up in Salt Lake City, and the headquarters for the Uintah was moved to Provo. 

The Ashley National Forest comprised approximately 1,115,000 acres. It takes in both sides of the Uintah Mountains from the divide between Rock Creek and Lake Fork on the south side and the divide between Swiths Fork and Black's Fork on the north side running east to Diamond Mountain. It so happens that the Uintah

Mountains are the only range of mountains in the United States running east and west.

The First Supervisor on the Ashley Forest was Wm. M. Ander son.

He was followed in successive years by Charles DeMoisy, Jim 

Stewart, A. L. Taylor, Earl C. Sanford, Robert H. Park and Ona 

A. Harrison who is present Supervisor.

In the early establishment for the National Forests the Rangers were not located on districts as they are now, but were more or less Rangers at large assigned to work anywhere on the forest where there was work to do. In 1908 the forest was pretty well divided into Ranger districts, as it is now, varying in sizes from 200,000 to 250,000 acres. There are five Ranger districts on the Ashley

Forest. A ranger is in charge of each of them and he is held responsible for all activities on the district. 

The first forest rangers had their headquarters at Vernal, and they worked from there out and back. These men were: Grant 

Carpenter, followed by Burt Hardy, John D. Clyde, Charles De-

ALTONAH 

153 

Moisy, Parley Matterson, Harry Van Tassell, Seth Perry, Albert E. Blood and Clyde Lambert. 

In the fall of 1930, in Altonah, A. G. Nord began the construction of a year round station for forest rangers. Jim Stewart finished the building.

Where the lake forks limpid water from Moon Lake flowing down, Joins her Yellowstone twin sister, passing through our thriving town. 

Where the sportsman in the summer, with tackle at his side Draws many a speckled beauty, from the waters clear and bright.

Where the Indians hunt the wild deer, Jack and lone Bunny,

On the hill side near Altonah, which is good enough for me. 

THE EVOLUTION OF ALTAMONT HIGH SCHOOL 

-By Adaline Anderson 

In the year of 1920 the only High School in Duchesne County was located at Roosevelt. The people of the upper county wanted very much to give their children an education, but they felt they could not afford to send them away from home. John L. Allred and 

Joseph Jensen decided to ask the Board of Education for a school in the Upper part of the County. They had in mind, as an instructor, a teacher who lived at Talmage and was experienced in the teaching profession.

They contacted the Superintendent early in September. As no money was provided, Mr. Jensen and Mr. Allred suggested a home teacher, and that she be paid by the parents of the pupils who were to attend. This was approved providing the qualifications and standards of the teacher would be approved by the School Board. 

Mrs. Louise Anderson was suggested, and accepted because she held a Utah Grammar Grade Life Certificate. 

The School Board furnished report cards as the High School students used. The pupils were given their school books at cost. The school was under the direction of the School Board and was visited by the Superintendent on his way to grade schools at Talmage. 

There was no public building in which to hold the school, and no money was provided for rent or janitor service, so it was

decided to use the Anderson's front room with the dining table extended instead of desks. Chairs and boxes were provided for seats. Mrs. Anderson acted as janitress. 

The teacher was paid $60.00 per month for her services. From the first it was difficult. They began with eleven pupils but only three attended the last month, so the teacher asked $10.00 for her services for this last month.

From the beginning her pay was hard to collect. She was paid in vegetables, chickens, pigs, calves, posts and cash, and it took two years before she was finally paid in full. 

The subjects taught the first year were English, Algebra, Physical Geography and History. The boys took Agronomy and the girls 

Sewing. 

The following year there were not enough to hold school. In 

1922-23, Bishop Austin G. Burton taught in the old Sam Draper house (Donohue's). This was the same house that the first public school was held in Talmage.

Following this, school was held for several years at a central point between Mt. Home, Talmage and Boneta known as the Mc- 

Intire place, and there were two teachers hired and paid by the school district. The pupils attending were from all three towns, and they rode horse back all winter. It was known as "Central High

School." 

Later it was moved to the Boneta meeting house, and as the school was so far from either town, it was not satisfactory. Some of the students continued to go by horseback, and some camped in sheep wagons. There were three teachers that year. School continued there a year or so, and the school board decided to move it to Mt. 

Emmons. This was opposed by the people on the West of the Lake 

Fork River, and the school was not well attended by students from that side. It was necessary for students to board away from home in order to attend, and many of them were sent to schools out of the Basin. 

After a few years, a building was erected west of Mt. Emmons and a small town was begun which became known as Altamont. 

From the small beginning of eleven students the present Altamont High School has grown to several hundred. It is supported by students from the East, North, and West. Buses run from each town and students are given the opportunities that they might enjoy at any high school of its size in the State. 

The first 9th grade students were Rulon Beckstead, Luran 

Allred, Zelphia Pearson, Laverda Jensen, Elma Jensen, Zola Har wood, Elton Rust, Ephraim Perkins, and three others whose names 

Mrs. Anderson could not remember.

Mr. and Mrs. Jeremiah H. Clark have been residents of 

Altonah for thirty-nine years. They moved to Altonah from Vernal,

ALTONAH 

155 October, 1909. Mr. Clark was a miner and sheep shearer for thirty-five years then took up farming. 

He was born March 4, 1885 at Vernal, Mrs. Clark was born 

June 22, 1888 in Vernal.

They have been active in civic and church affairs in Altonah. 

Mr. and Mrs. Clark have a family of eleven children, nine of whom are living.

Mr. and Mrs. Brigham Timothy and family have been residents of Altonah for forty years. They moved to Altonah from 

Uintah County in the spring of 1808. He was the son of John G. 

Timothy adn Martha Davis and was born June 21, 1859 at

Slanelly Cornarthan, Wales. Mrs. Timothy was the daughter of 

James Sabey and Eliza McCalony and was born January 18, 1866 in Lehi, Utah. They were both active in Civic and Church work, and Mr. Timothy helped to build churches and schools, and Mrs. 

Timothy was the mother of eleven children.

The first community center was at the home of Mrs. Bertha 

Gibson. She was the first school teacher, and taught the children at her home. It was located above the canal on the present Lyle 

Young place. This was 1912. In 1916 they built a larger school building and employed two school teachers. Among the early teachers were L. C. Winslow, Thornton Richardson, Homer Robison,

The Government built the Grey Mountain Ditch for the Indians, making it possible for this community to produce hay. This was quite remarkable as it was the only hay for miles around. 

One of the largest Indian Villages in our locality was on the river where the Leavitt place is now located.

Written by Mrs. Shelby Lisonbee 

Julius P. Christensen and Sarah E., his wife, were among the first four wihte families to come into the Antelope Country, now known as Bridgeland. They came from Colorado in 1905, and proved up on a homestead at the mouth of Antelope Canyon. Mr. 

Christensen was known as "J.P.” He was a farmer and a cattleman.

He helped to get the timber from which the first school house was constructed. He served as the school trustee for many years. He worked to construct the canals and roads in the Antelope country. 

He was a friend to the Indians and was often called upon to help settle the differences between the Indians and the Whites. 1867. 

Julius P. Christensen was born in Salt Lake City, July 18, Sarah Elizabeth Bromley was born January 11, 1885, at Heber

City, Utah. She was a real Pioneer mother and wife, moving into a raw Indian village with her husband and two small baby girls. 

During the first months after they arrived, her husband worked at a saw mill to secure the material for their first home. She remained alone with her babies, cared for a cow and pig, and carried water from the Duchesne River, a mile away, in a pail. The closest neighbor was an Indian camp which was but a few hundred yards from their land. She helped to rail the brush from their land. Her third baby was the first white baby to be born in Antelope. 

Mr. and Mrs. Christensen were the parents of three daughters:

Elsie Christensen Dean, Bertha Christensen Rasmussen, and Elva Christensen Lisonbee. 

They moved to Emery County in 1925, where they lived until the death of Julius P. Christensen, December 4, 1946. They were both members of the L. D. S. Church. 

From Lyle Young 

Antelope received its name from a creek running through the territory in which many settlers came in the early history of the 

Basin. 

Later President William Smart re-named it Bridgeland. 

Among those who have helped to make a fine community, even if it was scattered, are the following families: Ray Beal, Harry

Veach, Thompson, W. Hamilton, James Buck, Webb 

Sumpkin, Clifford Young, J. P. Christensen, Benj. Bromley, Sam

Bridgeland was the home of the C. C. Camp. The account is given in the first part of the history under the topic of irrigation. 

Lyle L. Young was born at Kamas, Summit County, Utah,

February 16, 1886. That same year his parents moved to Vernal, Utah, where he resided until 1908. He came to Duchesne County and homesteaded in Bridgeland.

In July 1916 he married Clara Bingham. They are the parents of four boys and two girls. He was County Commissioner for eight years; Sunday School Superintendent in the Bridgeland Ward;

President of the M. I. A. and served as Bishop for three years. 

Clara Bingham was born at Mt. Dell, Uintah County, Utah, July 25, 1893, the daughter of Perry Colvin and Olive E. Bingham. 

At the age of thirteen her parents moved to Roosevelt, Utah where she lived until her marriage to Lyle L. Young in 1916.

ARCADIA (REDCAP) Written by Mrs. Hale Holgate and contributed by Mrs.

Florence Madsen, chairman of the 

Duchesne History Centennial Committee. 

HISTORY OF ARCADIA (REDCAP) 

At the opening of the Uintah Reservation, in the fall of 1905, the Indian land was open for settlement by the white people as a homestead, which had been arranged by the Government. 

In the spring of 1906, the white men began to come into the Red Cap district which was named after Red Cap, an Indian Chief of the Ute tribe. His home, or "Wik-ke-up", was near the Lake 

Fork river, about the same location as the Ray Gill ranch. Some of the early settlers were: Michael Kundson, Geo. Summers, Geo. 

Smith, Ben Naylon, Byron Thompson, and James C. Solomonson..

These men spent the first spring and summer living in tents and covered wagons. They cut cottonwood logs and hauled then by teams or wagon from the Lake Fork river. They hauled enough timber to build a one room log cabin with a dirt floor, just enough to get along with until they could get irrigation water to their land.

The Red Cap canal was the first to be built by the supervision of the government officials. The Uteland ditch was made by the men who had land under the division. The land is irrigated by canals and water ditches which tap the Lake Fork river at different points.

These men put in long, hard hours, as the only tools or machinery available at that time was a pick and shovel, and a team scraper. The only feed for their horses was hay and grain which was brought from Vernal, a distance of 60 miles by team and wagon; it took four days to make the round trip. This being a farming district, all kinds of grain, hay, corn, potatoes, and raising of livestock was engaged in. Some very fine Alfalfa seed has been produced in this section at different times. 

Dairying is becoming one of the chief industries throughout this part of the Uintah Basin. 

The early settlers had a little trouble with the Indians. Tim 

John and Savare John were two Indian brothers who were very savage, bad to fight, especially when they were intoxicated. They not only quarreled among themselves, but also with the white men. 

They would go into some of the homes intoxicated, and with a gun they would order food to eat and insist that it be cooked by the lady of the house. Tim John, had the misfortune to lose the sight 161

of one eye while in a fight with some white men. After that he was commonly known as "One eyed Tim".

After the Indians found out that the white men were not afraid of them and that they had come to stay, there was no more 

Duchesne Stake opening social and flower festival

ARCADIA 

163 trouble between them. The Indians proved to be men of their word and honest in their dealings with the white men. They would 

Arcadia. He has been county commissioner buy eggs, milk, and garden produce from the settlers. They said they would pay them at sundown, and they would wait until the sun was just setting to pay the white men.

There were no schools here in the early days, so it was necessary for the mothers to take the children into the towns or send them away for school.

Other settlers arrived in 1908 and 1909. 

Among these were: Francis Marion Ross, George 

Leander Gilbert, Bird, Charlie Robinson, Henry Richens and John prominent citizen of Hofeltz, who became postmaster of Red Cap. 

There were now enough who had taken up land which was not alloted to the Indians to have a school. A one-room school house was built on the Samuel McAfee homestead, with one teacher for eight grades. and aided in Moon 

Lake project as well as other civic enterprises.

These early settlers became identified with the Midview Branch which was organized July 10, 1910. When Midview was organized as a ward on Feb. 15, 1914, the north end of the Church was organized as a branch and was called the North Midview, with Alma A. Burgner as presiding Elder. He was succeeded in that position by Solomon O. Solmonson, in 1914, and on Sept. 14, 1917, the North Midview Branch was organized as a ward with Brother Solomonson as Bishop. The ward was given the name of Arcadia, after the ancient Arcadia in Greece, meaning the land of flowers and beauty. This name was suggested by Wm. H. Smart who was then president of the Duchesne Stake. It was passed on by an unanimous vote of the people. Later the Red Cap post office was changed to the Arcadia post office.

At a special meeting, Ozza Leandrew Gilbert was chosen as superintendent of the Sunday School with James C. Solomonsen, and Oliver L. Sagers as his assistants.

A Relief Society was organized by the Stake Relief Society president Aroetta Hale Holgate, with Sister Almeda Bird as presi dent. She chose Mary Ann Velerida and Eliza R. Smith as her counsellors. Sister Bird passed away and was succeeded by Nellie

Gordon, April 20, 1919. These women have been chosen as Relief 

Society presidents since that time: August 29, 1920, Aroetta H. 

Holgate, succeeded Nellie Gordon. On Aug. 20, 1924, Cecilia Gilbert was the next president. Sept. 12, 1935, Elsie S. Neilson was chosen. Sept. 1937, Ora N. Holgate presided until February 28,

1945, when she moved away and on June 10, 1945, Rose D. Gilbert was sustained to take her place.

Thomas M. Gilbert was chosen a Bishop in 1923, with Marvin 

G. Shields and A. Hale Holgate as his counsellors. Bishop Gilbert presided for fifteen years. During this time a beautiful Ward Chapel was erected which stands as a monument to his credit. 

On October 2, 1938, A. Hale Holgate succeeded Bishop Gilbert. 

He chose Ozza Leandrew Gilbert and Francis Marion Ross as his counsellors, with Austin Cope as Ward Clerk. He moved away on

April 1, 1945, and Francis Marion Ross became Bishop of the Ward with Wanless Shields and Charlie Bird as his counsellors; Marion 

S. Shields, ward clerk.

In December 1930, the Arcadia Ward had a membership of 134, including 36 children. In December 1946, it had a membership of 250 men, women, and children.

BONETA 

The first land filing on the Boneta flat at the opening of the 

Indian Reservation was made by Maroni Fisher of Vernal, Utah. 

Edward E. Cox, Peter O. Madsen, John W. Moffit, Fred Bench and Andrew Madsen, all of Manti, Sanpete County, Utah, Taylor 

Tidwell, and Thomas Merriwether of Fairview, Sanpete County, Utah, Wm. Jessen, and Ed. Reynolds of Wellington, Carbon County, Utah, made filings the same year.

During the year of 1906, and the spring of 1907, the Lake Fork Western Irrigation Canal was built. This carried water onto the Boneta Flat in time to plant crops. The sage brush was cleared and burned. Wheat, oats, potatoes, and a garden were planted by each of the early settlers. John W. Moffitt was the first president of the irrigation company, with Edward E. Cox as secretary. 

In the fall of 1907, Taylor Tidwell went back to Fairview to get a grain binder, with which he cut all the grain on the flat. 

There was no threshing machine available, so each man made a flail of his own, by taking two cottonwood poles from the river bottom, and put a cross piece from one pole to the other, and filled this cross piece full of spike nails. A wagon cover was spread out on the ground to catch the grain. Enough grain was flailed out in this manner for seed for the next year. The balance of the grain was fed to the animals in the grain bundles as no hay was available this side of Price or Vernal.

The next spring some of the men took their teams and wagons, and went to Price for hay that their teams might have feed while they were doing their spring work. The teams were poor, and the spring mud made traveling very difficult. They went by Myton through Nine Mile Canyon and were gone 16 days. By the time they reached the Lake Fork River, the last bail of hay that they had brought from Price was fed to their horses. 

These men did not give up. They turned around and went back after another load. The mud was somewhat died up by this time, and they returned with enough hay to last their poor animals until the grass was green on the river bottoms. This grass was used by the community as pasture. Many hours were spent hunting lost milk cows and horses.

Once a week for three years the people took turns going to 

Theodore (which is now Duchesne) to get their mail. Whoever went brought the mail for everyone on the flat. The first Post Office was in the home of Ruth B. Madsen, who was the first Post Mistress. 

The first little store was operated by James Mickelson. The first school opened in the fall of 1907. It was held in one room of 

Wallace Moffitt's home, which served as a school room in the day 165

1-Boneta conference visitors in Roosevelt; 2-Ed E. Cox family; 3-"Young Folks"

Margaret Moffitt was the first school teacher. 

John W. Moffitt of Boneta, was the first County Commissioner to be elected from the Uintah Basin at which time the country was a part of Wasatch County. He served in this position for twelve years, and it was during the period of time he was commissioner that the steel bridges at Myton, Duchesne and Utahn were built, and Duchesne County was divided from Wasatch County.
