BLUEBELL HISTORY OF BLUEBELL -By Leslie Goodrich 

When the Federal Government opened the Uintah Indian Reservation to white settlers there was a wild rush into this section. 

Homesteaders came from different parts of the country to find homes.

The first filings that were made on the land that comprises Bluebell and vicinity were made by the following individuals: 

Elmer Gale, Leslie Goodrich, Rhod Goodrich, Heber Powell, George

Spencer and Henry C. Wathen. All of the above came from Uintah 

County with the exception of Geo. Powell who was from Price and Harry C. Wathen from Holiday.

The first permanent settlers to come to what is now called Bluebell, were Elmer Gale, wife Delia, and two-year old son Jesse. 

They moved from Jensen, Uintah County, in a covered wagon drawn by a span of horses and arrived here on March 4, 1907. 

The following spring brought several more families to this section, among them were the following: Leslie Goodrich and wife 

Rhodes Goodrich, Heber Powell and wife, James Cook and wife,

Leonadis Lisonbee, Robert Alexander, John Ferrell, Harry C. 

Wathen and Roy Therning. 

These pioneers of Bluebell were impressed with the grandeur of the blooming bluebells waving their heads among the sagebrush and cedar grass during the months of May and June. This impression was so vivid that when they named the place, Heber Powell suggested that it should be named Bluebell, and all agreed. 

These first settlers, like all pioneers, had many difficulties to surmount. There was the clearing of the land, plowing and preparing it for crops, but no doubt the most difficult of all was getting the water from the Lake Fork river to irrigate the crops. This required a great effort and took a long time to dig the ditch. It was full two years before any irrigation water reached Bluebell. The historical facts of this part of this story are told by John K. Therning, one time secretary of the Farmer's Irrigation Company, in the following words: "Four or five miles west of Altonah, Dry Gulch No. 11 is taken out of Lake Fork river. This ditch is divided into classes, and Class 

B comes out of it five and one-half miles northwest of Bluebell coming down to one-half mile straight west of Bluebell, then continuing one mile farther south. "Class B was just a plow furrow in 1908, when water first came through it, and there was hardly enough water to reach to the end of the ditch for culinary purposes. The plow-furrow ditch. 170

BLUEBELL 

171 had been made under the direction of Heber Powell assisted by 

Henry Allred. 

"In 1910, the Farmer's Irrigation Company was organized to 

1-Elmer and Lillie Gale and family; 2-Mr. and Mrs. Lissonbee; 3-Mrs. Smtih, Mr. and Mrs. Lutellres Burdick; 4-Mrs. Goodrich and son, Alfred; 5-Effie Powell, Bluebell's first school teacher.

water what was then known as Alexander and near by. Alexander was located between the present towns of Altonah and Bluebell. "That water that is used in Bluebell is obtained from the Yellowstone river, a branch of the Lake Fork river. They also obtain some from the Payne Canal of Dry Gulch and the Class B Canal in Dry Gulch. 

“In 1945, the Farmer's Irrigation Company was consolidated with the Dry Gulch Irrigation Company, taking over the name of the latter."

Like other settlements in America, Bluebell has its Indian stories, but here at this later date these stories all tend to show the friendliness of the Indians rather than to show that they were enemies. Mrs. Lila Gale tell the following stories: "The Indians were our friends. Mr. Gale would be traveling around hunting his horses. When he would come to a little or low place in the brush, there he could see an Indian hiding. He found out that they were watching to see if he would steal or kill their cattle. They watched and watched, until they were satisfied that he would not take any of their cattle. Then they said, 'White man no take cattle'. "One time I became very frightened. My husband had gone up in the canyon and he did not get home until after dark. There was not a soul for miles around, so there was not anyone to call. 

I heard a loud coarse voice, it seemed more than one. I thought it must be drunken Indians, so I locked the door and did not make a fire. We had a large stove. I took my little boy and crawled behind it and wrapped him up, as it was cold weather. I did not make any light. "My little boy said, 'Scart, Mamme'? "I told him, 'No'. I did not want to scare him or make him cry. He knew there was something unusual. I told him to keep still until his Daddy came. Mr. Gale came soon afterward. One cannot imagine how glad I was to hear him coming. "It was a mountain lion that I had heard. It sounded so loud that I though there were many voices. My husband heard it and saw its tracks. They were the largest lion tracks he had ever seen. "At another time, three Indians came when my husband was after wood. I felt afraid. "They said, 'No be fraid, your man heap good. No hurt you'. "My little boy said, 'Mamme, why don't those men wash their faces clean"?" 

One day when Fred Goodrich and Jim Powell were getting dinner, two Indians rode up to their camp. They were building houses on their homesteads. The Indians wanted to know why they were building houses on their land. One of the men told them that the land belonged to them now. They talked to the Indians

BLUEBELL 

173 about the land for a while and gave them some dinner. When the 

Indians left, they said, "Good-bye, you my friend."

The first school at Bluebell was held for about two months in the fall of 1909 in what was known as Elmer Gale's granary, which has since been moved about one-fourth mile west of where it originally stood, and remodeled for a home for Jesse Gale and family. 

The remaining school term of 1909-1910 was finished in a dirt roof log cabin, which is now known as Gardner Goodrich's granary. 

At that time the school was known as Bluebell Public School, District No. 10, Wasatch County, Utah. Effie Powell was the first school teacher and she taught 29 students from beginners to sixth grade. The District School Board consisted of three members: 

Henry Allred, David Thompson, and Milton Alexander. 

By the fall of 1910 the main room of the Ward Meeting House was completed, and school from then on, until about December, 1927 was held in the Ward House. At the latter date, school was held in the present Bluebell School House. 

1-Goodrich homestead; 2-Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Anderson; 3-Mr. and Mrs. James 

Cook; 4-Mr. and Mrs. Levison Hancock.

Like school, the first Sunday School was held in Gale's granary for a few months; then it was held in W. P. Merrill's home until the 

Ward Meeting House was ready for use in the fall of 1910. In 

1939 a new chapel was begun but just a short time before it would have been completed, it burned down. The people did not give up, however, and started building all over again at once. This lovely new building was completed in the Spring of 1941. 

W. P. Merrill was the first Bishop in 1912, and he remained in that office for about twelve years. Bishop Leslie B. Goodrich was then ordained as Bishop of the Bluebell Ward. In 1938 Bishop 

Bernard Winkler was chosen as Bishop, and in 1946, he was replaced by the present Bishop, Gardner Goodrich.

The first store and Post Office was in one small room of Mr. 

Levison Hancock's home. Mr. Hancock became Post Master in about 1910, serving the people until about 1915, when Mary S. 

Merrill was appointed Post Mistress. She remained as such until 

March 1923, when John K. Therning was given the position which he held until he moved away in 1946. Hazel Goodrich was then appointed Post Mistress. 

The first mail came tri-weekly and was carried by horseback from Roosevelt by David Thompson. For many years the mail has been brought daily except Sunday from Myton by truck. 

The social life of the few settlers, who wintered in Bluebell during those first years was very limited. Mrs. Lila Gale writes an interesting account of how they spent Christmas in 1908. Here it is in her own words: "My husband said, 'Let's go to the saw mill a while and I will work there'. So we got ready to go. He worked there till the day of Christmas Eve. We started to go to Roosevelt. When we reached the gulch, an awful storm of snow came up. The horses just sat back and would not face the storm at all, so we camped in the cedars. 

My husband raised the wagon tongue and propped it up with the neck yoke, took a canvas and put it over the tongue like a tent, and making a bed we slept there. The storm lasted nearly all night. "Our little boy said, 'Mamme, will Santa come here in the storm? Will he know where we are'? "We told him, 'Yes, he will come'. So his stocking was hung on the cedar tree all covered with snow. In the morning he woke up and there in the snow on the tree was a stocking full of nuts and candy and nice toys. He was surely surprised. "The snow was four feet deep that winter. We began to think that we would be snowed in and thought it would be very hard to get out with a team and wagon. There was no hay to be had. We hauled straw from Roosevelt to feed our team and two cows. The rabbits sat on the snow and ate our young fruit trees clear down to the ground. Anyone would think they would have died, but they

BLUEBELL 

175 came out again and there are still lovely apples on them each fall." 

Until the Ward House was finished, the people danced in such places as Mr. Gale's granary and Gardner Goodrich's granary.

Ever since Mr. Gale planted potato peelings in the spring of 

1-Mr. and Mrs. James Larsen; 2-Mr. and Mrs. Wm. Porter Merrell; 3-Mr. and 

Mrs. Leslie Goodrich; 4-Mr. and Mrs. Herman Winkler.

1907 and they grew and made lovely potatoes without any water except rain, it has been known that Bluebell was a good farming district. For a while Bluebell was hampered in an agricultural way, due to the shortage of water. Before that time, however, this section was one of the heaviest producers of grain, hay, and livestock in the "Basin". Now, since the Moon Lake Reservoir has been completed, the farmers crops do not suffer so much because of lack of water. In most years there is enough water and consequently crops are better.

By Mrs. S. Lisonbee 

Alva L. Lisonbee and Mary R. Merrell Lisonbee were among the early settlers of Bluebell, Utah. Mrs. Lisonbee being the one that gave the town its name.

Alva L. Lisonbee was born January 23, 1871. He drove mail and stage coach during the early part of the Basin days. He moved with his family from Vernal to Bluebell in 1907 and helped to settle that country. He was among the first to freight and carried on with that work until the trucks replaced the freight wagon and teams. 

Mr. Lisonbee died Jan. 13, 1947. 

Mary R. Merrell Lisonbee was born April 18, 1875 in Hyrum,

Cash Valley, moving to the Vernal Country at the age of six years with her parents, Portor and Harriet Remmington Merrell.

Mrs. Lisonbee was a typical pioneer wife and mother, enduring many hardships to build up a new land. 

They are parents of twelve children, nine are living. They were both active members of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter Day 

Saints.

LOCALITY AND HISTORY OF CRESCENT, CEDARVIEW, MONTWELL AND MONARCH Compiled by the Daughters of Utah Pioneers, Roosevelt Camp, from diaries, city and church records, Roosevelt Standard, and personally contributed memoirs of original Basin settlers. 

Mrs. Myrtle Labrum, Roosevelt Camp Historian, and Mrs. 

Lila Nickell, Captain, were mainly responsible for this work. 

The history of Crescent, Cedarview, The Basin (not to be confused with Uintah Basin) and Monarch is so intertwined it is hard to write them. Later they were one community, then still later divided into two wards and now (1947) are one ward again except Crescent, which now belongs to Roosevelt Ward. 

Crescent, so named for the shape of the valley, bounds the section on the east and divided from them by two benches. One bench was chosen for the townsite and named Cedarview, because of the view one could see in all directions. A gulch runs along the valley between Cedarview and a neat little basin tucked in on three sides by wooded ridges or hills. This promptly became "The Basin". 

To the north was another dell with the same gulch flowing thru and was named Monarch. 1-The old church and school house; 2-The Relief Society room; 3-Mr. and Mrs. 

John G. Donis; 4-Milt Nickell homestead; 5-Mr. and Mrs. R. A. Powell. 177

James J. Sprouse and wife Emma and six of their children, were the first family to come to Crescent in the spring of 1906. They hauled a two-room log house (the first house built in Vernal) from Vernal to Crescent to live in temporarily and set it down in the middle of sagebrush, and pricklepears (cactus). One room still stands at this time 1947.

A few of the first families to follow them were Betterworth, after whom the place was first called, Lewis, Will Okes, Austin 

Glenn, Jake Sprouse, Joseph Timothy, Will Johnson, Wm. O'Neal, Elmer Pope, and families of Rudy, Crowley, Madsen, Hunting, Murray, Nickleson, and Rasmussen.

The first six families to settle in The Basin, were George 

Roberts, Thad Blackburn, Thomas Domen, Edward Hunt, David 

Brown and Joseph Merrill. 

The first families to settle Cedarview, were Balis Sprouse, Fred 

McCallister, R. A. Powell, Ed Huntsman, Ed Hogland, S. A. Russell, Mark Russell, Frank, Joe, and Shelby Fishback and John Davies, who became the first Justice of the Peace. 

Candus Hunt was the first woman to live in The Basin. Her husband, Edward, filed on his homestead about August 6, 1906. 

They were married on August 8, 1906. November 1st they moved to

The Basin with two teams, a sheep wagon and a buggy. Mrs. Hunt says that she will never forget the howl of coyotes around their camp. She did not know coyotes and associated them with wolves and was afraid of being attacked by them, so she stayed in the sheep wagon all day with the door shut while her husband and his brother Alva, went to look for the horses that had left during that first night, to return back home. The men were gone from daylight to dusk. 

It was late fall and there was a little snow on the ground and Mrs. 

Hunt says she nearly froze all day but was afraid to get out of the wagon to get wood for the fire until the men came home that evening, with the horses, hungry and tired.

The first settlers set up tents, lived in sheep wagons or dugouts until they could haul logs from the mountains and build cabins. 

Their homesteads were covered with sagebrush, greasewood and prickly pears. They soon discovered that if they hung out a washing and the wind blew, the clothes would be covered with fine prickly pear slivers and could not be worn until they were picked out by hand, a very tedious job.

As there was no feed in this new country it was necessary to haul the hay from Vernal. It then took several days to make the trip by team. Part of the time they paid from $30.00 to $40.00 a ton for baled hay. When they arrived home with it, they either had to put it in their tents or pile it on high sheds to keep wild horses from eating it as there were hundreds of them roaming over the country.

CRESCENT 

179 As the men and boys were busy getting out logs, fencing, hauling hay and building ditches and roads, the women were left alone a good part of the time. They soon learned to use a gun, and often protected their property from wild horses and coyotes. 

There were no markets and nothing to sell. The only source of income was hauling freight from Price or hauling lumber from the saw mill to Roosevelt for C. C. Larsen, in exchange for merchandise. The first cream market was in Myton. Neighbors doubled up and hauled it down by team on Friday of each week. 

The first water from the new canal reached the settlers in the spring of 1908. The first few years water was hauled for drinking and culinary purposes from springs that luckily were here, or melted snow or ditch water was used, which they improved by putting split prickly pears in it to settle the mud. This section of the Uintah Basin was very fortunate in water. As soon as people learned to dig for wells or locate for springs nearly everyone had one. A trial was made for artesian water in 1913 on David Brown's homestead. When water was struck in this first well, the big whistle sounded the good news over the entire settlement. Wells were then dug on a number of farms, and domestic water was plentiful. 

By 1907-08 nearly every homestead was taken up. Wm. John and Alva Labrum, Carl Shirts, Stephen Adams, Arthur and Scott and Willard Goff, Van and Joe Southers, Row Campbell were new in The Basin. R. A. Nielson, the father of the Southers Brothers, Q. Allred, David Carrouth, Renn Fenn, Ed and Charlie Green and in 1909 Milton Nickell came to Cedarview.

SCHOOLS 

The first school was in 1907-08, the second winter people were here. The school in The Basin was held in one room of David 

Brown's home and taught by Nana Fishback. The first school of 

Crescent was in the same year and held in a tent with a stove in the center, but when the snow kept falling so heavy, they moved into 

Jos. Murray's granary to finish out the year. Fred Holmes was the first teacher. A short time later people joined together and went to the mountains for logs and made a one-room school house. They held school here for four or five years, with the first eight grades in one room and under one teacher. This same log school house was also used for church and Sunday School with Joseph Timothy and 

William O'Neal taking charge. The people of Cedarview and The Basin built a log school house on the gulch bank, on Geo. Robert's property. The school accommodated the children from the commuities of Monarch, Cedarview and The Basin. The children came by team and horse back, some of them traveling as far as six or seven miles. Miss 

Valeria was the first teacher and Mrs. Linnie Baker taught the fol-

lowing winter. These teachers took all grades in one room. The beginners learned A B C's first, then the first graders learned to read by the help of a large chart that stood in front with dozens of sheets that were turned over as each new one was learned. 

In 1910 a partition was put in and two teachers employed, Amy Remington and Hugh Barnes. They taught two years. For the year of 1912-13 the school was divided. The smaller grades of The Basin went to a tent on Thomas Doman's south east corner and were taught by Effie Davis. The upper grades of the entire section went to a one-room log house on the abandoned Ferman homestead, located in the junipers between The Basin and Monarch. This school was taught by Roy C. Corbett. In 1914 elections were going on to centralize a school which finally, after bitter debate and a lot of wrangling, resulted in three small separate schools built by the county, at The Basin, Cedarview and Monarch to accommodate the children of the scattered families. The old log school was moved to Cedarview townsite. Crescent children went to this school with Cedarview.

POST OFFICE 

The first post office was established in a little dugout on the gulch and run by Edward Hogland. Shortly after Thomas Roberts. came, in 1909, they took the post office to Cedarview townsite and kept it until it was taken over by Roosevelt rural route. 

The people of Monarch had to travel to this office for mail-a distance of eight miles, until a post office was established at Monarch SEDT Cedarview booth at the Hay Palace fair, 1912. Many varieties of vegetables and fruits were exhibited.

CEDARVIEW 

181 in C. A. Brown's home, and a bi-weekly mail served the people of this section. The mail carriers were Barney Miller, two years, Wm. 

Hamblin for eight years, then Ed Blanchard. They carried it from the Cedarview office to Monarch. R. A. Powell was mail carrier for four years, carrying the mail from Cedarview to Hayden and Whiterocks post offices. 

CEDARVIEW CEMETERY 

The nearest doctor was at Myton. One had to go for him on horseback and he made his calls by buggy. When sickness came, neighbors helped one another. The Elders were the only doctors used in many cases. The first two deaths were children. They were buried on the north edge of the woods in the Basin north of Raymond Neilson's home, on part of his land. Later when Annie Summerall 

Southers died a meeting was held to choose a graveyard. The present site was chosen and Annie was the first one buried there. 

Aunt Wealthy Sheffer came about 1912 and was a welcome member. She was a midwife and was kept busy until she retired at the age of 75 or more, she would go to the home and do the cooking, wash, and care for the mother for two weeks. She delivered more babies in this neighborhood than any other person. It was not uncommon for her to be the only attendant as the doctor was so hard to get. It is rumored that she never lost a case in all the years she worked. She was also President of Cedarview Relief 

Society for a good many years. 

As soon as telephones came, a phone was put in Raymond 

Neilson's home to serve Cedarview, and one installed in Wm. 

O'Neal's home to serve Crescent.

STORES 

Small stores were necessary to serve the neighborhood as it was not always possible to go to Myton for every item needed. The first store was started by Thomas Roberts in connection with his Post Office. Mothers would say, "Run to the Post Office and get me a can of coal oil." The groceries were bought from Myton or direct from Z. C. M. I. in Salt Lake City. Later, Les Allen had a store in Cedarview, and Ed Hunt turned a room of his home into a store in The Basin. These only succeeded for a few years. Steve 

Shelton built a store across from the Allen store and the two of them ran competition to one another for awhile, until Shelton moved to Duchesne. R. A. Powell bought out Allen, moved the store to the other corner, and started a two storey brick store and home. 

This was about 1916, the brick store was not completed until about 

1921. In 1927, this store and home burned. No other store or city building was started. There was a blacksmith shop, post office, two school buildings, large church house, also quite a number

of homes with blocks and streets laid out for a city. Cedarview was too near Roosevelt to become a city. The Post Office was abandoned in favor of R. F. D., and children were taken to Roosevelt to school.

Living was hard for both men and women. Summers found them busy trying to raise a crop and clear the land. Winter would find the men freighting to get enough cash for necessary expenses. 

The roads were primitive, and it took three weeks in good weather to get to Salt Lake or Price. The road to Salt Lake was not used in winter. In the heaviest storms mail would be hauled as far as could be by team then hauled by dog sled over the top to meet a wagon and team on the other side. Homesickness had to be fought against, as sometimes six weeks went by before hearing from home or loved ones from "Outside", as everyone called the outside world.

Anyone can see why the freighters did not handle perishable food. People went hungry for fresh fruits and vegetables in the winter. Every fall a few would undertake the trip to Vernal to bring back fruit. Each neighbor would order all they could get then to bring in a buggy as it took too long in a freight wagon. The mothers had a hard time getting any to bottle as the children were so hungry for fresh fruit. One never got enough for one's needs and as sugar was cheaper than fruit and preserves lasted longer than 'fresh fruit most of it was preserved. Several pioneers tried to raise fruit, but were never successful in this country, except in a few scattered places.

Children had to learn to be useful. Lela Neilson Labrum tells of one of her experiences: "My sister and I were playing one evening at bed time. Father came and with a look of mingled worry and love said, 'Mother is sick, can you go and get Aunt Meetta and

Uncle Roberts to come?' We said we could. It was the first time we had been out of doors at night alone. Clea was very timid and clung to me tightly. It was a mile to walk. I reassured her that there was nothing to be afraid of. When we reached the gulch, we had to cross on a log that was flattened, and Clea became frightened by the distance reflected by the stars, in the water. We had to straddle across. I thought I'd never get her over to the other side. But we met no Indians or wild animals and we soon returned with our neighbors." 

Thos. Roberts operated a molasses mill across from his post office. He took one third of the molasses for pay. This home-made molasses furnished many a family with sweets. Byron Roberts tells how he and his brother, Sam, had many duties helping in the cooking and grinding of the sugar cane. 

Most folks remember the sagebrush and cactus, but the country side was a flower garden in the spring and summer. Every wild flower grew in profusion everywhere. Especially appreciated were

CEDARVIEW 

183 Sam Page and Jimmy Bacon on the Centennial Trek from Nauvoo, 1927. Mr. Bacon has been one of the most helpful and successful men in this section.

184. 

the blue lupins and bluebells. Lela Labrum writes: "I remember crying at the sight of a neighbor plowing a field of bluebells. I said, 'Why doesn't he put his house there and plow the ugly knoll his house is on'?" The flowers decorated the rocky hillsides. Sand lillies beautified the sandy places aided by the beautiful sego lilies. Even the hated cactus sent forth beautiful yellow, white, pink and red blossoms in the spring. This condition lasted until about 1920, when the sheepmen roamed their flocks throughout the country. 

AMUSEMENTS 

There were good times during those first years, not all was hard. Dances were held in David Brown's home with Ed Cloward and his banjo as orchestra. It was not strange if they danced until morning. They would meet at different homes to spend the evening singing or for a molasses candy pull or chicken, sage hen or rabbit supper..

The boys had great sport lassoing and riding wild ponies, even to the extent of some of them being expelled from school for going out on the foothills and catching ponies during the school hours. 

Everyone boasted a fine riding horse and team. Raymond Neilson raised race horses for sale. The boys of the community, mounted on fast and fancy steeds, would let the world know they were coming to town by shooting guns with both hands while holding reins in their teeth.

At the parties all furnished their own amusements. A number of them soon obtained pianos and everyone was called on to play, sing, or step dance, or speak a piece.

Sleigh riding during the winter was another favorite pastime, and many times they went over to church services and socials at 

Cedarview by the wagon box load on sleighs. 

There was a grand celebration every 4th and 24th of July, complete with Goddess of Liberty, parades, races, games and programs. Some very kind freighter would bring in a few oranges for the celebration. Everyone wore her new home made 4th of July dress that had been the topic of discussion for a month. Nothing nowadays can equal those celebrations for excitement. Funds for the event were raised by voting for a Goddess at a penny a vote, at a very exciting, crowded, pre-fourth dance. Everyone was admitted to the celebration free. Montwell still has their annual Labor Day rodeo.

In 1912 they boasted a band, led by Lars B. Neilson. It started as the Neilson band as Raymond, and wife Laura, and daughter 

Lela, a nephew William Hawkins, L. Neilson, Effie Davis Neilson were members. Later they added all the others who wished to join

MONTWELL 

185 and had a full sized band. Lars Neilson arranged all the music by hand.

Every year, drama, cantattas, parties, and programs were given. 

A choir was assembled for special occasions with Raymond Neilson leading. The Primary and school both presented a cantatta every winter.

CHURCH 

The first Church services in The Basin was a little Sunday School organized in the fall of 1908 by President Smart in the home of David Brown. Then a branch was organized with Gilbert Richardson as presiding Elder. The people of Cedarview were also under his direction. Church services were held in the little school house on the gulch as soon as it was built. In 1910, a Church house was built in Cedarview by the work and contributions of the whole community from Monarch to Crescent, under the direction of S. A. 

Russell, who was chosen Bishop.

In 1917 a Basin Primary was organized as a Neighborhood of Cedarview. The first Primary was held the first day of May with an enrollment of 36 children. Florence Richardson was the first president with May Labrum and Sarah Adams as counselors and Amy 

Scrogings as secretary. Four years later, Sept. 4, 1921, a new Branch was organized by President Lambert. Övey A. Richardson was chosen as Branch Presiding Elder with Thomas Doman and Wm. 

Labrum counselors and Arthur Goff, clerk. The name of Wells was chosen for this Branch because of the many artesian wells in the community. Church services were held in a log school house which had been purchased by Edward Hunt from Crescent and moved to Wells.

Two years later a Ward was organized at Wells by the Stake 

Presidency. Ovey A. Richardson was chosen Bishop with Wm.

Labrum first counselor, Arthur Goff as second counselor, and Effa Goff as clerk. As there was already a Wells ward in the Church it was necessary to rename the new ward. President Fredrick S. Musser proposed it be called Montwell as a short for the Mountains, Monarch and Wells, the two communities being joined together. Both the log community house and the one room school house were used for church services until the time of the W. P. A. when two more rooms were added to the school house and three teachers were employed for school, the children being brought from Cedarview, Monarch, and Montwell in buses. Then three or four years later the schools were all consolidated, and all grades brought to Roosevelt. The Church and the people of the three communities then bought this school house for a chapel, remodeled it and added a furnace room. They tore down the log school house and used part

of it in construction of the new church house; under the direction of Bishop Stephen Adams, it was dedicated. Cedarview was made part of Montwell ward on Oct. 28, 1935.

MONARCH 

Among the first homesteaders in Monarch were Frank Winn, the four Woodward families, Morris Charles, John and Don; Hugh 

Barnes, the three Huff families, Byron, Job and Frank; Dennis 

Anderson, Barney Miller, Lester and Earl Brown and Scott Murray.

Some of the men left their families and went to the saw mill to work. Mrs. Morris Woodward relates how they lived in fear of the 

Indians (although they were never molested by them). She tell that they got so hungry for fresh vegetables that they cooked the leaves of the grease wood . . . They have a taste like string beans, they found. They made Brigham tea from Mountain rush. 

Mrs. Golda Adams tells of her mother, Mrs. Dennis Anderson, grinding sugar beets and cooking them. She would then drain off the liquid, boil it down and make candy of the syrup. They also raised ground cherries which made delicious pies, when other fruits were hard to get. They often had fried rabbit or roasted sagehen. 

Clarence Brown rode to Duchesne, a distance of about 45 miles, on horseback to meet with the school board, and asked for a school in Monarch, for the welfare of the children. It would have been impossible for the little folks to attend school in Cedarview as there were no transportation facilities. The first Monarch school was held in a log cabin on the Hugh Barnes homestead in the year 1911. The teacher was Miss Effie Davis. School was held five months out of the year for about five years. In 1915, a school house was built on land bought from Barney Miller. The small grades attended school here while the older grades were taught at the Cedarview school. 

Primary was held from the first year of the opening of the reservation. Jennie Woodard was one of the first teachers.

The post office in Monarch commenced in 1914 in Clarence A. Brown's home where it remained for 17 years. In the meeting to get their post office and give it a name Mrs. Lee Brown suggested 

Monarch which was agreeable with everyone present. When they discontinued the post office, the mail came to Cedarview and was distributed from there up the country twice a week to Monarch by Barney Miller as the first carrier. Each family's mail was put in a separate sack and left at the homes. 

In 1930, the present R. F. D. was started. They still used the sacks and if anyone wanted to send a mail order or mail parcels or letters, they would put them in the sack for the carrier to pick up on his return trip to the Post Office in Roosevelt. A few years later

MONARCH 

187 the sacks were discontinued and the mail left in the boxes, as at present.

The following incident is told by Mr. and Mrs. Charles Woodward:

Henry Hall, his wife and two or three small children came from their home in Hayden to George F. Yeager's in Monarch with a team and wagon, for some potatoes. There was no bridge across the Dry Gulch, and when they came back on their way home there was quite a stream of water. They thought it would be safe to cross over so they drove in, but it was deeper than they thought. 

It continued to rise rapidly until at least five feet of water had run into the wagon box. The muddy water and rolling brush and weeds forced them down stream until the wagon caught on a small cottonwood tree which held and kept them and their team from being drowned. 

Mr. and Mrs. Woodward had been visiting that day at John

Woodward's home. They did not know there had been a flood and were on their way home when they found the Halls in the water. 

Mr. Hall unhooked the tugs, got his wife and children onto the horses then got on himself and they reached the bank safely. Mr. 

Woodward helped them to get the wagon out and back up onto the road, then they went back to his brothers and stayed all night. 

There had been no storm in Monarch but evidently there had been a cloud burst in the mountains above.

EARLY HISTORY OF DUCHESNE 

This information was gathered by Maxine Burdick through personal contact with the following first settlers of Duchesne and vicinity: A. M. Murdock, commonly known as "Al" and also spoken of as the Father of Duchesne, now deceased; L. C. Winslow, or "Bud", the earliest resident now living in Duchesne. He came on Aug. 11, 1905, and is still living here; Carrie Murray, widow of R. Ellison Murray, Duchesne's first Marshall; Mrs. O. A. Wilkins, widow of "Dad" Wilkins; Ollie Schonian, widow of E. W.

Schonian, a long-time merchant of Duchesne; Andy Clemmons, a bachelor, but a builder of many of the fine homes in Duchesne today; Emma C. Wimmer, my mother, widow of J. D. Wimmer, a farmer and stockman who held the plow while Jos. Cowan, her brother, drove the team to break the first plow furrow for the 

Rocky Point Ditch; and Wm. H. Fitzwater, the first post master and at present, Mayor of Duchesne. 

MARKER No. SIXTEEN-FATHER ESCALANTE 

Duchesne, Utah. Dedicated October 23, 1936 "In commemoration of the Catholic priest, Father Escalante, who, in 1776 came into Utah. He crossed the Green River at Jensen, and camped two days at that place. His diary shows that he camped at the Junction of the Strawberry and Duchesne Rivers one night and then traveled on northwest up through what he called "The Canyon of the Swallows." This is the canyon in which Ivie's ranch is now located. This became an important intersection in pioneer days as distances were reckoned from this point.— 

Duchesne County Company, Daughters of Utah Pioneers." (Marker inscription) 188

LOCATION AND BUSINESS 

´HE LITTLE CITY of Duchesne is located just above and be tween the junction of the Strawberry and Duchesne rivers. 

It is the first town you find traveling east on Highway 40 after 75 miles of mountain roads from Heber, and 60 miles west of Vernal. These were the nearest communications when the town was first settled and in those days I must say, the distance was much greater due to poor indirect winding roads. Today we have our fast automobiles, and the distances is comparatively short but in 1905 when saddle horses were the fastest communication, it was considered a very lengthy journey.

History and markers along Highway 40 give the information of how Father Escalante, a Catholic Missionary, and his party, the first white men in this territory, came across Colorado, crossed the Green River down by White River, and came on west up the Duchesne River. This was in 1776. They camped at the junction of the two rivers, then went on west up the Strawberry river, camping again at the old Ivie ranch, giving the canyon there the name "Valley of the Swallows." They went on seeking a route to California. This junction became important to the people of the west as distances were reckoned from this point. 

A beautiful marker was erected at the campsite of Father Escalante's party, during October 16, 1936, by the Daughters of 

Utah Pioneers of Duchesne County, under the direction of President of Theodore Camp, Mrs. Billings. Mrs. M. J. Edwards, county camp president at that time, started the movement and worked unceasingly until it was completed. 

In 1905, the United States government opened the reservation for white filings of 160 acres each. Registrations were made at Price, Provo, and Grand Junction, in Aug. 1905. Drawings were made for filing numbers, filings were made at the Vernal 

Land Office. 

A lot of people, of course, came in to look the situation over. 

The first general gathering took place about June 1, of that year. 

A. M. Murdock, then an Indian trader at White Rocks, got permission from the government to set up up a post between the junction of the Duchesne and Strawberry rivers, the Strawberry being named for the red valley called the Strawberry Valley. 

By the 6th of June, 1905, Mr. Murdock had pitched a large circus tent just west of the Murdock residence, and had it well supplied with hay, grain, and food supplies. There were 52 men, and one woman, Dora, daughter of Mr. Murdock, and one Indian, Sugoosie Jack. This was the beginning of the colonization of 

Duchesne. They organized and decided to call the place Dora, for 190

DUCHESNE 

191 Miss Murdock. A few weeks later the government sent surveyors in to survey a townsite and lay it out. Beginning at the Southeast corner the streets running east and west are lettered a, b, c, etc., and the streets running north and south are numbered 1, 2, 3. They gave it the name of Duchesne.

This survey was accepted by the government October 18, 1905. 

The name, however, was not accepted at the first, and the people called it Theodore, for

Theodore Roosevelt. After Roose velt was named, to avoid confusion with the mail, people had to call it Duchesne. This was in 

September, 1911. 

Arthur Duchesne Marsh was the first white child born here in Duchesne in Sept., 1905. Dr.

Bjarnson of Vernal had been here and filed, but had returned to 

Vernal, so Arthur Sr., the boy's father, delivered him. 

Madora Steel, daughter of Mr. 

and Mrs. Oliver Steel, was the first girl born here in April 1906. 

Her father went to Vernal as was the custom, for large amounts of supplies of food, hay, and grain, and made arrangements for Dr. Bjarnson to be in Duchesne for the baby's arrival.

A. M. MURDOCK 

The first cabin built in Duchesne was built by Arthur Marsh. 

He went up Jones Hollow, up Indian Canyon, and got red pine logs. Harve Partridge, and his son, Harvey Jr., a Mr. Baldock and several others helped to build it. It was located about where Jimmy 

Hatch's residence now is. Some five or six years later this cabin was moved over on the Andy Clemons block, where it served as Mr. Clemons residence until it was destroyed by fire about 1923. 

Murdocks store served as a social center the winter of 1905. 

It was built in the late fall of that year, and was the first business house. They danced between the counters to the tune of Bud Winslow's guitar, and it was after the Christmas Eve dance that Bill 

Pickering froze to death. He had left the store for some reason, and when he returned, the store was locked, so he started hom without his overshoes and coat. He was living four miles east of town in a cabin with Reuben Whitehead, which they had built in

the first barber did his work in the saloon. In 1906, they built a larger building on the Conoco Station corner. 

Roy Cook and his wife, Sarah, and C. I. Dickerson had the first eating place. This was in 1906, and was built in the alley across from the hall. It was a dugout with a tent behind it. Later they moved to the Allen Oil corner, and Pearl Jasperson became a partner. Mr. Dickerson started a watch and clock shop in connection with it.

Old Lady Sutton also ran an eating house where the school board offices now stand. It burned down in 1906. 

Perry Dean had the first meat market, on the corner where the 

Shell Service now operates. 

Wilfred Perry, in 1906 started a Drug Store in a tent back of

Murdock's store. He later moved into the Gilner-Harter store, west of the Clemons-Munz Saloon. This building burned and he moved into the saloon. In 1915 he moved again, this time to where the 

Rexall Drug still stands.

C. I. Dickerson, with Marcellus Pope and Bud Winslow organized the first band. They all played brass horns.

Andy Clemons and Oliver Steele built and operated a saloon on the corner of the Mission Motel.

Buell's Pioneer Store was the first building of any importance. 

It was large, two storied, and located on the north side of Main Street between 5th and 6th streets. It was stocked as any General 

Store, John Meadows store was on the corner just west.

Since Duchesne still belonged to Wasatch County, there was a small court house, built from lumber by Bud and Earl Winslow, just west of the Allen Oil corner. Judge J. R. Wilson dealt in law and real estate and had a little office just west of this building. 

M. M. Smith was appointed Justice of the Peace, and served until 

1909, when an election was held and Bud Winslow was elected to hold that office. Bill Jolley was first Deputy Sheriff, and J. Elison

Murray had a homestead up the Duchesne. He was the first Constable of the town, and he held this position for 15 years. He was the biggest man in town, weighing 220 pounds. 

The oldest house now standing in Duchesne is the old Marsing Home on Main Street. Bud and Earl Winslow made the brick for that school house at their bridge across the Strawberry. A carpenter by the name of Atkinson did the architecture and carpenturing. 

The Dan Powell home, across the street from it, was built soon after. 

The first telephone in Duchesne was the eastern Utah line.

Mr. Murdock helped finance this project, and the telephone was

DUCHESNE 

195 installed in the Murdock residence, which had been the first school house.

In 1915 the first telephone office was established in the Grant Hotel. Susan Grant was the first telephone operator. It was later moved to the present Duchesne Hotel. In 1930 the Mountain States 

Telephone and Telegraph Company purchased the line from the 

Uintah Telephone Company. 

The first feed and livery stable was owned by Mr. Murdock, and “Daddy Mahoney" took care of the horses. 

Abe Gilner and Harter had a general store west of Emil Munz

The store closed in two or three months.

Another fine hotel and store burned in 1921.

It occupied the space of the Plaza Hotel and was the same size and structure. The fourth, the Cottage Hotel, located just east of the "Little Church," was built about 1912, and was destroyed about 1928. 

The Plaza Hotel was built immediately following the destruction of the former hotel and was run for many years by Etta 

Kirk, widow of Char les Odekirk. 

Margaret Odekirk, or "Grandma" as she was known to every- one in Duchesne-mother of Katie Firth and Charles Odekirk, kept a boarding house at her home for many years.

Joe Lewis had the first Blacksmith shop in Theodore, and Allen J. Feller had a very good shop across the street from the 

Mission Motel. Mr. Feller was all and more than the "Village

Blacksmith" called for. He came to Duchesne about 1912, and was very active in his business, until 1931 when he was killed in an automobile accident.

James Hair came to Theodore in 1905. For some time he lived on their homestead about 5 miles east of Theodore, now the Clarence Wilson farm. About 1912, Mr. Hair, Fred Davis, and Steve

Shelton built the Anna Nielson building and ran a store there. Later they dissolved partnership, and Mr. Hair took the groceries and dry goods portion, and bought the west portion of Kohl's store and ran

DUCHESNE 

199 a grocery and dry goods store there until 1926 when he moved to 

Salt Lake City. 

Lucy Crites had Theodore's first millinery shop. Louella Washburn had one beside the old Washburn home on Main Street. 

Mart L. Marsing built the building where the cold storage plant now stands and operated a general merchandising store there for a good many years. Wm. Baker laid the brick for this building. 

In 1914, a group of people from Topeka, Kansas, came into 

Duchesne. The Deans, Billings, and Greys organized the Bank of 

Duchesne. Laird Dean was Bank President and Fern Grey was

Cashier. This bank went into the hands of receivers during the panic following the first world war.

The Billings organized a real estate agency, too. It was "Billings, Dean, and Billings", with offices in the old Perry Drug Store.

The Duchesne County Abstract Company was organized about 

1915, by A. N. Alt. 

James Dalgleish ran a confectionery store in the east section of the hotel for a good many years.

Ernest W. Schonian had Duchesne's first Furniture store, located one half block north of main street. He was also the first 

Mortician. He managed both businesses together. In 1924 he built a large furniture store on Main Street, now being occupied by the 

Moore's Cafe, The Record Office, and a Furniture store. 

George Kohl came to Theodore in 1905. He worked some time as a surveyor and filed on a homestead in the right hand fork of Indian Canyon. In April, 1916, he opened a store in the Al 

Murdock building on the Allen Oil corner. After a few months he took Tom Firth in as a partner, and they moved down to the present location of Kohl's Market.

Shortly after, Mr. Firth sold his interest back to Mr. Kohl. Mr. 

Firth and his wife, the former Katie Workman, bought the Golden

Rule store from Tom Kelso. This was located where the Commercial club now stands.

In 1922, James Grant, or "Daddy" as he was called by everyone in town, built Duchesne's first Service Station. The County 

Garage now stands in it's location. Mr. Grant's son, Perry, operated the Station. At the present time Duchesne supports ten Service

Stations. 

The east section of Kohl's store was a cafe for many years, and was run by the Caldwells, Wrights, Killians, and others.

The Skagg's building was a hardware store for many years, operated first by Paul Billings, Guy Whitford, and in 1929 by - 

Arthur Orr. Mr. Orr ran it until 1936 when he closed it. 

L. A. Hollenbeck moved into Duchesne on April 1, 1914. He was an excellent lawyer. He and Laird Dean formed a company,

and built all the buildings on the Post Office corner. This included the Drug Store building and also the Skagg's building. 

Wattrus and Bunn moved their newspaper business into the building south of the Post Office, and until 1938, it was the home of the Newspaper. 

Walter and Harry Walker ran the drug store, where Harry practiced Dentistry.

NAMES OF EARLIEST SETTLERS 

In listing the names of the early settlers, it is understandable why A. M. Murdock's name would come first. Mr. Murdock has always been called the father of the town. He held many public offices, and should have much credit for the early settling of the 

Uintah Basin.

CIVICS March 13, 1914 the Wasatch County Commissioners, J. W. 

Moffat, Daybell, and Cummings advocated the creation of a new County. Inconvenience to larger part of the country, lack of representation, over use of deputies, and apparent geographical conditions were given as main reasons.

An argument arose as to where the County Seat should be located. Campaigns were carried on in the local papers. Wm. H. 

Smart of Roosevelt was one of the main bootsers for Roosevelt; 

B. L. Dart for Myton; and A. M. Murdock, Laird Dean, Paul 

Billings, and Rock Pope helped boost Duchesne over and won the election. were:

The County officers elected to take office January 1, 1915, Edward Mackey, County Clerk and Recorder; Lula Hood, Treasurer; C. W. Smith, Assessor; Ray E. Dillman, County Attorney; County Commissioners were J. E. L. Carey, George Lindsay, and Russell. J. A. Washburn was elected County Superintendent.

Some of the men residing in Theodore that officiated in the government under Wasatch County were: Rock Pope, State Representative; Orson Ryan, County School Superintendent; C. W. 

Smith, Deputy Assessor; B. L. Dart, Deputy County Treasurer;

M. B. Pope, Game Warden. 

B. L. Dart collected the poll tax. This was a tax for the roads, and was assessed to every man when he became voting age. It amounted to about $2.00 per year.

In 1906 Theodore held their first election. 146 people registered for this election, with 107 voters at the final elections, three of which were Indians. The judges of elections were: John A. Fortie, L. Claire Winslow, Parley P. Pratt.

DUCHESNE PIONEERS 

Now who of you has won the right 

To be called Duchesne Pioneers 

Some of you came in 1905 

And some in later years. you 

Who remember the winter

There were things could not buy 

And to go out over the frozen trails 

You did not dare to try. From friend I heard this story 

I suppose the tale is true 

And I am going to pass it on 

As I heard the tale, to you 

That a neighbor Charlie Brown 

One of our Pioneers

Had been used to eating hotcakes 

Every morn for forty years

It is quoted of him saving, "Wife some bacon must be found 

For I must have my hotcakes 

Or I'll soon be under ground." 

She saved she said some bacon rind 

To make a bit of soap 

And now for Charley's hotcakes 

They were her only hope. 

She says she greased the griddle 

With those rinds two months or more 

Before they got some bacon 

In A. M. Murdock's store. 

Then the sugar too it would give out 

No coal oil could be found,

As these people in the Basin 

Were for many weeks snowbound.

Of roads there were not any, And the trails were pretty rough 

The weaker hearted Pioneers 

Sure felt they had enough. 

And when the snow had melted 

And they thought their troubles o'er 

The Duchesne River raised so high 

They could hardly reach the store.

At the junction of two rivers 

After a heavy winters snow 

What they did not learn one 

They were sure the next to know. season

It was no place for faint hearted It took men and women too 

To learn to make all they needed

These people had to do. 

Ed Hart was one of the first here 

Clare Winslow was here too

They can tell you the story 

Much better than I can do.

Of A. M. Murdock I've spoken 

He ran the first good store

Put in the first feed stable 

And I can't say how much more.

He was a leading figure 

In all those early days 

And helped put Duchesne on the map 

In a good many ways. 

George Bowers our present Editor 

Of the Courier you know,

He too can give you the story 

If to him you care to go.

DUCHESNE 

205 

And Charles I. Dickerson is another 

Who lives here yet you see, Then Edward and Jackman Herrick 

For a true tale ask those three. 

All helped in making new History

Each in his different way many And 

Our rejoicing on Bridge Day. of will remember you 

Thankful in our hearts we were

For that steel across the stream After our many hardships 

It did seem like a dream 

All remember the pretty little girl 

Who christened the Bridge that day

The first child born upon this soil 

Now she has moved away.

Pretty little Medora Steel 

You are not forgotten quite, Your name tale will be spoken with this 

On many a summers night. 

And a woman you all remember 

Stands for her worth alone

She has passed on ahead to the great beyond 

May she be seated near the throne. 

She was Mrs. J. R. Lewis

Called the mother of this town 

For her goodness love and mercy 

Has this woman won renown.

There were many many many more But this tale is growing long, If I've left out deserving names 

I have not meant to wrong.

There have been so many hardships 

For those who came to stay 

And we haven't many luxuries yet 

Thought we still all work and pray. 

If they will give us now thre railroad 

And a market within reach

It will surely mean much to us 

And will wisdom to them teach. 

-Written by MRS. WILDER FAIRBANKS EARLY HISTORY OF DUCHESNE AS TOLD BY BERNICE PETERSON MECHAM 

We went to Duchesne in November, 1905, over the pass from

Colton, with two wagons and driving milk cows, a distance of 55 miles. We were two nights on the road and a wet snow fell on us all the way. We stayed at the sawmill in Indian Canyon the second night. Charley Clapper, a Duchesne pioneer worked there at the time. The Indian Canyon road seemed endless and strangely enough has never become any shorter, cars or not cars. 

B. Y. Spratt was with us. He and his brother-in-law lived there the first winter. His name was Bill Brennick.

The town of 8 or 10 cabins was called Strawberry, then Dora, for Dora Murdock, then Theodore, for Theodore Roosevelt, and finally Duchesne.

The townsite was just above the junction of the Strawberry and 

Duchesne rivers. A. Murdock's store and Post Office operated by

Derry Smith was at the bend of the Duchesne, and later moved down in the flat. The first winter, the following people lived in town: Mr. and Mrs. Derry Smith, Lois, Eurma, Daphne, Sammy and two babies, Mr. and Mrs. Roy Cook, Mr. and Mrs. O. C. 

Steele, Mr. and Mrs. Jow Lewis, Mr. and Mrs. J. B. Peterson, Bernice, Max, and Jay Peterson, Arthur Marsh, wife, and Myrtle

Piney, Theresa, and "Boy" Marsh (the first white boy born there), Mr. and Mrs. John Meadows, and two children, Mr. and Mrs.

Charley Dickerson and Helen, Andy, and Al Murdock and Dora, Emil Munz, Gene Hamilton, Bud Winslow, Homer Brandenburg, Andrew Larson, John Clyde, the law, John Grant, "Toot" Jasperson and a fellow by the name of Harter and a dozen more maybe.

One Sunday in December when the river was frozen solid, the whole town had a picture taken on the ice. The families were congenial, and we played High Five and Whist in the long winter evenings. The time machine had not ground out 500 and Bridge as yet. We danced every Friday night, first in the new Murdock store, and then in the 40x50 amusement hall. Sometimes on a holiday, as the Fourth of July, we would dance three nights straight.

The Indians enjoyed watching, though they disapproved our style. 

Phonographs, with cylindrical records and later discs, and all equipped with large horns, were appearing in the homes. “Dearie” and "In the Shade of the Old Apple Tree" assaulted the ear, but the old time fiddler still played for the dances when we danced the waltz, two-step, and quadrilles. Can we ever forget Dan Wheeler, his carpet slipper beating time until the rafters shook and the sun came over the mountain, telling us it was time to retire? I can imagine him in the Elysian Fields, high over Wasatch Range, still fiddling the happy hours away.

The first summer, campers bedded down anywhere on the townsite. One night, a wag, armed with two cowbells, charged over a tenderfoot's bed. The T. F. literally took up his bed and ran to avoid the stampede. The Indians used to spread their blankets and play monte. After losing pony, saddle, etc. an Indian would rise, dust off his pants, say "busted"! and leave the flat. The majority of the settlers were from Wasatch and Uintah counties. 

Bernice Peterson used to send news items to the Vernal Express when Dan Hillman was editor. She had a long distance feud on with Johnny Jenson, who sent in the Jenson items.

J. B. Peterson was the first surveyor. He ran the lines for homesteads and canals and had a first hand knowledge of the reservation topography. One hot day he shed his overcoat, hung it on a tree and forgot it. Months later he saw it on an Indian. 

The same Indian who, when asked why he did not get the doctor for his wife said, "Maybeso if she not die by Monday, I will."

Located on the Uintah Indian Reservation, the town was headquarters for the Indians who traveled by pony express. Hammerheaded ponies, tough as pine knots. The Indians used to have the mating dance and bear dance, in the spring at the Indian Village below town. There they traded in the old wife for a new and often swapped back again the following spring. A Ute Indian, Moroni, who lived at the Indian Village, was the big cattleman of the

DUCHESNE 

207 tribe. His cattle were ornery looking, piebald, long horned scrubs.

Ephraim, another Indian cattleman, lived up the river. 

Mrs. Joe Lewis had the first garden. She was born in Alsace- 

Lorraine and had Prussians held up to her as bugaboos when a child. Steele and Munz had one of the first saloons, all modern, brass rail, cuspidors, coal oil lamps and hitching rail in front. A schooner of beer was five cents, with a free lunch thrown in. The well-dressed man wore German sox inside overshoes, ear muffs, mufflers, mittens, turtle neck sweaters, and sheepskin lined coats. 

Suits were cheap. One boy bragged that his wedding suit was good, and it ought to be, it cost $11.00. The well dressed lady wore a fascinator, high topped shoes, and her dress touched the floor.

Calico was five cents a yard and a two cent stamp took your letter anywhere in the United States.

Dill pickles and saurkraut were in kegs. Crackers, prunes, sugar, raisins, etc. were in bulk. If you wanted molasses you took the family jug, and it was drawn out of a keg. Shoes for the baby cost $1.50, and Mom's did not cost much more. Men's overalls were 75 cents. 

The wood pile out back afforded the boys and sometimes the girls, an opportunity to work off some of the energy which nowadays backfires into juvenile delinquency. The Chick Sales adjunct to all houses often had no roof, but sported a good wool sack door. This arrangement, in a day when air conditioning was unknown, made for an unobstructed view of the scenery, and there was always something to see. 

Lula Hood was bookkeeper in Murdock's store. Miss Dora Murdock, the popular young daughter of Al, was her father's right hand in the store. Mrs. Mary Spratt who kept open house for all the young fry for miles around, had a pretty daughter, Della, who was the belle of the dances, and another daughter Jenny, who is still loyal to the old town. The Barton brothers, Bill and Charley, Charley 6'6" swung the girls off their feet in the square dances. 

In looking over this account I see continual references to the cold. It was cold in the winter time, but the summers were ideal. 

The high water which came in July left many black waters and mosquitoes were a pest. Every house had its smudge tenders in the evenings. Well, those days are gone forever, but we look back with nostalgic yearning to the simplicity of those times; life was uncomplicated, the atom bomb was unthinkable, but after all the world moves, and we must get in the parade.

THE MAIL 

In 1905 before the mail route could be established it was important that the town of Dora should get in touch with the nearest post office, which was Myton. Arthur Marsh was hired by individuals to go to Myton twice a week on horseback for the mail.

One letter in particular, mailed in the East and addressed to 

Emil Munz, California, was forwarded to Fort Duchesne, then Myton, then Dora. It was postmarked September 27, 1905. Another letter still in existence, was postmarked October 11, 1911, addressed to Theodore. Bud and Earl Winslow wrote a letter to their mother in Iowa. It was the first letter postmarked Duchesne. For this reason they had it returned to them.

Dora Murdock was the first postmistress and had her postoffice in the right hand corner of her father, A. M. Murdock's store.

Charlie Pitt drove the first mail from Myton to Duchesne, in a buggy, in December, 1905, and continued this route until John Holgate took the contract with A. M. Murdock as bondsman in 

January 1907. Mail came every day.

In 1908, Wm. H. Fitzwalter went to work in Murdock's store and became Duchesne's post master. But long before these white settlers had come to the basin, when a few Indian traders and soldiers of the fort were the only white people here in 1879, mail was brought to Ashley near the place where Vernal now stands, from Wyoming over the Diamond Mountain. S. P. Dillman, father of Ray E. Dillman, of Roosevelt, crossed the mountain on snow shoes and carried the mail. In the early eighties Fort Duchesne was established as a military post, a road was built from Price to 

Fort Duchesne over Duchesne Bridge, the place where later Myton was built up, and mail was brought in by Price people to the Fort and from there to Vernal.

In 1906, after the Uintah Railway Co. had completed the line from Mack to Dragon, the mail went from Dragon to Vernal, Fort 

Duchesne, Myton, and Duchesne for a short time.

In the meantime the basin had been opened to white settlers, and Duchesne commenced to be a pretty good sized settlement. 

Early in 1909 A. M. Murdock established a stage line from 

Colton to Duchesne. The stage left Duchesne on Mondays, Wednesdays, and Fridays, and returned the alternate days. June 10, 1909, Wm. F. Fitzwater received a telegram from the Chief Inspector of mail at Denver, and ordered all mail to be sent by way of Colton, that there was a sack of mail for Theodore waiting at Colton. On 

June 17, 1909 the first mail came to Theodore from Colton via

A. M. Murdock's stage. The permission to have the mail come by way of Colton made a tremendous saving in time. The stage got in about 5 o'clock in the afternoon, which gave ample time to answer letters before the next day.

Mr. Murdock received no compensation for handling this mail since no additional expense was to be incurred, and in the slang

DUCHESNE 

209 vernacular of the street at that time, Mr.

Murdock was the "fall guy." 

Under this new system, Salt Lake mail was only one day old when it reached Theodore.

After Theodore's success in having the mail come through from the west, the other towns, Myton, Roosevelt, and Vernal, asked for the same system.

The town continued to grow, and when in the middle of summer of 1912 the government advertised for bids for the transportation of mail, a number of public spirited citizens of Duchesne called a meeting to discuss the matter. Eleven of the men, A. M. 

Murdock, Clyde H. Stephens, Dan T. Powell, M. L. Marsing, Ed.

Gardner, J. D. Van, G. V. Billings, Harve Partridge, H. C. Nicol, 

R. M. Pope and M. B. Pope, the mayor at that time, organized a company, called the Duchesne Stage and Transportation Co., for the purpose of carrying mail with individuals only; Clyde H. 

Stephens applied for the contract while the other stockholders signed the bond that has to go with the application. G. V. Billings, was the first manager of the company. The contract was awarded to C. H. Stephens, and A. M. Murdock together with M. B. Pope, signed the bond for him. M. B. Pope and John Fortie went to 

Salt Lake City to buy two Buick cars, and when they were brought to Duchesne by R. W. Kroupa and Monte Young one Sunday, the latter part of November, the whole town was on the streets to look them over, and rejoice over the important occasion. Services, just being held, were being interrupted and the preachers stood with the crowd gazing at the new cars. December 1, 1912, for the first time, the mail was brought in by the Duchesne Transportation Company.

After a few months operation, when the parcel post was extended to twenty, and two months later to fifty pound parcels without the government making any compensation for the new services, the contract was turned over to A. M. Murdock who, then with 

Monto Young continued the contract for the balance of the two year period. December, 1914, the contract was applied for by and awarded to Monto Young with his uncle and A. M. Murdock as bondsmen. After the expiration of the term in December 1917, the contract again went to A. M. Murdock for a new four year term.

In July 1918, Mr. Murdock reorganized and enlarged the business, engaged Jas. Frontjes as manager, installed new equipment and changed the name of the company to Duchesne Transportation Company. Everything went smoothly and satisfactorily until December 1918 when the government decided to take over the mail service and made the Transportation Company continue in the meantime on a basis of an agreement from day to day. The service

was to be taken over January 1, 1919, but unexpected difficulties arose and several times the day of commencing government service was postponed, making it very difficult for the Transportation Company to live up to the contract and to give the people adequate service. The able management, however did the best it could and was deserving of the gratitude of the population for bringing in mail the way it did. 

BRIDGES 

The location of the town between two rivers made the construction of bridges very necessary. The first bridge was built across the Duchesne at the east end of C Street in 1905. It was constructed of logs with a rock pier or abutment in the center of the stream. 

Funds and labor for the bridge were received from poll tax. 

The government had completed a bridge across the Duchesne river at Myton, in 1910. Among the many people attending the 

Dedication, was Theodore's post master, Wm. H. Fitzwater. U. S.

Senator Reed Smoot who had been a speaker at the celebration said to him, "Postmaster, why don't you people ask for a steel bridge to be put across the Duchesne at your community? The postmaster's reply was that the people were much in need of one, so Senator Smoot agreed to send them a petition as soon as he gɔt back to congress. The appropriation would come through the Indian 

Department as there were quite a few Indians living north up the river, so the bridge was to be built north of town on 7th Street. 

The original course of the Duchesne made several horseshoe bends swinging the river onto the townsite. Three large cuts were to be excavated to straighten the river, and hold it out of town. 

Work began on these cuts in August, 1910. In January 1911, Postmaster Fitzwater received a letter from Senator Smoot saying an appropriation of $15000 had been secured, and the job advertized for bids. A construction company from Omaha, Nebraska, got the contract for the sum of $9965.00. The construction began in 

March, 1911, to be completed July 16, of that year. The extreme high water during June and July delayed the completion date a week or two, and the bridge was completed later in the month. Local labor was used, and the price of $2.50 per ten hours work was paid. 

Wm. H. Fitzwater handled the funds.

Vera and Carey Murray drove the first vehicle over the bridge a day or so before the final planks were nailed in place. They were in a two wheeled cart, drawn by one horse.

On the day of the completion, George Robbins, with a fine team of blacks stood ready and waiting on the south side of the river to make the initial crossing, but Al Murdock had another idea. He said to Grant, and I quote, "Get over to the stables and hitch up 

Babe and Smit. Then go get Mother, and get around on the north

DUCHESNE 

211 side of the bridge. If you don't put her team across first, I'll kick your butt!"

When that final plank was nailed down, Grant was ready. He gave the team a grand lash. Both teams sprang onto the bridge. The buggy wheels locked, and Grant drug Mr. Robbins' vehicle back across the bridge. At the completion of the bridge, a large celebration was held for the dedication. 

The speakers were Pres. Wm. H. Smart, Senator Reed Smoot, and Judge J. R. Nelson. Pretty little Madora Steele christened the bridge, and named it the "Steele Bridge." Charlie Barton, Buddie 

Hicken, Bud Winslow and Bill Craver put on a clever clown stunt, several beef were barbecued, and everyone had a wonderful time. 

In 1912, the Dickersons with the aid of neighbors built a long swinging foot bridge across the Duchesne where the new bridge east of town is located. The highway originally was on the north side of the river coming into the Duchesne from the East. In 1914 the 

State constructed the road on the south side of the river, where it remained until Highway 40 was completed in 1929. There are three large bridges across the Strawberry, and two across the Duchesne, in or near the town.

SCHOOLS 

In the winter of 1905, Berniece Peterson taugh school to the Marsh children and a few others, in a tent. This school tent over by the old Murdock barn was used by freighters at night. Every morning the desks had to be rearranged and the ice broken in the water bucket. The meanest kid in school (and it was not a boy), would go outside and make silhouettes on the tent. The ten pupils were: Lois, Emma, Daphne, Sammy and 

Smith, Myrtle,

Piney, Theras Marsh, Melvin Wood, and Helen Dickerson. The teacher was paid in painting lessons, and some cash. 

This country then belonged to Wasatch County. In the late summer of 1906, Al furnished the logs and the men of the community erected the first school building. It has since been turned into a residence. Miss Morrison taught the children that winter. 

There were two Bennets, three Murrays, three Wilkens, three 

Marsings, three Powells, three Marshes, and perhaps a few more. 

Dan Powell and Mort L. Marsing were school trustees. Mr.

Broadbent, a school board member from Heber, made a trip out in 1907, and broke out with the smallpox. There was quite an epi-. demic. Mrs. Ollea Schonian remembers Bill and Charlie Barton having smallpox while living in a tent just west of Buell's store. 

They got out of quarantine on Christmas Day of that year, so with a group of their friends they set fire to their tent and danced

around it. Two children from among the Indians died of this dreaded disease that winter.

In 1907, some of the children went to school in the town hall, as the log building was not large enough to house all the children. 1-Duchesne School, built 1907; 2-Early group of school children;

3-First Brickyard, 1906.

DUCHESNE 

213 

Lucy Stott, LaRena Gee, Mrs. Seaton, Emma Redmund, Mrs. Geo. Stott, Mrs. Parker,

Alta Stott and Berthena Holfeltz; (Emma and Henry) Mr. and Mrs. Holfeltz; Juanita 

Relief Society; The Merkleys.

A. A. Washburn and his wife Louella taught the school that year. 

In 1908, the first brick school was started. It was on the school lot just behind and east of the elementary school building. Bud and 

Earl Winslow also quarried the rock for the foundation. John 

Grant made the brick and they helped him. The brick, this time, was made up on the Strawberry river about a mile, which is known as Grant Rocks. J. A. Washburn was the architect for this building and general boss of the construction. 

There was one room built on at the back, covered with red tin, and a door on the east. This served as the third grade, and for nearly 20 years, Fanny Bjornson was the third grade teacher. The first and second grades were held on Main Street in what was then the old Jim Hair Place. The first grade was held in the room nearest the street, where Florence Madson was the teacher. The second grade was in the back part of the building. 

The elementary school building as it now stands was constructed in 1926 and the high school building in 1936. 1926 was the beginning of consolidation of schools in Duchesne. The school children from Strawberry, Utahn, and Juanita were taken into town for school by bus. 

The red school house of Juanita was located six miles east of 

Duchesne on the then country road; now Highway 40. George Dean, a resident of the community and a life long school teacher, gave an acre of land to the school district, and the building was erected in 

1914. Hazel Hemphill was the first teacher.

IRRIGATION 

The people that first came to the Uintah Basin found that water was going to be one of their problems. They must have water for irrigation if the people intended to make their homes in this isolated, raw territory.

The day after the opening, Ruben Collett went into Salt Lake to the State Engineer's office and filed for two districts. No. 1 filing was the Holgate or Pioneer Ditch, which comes out of the 

Duchesne river some six and one half miles east of Duchesne. This water covered some land before reaching the Holgate Flat, later called Midview.

The second filing was for the district under the Rocky Point Ditch Company. This ditch heads about five miles north of town and covers some land above the junction, and all the land east of Duchesne, as far as the Holfeltz Ranch, eight and a half miles down the river.

The first meeting of the Rocky Point Ditch Company, was held in April, 1906, in a log cabin on the Andy Larson place. This place is now known as the Tom Thomas farm. E. H. Hammond was elected President, Wm. Seaton, Vice President, Andy Larson, Secretary. Of the men who incorporated the ditch, Wm. Seaton was

DUCHESNE 

215 the only one to remain in Duchesne after the incorporation. The second meeting to make final arrangements to begin actual work on the ditch was held in town under some trees. 

There were twenty-two filings under this ditch, and work began early in the summer of 1906. J. B. Peterson ran the survey for the company, and his son, Max D. Peterson, was rodman for his father. This first summer only men with filings under the ditch worked on this project. 

In the spring of 1907, a new election of officers was held. 

John D. Wimmer took Andy Larsen's place as secretary and treasurer of the ditch. Jos. B. Cowan was boss of the Main Ditch, from the beginning in 1906 until his death in May, 1944. He also held other positions such as director and vice president from time to time.

The same spring the ditch was bonded and $5000 was borrowed from A. M. Murdock to help speed the work. Quite a few men were hired. Wm. Lusk's wife cooked for the men and took 

Book Stock for pay. There was some water put through the ditch that fall though not in time to raise any crops. In 1908, there was a little more value received from the project though not too much since there were ever so many breaks for the banks had not had time to set. It was 1909-practically four years before the ditch began to pay. Some of the filing numbers under the Rocky Point Ditch were: 

Roy Daniels, No. 1; Noah Bladock No. 2; Jack Whiting No. 5; 

Harve Partridge No. 20; and J. D. Wimmer, No. 36.

Water for the town of Duchesne was taken out in a ditch on the Duchesne river about one half mile above town in 1905. 

The O'Toole brothers contracted putting the water system in for the town in 1917. This system had a cistern or storage supply on the Blue Bench north of town to give the necessary force, and they took their water out of the Rocky Point Ditch. "Once while living near Mr. Murdock, I had need to ride up to my claim. I saddled my horse and found something had happened to her foot, and she was too lame to ride. Just then Mr. 

Murdock came along riding his single footer. He stopped and said, "What's the trouble?" I told him that I did not know. 

Getting off his horse, he said, "Here, ride, a good horse once. 

I'll ride old Twenty-five Cents today." 

Then he told me that "Old Twenty-five Cents," was a worthless colt he had bought from an Indian for 25c. Surprisingly it had turned out to be a favorite horse he often rode. I was just a neighbor, and he was that manner of a man."-Mrs. Farbanks.

ORGANIZATIONS 

Organization of the Latter Day Saint Church was effected just before Christmas in 1906, under the Uintah Stake. Ephraim Lambert was sustained as bishop, and a meeting was called for December 23,

to organize the Theodore Branch. John A. Fortie was substained as 

Branch President; John W. Grant was chosen as Sunday School 

Superintendent, Dora Murdock as Sunday School Secretary, and 

Emma Marsing, organist.

The Relief Society was organized at Quarterly Conference in

2nd counselors. Ruby Nielsen was secretary.

Mrs. J. R. Lewis, often called the mother of the town, organized the Episcopal Guild. It's purpose was to sew for the church, and once a month they played cards. They did lovely handwork, holding annual bazaars. They gave all proceeds to the church. They also made clothes for the people that needed them. 

The Standard Bearers met every Wednesday to play 500. It was organized by Fanny Medeiras, with 12 charter members. The purpose was to provide more cultural entertainment for the ladies of the town. 

The "Boosters" club was an early organization of "Theodore,” "The Gem of the Uintahs." Their motto was: "We boost for 

Theodore and knock on no one." The Boosters club is Theodore's

Board of Trade. We take pleasure in promptly and truthfully answering all letters of inquiry, regarding the prospects, industries, and resources of Theodore, the heart of the newly opened Uintah. 

Indian Reservation. We have many good things to tell you which we can't get on a letterhead or envelope. Read the back of our envelope carefully, then address a communication to the Secretary of the Booster's club, and the desired information will at once be supplied. We are at your service, command us.” 

Their officers were as follows: M. M. Smith, president; A. M. 

Murdock, vice president; Eugene Hamilton, secretary; John A. 

Fortie, treasurer; advisory board: A. M. Murdock, Eugene Hamilton, O. C. Steele, C. C. Clapper, Dan T. Powell, Wm. C. Perry.

Their purpose evidently was to further Duchesne in every way possible.

A burlesque of a ladies club was organized by several of the bachelors of Duchesne. They had their headquarters in Charlie

Barton's cabin, on the street, between C and D streets. This club was known as "The Buzzard's Roost" or "The Angel's Roost." Mrs.

J. R. Lewis mothered them, did their laundry, and always cooked their Christmas dinner. The members were: Bill Barton, Charlie 

Barton, Fred Johnson, Herman Ford, Rawley Rosenbert, Ernest 

Wilson, and others.
