THE TOWN HALL 

In the spring of 1906 many more people came to Duchesne, and it could be seen that there was much need for a place for social affairs, religious purposes, dancing, etc. "Al” Murdock aroused the interest of the community. He called a meeting in his store and organized a community group. It was decided it would be a stock holders organization. Each man would receive stock for his labor.

Bud and Earl Winslow quarried for the rock for the foundations. John R. Wilson was the architect and carpenter and general boss of the work. He also built the pulpit which is still in use. 

Al always "Grubstaked" people on this project.

There were so many men working on this building that it was completed in a very short time. At that time, the door was on the west end. The stage sat up about 16 feet nearer the front and there was a door at the rear for exit. It was known as the Town Hall and was used by all organizations in town and also for school at odd times. In 1921 the Latter Day Saint Church purchased the building for the sum of $2,750.00.

The building was cold and hard to heat, and for some time the L. D. S. people had been renting the theatre over on Main 

Street for Church meetings.

In 1925, while J. M. Michelson was bishop, the two south rooms were added, also porch with the door moved onto the south. 

A heating plant was installed in the basement under the south room. 

The rooms were completed about the last of December. They were then rented to the school district for school. The seventh and eighth grades were held in the east room and the high school classes in the west room.

In 1930, while Rulen J. Larsen was bishop, there was more construction on the building. The two east basement rooms were added and the stage was moved back to its present place, and the rest rooms were constructed on the east of the old south rooms. 

In 1907, a noted artist, Samuel Jepperson, who had homesteaded up the Duchesne river, came down and painted the first curtain for the stage. It was a beautiful scene of a bull fight and the arena, with all its splendor. In 1925, the curtain had become worn and shabby so he was again hired to paint a canvas for the stage.

This was a lovely picture. A little lake, with a brook and two graceful deer feeding. Then in 1941 the Relief Society members. made the beautiful red velvet draped curtain which has taken the place of the other curtains which rolled up and down. 

DOCTORS AND DENTISTS 

Duchesne has never had many Doctors or Dentists. Dr. Bjornson was the first doctor here, and came in 1905. He later moved

to Myton, and Dr.

Gossett began practicing here in November 1910. 

Lucy Crites ran a cafe where the Shell service station now stands. Later she started the first millinery shop, and married Wm. 

Sweatman. They moved near the steel bridge on the west side of 

1-Emma C. Wimmer, 1905 and Today; 2-John D. Wimmer "J.D."; Mr. and Mrs. 

Joseph B. Cowan, "Joe and Zell"

DUCHESNE 

221 the street. She had a store there. She ran a maternity home, and practiced as a midwife for a good many years. 

Alta Cloward, wife of Bill Cloward was also a midwife for years. They had a homestead about two and one half miles east of Duchesne, and she served as an angel of mercy and brought hundreds of babies into the world.

Dr. Paggett was in Duchesne from about 1915 to 1919. 

Duchesne's first dentist was Dr. Zimmerman, then Dr. Harry

Walker. Dr. Howard in 1925, and Dr. Bishop in 1929. 

Dr. D. T. Wood came to Duchesne in 1922 from Myton, and practiced a number of years here. He established a hospital in the 

Telephone Building on the north side of the street between 5th and 6th Street in 1925 and with the aid of Dr. Judy from Price, he performed a number of operations there.

Mrs. Emma C. Wimmer related the following story: 

Our party left Payson on the first day of June, 1906, to make our home on the Uintah Indian Reservation. The party consisted of my brother, Joseph B. Cowan, his wife, Zella, and their six months old baby, Shirley; John D. Wimmer, my husband, myself, and our little boy, Harold, who was about three; and Mr. Wimmer's nephew,

Victor Green, 14. 

Joe and J. D. had already made the trip in March, after registering in Provo, and securing a filing, to look over their places, some three and four miles east of Theodore, on the Duchesne river.

We had eighteen head of cattle, two wagons, and a buckboard. 

On the first night out, we camped at Castella Springs. Making camp consisted of pitching tents, making campfires, and caring for the stock. That night Shirley was quite sick, and we thought we would have to turn back, but by morning, he was better, and the journey resumed. The second night, the camp was made in Spanish 

Fork Canyon, where one of the horses became frightened by a train, and jumped into a barbed wire fence, cutting himself very badly.

This caused a two day lay over, for the horse almost bled to death. 

When resuming travel, Fred's riding horse was used in place of the crippled one on the buckboard, and Fred walked, driving the cattle and leading the injured horse. The men drove the wagons, and 

Zella rode with me, and I drove the buckboard team.

When we reached Soldier Summit there was still snow on the ground where the wind had blown huge drifts, for the previous winter had been very severe, and there had been lots of snow. 

There was still lots of snow in Willow Creek, and a full day was spent getting up the bottom of this canyon. There wasn't much more than a trail, and Joe had to lift the wheels over the rocks, and move logs, etc., to make it easier on the wagons and buckboard.

On reaching the top, we came down into a very pretty little park, where we stayed for three or four days, while Joe went back to Colton to get J. D.'s brother Bob, and his bride, Katheryn Riley, 1-Mr. and Mrs. L. Clair Winslow; "Bud" the "earliest" resident of the county still living. Early school teacher and long time School Board member; 2-John P. Madsen, pioneer, civic worker and State Representative; 3-Mr. and Mrs. Wlider Fairbanks, pioneers; Florence B. Madsen, first primary supervisor and has taught school for many years.

DUCHESNE 

223 who had been married on the 10th day of June, and were coming to Theodore for their honeymoon.

The last camp was made on the 13th of June, and was just a mile or so up Indian Canyon. We could see the green trees, and green vegetation, and it looked very good after the dry travel through Indian Canyon. We hurried and broke camp on the morning of June 14, and reached the Strawberry river about 9 o'clock.

In 1906, the Strawberry was much larger than it is now, and it being that time of year flood waters were overflowing the banks, but the river had to be crossed. The water ran over the horses backs, and almost floated the boxes off the wagons. The crossing of the cattle and all caused a little commotion, and several town folks came to look on. Among these was Fannie Bjornson, wife of 

Theodore's first doctor, and she graciously invited us to come over and see her home, for she said, "I have the very nicest house in town." Her bragging struck us as very funny, and Zell laughed out loud. Mrs. Bjornson said, "It is good to see women who can laugh after such a trip as you have had.

Her house was very small and made of rough boards, while most of the cabins were cottonwood logs. It was built in a nice little grove of cottonwoods, known for a long time as Bjornson's Grove. 

There was a cottonwood pole bridge across the Duchesne river, just east of town, which we crossed on making our way to our homestead on down the river. We reached there in the afternoon, and all I could see was a great sage brush and Greasewood flat, with a few cottonwood trees. There was a cotton-wood cabin, about 

14'x14' with a window and a door in the north, and one window in the south, with just a dirt floor. There was also a little log chicken coop. These had been built in August of 1905 by Bill and Wess 

Pickering. We pitched out our tents for sleeping, and used the cabin for cooking and eating.

There were ups and downs all through the trip, but our equipment was all new, and our horses the best. We had lots of fun, and laughed a lot. Anyone who knows Zell, will remember that she has a hearty laugh and a sense of humor to cheer up any situation. 

One day, Shirley had such a terrible crying spell, we were afraid something was wrong with him. Later in the afternoon we discovered that a loose pin in his mother's dress had been sticking in the baby all day.

On another occasion he was not feeling well, and we had given him Castoria. When we stopped for dinner, Zell droped the bottle of

Castoria as she was getting into the Buckboard, and of course it broke. She began to cry, but J. D. said. "What are you crying for?

Em's got a big bottle of consecrated oil, and that beats all the medicine that was ever made." She quit crying and the Castoria was never mentioned again.

One day Zell and Harold kept laughing. Harold had something in his pocket, and wouldn't tell me what it was. That night when 

I undressed him I was astonished to find several great big grub worms. They had found them early that morning, and Zell had kept the little boy entertained all day, by putting them in his pocket and playing a joke on me.

Being quite a distance from the river, J. D. and Joe dug a well. Since there had been no irrigation, the water was good. They also went to the mountains and hauled down enough red pine logs for two cabins. This and work on the Rocky Point Ditch constituted most of their work that summer. 

Though we didn't have any violence from the Indians, Zell, Katherine, and I were in constant fear of having them confront us. Three of us with the two children were in the cabin one evening, when we heard yelling and war hoops. Zell said, "I don't know what you are going to do, but I am going to take Shirley, and get in the bucket and go down the well." "I'll just take this six shooter, stand up on a chair, stick it out the hole where the chinking is gone, and if one of them comes near this cabin, I'll shoot," I said. But to our happy surprise, it was Bob, looking for a horse that had gotten away. Of course our fright became anger, and he received a tongue lashing for his practical joke. 

There were not a lot of Indians around Theodore at this time. 

Dick Wanrodes outfit was up the Duchesne, and Rock Creek. 

Sugoosie Jack and Sugoosie George had their wickiups about half a mile west of our cabin where our west cow pasture now is and just around the bend of the river, was a large meadow which belonged to Joe's filing, which of course the Indians claimed. Though all in all they were a pretty friendly lot, you could still sense a feeling of resentment towards the whites for moving in and occupying what had been their territory.

There was evidence of crude plowing and gardening on the east end of our place. A ditch had been dug, and water taken out of the river, proved that the Indians had irrigated. 

Their burial ground is just a half mile north and a little east of our place, just under the Blue Bench. There are two graves each with its cottonwood pole fence around it. Victims of a smallpox epidemic are buried in one grave. In the other, are four Indians, one being Sugoosie Jack's son, who was killed when a team he was driving ran away with him. 

Sugoosie Jack had 32 head of fine horses killed near the grave for the boy's use in the happy hunting ground. One beautiful grey horse, and a very mean one that had belonged to the boy, he refused to have shot, for he was afraid the horse would hurt the boy in the beyond, so he sold the horse to J. D. for $35.00. A new wagon, a plow, and other property were destroyed for the boy's use.

DUCHESNE

227 Barton rode in with his horse and carried Zell and me out. 

Everyone had brought his picnic, and we spread blankets and quilts on the ground, on which to eat. After dinner, everyone who could talk got up and made a speech. I remember particularly, Judge Smith, who gave a lengthy talk on the possibilities and future of Duchesne, and its surrounding territory. 

Zell, Katheryn, and I were sitting in the cabin a few days after our arrival. The door was open, and I glanced up to see an elderly well-dressed lady, standing on the threshold. We didn't know there were any women closer than town, so you can imagine our surprise when she said, "I'm your neighbor!" She was homesteading the 40 acres adjoining our place on the east. She was entirely alone, and had walked half a mile through high sage brush, to make her visit. 

I enjoyed many years of Fanny Madeiras friendship.

Mrs. Grace Hale Relates the Following Story: 

My husband, Frank B. Hale, with our two little girls moved from Jenson to Midview in March, 1906.

Our first home was a one room log cabin, located on the Holgate Flat. Lake Borem covers this ground now. 

We were very happy. Our third baby girl was born on May 8.

Medical aid was very hard to obtain in those days. She was the third white baby born on the Reservation, and the first baby born at Midview. She was blessed and given the name at the first meeting held on the "Flat".

That winter everyone moved out to send their children to school, and we were the only family left in that locality. Frank was away most of the time getting out house logs and cedar posts for fences. We were very much alone, the closest neighbor being five miles away.

The Indians were not too friendly. They definitely were not in favor of the white men building on their reservation. There was an 

Indian Village just across the river from our place. At night we could hear them shouting and the barking of the dogs. I was frightened at first, but as the weeks went by the Indians started coming to our house, and I soon learned that they meant no harm. 

Mail was delivered three times a week, coming through Nine

Mile to Myton and back up to Duchesne. The mail was carried by Stage Coach. They transported everything by freight wagons.

Transportation was very slow, it took four and one half, or five days in a wagon to get to Salt Lake.

In the spring months Frank worked shearing sheep. The men worked hard on the irrigation canal which was finally finished so the water could do some good.

Everyone planted crops, mostly alfalfa and gardens. Indians would trade anything they had for fresh vegetables or watermelons.

Mrs. Susan Grant relates the following: 

In the spring of 1909 we moved on our ranch. The Strawberry Dam had not been built, and the river came swirling down in violence.

In the evening of May 9, our cows crossed to the other side of the river. My son, Relison, said he could make it across a wire bridge to get them. In the flood a pole had lifted the bridge sideways, so he took an axe to move the pole.

When the bridge flipped back into place, the jar knocked him off into the river. We never saw him again. The men dragged the river, and some even dived, but they could find no trace of his body. On July 24, Mr. Murdock called a search party, and in an hour's time Paul Cluff found his body washed out on a small island.

He was 18 years old and had carried the mail a year to Boneta, twice a week, on horseback.

The first cemetery was up on the West Bench, which helps form the division of the Strawberry and Duchesne Rivers. This point extending down toward the town has always been known as West 

Bench Look Out, and later Old Cemetery Ridge.

The first person buried there was Hans Jacob. He died in 

1906. A queer incident in Duchesne history was the death of "Bug

House Bill," otherwise known as Will Robinson. He was a Spanish- 

American War Veteran, and had filed on a homestead located in the mouth of Indian Canyon. His living quarters consisted of a dugout in the hill.

He was found dead in bed with a gun in his hand. He had evidently been dead about a week. When he was picked up, the hand clutching the gun fell off. Whether or not he committed suicide, or was murdered, was never solved. He was buried on West 

Bench in 1908.

It was very difficult to dig a grave in this cemetery, the rock formation being very hard to break through. Sometime even the use of powder was necessary. It soon became evident that a new cemetery location would have to be found.

In 1914 the West Bench was abandoned and the Indian Canyon became the new cemetery location. 

The great expanse of land laying north of Duchesne on the Blue Bench has long been a dream of people coming into the Uintah Basin. A lot of people filed and homesteaded farms there among them: Schonians, Stevens, Atkinsens, Barlows, Axel Pearson, E. C. 

Abbott, Hemphills, Bowers, Harts, and Birches. Joe Birch organized and took the leading part in the original Blue Bench setup. 

These people went up as far as Rock Creek, and built a canal

DUCHESNE 

231 to put water on their property. This canal was built under supervision of Mr. Birch, but their canal was long; there was much loss of water by evaporation, and the Blue Bench was a poor place for ditch construction, as many miles of fluming had to be used. For these reasons the people could not make the ditch pay. 

They had a school there, called Blue Mesa school, with Mrs. 

Ollis Schonian as teacher. 

Jesse K. Knight took over all the land on the Blue Bench from the homesteaders, and built an expensive canal and put enormous sums of money into buildings. It was made an agricultural project, but their inability to keep water in the canals broke the 

Knight Investment Company.

The people of the Uintah Basin are still hoping for some water project by reservoir water storage and a new canal system which will cover these vast acres of land and make them tillable again. 

SARAH ANN GIBBONEY 

Sarah Ann Arbuthnot was born in Athens, Ohio, July 24, 1847. 

Her father's name was Robert Arbuthnot and her mother's maiden 

Sarah Ann Gibboney name was Jane Holden. They. moved from Ohio to Iowa when 

Sarah Ann was 4 years old. 

Her father bought land there for $1.25 an acre. There was plenty of timber on this land, and all kinds of wild fruits, such as plums, cherries, grapes, strawberries, blackberries, apples, etc. 

She remembers going out early on windy mornings to pick up hickory nuts.

They cooked on a large fireplace as no one had stoves there at that time. One neighbor got a stove, and not being familiar with such modern ideas, made a fire in the oven. 

Sarah Ann was nine years old when she started to school, as prior to this time there had been no school near enough for her to attend. The first day at school she was called upon to recite a poem which she still remembers very well as follows:

A red sugar house, such a beautiful one

I bought it and ate it so now it is gone. 

The friendly Indians used to come from their settlements with a pony team and tar bucket wagon (tar buckets tied on the rear to catch the tar as it dripped from the axle to be used again). The 

They gave them pumpkins and corn which they dried for winter. 

In February, 1857, Mr. Arbuthnot took a load of wheat to Cedar

Rapids, a distance of 40 miles, to obtain a grist of flour. On returning home, his team fell through some ice while crossing a river. He got out of the water but the team was drowned, and the wagon and flour lost. He developed what was then called Quick Consumption, from exposure, and died soon after, leaving the family in unfortunate conditions.

She has seen four wars, her brothers, sons, or grandsons taking an active part in each war. Three of her brothers fought on the 

Union Side in the Civil War. The family usually did not hear from them for many months at a time. Their letters always came by pony express.

L. A. Hallenbeck, "Judge", lawyer and businessman since 1912.

Her eldest brother Samuel, 24, joined the army in 1860. He was wounded in May, 1864, during the battle of Gettysburg. He returned home in February, 1865. Daniel, entered the war in 1863 and after a few months of service was stricken with illness, and spent the winter in a hospital in St. Louis. He was released from the army in February of the next year. A third brother, James, and a brotherin-law, Marion Low, entered the army in January, 1864. Marion 

Low was killed in action that December. 

Sarah Ann was married to Alexander Gibboney at Irving, Iowa, January 1, 1869. When their first baby, Stella, was five months old, they moved to Cuba, Kansas, where Mr. Gibboney filed on a homestead. They had seven more children born there. In 1885 Mr. 

Gibboney died, and Mrs. Gibboney reared the children alone, remaining on the homestead until the children were able to make homes of their own.

Her son Wattson, enlisted in the Spanish American War, 1898, and served two years in the Philippine Islands.

In 1905, she and Wattson came to Myton, Utah, and each of them filed on a homestead northeast of Myton. She remained at the post while he went to Arkansas, and in May, 1906, he brought his wife and two little boys to Utah.

For a few months they camped on the banks of the Duchesne 

Lester Maxwell 

Arthur Goodrich

river at Myton and in June they almost got drowned. The water came up around their tent, and they had to get a man and team to help move them out. Mr. Whitmore's team got down in the mud, and they had quite a time getting all their things back to drier ground over near town.

Later they went to Arkansas for a few months, and then returned to make final proof on their homesteads. They lived in an 

Indian house for a while, until they were able to build some log cabins for themselves.

When Mrs. Gibboney's son, Samuel, died in California, in 1911, the whole family went to Los Angeles and remained there; after the death of Wattson's wife in 1919, they moved to Oklahoma for a short time, finally coming back to their homes in Myton. Mrs. 

Gibboney then became the "Mother" of Wattson's two boys, Ralph and Edgar.

In 1936, Wattson died in the Veterans Hospital in Los Angeles, and Mrs. Gibboney moved to Argyle Canyon, to live with her grandson Ralph, while he proved up on his homestead there. 

The last five or six years she has spent her time living in 

Myton and Duchesne,

Sarah Ann Gibboney has spent almost a hundred years pioneering in America, and the west. Her birthday is of special interest to us as she was 100 years old, July 24, 1947.

She is an honorary member of the Duchesne Camp of the 

D. U. P. and as such received special recognition on July 24th of this year at Salt Lake City. 

Mrs. Gibboney had several nephews in the First World War, and four nephews in World War II. 

She has outlived all her children, except two sons. One of them is living at Long Beach, California, and is 76. One son is in Holden, Kansas, and is 70.

WILDER FAIRBANKS 

My first trip to Duchesne was August, 1905. I came by way of Colton, with a buckboard loaded with supplies, for Rock Pope who was mining elaterite at the mouth of Sam's Canyon on the Strawberry River. When I entered the flat which later built up as 

Duchesne, there was one wagon and one tent. The tent served as a saloon. Two barrels with a plank between served as a bar. There were two men there, Andy Murdock selling beer, and Bob Reynolds, his only customer until I arrived. My buckboard was the first rig to travel up the Strawberry and I had to follow the creek bed nearly all the way.

Later I went down on the Strip which is just East of Fort 

Duchesne or Moffett. In 1907, I located a ranch on Red Creek where I resided and raised my family. In 1937, we moved to 

Duchesne where we now live.

FRUITLAND Presented by Mildred Dillman FRUITLAND N 1907, TWO YEARS AFTER the opening of the reservation for homesteading, a group of doctors, lawyers, and highly educated people from Nebraska decided to come to the

Uintah Basin and form a community of their own that would be a wonderful place to live. There were over one hundred that came. 

Many of them settled on Red Creek on the Dead Ox Flat and on 

Rabbit Gulch. 

A. M. Murdock gave to Mildred Dillman the following reason for naming this section Dead Ox: 

"Preston Nutter ranged his cattle in Dead Ox County for many years. One year there was little feed, and the cattle were very poor. 

It was a long way between drinks for both man and beast. One of his steers died and was just skin and bone. Someone stood it up and left it standing for a year or more. The people spoke of that section as, 'Where the dead Ox was standing'. Later it was just called 

Dead Ox."

Among those who first settled on the Rabbit Gulch Flat was the 

W. E. Lewis family. They came May 29, 1909. The corner stone survey was on their homestead. Mrs. Eva Lewis had more children under her supervision than any other teacher in the Basin. She was efficient and scholarly, and had a deep understanding and sympathy that won the confidence and love of her pupils. Many pupils were able to finish their school year because of Mrs. Lewis. One case illustrates her wisdom. It was just one of many. A little girl had been ill with a nervous disorder and had been unable to continue school for the first three years. Mrs. Lewis was her fourth grade teacher. The nervous condition returned and in order to let her finish, Mrs. Lewis told the class that she must have special privileges and must walk around the room or the hall, and that the class was to pay no attention to her. It gave the needed relaxation and she was able to finish her grade. 

The first school in Rabbit Gulch was held in Mrs. Lewis' front room; she was the teacher. The next year she had school for the first half of the year and children came from miles around. The last half was held over in Dead Ox, and Kate Knab taught in the Peter Van Horns house. School was held in Red Creek district in the Carey home, and Clara Carey was the teacher. 

Mrs. Lewis later taught at Duchesne, Red Cap, Altonah, Mt.

Emmons, and almost thirty years she taught in the Roosevelt Elementary School where families had all of their children under her supervision. Her daughter, Mrs. Minnie Angus, said that they fol- 239

lowed schools as it was the desire of her mother to have the best in education for her family. That was the reason for coming to 

Roosevelt. 

The first home was completed for R. U. James, a brother of 

Mrs. Lewis. The Lewis home was the last to be finished that year. 

The mail was brought in from Heber. The people petitioned 

Congress for a post office because of the irregularity of receiving the mail. Anyone that went to Heber brought it. 

They did not like the name of Rabbit Gulch for the name of the new settlement. As the land had been so very fruitful, and the gardens prosperous, the name of Fruitland was chosen. Mrs. Jones was the first postmistress and was followed by Mrs. Margaret Anne 

James. Mrs. Peter Knab was appointed upon the resignation of Mrs. 

James and remained in that position for years. 

The first Fourth of July celebration is remembered by the children as a wonderful experience. Games, a fine program, and all that makes a 4th of July celebration worth while and American, was held in the Dead Ox community by about one hundred and fifty people; among them were many professional citizens. 

The Gidding brothers, a doctor and a lawyer, are remembered as fine people. The doctor contributed much to the Literary club that was held each week in the different homes. In these meetings the Lewis girls, Rowena, Edna, Otey, Minnie, and Clora learned "Part Singing."

Many homesteaders settled in this section, George Walkup was a very early citizen. There are many more whose names were not available.

In Red Creek, J. L. C. Carey was one who came from Nebraska and later contributed much service to Duchesne County. He was instrumental in aiding with the irrigation problems of Fruitland and other sections of the Basin and was one of the first County Commissioners. His brother's family was also influential in settling the Basin. 

Because of misrepresentation of the country many became discouraged and returned to their old homes. The people of Rabbit 

Gulch sold their interests to the Smith sheep owners at Heber. Many in Red Creek sold their interest to a Chicago Land Company Many drifted into other sections of the Basin.

EARLY CHURCH MEMBERS

HANNA Contributed by May Lambert HANNA HISTORY 

ANNA CONSISTS OF TWO SMALL VALLEYS 

on the upper 

Duchesne which are sometimes spoken of as North Hanna and South Hanna. These take in the valley below the dugway as far south as Farmcreek. 

In 1905, when the Uintah Basin was thrown open to the white man, a townsite was started at the fords of the river. It was named Stockmore by two men who established it, Mr. Stockman and Mr. Moore. According to the real history of Stockmore, it was known as a "boom town." The site was laid out in lots and several were sold but later proved unsuccessful. 

Chancy Lee was the first white man here before the opening that we have any record of. In 1902, he chased and captured wild horses, which were sold at Heber, Park City, and Salt Lake City at $5.00 to $15.00 per head. This was done twice during the year. 

The first cabins were built up the North Fork of Duchesne River by an old prospector, Riley Shiddler, his son Bill, and a man by the name of Tannerhill. 

Carl and Martha Wilcken with their family of seven children lived at the Big Spring in 1905. The same year a bridge was built over the North Fork of the Duchesne, with Tom Potts as road foreman, helpers George Wilcken, John Toops, Wm. P. Hanna,

Oliff King and Charley Lee. 

In the fall of 1905, Thomas Rhoades and son Will built a cabin. The following spring he moved his wife Martha and seven children from Helper, Carbon County, to the new quarters. That year they cleared land, planted a crop, surveyed, and got out a canal which was taken from the Big Spring. In the fall they realized a good harvest, threshing 317 bushels of grain. This was the first crop raised in the upper valley, and their cabin was the first to make its appearance.

In the fall of 1906, Wm. G. Michie and wife with three children came from Woodland, Utah, locating at Farmcreek. They brought the first milch cow. At this time also came John M. Reid and son Parley. They built a rock cabin, and the following Spring 

Mr. Reid brought his wife Sarah and his family to their new home. 

The only Indians who lived in this locality were John Henry 

Cody, his wife Josephine, Cutlip Jim, Frank Bannett, and Jesse Copperfield. 243

1-Early Sunday School; 

2-Frank Defa, one of the businessmen of Hanna; 

3-Chauncey Lee, an early settler. He was interpreter for the Indian Service in early days.

HANNA 

245 Later settlers were Frank Defa and family from Salt Lake, John Piling from Helper, Utah, Wm. P. Hanna from the East, also Jim and Jennie Maxwell and son Dana, Bert Atwood, the 

Thompsons, Gertrude Gillman, May Smith and family, Shorty 

Gates, and John Toops.

The first dances were held in the old Stockmore Hotel. Later we danced at Chirilli's and at Hy Jones' place below Farm Creek. 

The hotel at Stockmore was owned by Tom Potets. Rudolph 

Joe Wilcken furnished music, playing mandolin and guitar.

Tom Potts later sold his Hotel to Chancy Lee, who also held dances and played the accordian. He made a little man of wood and placed it on a cigar box to show the people how to step lively. 

One night in the "Good Old Summer" a trout supper was given at this place. All the best fishermen turned out and donated their catch. The centerpiece was a long trout all roasted golden brown, hung by red ribbon over the center of the long table. The trout was 30-inches long. What a feed we all enjoyed! 

Fife and Frank Chirilli built a saloon at Stockmore. Later, Frank Defa built one, called the "North Fork Saloon." Hurst later bought them out.

Hy Jones and McCullen built the first store and hauled their goods in by team over the Woolf Creek Divide. It seemed good to the settlers to know where they could sometimes buy a piece of bacon. 

Cordon Phelps later had a store which he maintained for several years. Bert Atwood and his wife operated a butcher shop. 

Doc Beaver raised the first garden and had parsnips 6-inches through and 18 inches long. 

The first 4th of July celebration was held at Hanna's grove. 

The year following, Wm. P. Hanna and T. Wm. Rhoades built an open air dance hall and purchased a phonograph for the music.

In 1908, the first school was held at the Hy Jones' place below Farmcreek with Alford Duke as teacher.

The next people who came here to settle were Earl and Mae 

Atwood from Vernal, Carl and Rosy Leamaster and family, Heber, and Etta Moon, John L. Larsen, Monroe Giles and wife Martha.

About 1914, Mrs. Minnie Phelps was very suddenly taken ill. 

Dr. T. A. Dannenberg of Kamas was summoned and came to her assistance. She had a ruptured appendix and was operated on in her own home. People took turns helping them out, and she was nursed in that way back to health.

Later on Linda Moon was stricken about the same way; she was given the same good care by volunteer nursing, but she

passed on leaving a large family to mourn her loss. 

At the time Influenza came, many homes were stricken. Many times a whole family was down. Other times there were one or two who escaped it; they had plenty of opportunity to show their skill in nursing.

There was a time when a day was not long enough for Bert Atwood to go from place to place to do milking, feeding and watering of animals. He was indeed a faithful servant to his neighbors. Weeks later he contracted the same disease, and it claimed his life. Monroe and Giles were very helpful people, going among the sick and helping wherever they could during these trying times.

Mae Atwood was very helpful as a nurse to many mothers during confinements. She also nursed Mr. and Mrs. Hanna. She was a friend indeed to the sick.

Over near the west mountain in our valley are some wonderful "warm springs." Many is the time we have enjoyed a good dip in them. The water is nice and warm all winter long. Even in the coldest winter weather great clouds of vapor can be seen a great distance. There is an abundance of crisp watercress that can be obtained even in winter.

Coasting was a winter sport that was much enjoyed. We enjoyed pinenutting in the fall, and we always gathered plenty to last through the winter.

Each year we always looked forward to the one grand party, put on by the "Jolly Bachelors." This was really an enjoyable affair.

About 1909, Thomas Rhoades met with a very painful accident. It was just past lunch time when he went on horseback east of his house over the hill to see how his horses were doing. He only went a short distance when he found them. He stopped to look them over. While doing so, they came up close and before he realized it one of the band blazed away at his horse. Mr. 

Rhoades tried to avoid the blow, but in doing so he overbalanced in his saddle and was struck by the animal on the leg. The blow broke his leg and also the stirrup of his saddle. He fell to the ground.

He was too badly hurt to remount, so he turned his horse loose. He knew he must do something to attract the attention of his folks. It was one of those raw chilly days of springtime with a sprawl or shower occasionally. From the great shock he had experienced, he was becoming very much chilled. Laying the broken leg over the other leg he dragged himself on his hip about one fourth of a mile through shrubs and cactus and rocks to the brow of the hill where he felt he could be heard; he also set a cedar

HANNA 

247 tree on fire to attract attention. He called and was soon heard by homefolks, and they went to his rescue. It was necessary to make a stretcher. It was just growing dusk as the six men carried him down the steep trail to his home.

There being no doctor near, he set his own leg with the assistance of a neighbor lady, Mrs. Rose Leamaster. Both the main bones were broken and the leg badly bruised. It was necessary to change the dressings each day as the bruise was covered with large blisters. Mrs. Leamaster was very efficient and Mr. 

Rhoades got along very well. When he was able to make the trip to Salt Lake months later, he went to a good doctor for a check up. The doctor told him that he had been very well taken care of, and he could not have done a better job himself. 

Early in 1911, a school house was built in the north valley with Tommy McMullin as the teacher. Joe Wilcken was a teacher later. The new log house besides serving as a school house was used for church, dances, plays, religion classes, and was the community center. Prior to this one room being built, Sunday School was held in the homes. M. May Rhoades was secretary for a period of ten years.

The first mail was carried by Chancy Lee, bringing it from Kamas. A few years later a Post Office was erected at Hanna, it being named for Wm. P. Hanna, the first Postmaster; Mr.

Hanna also served as Notary Public. 

February 11, 1911, Ray Rhoades died of typhoid pneumonia.

He was ill 32 days. He was the first to pass away in this community. 

In the first years of our pioneering, this seemed the hardest trial for us to bear.

George and Myrtle Wilcken were the first couple to be wedded. 

The following was the most embarrasing situation: 

One night, past midnight, a young man who had been visiting an older daughter, decided to spend the rest of it with her brother, whose bedroom was upstairs. A curtain answering for a partition divided the boys room from the girls. We were all suddenly awakened by a hurried knock on the door; a neighbor in need of a better light wanted to borrow a lamp.-Mrs. - - - - - hurriedly filled, trimmed and cleaned it, and they were soon on their way galloping up the road. Just getting settled again, they were startled by the same pounding. This time it was for some articles of clothing as an expected package had not yet arrived, due to the delayed mails. 

The next trip they needed olive oil and safety pins. By this time the boys upstairs were chuckling aplenty. It was now growing daylight and the old white horse was about played out; however, through the efforts of many, the little newcomer was satisfactorily supplied with the bare necessities. It was their first born.

About 1913, there was an organization of The Farm Creek 

Sunday School with Heber Moon as Superintendent; John M.

Reid as First Counselor; Joseph E. Rhoades as Second Counselor; 

Ellen C. Rhoades as Secretary.

The first settlers belonged to the Tabiona Branch. It was organized with Thomas Rhoades as Presiding Elder. He was succeeded in 

1917, by Heber Moon, who presided until 1918, when the Stockmore Branch was organized as the Red Cliff ward. Heber Moon was chosen as Bishop; Wm. G. Mitchie and Joseph E. Rhoades as counselors. August 20, 1918, M. May Rhoades donated to the Red Cliff

Ward one acre of ground, where a new ward house was erected. 

President Wm. H. Smart was present at the time and told Miss

Rhoades that she would be blessed for this donation. 

February 1925, when Bishop Moon was released, Wm. G.

Mitchie was chosen as Bishop, with Jos. Rhoades and Clyde Van 

Tassell as counselors. They served until 1928, when Mr. Van Tassell moved to Kamas Valley. Robert Moon took office as Bishop. Earl 

W. Atwood has served as ward clerk since the first organization until March 1947. He has served as Postmaster since 1918. 

Redcliff Camp of Daughters of Utah Pioneers was organized November 16, 1937, by County Officers Dorthella P. Dastrup, Coun ty President; May R. Lambert as first assistant; Margaret M. Maxfield as County secretary-treasurer; Martha B. Giles, as Captain; 

Florence W. Roberts as first Vice-Captain; Etta Van Tassel Moon as second Vice-Captain and Essie Reynolds Collett as Secretary.

We now have a good store, the largest dance hall in the Basin, a lovely park and cabins, a saw and planing mill, some of the largest sheep and cattle ranches, and co-op owners in all the district. 

All of the early settlers of this community knew the sweetness and glory of service rendered to one another. They shared their joys and sorrows together. Many of them journeyed to the great beyond, but their memories still live. They are the Pioneers of the 

Uintah Basin, who made the sacrifice of their own homes, in order to make the building of this country possible. They have handed down a wonderful heritage to their children who now live in these valleys to enjoy the homes that have been left for them. 

The sun shines down upon these fertile valleys full of God's children, and we can justly say "In the sunlight and in the starlight, in the moonlight and the firelight, rests the peaceful little valleys; valleys of those grand old Red Cliffs and we've become a prosperous people. Let us live expressing our gratitude to our God, our parents and to one another."

AYDEN IS LOCated five miles south and west of Whiterocks and two miles east of Neola Church. The men who homesteaded Hayden are as follows: Newton Breeden, J. C. Peterson, Steve Wilson, Hardy Perkins, Dave Elmer, Charles 

Kyle, Gilbert Pererson, James H. Peterson, Dan Larsen, W. P.

Coltharp, Wilbur Burgess, G. Allred, John Odiscoll, Eddie Oaks, 

John H. Cook, Samuel Gines, John Bryant, Henry Hall, Jess Neilson, Dave Peterson, James Mecham, Charley Oakey, Charley Elmer, Dr. Loyd Henry Olsen, Matt Jager, John Burgess, Fred Mowers, Hyrum Gurr, John Horrocks, Joe Sessions.

The first school was held in two tents on the Session homestead. 

The teacher was Leona Ashby. The people secured logs for a school building at this place, and it was to be called Louisville. 

Before the building had been started, Stake President William 

H. Smart with his counselors Ephraim Lambert and Fredrick S.

Musser came and located the Hayden townsite. The hay grown here at that time was so outstanding that the name Hayden was chosen by these men.

The log building was erected southeast of the present old Bastian home. The school was moved into this building for the remainder of the term.

The Church also used this building for meetings as well as amusements. A bowery was erected close by with a dance floor. 

Presiding Elder Chris Peterson with John Horrocks and Rastus 

Bastian as counselors and J. C. Peterson as clerk, had charge of the

Latter Day Saint Church. 

Chris Peterson had the first Post Office and little store. Later 

Roger Horrocks built a store. 

Hayden Ward was the second ward organized in the Duchesne 

Stake with Roger Horrocks as Bishop, John Olsen and Rastus 

Bastian as counselors. John Horrocks was the Superintendent of 

Sunday School, Julia Owens was second Postmistress. The Owens were the orchestra for the dances. 

Trustees John Horrocks, Edward Oaks, Lon Mitchell were the men in charge of the first school building. John Horrocks, John

Burgess, Rastus Bastian from Uintah County, David Zebreskie, 

Aveck Warren, Jud Mitchell from Duchesne County built the new school building. This building was used as Church House until the ward was moved to Neola.

Bishops presiding over the ward while this building was being used were: Louis Allred, Charley Oakey, Dan Mitchell. 250

Presented by Mildred Dillman HARPER-NINE MILE EW PLACES IN THE WEST have more colorful history or story than Nine Mile Canyon that is situated on the Minnie Maud Creek. It is about half way from Price and Myton. 

Through part of it came the Old Stage and freight road of old 

Fort Duchesne and the Basin. 

Beautiful coloring in the rocks against the background of dark Cedar trees make it exceedingly picturesque. Nine Mile deepens before it enters Green River, into a walled V shape canyon of unrivaled beauty of any in the State. For miles the sheer ledges and cliffs make the most fantastic castles. Pinnacles may be found in many of the numerous side canyons that have such intriguing names as Pete's, Devil's, Cottonwood, Dry, Frank's, Bull's, Argyle,

Gate, and many more all suggesting some name or incident and as matter of fact as the people who lived there. 

From its early history it has been a refuge and a haven to many civilizations, as yet there has not been enough research to know how long ago people first came here, but Basket Makers, Puebloes, Fremonts, Utes have all at some time been residents of Nine Mile. It is an Archealogists paradise. On the high ledges and walls are many Cliff Dweller's homes and granaries. Among the best petrographs and pictographs telling of hunting scenes and various other activities of the ancients are found in Nine Mile. 

Artifacts by the thousands have been carried out of the canyon, and scattered all over the United States. No law has been enforced even to preserve the few remaining ruins or carvings. Even they are being destroyed rapidly at the present time by any "Pot Hunter" who happens to want to satisfy idle curiousity.

In Nine Mile came many famous Outlaws. The rugged outline and inacessability provided protection and shelter. They had a rendezvous near the mouth of the creek and could come and go in almost any direction. There are many old forts built and in one of them is Spanish writing. On a high rock, at the mouth of Dry 

Canyon, is inscribed names as early as 1839. There are other dates as early as 1849 on the ledges. Many names are painted or scratched between 1886 and 1899. 

Settlers came into Nine Mile long before 1880, however most of those known came between 1880 and 1900. No date could be found when Brock built his saloon near the mouth of Gate Canyon on the Nutter Place. The old Bar and the Saloon itself still stands. 

When Fort Duchesne was built it was the nearest way to the 253

railroad. A telephone and a telegraph line ran through the canyon, the Poles were made of iron to preserve the line. 

At the top of the canyon the Grames family came before 1890. 

1-Aritifacts found by Clark Shaw on the old Miles ranch; 2-3-Houses and granaries on high ledges; 4-5-6-Lee Snyder and petroglyphics.

Everybody remembers Freddy and that his Dad "Fell off the farm when he died and Freddy would rather have lost the best cow on the ranch than poor old Dad."

Will Hamilton and his family lived next to the Grahams. The 

Jim Hamilton's also lived near for many years. 

The Russells are almost as much a part of the canyon as the rocks and cattle, some of them are still native. In the family were

Don C. Johnson, whose initials were G. C., had one of the most popular places during the early days as an Indian woman by the name of Martha cooked for the freighters and cattle men.

The Ed Lee Place was the stopping place for passengers on the stage line for many years. The Post Office was put here. Mrs. Lee was a most charming hostess. They had a large family. Their children were: 

Ray, Cliff, Cora, Bert, Scott, Blanche, the twins,

Clyde and Claude, and Sharp. They moved in the spring of 1919, selling their interests to the Murray Sheep Company. The Tom 

Taylor family settled early in Nine Mile but remained only a short time.

In the early cattle business, associated with Mr. Lee was Dr. 

J. K. W. Bracken of Bingham, and Harry Sherman of Salt Lake

City. Both were outstanding men of the State of Utah. They helped in many civic improvements later in Roosevelt. 

Just below the Lee Ranch, Argyle canyon entered Nine Mile from the north, Bill Warren and his family moved into the canyon. 

In this family were Alma, Lurrine, Glen, Noral, Roy, and Harold.

Bill died shortly after the family moved there and Mrs. Warren made a heroic struggle to raise her large family alone. She moved to Bluebell but still makes frequent visits to Nine Mile. 

Parley Warren came back from Klondyke in 1899, and operated a farm with W. E. Cox.

Early into Nine Mile came the Hank Stewart family and Neal 

Hanks. They came to kill wild cattle. Mrs. Stewart later married

Neal Hanks. She built a lovely home in the canyon and lived here until 1945; then they sold and moved to Price. There were four children in her family: Eva, Rex, Van, and Donna.

Ed Harmon built a rock house at which the stage stopped at one time. He married Josephine Babcock. The school house was situated near here and served as the center for recreation for many years. Many old fashioned "Hoe Downs" were held with just the old fiddle and a healthy toe furnishing the music. In an old fashioned way one could say, "A Good Time was had by all."

Frank Alger's place was next. It became the center of industry of the canyon very early. The stage stopped here, and at that time 

Mrs. Hanks was the cook. The only store for miles around was operated by Frank Alger, he even kept "Jelly Beans." The Post

Office was moved here in the early 1900's. Frank also operated a stage line from Price to Myton. To ride on the stage with its swinging spring of buckskin over that road was an experience not to be duplicated in many other places in the world and, thank heaven, not very often.

Egan's ranch housed the stage for a short period. At that time

many officers of Fort Duchesne were traveling the road. Someone thought that Nine Mile should have a hotel, and it was started here on the Egan place but never finished as the road from Colton to 

Duchesne became popular.

Nutter's Ranch. Many memories cling to the old Brock Place. 

Old Shed Lunt first built here. He had his own cattle outfit and was perhaps the first to bring stock into Nine Mile. He later moved to Nutter's summer range. A wonderful man, was Shed Lunt, he was kind and at peace with the world. Operating his own outfit for over sixty years, in his old age he was watchman for Preston Nutter on his summer range.

Pete Francis bought the old Brock Place and made of it a stopping place for freighters and the stage line. Pete was killed by one of the natives of Nine Mile. His wife died of the effects of a bite of a very unusual bug. Even the bugs are famous that live in 

Nine Mile.

Preston Nutter leased from Pete Francis in the fall of 1901, and bought a year or two afterward. Mr. McCoy came into Nine Mile. 

He was with Mr. Nutter for many years. Preston Nutter, one of the few real cattle kings of the west, is prominent in Utah history in many sections. Not only in Nine Mile did he hold interest, but in 

Emery County, Duchesne, Carbon, Uintah, and Southern Utah.

His holdings number into the millions of acres, and it has been thought that he never really knew the thousands of cattle that he owned. He made his home at the Old Brock Ranch and built a charming house. He loved horses, but for his own hard riding

He met Miss Katherine Fenton; she matched his keen mind and gracious manners, and they were wed in 1908. Their two daughters, Katherine and Virginia, grew into womanhood, leaving

Nine Mile in the winter time for schooling and returning to enjoy with their parents the enthralling ranch life. Virginia, named for 

Mr. Nutter's place in Virginia, studied Archeology under Dean 

Cummings. She has assisted in their research several universities and doctors who have come into Nine Mile. The Nutters still own and live here.

Dr. Cummings was the first to become interested in preserving and studying the ancient cultures of Nine Mile. 

Down the canyon, at the mouth of Dry Creek, was a ranch that Miron Russell homesteaded. His father, known as "Daddy"

Russell, sold it to a mining company. It was later bought by another old Nine Mile character, Oliver Rasmussen, who lived there until his death. This ranch seems to be the central part of the Archeology finds. Many universities such as Carnegie, Southern California, Utah, and Harvard have made excavations there. 

The Rabbit Ranch had been the home of various types of people. It was homesteaded by Joe Thompson, Hank Stewart, and 

Neal Hanks. Nephi Smithson once lived here with his family, Mrs.

Smithson was one of those unusual people who leave their personality and memory for others to know them for their love of beauty. 

She planted rare roses, grapes, and fruit trees.

Desbrough and his wife homesteaded a small place. They did not stay long. Ed Lee bought it for its improvement and sold it to 

W. A. Miles. Mr. Pace bought it from Mr. Miles, and Nutter bought it from the State Selection.

The story of Clark Elmer's place is typical of any Wild West Story. It was the home of the outlaws, and almost every Cowhand had a weird tale about the "Stills" that operated underground. If one believed all that was told, ghosts could be seen walking every night up the road, driving the women crazy, whole tunnels full of rustled cattle, butchered and taken to market at Sunnyside, covered the ranch, wells were filled with dead men and built over to conceal their secrets. After owning it for many years, the Miles family found it only a charming place to raise good alfalfa and to picnic. 

Then Babcock homesteaded the next place; Ed Lee also bought this place for its improvements.

The Old Miles ranch included the Clark Elmer, Desbrough, Babcock, and the Ed Lee, and Taylor Places. It extended from the 

Rabbit Ranch to the Bull Canyon. Mr. Miles bought it early in

Preston Nutter, one of the west's Cattle

Kings. 

1907, and it became "Home" to all of the cowboys in the canyon. 

Mrs. Miles proved Nine Mile could produce bounteous fruits and vegetables. Mr. Miles brought with him several pure blooded animals. 

The Miles has a home in Salt 

Lake, Utah, and various other ranches in other parts of the state but Nine Mile was their favorite. 

Like the Nutters, Eva Stewart, and Donna Hanks, they only stayed in Nine Mile during the summer months, returning to Salt 

Lake City for winter schooling. 

Menel Taylor came with Mr. 

Miles from the Ireland Ranch in Salina, Utah, and was the foreman of the Miles interests for eleven years. It was only recently that he sold his interests to retire in his old home in Salina. 

There are many others that belong to Nine Mile history Pete Logan (Nelson) who worked for Nutters first and then for 

Mr. Miles; his brothers Al and Jim, Ed. and Bill Guithers, Butch 

Cassidy, Earl Williams, Ed. Huntington, Lee Van Court, and several sheepmen, among whom were the Prices. 

Perhaps the most beloved was Neal Hanks. He worked for almost every cattle man in the canyon, becoming the foreman of Nutter's for many years, but finally, a short time before his death, owned and operateed his own cattle outfit.

ΙΟΚΑ 

Presented by Mrs. Bertha Angus 

IOKA 

N THE YEAR 1906, homesteaders settled in Ioka. However, some of them, who did not wish to make sacrifices to own the land and make homes, later sold to others moving into the country.

In 1907, a number of cabins were built to hold the claims and to be prepared for the settlers who moved in the following year. 

Of course, water was the first problem to be met. Most of the culinary water was first hauled in barrels from springs located on the north side of the valley. Some was also brought from Duchesne river at Myton. The animals were all driven to water where it was available.

The Dry Gulch Irrigation Company built the first ditch bringing water to the land in Ioka, but as it was taken out of Lake Fork river above Altonah, then turned into sand wash, the sand washed down and filled up the small ditches almost as fast as they could be cleaned. 

In June 1909, the Dry Gulch let a contract to John A. Angus to make a canal on the hill side west of Upalco. This project was worked on faithfully, but a part of the canal failed to carry the water although a flume was made and the project was not a success.

In the spring of 1910, John A. Angus and some of the settlers formed a company and called it the Lake Fork Irrigation Company. 

They went farther up the Lake Fork river and started a canal northwest of Altonah. Mr. Angus was criticized by some of the members of the Dry Gulch Company for withdrawing from the company, but later the Dry Gulch was forced to join the Lake Fork Irrigation Company. Ioka also has shares in the Moon Lake Irrigation Company which helps the water situation in the irrigation season.

In the fall of 1907, the settlers saw an urgent need for a school. 

They built a frame house 20'x20' from rough lumber. Ephraim 

Marrett was in charge of the work. For two years, Charles Fontain, one of the homesteaders, was school teacher. 

In 1908, Terry B. Hallett started a mercantile business in a brick building he had built on his place. This house stands two miles west of the Ioka ward house which is now used for all of the gatherings of the Ioka people. 

Tragedies and deaths occur in every community. The first death was a three-year-old child of Mr. and Mrs. Newel Knight. 254

IOKA 

1-loka Ward House; 2-Mr. and Mrs. James Ames; 3-Mr. and Mrs. John Angus; 

4-Mr. and Mrs. E. Anderson; 5-Mrs. John Spencer; 6-Mr. and Mrs. Chas. Percival.

The child died in the fall of 1907, and was buried on their homestead. Then in June of 1908, about eight months after the baby's death, the father, Newel Knight, died, leaving a widow with seven children and very little to exist on a small garden and one cow. 

The first post office was a mail route established in 1908, with Charles Percival as the first mail carrier. He made the trip three times a week to Myton and back with a horse and buggy sometimes, and other times he rode horseback. 

Terry B. Hallett was the first postmaster, but the mail was carried on trial for about three months before the contract was let; that is, volunteers would take turns bringing in and taking out the mail. Postmasters in their order were: Dan Lambert, Mrs. Asa 

Baird, Albert Anderson, Mr. Ruther, Mrs. Jim Jukes, LeRoy Tuft, Mrs. W. G. Gentry for 10 years, Celia Jones for 15 years, and James Ames.

Later another post office was established three miles east of the Lake Fork Post Office. This one was called "Ioka" and was located on the Dan Lambert homestead. Mrs. Lambert was postmistress. Charles Percival also carried the mail from Myton for this office. The Ioka Post Office operated until 1942. James Ames was the last postmaster. The Lake Fork office was discontinued when the Upalco office was established in 1920. Now we have a Star 

Route, the mail being carried from Myton to Upalco through Ioka. 

Before the consolidation of the schools part of the grades were housed in a building in the east of Ioka and part of the west. In 

1921, the school houses were moved to a lot joining the ward chapel in which the higher grades were taught. Three teachers were employed at that time. Later the Ioka schools were discontinued, and the pupils sent to Roosevelt in a school bus. 

The Latter Day Saint Sunday School was organized August 8, 1909, with John K. Lemon, superintendent; and was called the "Ioka Sunday School." Until April 1910, no other church organization existed in Ioka. At that time a branch including what is now 

Ioka, Upalco, and Bluebell, and then called Lake Fork, was organized with John A. Angus as presiding Elder.

October 22, 1912, Lake Fork Branch became a ward, and John A. Angus was appointed Bishop. The name was changed to the Mural Ward of the Duchesne Stake, but in 1919, the word "Mural" was changed to Ioka, the same word as the post office. 

The ward was fully organized. 

The Ioka Ward House was built in 1915-16, under the direction of John A. Angus, bishop; John J. Benson, first counselor and 

Archie Benson, ward clerk, and John K. Lemon as second counselor.

The corner stone of the chapel was laid in February, 1915. Men turned out and hauled voluntarily the rocks for building from a

IOKA 

1-P. C. Johnson; 2-Mrs. Johnson and her daughters; 3-Arch and Marie Benson; 

4-The Benson family; 5-An early loka home.

place two miles north of the site, and in two days they had almost twice as much as was needed.

These men also went to the mountains and got the logs which were sawed into lumber at the Petty Sawmill and then hauled to the building site. John J. Benson contracted to lay the rocks and put in the floor joints and rafters. A mason by the name of Jarvis was hired to lay the walls. His assistants were Murela 

Benson, William Jenson, John J. Benson, and Archie Benson did the lumber work preparatory to laying the floor, and most of the men of the community helped finish the building. It cost $8,000 and was ready for use in less than a year after the project was started. At that time it was considered the best building in the 

Stake although Ioka was the smallest ward. sus- John A. Angus was Bishop from September 22, 1912, until 

June 1920, when Melvin J. Benson took his place and officiated until September 1929. After him, John Edmund Webb was tained, and he held the position for seventeen years, being released on January 10, 1946. Clifford Drollinger is the present Bishop. 

Death has taken some of our earliest settlers, and some have moved away. At present Ioka has fifty-two families. 

Ioka is a farming and stock raising section. Several people have range cattle and all of them have dairy cows from which cream and milk are produced for the creameries and dairies. Many of the families raise chickens on a large scale. However for several years the main livelihood has been made from the sale of alfalfa seed crops. As far as gardens are concerned, the Iokans boast of the best vegetables and melons grown in the Basin. 

Of the earliest settlers who came in the year 1908, William H. 

Stone, James B. Lemon, Charles Percival and wife, John A. Angus and wife, are still living in Ioka. Those who have passed away are: 

Mr. and Mrs. John L. Miles, first wife of James B. Lemon, wife of 

Wm. H. Stone, wife of James Miranda, and Harlem Gines. 

Those moved away are: P. C. Johnson, wife and family; 

Francis Ross, wife and family; John K. Lemon and wife; Walter 

Baird and wife; John H. Palmer, wife and family; and Robert 

Krebs, wife and family.

At the time of the first World War all of the Ioka boys returned, but during the last World War, of the forty-two boys who went, all came back but two: Howard Bert Jenson, killed on the Island of Guam, June 27, 1944, and Lynn Franklin Angus, killed in Germunden, Germany, March 27, 1945.

Most of the recreations have been dances, plays and parties arranged for and supervised as community functions and produced by home talent. 

William G. Gentry, Miss Katheryn Fenton, John Miles, John

IOKA K. Lemon, George and Jim Maranda were among the first settlers. 

Mr. Gentry has been one of the leading citizens of the Basin, giving his life to public service. He is noted for his fine breed of cattle. 

He has been one of the first county commissioners, a director of the 

Dry Gulch, the Myton and the Roosevelt State Bank. Mrs. Gentry has also served many long years in the community.

Compiled By F. Earl Case THE HISTORY OF MT. EMMONS EFORE THE UINTAH Indian Reservation was opened for homesteading entries in the fall of 1905, 12,000 acres of the Mount Emmons Bench was allotted to the Indians, mostly to the Uncompaghre Tribe or Ouray Indians. There was a strip of tillable land unallotted left on the west rim of the Sandwash, varying in width from a few rods to ½ mile. During the spring and summer of 1906, this land was filed on by men who held claim to the same for only one year, then relinquished their rights to others, with a few exceptions. The exceptions were: James H. Evans; 

Simeon E. Atwood, and Chester H. Hartman.

The first house to be built on the bench was on the Payne homestead east of Altonah. The Blood brothers, Ed and Al, built their cabin during the summer of 1906. On October 25 that year, Chester H. Hartman started construction on a frame house on this ranch one half mile north of Mt. Emmons townsite, but everyone left the bench for the winter. 

Messrs. Hartman and Simeon E. Atwood moved on their ranches permanently on April 2, 1908. Fred A. Case filed on his place during the summer of 1907 and that fall built his first lumber cabin, also James H. Evans built a cabin and made preparation to bring his family the next year.

During the spring of 1908 some land was cleared of sagebrush on the James H. Evans, Fred A. Case, Ed S. Robins, Simeon E. 

Atwood, Chester H. Hartman, Dave Scott, and Blood Bros. ranches.

Canals had not yet been completed to the east side of the Bench so Fred Case with a team and sulky plow went to a government canal east and south of the Cedar Knoll in the center of the Bench. From this point he plowed a furrow south-east through the S. E. Atwood farm and on to his farm. With this small ditch he was able to raise about 5 acres of wheat, 3 acres of corn and seeded to alfalfa hay some 10 acres.

During the summer of 1908, C. H. Hartman, the Blood brothers, S. E. Atwood, and Ed S. Robins each did some planting. That fall the Robert Marshall threshing machine came up on the Bench from 

Upalco (then Lake Fork) and in a short time threshed all grain raised on the Bench. That fall everyone moved away for the winter for work or to be near schools. 

The Blood brothers, (two bachelors), stayed at their ranch.

Ed. S. Robins with his wife moved to the Fred A. Case home as 270

MT. EMMONS 

271 it was a much warmer house than his own frame building. 

The winter of 1908 and 1909 was a severe one as there were from two to three feet of snow over the Bench. For over two months that winter Mr. Robins and the Blood brothers were snowed in.

During March 1909, Fred A. Case, with his son Earl and Lloyd 

Stewart broke a road up on the bench and found the two families alive and well.

During the spring of 1909 men arrived with their families, and at once all commenced clearing the land of sage, plowing and putting in crops. Men and boys would work through the long hours of the day dragging and grubbing brush. They would pile it into great piles and at night it was a familiar sight to see families, father, mother and children out burning sagebrush.

A greater part of the time of men folks during the years 1908 to 1910 was used in canal construction, although before this the government had constructed canals to cover the Indian land on the bench. The Dry Gulch Irrigation Co. built what is known as the 

Lake Fork No. 1 canal which covers land in Mt. Emmons, Bluebell, and Hancock Cove, land west of Roosevelt, Utah. This canal made possible farming of the strips of land west of Sandwash, then known by the settlers as Poverty Row. 

During the years of 1911 and 1912, some lands changed hands. 

The land by the Cedar Ridge in the center of the bench owned by Lloyd Stewart was sold several times and finally Albert Dastrup bought it. Frank Wiseman came into possession of the south half of the Cedar Ridge which he afterwards sold to Austin B. Taylor. 

Gurr; Dave Scott sold his place to Andrew Pearson, and the Blood brothers sold to August Pearson.

It was in the summer of 1909 that the first child was born on the bench to Mr. and Mrs. Ed S. Robins and they named their daughter Beulah. Mrs. Mary A. Case was the God Mother to this child. The Robins moved here from Canyon City, Colorado, in 1908.

Each year at the approach of winter men with their families moved away that their children could be near a school, as there were no schools on the bench and our nearest Post Office was at 

Ioka, then called Lake Fork, some 10 miles distant. 

April 27, 1913, a meeting was called by Bishop Wm. P. Merrell of the Bluebell Ward, at the home of Simeon E. Atwood. President

Wm. H. Smart of the Duchesne Stake Presidency and C. C. Larsen a high counselman were in attendance. These men stated the purpose of the meeting and said they thought the time right to start a branch. of the Church here. This met with the approval of all present. Fred 

A. Case was chosen as Presiding Elder and he chose James H. Evans as his first counselor and Josephine Atwood acted as Branch Clerk. 

They named this the West Branch of the Bluebell Ward. Under this

Fred A. Case and Mary Ann Humble Case came to Mt. Emmons in 1907 from 

Jensen, Utah. Mr. Case was the first Branch President and later a counselor to (See opposite page)

273 organization, meetings were held each Sunday at various homes. 

The boundary of the branch was fixed as follows: The north boundry to be the Cedar Ridge in the center of the bench, west, the Lake Fork River, the east boundry, the Sandwash and the south, the 

Waterfall. 

At the meeting held August 24, 1913, it was decided not to organize a Sunday School until they could get a regular District School. At a meeting held Sunday, Sept. 28, 1913, notice was given out that school would start the following Monday morning in the

Richardson house, a place six miles south of the present Mt. 

Emmons. 

October 6, 7, and 8th, 1913, Chester H. Hartman, S. E. Atwood, and Elijah Biglow went into the mountains and got logs to build a school house while Fred A. Case went to the sawmill and got lumber for the roof, flooring, doors and windows. School was started in this building in November and was known as the Cataract School. 

On Sunday, May 11, 1914, our first Sunday School was organized with Wm. Ewell as superintendent, S. E. Atwood as first was a haven to

Their lives were spent in pioneering and the latch string of thir door hung out to the weary travelor wherever bound. 

Simeon E. Atwood and Josephine Brannon Atwood came to Mt. Emmons from ernal, Utah, in 1906. In his home the Ward Branch was organized.

He served years as Sunday School Superintendent and 1st Counselor to Bishop F. Earl Case.

Edward W. Crosby and Etta May Lloyd Crosby came to Mt. Emmons from

James H. Evans and Anna L. Woodard Evans came to Mt. Emmons from Kamas, Utah, at the opening of the Reservation in 1906. He served as counselor to Fred A. 

Case in the Branch Presidency from 1913 to 1916 and was then a Counselor to Bishop Owen Bennion and later Jesse L. Smith and for years was a member of the 

Stake High Council in the Old Duchesne Stake. Sister Evans has served in the Relief 

Society all her life. They raised a large family, all community leaders. Their son, Wm. R. Evans, is our present Bishop.

During the years 1913 to 1916, the Indian lands on the bench were leased, allowing men a price for clearing sagebrush from the land or a free lease for a period of three years. Owen Bennion at this time came here and took 640 acres of this Indian land to clear and fence. Other leasers were E. W. Crosby, Will Ewell, Albert 

Adams, Jesse L. Smith, Wm. V. Osborn, Rufus Rogers, Leroy 

Thacker, and others.

Dan T. Powell was chosen as the second counselor in the 

Branch Presidency, May 23, 1915, and was set apart to that position August 4, 1915, by Bishop Owen Bennion of the Altonah Ward. 

About this time we were made a branch of the Altonah Ward. 

At the Sacrament meeting held in the Cataract School house June 20, 1915, a name for the new townsite which had been chosen a few days previous was discussed. Bishop Bennion said the town and Post Office should each have a similar name. The names considered were Yellowstone, Clearwater, Superior, Banner, and Mt. 

Emmons. Mount Emmons was chosen and the name was presented by Iva Marsing. 

Early in August, 1915, the need was felt for a larger school building, one that would accommodate two teachers. The School 

District at that time was unable to give any financial aid in the construction of such a building, but the trustees told our committee that if the people would go ahead and build a building to meet the school needs, that as soon as the district was financially able they would reimburse the builders for their time and money spent. 

Accordingly on August 15, 1915, Chester H. Hartman and Floyd

H. Case hauled the first lumber for the building. Before this, however, Fred A. Case had laid a rock foundation; materials had been hauled from Sandwash by men of our community. During September, October, and November the following men gave most all of their time on the building: Fred A. Case, Simeon E. Atwood, Chester H. 

Hartman, Charles Johnson, and Lawrence J. Mahaffey. Other men of the community gave cash and freely of their time in this building program. On Sept. 20, 1915, Fred A. Case was appointed by the school trustees as manager of the new school building. The privilege of holding Latter-day Saint meetings in the school house when completed was granted to the community. 

To meet further needs of lumber doors, windows, etc. Chester 

H. Hartman made a trip on horseback to Myton and from B. L. 

Dart got $300.00 to finish the building. Fred A. Case, Chester H. 

Hartman, and Lawrence J. Mahaffey signed the note as security.

After much hard work, the first meeting was held in the building

On April 9, 1916, the first Relief Society in the ward was organized with Mary A. Case as president. She chose as her counselors 

Lillian Ewell and Mary M. Barney, and Louie Musser as secretary.

During these pioneer days all families worked in harmony together. Quite often they would meet at some one's home, enjoy a chicken or trout supper, then spend the evening dancing, singing or in social games.

They also were united in helping the less fortunate; in this, I will cite just one instance: On April 17, all men of the community turned out and gave one day's work in clearing land and putting in crops for Bert Jacobs. The following men with teams responded: S. E. Atwood, Milan Rogers, W. J. Osborn, Ed. S. 

Robins, Wm. H. Case, Lars J. Marsing, Lorin Caldwell, Arthur 

Barney, S. K. Daniels, Ivan Bird, and Edward N. Morgan, and the following single hands: Charles Johnson, Roscoe Musser, and Bert

Jacobs. That day these men were successful in clearing 20 acres of land and planted 17 acres to wheat and 12 acres to potatoes. Thus we see with much work and unity the sagebrush gradually gave way to fields of golden grain.

By this time men who were leaders, men of understanding and vision, foresaw what our community would be like in the future; men like Owen Bennion, Fred A. Case, James H. Evans, Chester H. Hartman, and many others who built for the future. 

The next move for community improvement was made at the Sacrament Meeting held May 1, 1916. Those assembled decided to buy a piano. Accordingly, on May 21, 1916, Fred A. Case bought for the community a piano which was to be paid for in quarterly installments. 

At a meeting held May 28, 1916, President Fred A. Case called attention to the balance of $100 now due on the school house note, and asked for two signers to the note at the Myton State Bank. 

James H. Evans and Rufus Rogers agreed to sign with Mr. Case. 

During the summer of 1916 other community improvements went on. A group of men organized the Mt. Emmons Co-op., to sell to the public dry goods and groceries. That year they built a brick. building and put in a good stock of merchandise with Ira B. Cannon as manager. The following were stockholders: Owen Bennion, Harden Bennion, Ira B. Cannon, Fred A. Case, John Farrell, L. J. served as director of the Dry Gulch Irrigation Co. for years, also as County Commissioner and Bishop of the Mt. Emmons Ward. Edith taught school for a number of years and was Stake President of the Y.W.M.I.A. They have one son and 5 girls all serving the community; 3-R. E. A. Power plant; 4-R. E. A. Dam on 

Yellowstone.

Mahaffey, Ida Preece, and S. K. Daniels. 

On June 21, with two crews of men President Fred A. Case cleared the main road of rocks between the waterfalls and the Lake Fork River this side of Boneta in preparation for a Stake Conference which was to be held at Boneta June 24 and 25, 1916. 

At a Sacrament meeting held here Sunday, Sept. 3, 1916, Duncan Marchant and J. Robert Lambert were in attendance; a ward organization was discussed. Those present were favorable to the proposition. Accordingly at our Sacrament meeting held Sept. 17, 1916, R. S. Collett of the Stake Presidency, said the time had arrived to organize a ward. By unanimous vote the name Mt. Emmons was selected. President Fred A. Case with his counselors James H. 

Evans and Dan T. Powell and clerk Edward N. Morgan were released. Owen Bennion was sustained as bishop with Fred A. Case and James H. Evans as counselors and Edward N. Morgan as ward clerk; these men were set apart to their respective positions at the 

Quarterly Conference held at Roosevelt, Utah, Sept. 24, 1916; thus commenced the Mount Emmons Ward. 

After one year Bishop Bennion was called to service in the Stake High Council and on Sept. 15, 1917, he with his counselors were released and Jesse L. Smith was sustained as bishop. He chose 

Fred A. Case and Ira B. Cannon as his counselors. 

At a bishop's council meeting held March 15, 1918, the need for a ward amusement hall and a cemetery plot was stressed. It was The Altamont high school located on the highway between Mt. Emmons and Altonah. 

The large building to the left is the school proper. The smaller building is the L.D.S. church seminary and barely dicernible to the far right is the newly completed shop. 

The group of buildings makes a very attractive campus.
