279 on August 25, 1918, the ward decided to buy for a cemetery the 

S. E. 1/4 of the N. W. 1/4 of Sec. 35 T 1 So., Range 4 West con taining 40 acres, from Lafayette Holbrook for which they paid $200.00. 

Jesse L. Smith and counselors were released June 22, 1919 and Ira B. Cannon was sustained as Bishop with F. Earl Case and 

Leroy W. Rust as counselors.

On October 15, 1919, the first two missionaries left our ward to serve in the Southern States Mission; they were Wm. Lee Powell and Branch Babcock. 

May 23, 1919, the men of the ward cleared the grounds which had been purchased for a cemetery. This work was finished by 

2:30 p.m., and they returned to the school house where the sisters had prepared a feast after which dancing was enjoyed during the evening. August 8 and 9, 1919, Austin G. Burton surveyed and made a plot of 5 acres in the north-east corner of the forty. 

On June 27, 1920, the Duchesne Stake was divided, the east half taking the name of Roosevelt Stake and the west half with the 

Dry Gulch east of Bluebell as the east boundary line retaining the name of the Duchesne Stake. Owen Bennion was sustained as president of this new stake and Mt. Emmons was to be the headquarters. 

Ira B. Cannon was chosen as a counselor to President Bennion and that day was released as bishop of the Mt. Emmons Ward. F. Earl 

Case was then sustained as bishop with Dan T. Powell and Leroy 

W. Rust as counselors. Rudger Clawson of the Twelve Apostles was present and set these men apart to their various offices. 

Bishop John Wells visited our ward on August 8, 1920 and was the principal speaker. On January 6, 1922, a missionary farewell was given honoring Birch F. Bennion and Joseph Case who left a few days later to fill a mission in the Northern States. 

The next missionary to leave our ward was Darrell E. Evans, son of James H. Evans. A farewell was given him October 12, 1923, and a few days later he left for the Central States Mission. 

Early in November 1923, the Priesthood were called together and all expressed their willingness to get behind the work necessary for a new Church Building. Bishop E. Earl Case was to make application for a permit for timber to cut 60,000 feet of lumber from the National Forest. President Owen Bennion was to get from the 

Church General Authorities a plan for said building. 

In the early part of December the timber was spotted by Forest Ranger Blood, and S. E. Atwood with a crew of men went into the mountains and cut and logged up about two-thirds of this timber. 

Owing to light snow at that time the job was stopped for the time being.

On February 20, 1923, Bishop F. Earl Case with his counselors 

S. E. Atwood and Austin B. Taylor and a crew of about 12 men

again went into the mountains. That day and the next we witnessed the heaviest snowstorm of the winter after which we had plenty of snow to cart logs off the mountains and haul them to the McKune sawmill.

During the early summer of 1923 from these logs about 65,000 feet of lumber was sawed and all but 10,000 feet was hauled to Mt. 

Emmons where it was stacked to dry. The lumber left at the mill 

Iwas finish lumber which was also stacked so that it would cure before being planed for rustic on the outside of the building and finish for the interior. 

December 1923, and January 1924, found our men busy quarrying rock in the Sandwash and hauling it to the new building site. 

On the first of February we commenced digging trench for the foundation which was completed during the month. Elijah Campbell who came into the ward February 25, was given complete charge of the construction work. Local men who helped with the carpenter work where Clyde Fullmer, George and Paul Cluff, Fred Thacker, and Wilford Wilson. Then too every man living in the ward gave a helping hand with the hammer or saw, team or truck; within 60 days we had the building under roof. On June 9 and 10, Clyde 

Fullmer and Bishop Case poured the cement for the front steps. The building was painted and ready for use on June 23 and 24, when the Duchesne Stake Quarterly Conference was held here with an average attendance of 522 members. The building cost $12,000.00; the Church gave $4,000.00.

During the years 1922 to 1930, the need for better housing was felt. Accordingly men were constantly in the mountains getting logs and lumber with which to build. Gradually we saw families moving from their homestead log cabins into comfortable homes. 

With the exception of doors, windows and shingles, they used mostly local materials in this building program and within a short period of time most every owned farm had a good home built on it.. 

During the pioneer days the home of Mary Ann Case was the stopping place for travellers going to and from one end of the Reservation to the other; and especially was this so for Church officials and officers of the Dry Gulch Irrigation Company.

It was in August 1912, that Mrs. Case had the honor of entertaining royalty. Count Baron Von Horst, a German Baron and 

Miss Lillian Troy, an heiress, owner of the Castle of Troy in Ireland, came to inspect some Indian land which they had bought the month before at a land sale held at Provo, Utah. They, with Ed 

Harmston, looked over their new holdings in the Sandwash. Mrs. 

Fred Case entertained the group that night in her two-room log cabin.

It was in September 1918, that the first community fair was held here. Sports of all kinds were enjoyed during the day. Lars J.

MT. EMMONS 

281 Marsing had charge of the exhibits; he arranged a wonderful display of many things in the old Peacock Building. The handiwork of our ladies was displayed, as well as the grains, fruits and vegetables. On the fruit stand we saw many kinds of apples, peaches, pears, plums and grapes. This display made every farmer proud that he was a part of this thriving community. 

At a Stake Conference held here February 9, 1930, Bishop F.

Earl Case and counselors were released and LeRoy W. Rust was sustained as bishop; he chose as his counselors Floyd H. Case and 

Volney B. Boswell. Bishop Rust served until March 14, 1937, when he was released and Birch F. Bennion with Volney B. Boswell and 

Henry T. Chapman were sustained as the new bishopric. 

Birch F. Bennion and counselors were released two years later and Floyd H. Case with Evan O. Bennion and Leonard P. Wall were sustained. Floyd H. Case served one and a half years when he was released and Walter K. Kerksiek was made bishop on October 

1, 1939; under this leadership a ward beautification project was started. On the church grounds lawns and flowers were planted; so by the close of 1940, we had the most beautiful church grounds in all the Uintah Basin.

By this time through the R. E. A. we had electricity on our farms; so again a new era had dawned. Men began to paint up, and on each farm a beautification project made its appearance. Water systems were installed, and homes modernized. The World War II Mount Emmons ward chapel and a group of ward members ready to begin work on beautification project which changed building and grounds into one of the Basin's beauty spots.

made materials difficult to obtain, but work continued until today most families of Mount Emmons are enjoying city conveniences on their farms. Today men are working hard to get modern barns for their cows so that by July 1, 1947, they hope to meet the requirements set up by the government so that they can all be delivering grade A milk to the creameries.

THE HISTORY OF MOUNTAIN HOME HE FIRST SETTLERS who came to Mt. Home came in the year of 1908. When they crossed the flat, they could see only a plain of sagebrush, rock and a few scattered cedar trees where Mt. Home is located. 

Some of the early settlers who came were Oscar Wilkins, Less Stevens, D. B. Farnsworth, Don Miles, E. F. Palmer, Abe 

Lyons, L. M. Miles, E. T. Stewart, J. D. West, J. L. Thayne, Alma 

Thayne, and Brigham Stevenson.

Lester Stevens came to Mt. Home in the year of 1908 in the month of March. Mrs. Stevens and her son went back to Oakly but 

Mr. Stevens stayed to make a log cabin before returning. It snowed so hard, Mr. Stevens had to cut his stacks to get the straw out to feed his horses. They used to cut pieces of their houses for firewood that winter.

OSCAR WILKINS 

Oscar Wilkins was born in England on February 14, 1851. He came to America when he was 14 years old and settled with his mother in Peoy, Summit County in 1865. He acted as guard in the 

Black Hawk Indian War. He became a farmer and stock raiser in 

Peoy, Summit County. He also owned and operated a general store there. He sold his store and farm then moved to Mt. Home in 1908. 

He was ordained bishop of Mt. Home Ward on September 10, 1911, in which capacity he served until July 2, 1921. He was ordained Patriarch of Duchesne Stake on July 24, 1924. He also served as an official of the Fårnsworth Canal and Reservoir Company for many years. He died June 11, 1930. He was the father of 

13 children. (Information from the Family Bible.) ABE LIONS 

Abe Lyons lived in Peoy, Summit County. Before he came to 

Mt. Home, he worked in a mine. He came to Mt. Home across the Wolf Creek Pass, then down the Duchesne River then up through Utahn and on to Mt. Home. He lived just north of where Cliff 

Stevenson now lives. Mr. Lyon's lived in a tent until his home was built. Later he built the house in which Cliff Stevenson now lives. 

He also broke up and homesteaded the farm. 

He came to Mt. Home in the year of 1908 and worked four years on the Farnsworth Canal. Abe Lyon's wife had the first child 

17 years was Clerk of the Duchesne and later Moon Lake Stakes. He was ordained a Patriarch November 5, 1933 by George Albert Smith and has served in that capacity since.

In February, 1914 he married Pearl Kynaston of Bancroft, Idaho in the Salt Lake Temple. To this union was born nine boys and one girl. Mrs. Case served as President of the Y.W.M.I.A. and President of the Ward Relief Society. 

They and their children are all active church members. 283

that was born in Mt. Home, it was born in the one-room cabin. 

His name was Myron, he was born February, 1909.

D. B. FARNSWORTH 

He was born in Beaver City, Utah in 1859. Then he moved from Beaver to Mona, then from Mona to Moab then to Price, Utah. 

There he was a freighter using six and eight horse teams to pull the wagons from Price to Vernal, Utah. 

He was married to Minnie Johnson at Provo on September 6, in about 1885. He moved to Mt. Home in the spring of 1908, coming from Price by way of Nine Mile Canyon or the old Price-Myton road.

Their first camping place was at Boneta. They camped here while the places on Mt. Home flat were being surveyed and located, then they moved on their homestead and began building a cabin. 

He was the first vice-president and manager of the Farnsworth Canal and Reservoir Company. When the company was formed it was given his name. He lived on his farm in Mt. Home until the time of his death in 1931. He was the father of 11 children. (Information from his son, Mr. Dave Farnsworth).

E. T. STEWART 

E. T. Stewart was born July 26, 1874. He was the son of Mr. and Mrs. Van Stewart. He came to the reservation in 1905 at the time of the opening. He settled in Mt. Home in 1908; he helped to build the Farnsworth Canal and Reservoir, and to put the first water into No. 1 lateral. Mr. Stewart was also a director of the canal company for several years.

While he was in Mt. Home he was a farmer and stock raiser. 

Then he moved to Wellington, Carbon County, Utah, in the spring of 1931, where he lived until the time of his death on September 

6, 1940. (Information from his son, Mr. Milton Stewart).

BRIG STEVENSON 

Mr. Stevenson was born June 9, 1882, in Escalante; then he moved to Montcarma, from there to Orangeville, to Desert Lake and later to Sunnyside where he worked in a mine. He came to 

Mt. Home by way of Nine Mile Canyon in the year 1908. He built his first home about 100 yards east of where Del Anderson's home now stands. Mr. Stevenson spent much of his time, as did all the other settlers, working on the canal. In 1913 he started the third store in Mt. Home in the same house as the Post Office. About a year later he bought from Mr. Enoch Lewis the store he now owns. He has lived here ever since. He is the father of four boys. (Information from Brig Stevenson).

JOSEPH D. WEST 

Joseph D. West was born June 28, 1867 at Manti, Utah. After

MOUNTAIN HOME 

285 he left Manti he moved to Spring City, Utah, he lived there until he was 14 years old.

He then moved to Arizona where he lived for three years. From 

Arizona he moved back to Spring City and worked on the railroad. 

He was married at Annabella, Utah, January 29, 1887. He often came to Mt. Home in 1908 and homesteaded the place now owned by Mr. Nyberg. On this place he built the first house on Mt. Home flat. The first dance that was held in Mt. Home was in Mr. West's new home; he played the fiddle and one of his girls played the organ. Seventy people attended the dance. For amusement the people used to gather at each other's homes and sing songs and dance. 

Mr. and Mrs. West camped up by the canal. They were working the ditch all the time so they could get water for their land. 

They would go up in the spring and return in the fall. He then sold his place and moved up in Idaho where he worked in the timber. 

Then he went to California where he worked on the railroad. In about 1935 he returned to Mt. Home. He is the father of 8 children, three of whom have passed away. (Information from Mr. West). 

ALMA THAYNE 

Mr. Thayne was born in Woodland, Summit County, Utah in

December 30, 1885. He lived here until he was twelve years old, then he moved with his family to Desert Lake for ten years and worked on the farm. In 1905, when the reservation was opened they filed on their place; in 1908, they moved and he helped his parents clear their farm land. He went to Price that winter and worked on the Price Canal until spring. Mr. Thayne returned to 

Mt. Home and spent most of his time that summer working on his place. The second winter he went to Vernal, Utah. He stayed in 

Vernal on the ranch for a period of six years.

After this period again returned to Mt. Home and took up the improving of his place again. He spent a lot of time working at the sawmill to get lumber and logs to build with. While working at the sawmill he met his wife who was the cook for the mill crew. 

They were married in Mr. Thayne's cabin in Mt. Home by Bishop 

Oscar Wilkins. Mr. Thayne is still living on his place in Mt. Home. 

He is the father of four children.

LOUCHIOUS MILES 

Louchious Miles was born on June 24, 1872 in Peoa, Summit county. He was a farmer and stockraiser in Peoa. He moved to 

Idaho where he lived near Boise, Idaho, he was married at Boise.

Mr. Miles also worked as a contractor, building roadbeds for the railroad in northern Nevada for two years. He came to the reservation when it was opened for settlement, came to Mt. Home, worked in Price the winter of 1908-1909.

He returned to Mt. Home in the spring of 1909, spent his time

clearing his land and working on the canal. Mr. Miles lived in Mt. 

Home until the time of his death in 1939. He was the father of 

6 children. (Information from his daughter). 

GEORGE G. LINDSAY 

He was born in Scotland, June 29, 1871. He worked in the mines when a boy, before coming to America where he went to work in the mines at Park City, Utah. He then moved to Coalville where he was elected sheriff of Summit County, Utah. 

He and his boys came to the reservation in 1907 as prospectors searching for gold. When the Indian officials learned of their work, they made them stop because they were working on Indian ground. 

They did not find any gold but they did find a large vein of Gilsonite up in what is now called Telephone Hollow near the yellow ledge on the road to Yellowstone C. C. Camps.

Mr. Lindsay later settled in Mt. Home and has lived there and raised a family of 13 children. He has served as secretary of the canal company for a number of years and as a postmaster for a number of years. (Information from Mr. West). 

JOHN L. THAYNE 

He was born in Canada on July 8, 1859. He came to Utah when he was 12 years old. He lived in Salt Lake until he was married. He married Annabelle Reid at Salt Lake in 1875. They then moved to Woodland, Utah where he and his father operated a sawmill. They did this work until 1905. 

They then moved to Murray, Utah and lived there until they came to Mt. Home in the spring of 1908. He worked on his homestead until August 1908 and then he moved to Vernal, Utah, where he worked on a farm for four years. 

He returned to Mt. Home and lived on his farm here until the time of his death in August, 1930. He was the father of thirteen children. (Information from his daughter). 

When the people came upon Mt. Home flat all they could see from where water would come was the little meadow of wild flowers on Charles Alexander's place. They started digging a well and when they were about six feet deep they struck water but it was not good. So they still kept hauling water from the river. 

Mr. Stevenson and Mr. West finally decided to dig another well; so they started to dig on Mr. West's place. They got about 

65 feet down and struck water, but this water was not any good either so they moved about 100 yards west and began to dig another well. About 35 feet down they struck water and it was good. Then the people began to wonder how they could get water to irrigate their crops, they decided to take it from Rock Creek but then a man from Roosevelt (Mr. Smart) changed their minds, he said that it was easier to get water from Lake Fork.

MOUNTAIN HOME 

287 The canal was started in 1908. Oscar Wilkins was boss with 

Jack Draper second, and Mr. West third. The canal was finished in 1911, but the men came down in 1910. When they reached Jim 

Shirt's place, they killed a beef and had a great supper. 

The canal was only a small ditch about the size of a plowed furrow, even now in 1946 the canal is not a finished product. 

In the spring of the year the farmers had plenty of water because of the flood waters. Later the people of Mt. Home built the reservoir at Twin Pots. The next fall in 1926, when the flood waters came, it flooded over the dam and washed it away. Later the men decided that they needed the water, so they finally went to the bank in Salt Lake and borrowed $31,000 and paid the men who worked on the reservoir their wages. Later the men from all around Mt. Home got together and decided to build a dam across 

Moon Lake. This is called the equalization agreement. 

The people of the Uintah Basin are slowly but surely paying back the money they borrowed to build the dam across Moon Lake.

When the people first came here, Boneta was already being settled. Boneta was not a big town, it had from 7 to 10 families living there at the time. Most of the people came from the same part of the country and they were going to make Boneta and Mt. 

Home one big town, but the people who already lived at Mt. Home decided they did not want the towns put together. They first thought of building the town down in the southeastern part of Ged Lindsay's place.

The men of Mt. Home decided to go up and get out lumber and build a ward house. This was used for Church and dances and lots of fun. Later the men asked the bishop if he wanted to buy the ward house just for the church house and he said he did and it was sold to him. In 1944 this church house was burned down. 

Mt. Home did not have any schools, the people decided to have a school for their children. Mrs. Pace took the children to her home and taught them how to read and write. Later the people went down on the place below Aaron Stevenson's place and made some bricks to build a school house. These bricks had too much lime in them, so they went up to the Stevenson's place and made some more bricks, these were good and a school house was built with them. 

Brig Stevenson started a small store in his home, just east of

Dell Anderson's, and the few things sold he got from Price. He also owned the first post ofifce in Mt. Home. Enoch Lewis then built a store which is part of the store now owned by Brig Stevenson.

Later Mr. Stevenson built more on the store. Over a period of years there were a number of other stores in Mt. Home. Mr. Peck owned one across from the Church House; it was burned down. Another

store was built across from the Church House, and it was burned down; it was owned by John T. Rowley. The next was owned by 

Al Roberts and it was torn down. Then Mr. Fean Chattlen started a store in what is now the post office. He operated it until his death.

After about two years of being in Mt. Home the people still had no post office or store. The mail was delivered to Boneta and the people had to go there for their mail. Then Ed Farnsworth carried the mail on a horse from Boneta. Mr. Stevenson built a post office in his store. Later, Ellis Shirts carried the mail to the post office from Boneta. Then the mail carriers changed and the government made a contract for the people to carry the mail. D. B. 

Farnsworth, Mr. Holt and B. B. Mecham were among the first to carry the mail. Mr. Stevenson did not keep the post office very long. He gave it to Mrs. Pace who was living where Mrs. Lyons now lives. Then to Mr. Bounce. About 1931, Mr. Lindsay took it. 

He kept it in the north room of his house. 

Miss Nellie Stevenson built a small house by the south side of the store, here she took the post office over. Pete Smith and Geo.

Robins were the mail carriers then, only they carried the mail from Duchesne and not from Myton. After Nellie had had the post office for two years, she moved to Price. Mrs. Mary Brown bought the post office and now runs it. Floyd Farnsworth is now the mail carrier. 

He still carries the mail from Duchesne.

MYTON Contributed by Mrs. Florence Madsen of the 1947 Centennial Committee FOREWORD 

At the request of Miss Josephine Hunt I attempted to recall and notate events regarding the town of Myton. This was to be used merely for Miss Hunt's work in a Teacher's class. 

At the time, I debated as to using names and decided negatively as there were too many people who had participated in the town, whom I had forgotten or had never known, due to absence from the State, so I could not do full justice to all involved. 

Also, my own personal viewpoint is, that everybody who by labor and effort helped build this district, was a material benefactor of the town, and its growth and business interests. 

In any case I would make an exceedingly poor biographer. 

ETHEL F. BARIL 

Myton, Utah

November 3, 1946 

MYTON 

​REVIOUS TO SEPTEMBER 1905, the ground on which Myton stands was a part of the Uintah Indian Reservation. In that month the Reservation was opened, by a drawing, for Homestead Entry. This entry was not free, for a price of $1.25 per acre was charged.

The United States Government designated three Townsites for this territory, and named this one in honor of H. P. Myton who was at one time in charge of the Indian Affairs on the Reservation. 

At this point was a bridge across the Duchesne river, this being the only bridge over the river at any point. An Indian Trading Post was located here and the place was called "The Bridge." 

Immediately after the opening, people began to establish themselves in the town, and business buildings and homes were at once being erected, mainly on the flat along the river and close to the bridge near which a number of Indian Trading Post buildings stood.

Ere long the town had several general mercantile stores, a drug store, barber shop, blacksmith shop, furniture store, lumber yards, and a hall which was owned by a stock company, was erected and 289

1-Old town of Myton; 2-One of the first ball games; 3-Early mine and real estate building; 4-New Hope canal flume. -Picture courtesy Alice Todd who has served efficiently for years as Myton's 

Postmistress.

MYTON 

291 was used for many purposes such as church, funerals, business meetings, shows, dances, and also for elections. 

A school district had been organized and the first school was held in a small building on the bench, and was taught by an attorney who had located in the new town. The next term, also taught by a man, was held in a log building on the flat,and the third school, that of 1908-09 was in the hands of a woman teacher and was held in a room of the Columbia-Bain building which was on the bench. The first school was the term of 1906-07.

A little group of homes had been erected about a half mile west of the bridge but in the townsite, this little location was long known as Smithville.

By the summer of 1907 the town was spreading out and growing fast, with buildings being erected on the bench. The big drawback was the difficulty in securing lumber in needed quantities, as this very necessary commodity had to be freighted in from the mills thirty and forty miles distant in the mountains, and the hauling was all done by horses over very poor road, those same roads being almost impassible from mud, sand and snow, very often. 

Early in the life of Myton, probably before it was a year old, trouble arose which culminated in the killing of an Indian by a white man. The Indian had announced his intention to kill the white, and so the man protected himself and immediately left for distant points. But this aroused the Indians who promptly banded to avenge the killing of one of their tribe. So unsettled did the matter become that a number of the white women and children were loaded in a "white-top" and started to Fort Duchesne for protection, but they met a troop of soldiers from the Fort coming to Myton.

The Commandant at the Fort had been notified of the uprising by telephone, and ordered out the troop to come to Myton and squelch the matter. By some means the Indians had been persuaded to drop the matter and the troop was returned to the Post without having to further participate. For a short space of time the people of the town were in an alarmed state, to say the least. Indian's name was Fenno.

In these years, the United States Government had a number of corps of surveyors laying out lines for ditches for Indian lands; also private ditches were being surveyed. Large herds of sheep wintered and were also sheared in proximity to Myton. Homesteaders also drew on the town for supplies. The town was growing. 

The year of 1908 was largely a continuance of the former years. 

More homes were being built, lands surrounding the town being cleared and cultivated, canals and ditches carrying more water to the land, and more people coming in from other places. The school had more pupils. But the winter of 1908-09 was very severe. 

At this time the town had two cafes as well as the hotel which was here at the time of the opening, and these places were being

kept busy. There were also Feed and Camp yards, and a Pool Hall. 

Myton was a voting precinct, had its first Presidential Election in 1908, that being the year that Wm. H. Taft was elected as president of the country. 

Also, our first physician located here.

Winter came early and was very severe. Hay and grain were hauled in from such points as Randlett, where Indians raised it, and grain was also brought from Vernal. The price in each case was high where it was raised. Stock suffered and the sheepmen stood heavy losses. Many staple groceries were none too plentiful as it was not an easy winter for the freighters to bring through the supplies from Price.

By the spring of 1909, there was a bank established. This was largely a group of local stockholders and the business was done in a log building on the Flat. There were also two new realty companies doing business.

Spring was late and the mountains were heavily covered with snow which did not melt early. It was well into June when hot days brought down floods of water. And then Myton suffered a severe shock by the over full Duchesne cutting a new channel not very far north of the bridge and leaving the bridge high and dry and no way to cross the swollen stream. As the mail came from Fort 

First Uinta Basin baseball team. Top row: Harry Seaton, Rowland Burdick, Heber All red, Mr. Lloyd, Joe Means. front row: Joseph Bryant, Bill Morrison, H. D. Hurt, Wildur Bailey.

MYTON 

293 Duchesne, ways and means to bring it across had to be devised, and were by horseback, by team and by row boat. There is little question that this did hit the business to some extent. However, ere long, action was being taken and soon surveyors were selecting a site for a new and better bridge. The site chosen was where "Smithville" stood, and there is where the bridge was built. 

During the summer of this year a schoolhouse was in the course of construction and was ready for occupancy when fall came. There were two rooms, and the two teachers were employed that term. 

The location was on the bench and was the site at the east of town which was laid off for school purposes when the town was plotted. 

With the site of the new bridge determined, the trend was for homes to be built on the bench rather than on the flat. 

The winter of 1909-10 was another severe one, again; but not so late a spring. There had been great difficulty in getting freight through from the railroad point of Price due to the heavy snows between Nine Mile and Price, making Whitmore Park about impossible for teams and at one time it took twenty-six days for teams to get through from Vernal and back there, about four days less from Myton. In the meantime, staples were missing from the stores, and when the teams did arrive, everybody was wanting them, so sharing them was the only way about it and that is what was done, even with the flour which was hauled from Vernal, in the emergency. 

Promptly when spring arrived the work for the construction of the new bridge was commenced with a full force of men. This was the main topic of interest to people not only in Myton but to those on both sides of the river all throughout the Uintah Basin. 

In July a second newspaper was established. The first having been published in 1908, and called "THE UINTAH CHIEFTIAN," (The first, not the second). 

And then came Bridge Day! September 10, 1910. The day when "All roads lead to Myton." It was indeed an occasion for rejoicing, and the people came from every direction to celebrate the bridge.

Now, 1911. Two special improvements added to Myton's advancement. First, was the addition of another room to the school building, made necessary by the increase of pupils. The second was the erection of an opera house of considerable size. This was owned by a stock company. It was opened with a Minstrel Show given by local talent, on September 19th. This building was used for a great many purposes other than shows, and was later bought by the L. D. S. ward and used as a church and meeting house. 

In 1912, there was much activity in building on the bench and beside the number of more substantial homes there were several business buildings of good size.

A "Parent and Teachers' Association" was being discussed, as was also the incorporating of the town, and with the coming of the next year's term the former was in action, while the latter became 

COME MYTON 

LDS 

SUNDAY SCHOOL 

1-Float in 1911 parade: 2-Charley Sands; 3-First work on the new Hope Ditch; 

4-"Bridge Day" at Myton in 1910.

MYTON 

295 a reality. 

Myton also found itself very nicely supplied with entertainment, as local plays were frequently given; dances and entertainments of various other types were held, and in summer, baseball called out good crowds. Excellent rodeos were held early in Myton. 

The year of 1913 brought a substantial industry into action. A stock company of local men formed and established the first flour mill of this community. This was a small mill, located at first on the flat but soon a building was erected on the bench and here business was carried on for several years. Local wheat was used. Gas was the power used. This mill was later sold to a company at 

Duchesne; and the building was enlarged and is even now in use as a hotel and is situated on U. S. Highway 40, Myton. 

By this time a number of the businesses conducted previously in Old Town were doing business on the bench and many new businesses as well. Myton had for a time, been banned as to the sale of hard liquors, and Local Option had also held for a period. At a time, A. W. C. T. U. had formed here with good membership and was active for some years.

Then came the time when the Uintah Power and Light Company was formed for the purpose of furnishing electricity to the Basin. 

And so Myton granted them a franchise for the town and also contracted for lighting power. Lines were run; all work went ahead towards the turning in of power.

At this time, too, the Presbyterian people were moving to build a church. The actual work on the building was started and by the fall of 1914 the building was being used as an academy to handle 

New Myton School.

the higher grades. Church was held there also. 

A Fall Fair was planned for September 21, and was widely advertised. The exhibits were displayed in the Presbyterian Church and the sports were held on the Ball Park just across the street to the south. The fair continued for three days and drew big crowds. 

The Power had been turned in a few days before the Fair! 

At this time the power company was arranging to establish a modern and large flour mill, as the supply of grain being raised seemed sufficient to warrant it and the demand was big. This mill, while not in Myton, was just adjacent and was of much benefit to the town, as those bringing grain did their purchasing here while waiting for their grist.

About this time there was a movement of the Indians to spread out to various sections of the Basin as ditches were in shape to deliver water to their farms. Many of them had made Myton their trading post up to this time and their going was noticeable to the business houses.

The winter of 1914-15 was almost without any snow, one fall of two or three inches in February being the only one which covered the ground and that for but a couple of days as there was no very great cold that winter.

Myton needed a water system as the old way of hauling water, melting ice and using cisterns which were filled from ditches in the late fall and refilled by hauling water or ice, was not adequate any more. Finally, the question was settled by the municipality installing a water system, the water to be taken from the Duchesne river which at that time was very good water. A pumping plant and a water tank were part of the plan. Bonds were issued and the surveying done and finally water was delivered through the mains. A good system of fire-plugs was part of the water line. 

But fire had already destroyed much of one business block in July, 1915. Long before this the two newspapers had been merged into "The Myton Free Press" and had in the fall of 1914 been removed into a new building, adjacent to the opera house on the east. And soon a commercial club for the boosting of the town was organized and had its office in the same building. 

The next year went by much the same as the former ones, with steady improvement of streets and walks, some building and other advancement. The winter of 1915-16 calls for mention. It was a very light one until January 19th, when snow started to fall and kept falling to the depth of many feet and that storm held for days and finally cleared off to the tune of 35 degrees below zero. Severe cold kept up and the price of hay went up dollars a day. On the 

5th of March teams crossed the river in safety on the ice but the spring came immediately after that date.

MYTON 

297 World War I was in progress and America was in. A number of local men engaged in this conflict. Another fire broke out and destroyed about half a block. In fact, it did burn all but the last building in the block that had the fire in 1915. 

While the two old newspapers had merged, another had started publication some time about 1913, and continued to publish until the latter part of this decade.

The Armistice was called and Myton's returned men formed a Legion of Honor Post, bought a building for a barracks, and are still functioning.

Surrounding lands were being brought more and more into cultivation. Honey and sweet clover seed were paying products and signs were that Alfalfa seed would be a valuable product of the section around the town. Thus entered the decade of 1920-1930. 

The end of the first year brought another fire in which several stores and a cafe were destroyed. By this time the town had two banks, two doctors, and lawyers as well as gas stations and garages. 

In the early days of the place, a U. S. Commissioner had been stationed at Myton, and the office was still here, although there had been a change in the personnel.

During the war, interest had been turned to oil shale and a company of local people had become interested in shale claims. south of this town some number of miles. Although the war was over, this interest was kept alive for a number of years and at last an oil company sank a test well but gave out no definite facts except that they were satisfied. As to what, they did not say, but they have done nothing further.

Alfalfa seed did become a big crop for a number of years. 

Cleaning plants and seed houses were established and buyers moved in and located here. Trucking became very brisk and this was a busy place.

But both banks had failed and that was a hard blow. By 1925 water was ready to be delivered to the arid stretch known as Pleasant Valley and thus started a number of people to breaking up and seeding this tract. People came in and invested there. In 

1929, the depression that was already advancing hit with full force and that did not help growth. By this time there were many more pupils in the school, and a bus system was bringing many from outlying districts that had been discontinued, so a two-roomed brick building had been erected and a frame building was also moved in and enlarged; so six teachers were employed. A ninth grade was being carried.

The first transportation of children had been by team and covered wagon as far back as the second term of the Myton schools. 

This came from Lake Fork.

During the decade of 1920-30 the State was laying out and

building new roads. A short line from Salt Lake City to the East had been surveyed, and ran through Myton. This thoroughfare is now U. S. 40. The bridge in 1910 is on this road. The location of the bridge had caused some of the original business men to move from Old Town to that point; Smithville, as such, had passed off the map.

Alfalfa seed had kept its promise and produced heavily for several years, but, as the next decade was approaching, it was not doing so well and the price was lower a lot. In the old decade the town had a Health Board; and the town had also become a city of the Third Class. The Census of 1930 was 496 people. 

The new decade followed a different pattern all over the United States. And early in its first year another fire took another business building. Seed plants were feeling the strain; buyers failed to come, and resident ones left. Oil shale was of no interest; but one new business corner was erected. Then came C. W. A. to help and soon THE NEW DEAL with its Direct Relief, C. C. C., 

P. W. A., F. H.A., and W. P. A. Myton was not growing nor holding its own in improvements. The town was largely supported by its adjacent farm lands and so recently had the water been available for the land to the south and so soon after farm products became a drug on the markets of the entire U. S. that Myton was hit hard. 

The Indian Department did work on a drainage line; some improvement was done on homes through F.H.A., but the population was decreasing until the Post Office which had been advanced to 

Third Class, was returned to Fourth Class. There had been more fires, both business places (The Gravity Seed Cleaning Plant was one) and homes. One big improvement was added to the town; the erection of a modern and fully equipped school house. Here, ten teachers were employed. The original building was turned into a hot lunch room. The brick building became a music and band room, and the frame building was sold, torn down and moved out of town. At the school grounds, lawns were seeded, trees planted, also shrubs and flowers. The U. S. was beginning to look up again, and Myton's population began to increase once more. With this came the demand for a more reliable water supply, and better water, as the Duchesne water had become not so good after the irrigation became greater.

By the Census of 1940 the population was up to 467; and the 

Post Office has again been returned to Third Class.

Cream stations had been one of the town's best businesses for years but of course had suffered too, through the depression. In the latter part of the decade a Casein factory was established and whole-milk found a market. It is very helpful to the town, as it gives employment to people who reside here, and helps the farmers as well.

MYTON 

299 The bringing of mountain water to the town had been the greatest improvement since the decade started. While not yet, in early 1945, completed, the work on it will be finished. The war shortage of materials, made it almost impossible to obtain the pipes. 

From the early days of the town, lodges have carried on. 

Modern Woodmen and Odd Fellows and their sister lodges, Yoemen; and somewhat later, the Masonic Lodge, with the Eastern 

Star. With their members drawn from the entire Basin, Vernal included, their place of meeting has always been Myton. 

Something must be said about the benefit the Indian Department has been here, as until the last two years the Indian Irrigation Office was maintained here. The splendid buildings erected, the payroll, the erst-while and present additions to the social life has meant a lot to the section. There was also a fine house built for the Indian Farmer's residence.

And now, Myton's hat should come off to the Indians themselves, for during all the past they have contributed much to the growth of the place. They have established homes, their children attend the school, they farm their lands; many of them work on any project that offers occupation; they help on the farms, their tribal moneys have been part of the town's business life and income. They too, have had their part in the sports as they took part in rodeos and races; they patronize shows and other entertainments and try to be law-abiding citizens. They have a number of their boys in the war and more than one has won the Purple Heart. These are the 

Indians of Myton. In the past they held an annual Bear Dance here when large crowds came from every direction, but this was discontinued three or four years ago. These Redmen have certainly come a long way since the "Opening."

This decade has also brought loss by fire for on the 18th of February, 1941, the L. D. S. ward house was totally destroyed. Thus did the Opera House, opened in 1911, come to its end. But in this instance the loss was Myton's gain, for the L. D. S. people immediately proceeded toward the erection of a fine new church which faces on U. S. Highway 40 and is an adornment to the town, and the beautification of the grounds promises much. This is being steadily carried on, and it will be extensive. 

A new and very modern gas station was erected in the early Forties and this completed the moving of the business corner to one block west of the original main corner which had been built in 1910, two fine cement buildings which fires passed by. Now the main corner of business is in a direct line with the bridge. However, a miracle only, is responsible for its not having been destroyed along with much else and many people. In August of 1944, when a tanker of high power gas traveling on the highway and enroute east, turned over directly in front of the gas station. The escaping of gas can

be compared only to the escaping of heavy steam and resembled the mist at Niagara Falls. Alarms were spread, fires were smothered, no one-not even the driver-was hurt and no one struck a match so Myton was left to continue growing. 

APPENDAGE 

In the almost two years since I put my memories of Myton on paper, I have tried to recall items I might have missed. 

One I do recall, and had failed to mention, that in the early 1920's the town was incorporated and given a rating, of a City of the third Class. By this, certain advantages were available. 

In the two years past, Myton, like the balance of the nation, has been extending, improving and increasing. Old businesses have been enlarged; new ones established. New homes are in the process of building, and old ones are being improved. The water works and streets are going along as fast as labor, supplies and equipment will allow, and hence the new growth is with us. Also, an immense 

Alfalfa Seed Crop south of town aids this growth. This crop is that of 1946.

It is a fact that Myton had one situation which was, to say the least, unique. Three official mayors at the same time; it is doubtful if any other city in Utah, or in any of the other states can lay claim to a like distinction.

Erata-The Cream Stations previously mentioned were established before 1940, though mentioned following that date. 

Ethel F. Baril. 

Myton, Utah

November 3, 1946 FOREWORD TO HISTORY OF MYTON 

The desire of the writer of the history of Myton is to give principle dates of events as a measure of time and condition of affairs that contribute to the city's growth and vital needs of the whole section from which it drew its trade and support. 

There is no wish to criticize for failure, but to give the facts as collected, in an endeavor to portray events without criticizing any group or person, except where merited; but the suppositionthat each person does acts for his own benefit, and naturally upholds the dignity and honor of his particular town or community, and gives it his full support. 

The support of an historic account of a town, locality or undertaking must depend upon its industry, integrity and loyalty to its whole set up.

We apologize for the acceptance of some statements for which there is no given assurance of its correctness. 

We also wish to thank those who contributed with information of dates and detail events.

MYTON 

301 SM 

THE HISTORY OF MYTON, UTAH History written by LUTHER E. POTTER YTON TOWNSITE WAS FORMERLY an Indian Trading Post, granted to and established by Wm. Henderson of Vernal,

Utah, the rights of which were transferred to Mr. H. G. 

Clark, a former Army Scout under General Nelson Miles, in a military campaign with the Sioux Indians of South Dakota. He was cited for conspicuous service and recommended to West Point 

Military Academy. Mr. Clark sold his right to Mr. Hayden Calvert, who became the first Mayor of Myton, while Mr. Clark became 

Justice of the Peace by appointment, and served well that position with credit. He also served several years as City Marshall. 

The former named Bridge, so designated by the Postal Department, became only history to the timers, when Myton was named. 

Mr. Calvert, who had been connected with the White Rocks 

Trading Post, which was under the jurisdiction of the Interior Department, came to White Rocks in 1885 and held the position as Post trader until buying the right to trade at the Bridge, where Old Town is now located, and marks the former business center of Myton, now a residential district. 

Myton was named for Major Myton, by surveyors; Major 

Myton was a U. S. Marshal and lived in Salt Lake City, Utah.

The bridge proper was a two span structure supported by a pier, built crib like, and filled with rocks, the end butments built in the same manner, and only a memory of the former Travelors Friend is its desecration by removal. The bridge was the only safe crossing convenient to the routes of interest and business transportation. In seasons of high water the only crossing on the Indian Reservation over which the Military and freight passed was over the Duchesne River at this point. The Freight Road, as it was known, served to transport freight to Ouray, Fort Duchesne, White Rocks and Vernal, where it was extended to Jensen on the Green River, the waterway for trappers and hunters in the early days; also the route taken by 

Father Escalante enroute to California, from Mexico on a Catholic 

Mission service.

The Bridge was the safe passage over which passed thousands of head of livestock to and from the grazing lands both Spring and

Fall. This condition existed many years prior to the advent of the white man settlement.

The Freight Road had its beginning in the quite remote past, when the Indian looked for better hunting and fishing in other parts, and probably the Spanish explorers in search for gold and the Fountain of Youth then trod these trails resulting in the now famous trail or Freight Road which passes to the south west of Myton into Wells Draw, with a deep dug well, known as a great

watering and pastures and favorable Camping Ground sites. The road continued to the head of Gate canyon at the mouth of which is the Preston Nutter ranch. The homes of the ancient Cliff Dwellers may still be seen in this beautiful canyon. 

This canyon heads in snowy Whitmore Park also supporting another canyon of importance named Soldier canyon leading into Castle Valley, the home of Price City and the D. & R. G. Railway, from which the Uintah Basin freighted its supplies. Many homesteaders earned their living by freighting, as well as improving their home.

Myton at once organized its residents for civic and educational needs. Á Community Hall was built with contributed labor, materials and cash. The first pleasing result was a canvas covered structure, well constructed frame and excellent Oregon Fir flooring. The final results was a creditable recreation center, school house, court house and general assembly hall where all matters of the moment were thrashed out. The use was free, and business pertaining thereto, was in the hands of the Social Committee. 

The school was established by the Board of Education and Board of County Commissioners of Wasatch County, under John Moffat, then County Commissioner from the east side of Wasatch County; Mr. Moffat lived at Bonita and served well in the interest of roads and school.

Mr. Britton was the first teacher and by profession was an attorney at law (also Mr. Dubendorff). He was well liked. School was held in the city hall. It soon needed more space to accommodate the growing attendance and a new school house was built by Wasatch County. Mr. and Mrs. J. O. Hohn, retired teachers, gave valuable advice and assisted in teaching. 

The constant influx of home seekers, investors, prospectors, and those of mining interest, demanded more varied store rooms and warehouses and resident houses and varied were the activities. 

The selection of Myton as Headquarters for Indian Service employees on survey and canal construction contributed towards its growth, and store houses and residences demanded more laborers. 

The Government Quarters were built about one-half mile south west of the old townsite and "Bridge B" around which new business houses were constructed and days, not months, marked the completion of a structure. 

The 1908 and 1909 activities, Myton was considered the financial center of the entire Reservation Country. The Myton State 

Bank was established. It became an acknowledged success by paying 

110% in dividends in 10 years of service to its community until its hard time demise.

The Press influence of Myton has had a varied and interesting career being the first publication of special use in industrial develop-

MYTON 

303 ment, of farming and general building influences of the Reservation. 

On Thursday, May 14, 1908, the Uintah Chieftain started on its voyage to build a budding empire. This newspaper of the Reservation

was published by J. Morton Alexander, formerly of Colorado, and a member of that State's Legislative Body; his liberal views and Socialistic tendencies endeared him to the general reading public. The paper and successor, became a great asset to our new country, as a pioneer institution of lasting moral dignity and practical service, for the mass settlement of a great country in the making. As its influence broadened to mellowing the misunderstandings of the Indian and white man; many Indians, as well as whites, were given space in its columns as praiseworthy and influential citizens. Among such Indians were: Ouray, Chipeta, Atche, Red Cap, Acawana, Arapo, W. W. Weero, Mountain Sheep, Modock, Grey Mountain

Dick. Among the ladies were: Ioka, Revorue, the wife of Fenno. 

Many of these became known Voters, speaking good English, taking part in civil life, and a reasonable interest in Tribal affairs. The 

Uintah Chicftain changed to Reservation News and Myton Free Press. 

The Honor Roll of the Uintah Chieftain exerted valuable guidance on many social and economic problems of building for the future. The following are the names of the people who encouraged much needed public improvements: Judge Hardy G. Clark, J. O. 

Hohn, H. Calvert, D. M. Frost, E. M. Jones, Wm. Zowe, C. T.

Beegs, Sally M. Calvert, D. & J. Barry, I. W. Odekirk, J. M. Colthorp, H. A. Carmon, John X Palmer, John Wills, John R. Justice and A. M. Todd. 

The Uintah Chieftain served well, and passed its good will and property to a new publishing company, The Reservation News, which changed ownership many times serving the country as a local journal. 

The business men of Myton bought the plant; instaled an up-todate Linotype machine and started what became the most popular Press Service of the Basin, in size, advertising, and news print with the articles of up-to-date and national importance. It also became the Basin leader on editorial comments on subjects of vital interest to the entire Basin and the connecting outside points. 

Mr. A. M. Todd of Myton served thru its entire life, as mechanical operator and advertising editor and large stock holder. 

Mr. C. B. Cook was manager from its beginning until his death while on a vacation trip to Cuba.

Mr. Arnold Reef, now of Roosevelt, served with the Free Press during its latter issues, with Stanley Peatross as linotype operator, during its entire press service at Myton. 

Prior to the opening of the Reservation on Sept. 10, 1905, the religious teaching of the Indians, and what white people were living here in various occupations, and children of Government employees. were partly conducted under the First Episcopal Church of Utah, by Rev. W. S. Spaulding of Salt Lake City, and local work by Rev.

Hershey, of Randlett. During the fall and winter following, a Com-

MYTON 

305 munity Sunday School was organized, with I. O. Odekirk and Mr. 

J. O. Hohn as superintendents, alternating each a three month term, with Mrs. J. O. Hohn as teacher. Rev. J. D. Storemont, a missionary minister and homesteader living on Lake Fork, conducted services at the Community Hall and other points of the new country until 1907, when The First Presbyterian Church of Utah, by a commission composed of Rev.'s Josia McLain, J. K. McGillroy and

J. D. Storemont, established The First Presbyterian Church of 

Myton, with Rev. Storemont as minister.

He was succeeded by the following: Clayton S. Rice, Geo. S. Sloan, Arthur B. Rice, John 

Meeker, Willard Spence, L. A. Parker, J. D. Henry, Geo. B. Guild, Marth Fouts, J. A. Lyman, and James H. Hailwood.

A new Church was built by its membership and friends on subscription with matched sum met by the State Board of Home Missions, under the management of Geo. S. Sloan, who conducted an academy for advanced students and high school graduates, assisted by his wife, Mrs. Geo. S. Sloan, with merited results.

The established Community Sunday School and church services continued until the attendance was too large for the building's capacity. The Latter-Day Saints organization was under the leadership of the Presiding Elder I. W. Odekirk. He took over the many duties inherited with the office. His very capable wife and help mate, assumed the responsibilities of caring for the sick and needy and comforting the bereaved, and they deserve much merited praise for the noble work they accomplished.

April 25, 1915, another Mile Stone marks the installing of 

Frederick S. Musser as the first Bishop, with Andrew Rogers as first counselor and Delbert Collett as second counselor of the Myton ward. The officers following were: Chas. J. Wahlquist, bishop; with 

J. H. Eldridge. Mr. Eldridge became bishop in June 1923; he was followed by Lynn Stone, August 1938, Loyal Perry, Bishop Harold 

Crapo, and Cyril Dennis. Mrs. Mary Lydell was the first Primary teacher and president, and Mr. Abraham Lydell was the first Sun day School Superintendent. 

The L. D. S. church purchased the old Community Hall, which had been greatly enlarged and improved and owned by a Stock Company of original subscribers and promoters. This building was soon outgrown and sold, and new quarters were purchased in the Opera House which later was burned to the ground by an overheated stove. A new Church house was built on the same block at approximately $15,000.00 It is a modern, well equipepd Church for various functions, social gatherings, intellectual lectures, motion pictures, and program depicting character development. 

Myton's first water works came in the form of a flood, which change the course of the Duchesne river at the big bend, leaving

the bridge high and dry, making fording the river a dangerous necessity, and the result was the drowning of a Spanish sheepherder and a negro mail driver, who were buried about where they were found, and only a board slab marks, their resting place. 

The Indian Department Survey and Construction crews were virtually marooned from their activities, so much so that the County Commissioners were contacted in the person of our local representative, John Moffit of Boneta, Utah, and our political advisor D. M. Frost . . . resulting in a telegraph message being sent to U. S. Senator Reed Smoot from Utah, then in Washington. Ten days later a reply was received by Mr. Frost, announcing the passing of a bill appropriating $25,000.00 from the Indian fund, but afterward was assumed by the State of Utah for the new Steel Bridge. 

After the new bridge was completed, a grand celebration was arranged, and various Program Committees from most of our neighboring towns were invited to take part in the program and they responded to a man.

The Guests of Honor and speakers of the occasion were: 

Governor W. H. Spry, Hon. Senator Reed Smoot, and Pres. Wm.

H. Smart of the L. D. S. church, and local celebrities of neighboring towns. Many eats and the trimmings were evidence of the grand time at the celebration.

A very creditable tabernacle had been erected under the beautiful Cottonwood trees, with a good dancing floor made of hardwood, and dancing wound up the end of the day's festivities. 

A grand parade was formed by the Marshal of the day, headed by an impromptu band escorting the honorable guests, followed by many well designed and picturesque floats, Indian horsemen and cow ponies galore, taking their part from the Bridge to the speakers stand.

An excellent program was well rendered in the forenoon of the celebration, after which a frontier day of rodeo stunts were pulled off to suit the most critical observer, lasting until nightfall when dancing started and lasted until morning. They were all here and even now they remember its fun and frolic and enjoyment. 

The new Bridge stands and carries its ponderous loads of today's traffic. The celebrators came to visit, and a few stayed on to fill out their vacation. We still have the souvenir button with a picture of the bridge, a real utility marking a great achievement under great difficult conditions.

The first airplane to visit the Basin that we know of, landed on August 21, 1919, on the south Myton bench, where a strip of land had been prepared. It was a bomber of the U. S. Air Force commanded by Lt. C. C. Nutt who had been advertised, but was delayed for several days. The City, not daunted by delay, prepared to entertain the crowd each day with ball games and Rodeo and

MYTON 

307 horse racing, getting better each succeeding day until the plane finally dropped out of the sky to the greeting of the immense crowd. The mayor and his board of city dads entertained the "bomber" crew at luncheon which the ladies had prepared. 

The asphaltum ore of this particular section, is commonly known as Gilsonite, named so, for Mr. Sam Gilson, the discoverer in early prospecting days.

Gilsonite of Myton district a good source of production for shipping, is in good demand as material for paint, varnish, roofing, and building paper, electric wire covering, and most kinds of roofing paint, also valuable for timber, under-ground installation, and many plastic gadgets in electrical use.

Mr. Gilson discovered the valuable useful product about 1878 in Emery County and later in many places in the Uintah Basin where he experimented with it in various ways and was instrumental in widening its scope of usefulness.

Mr. Gilson, a picturesque individual, who by his personality and love of adventure, keen insight into the secrets of nature, and a product of frontier life, was able to sell his usefulness to our government to a position of U. S. Marshal, retired later to live in Salt Lake City, Utah. 

There are three well known deposits, namely: The Dead Sea, in Palestine area; a lake of asphalton on the Trinidad Island, a 

British Possession in South America; and our vast tonnage in the 

Uintah Basin near Myton, Utah. Much benefit has been derived from this industry.

The Uintah Power and Light Company's activities and definite installations gave good prospects for power and light for industries, especially for the new flour mill of 100 lbs. capacity per day. 

The mill was constructed, being rated as equal to any other in the state for high grade flour and many by-products, and at that time was shipped to outside points. The year 1914 marks the use of both power and light at Myton, used for lights and pumping plant; later its line was extended to the Parriett Mine and also to the 

Catsle Peak Mine southeast of Myton.

Myton installed the first pumping water system, and thru its service attracted much growth and wide range of development. 

Many residents were stockholders of the Power and Light and were influential in its final success. 

The climatic conditions and lack of water for farming, occasioned by poorly constructed canal systems, caused poor crops of alfalf hay and showed tendency to produce seed, and in many instances an exceptionally high quality seed was produced. 

When sufficient water was available, there was an over supply of hay grown for the country's demand, and a market must be found for products that could be shipped to outside points at a

profit. These products were: butter, cheese, alfalfa seed, sweet clover seed, and honey. The producer, the honey bee in fertilizing the alfalfa bloom, produced the seed.

Thus a good combination of cash crops were established, that for a term of years gave us a fairly stable return. Such crops, when properly cultivated and cared for, make a good winner and also leave a by-products of value for feed.

Corn was also extensively experimented with, and not a little success was achieved. It was used as a concentrate, the bulk was used for range sheep, which were numbered in the thousands. The winter supply of corn was shipped from Kansas and Nebraska, the freight of which was no small item. 

Under these conditions, Myton became a recognized Alfalfa Seed center for production and recleaning and marketing. Through the efforts of the Highland Farm Bureau, plans were started for recleaning alfalfa and sweet clover seed, from which a local committee was sent to establish ways and means to get a seed cleaning plant. The committee contacted the Western Seed and Marketing 

Company, and from this venture resulted the Uintah Basin Seed Growers Association in Myton, and its buildings, together with the 

N. L. Peterson and George H. Tingley recleaning plants known as the High.

THE CAREY ACT LAND 

The Carey Act Land, as it was known, lay to the southeast of Myton on the drainage to the east into Green River and was separate from the Indian Reservation, and was considered Myton's development. It was created by an act of Congress, on contract basis: wherein the person, company, or corporation received the rights from the Federal Government to procure water rights from the state, to build canals, or reservoirs, and any legal rights necessary to convey water from Rock Creek and Duchesne River from its point of filing, to the land in question, the price of which was determined by the Reclamation Service, the promoter to retain every other alternating unit as his own property, also regulated by an act of Congress. 

The land was reported on favorably by Engineer Dubenstine, as entirely feasible. The water filing for this track of land was made by Mr. Frank Lott of Denver, Colorado, and stimulated by President Wm. H. Smart, who it is said, worked tirelessly for its success against tremendous odds. It was thought by many and expressed by few, that its failure was due to grazing, mining, and water filing interests at a point further up the Duchesne River.

The water filing for this tract was cancelled or expired on 

November 30, 1916, and with it went 496 second feet of water filing.

The new town of Myton had the following merchants: Calvert and Waugh, Merchantile; Myton Opera House; Myton Hotel,

The year 1917, the South Myton Canal became a reality and was the occasion for great rejoicing, "Something attempted, Something done." They earned a day to celebrate.

At present there comes the notice of a move to get a Railway through Myton, from Price to Vernal, Utah, which seemed based on the immense tonnage of coal, oil and gilsonite from the Uintah Basin. This move seems to have considerable merit. 

Myton's greatest loss and lasting stigma was its disastrous fires, where in a few cases, it appears that fires were of incendiary origin that insurance might be collected, thus destroying the moral status of its institutions. Openly expressed opinion concerning events at that time were anything but complimentary.

The suspension of business at the Myton State Bank on August 1922 was another drawback. The cause of its failure was not so important as its effect upon business at that time. It was a very embarassing position for its patrons, stockholders and depositors.

Business generally was at a standstill, and new contact had to be made with other financial institutions, making or causing much delay and confusion. 

The Duchesne Bank suspended on March 10, 1921 caused some delay in business in Myton.

The death of B. L. Dart in August, 1922, precluded the possibility of continuing and refinancing of the Myton State Bank, and it was put in the hands of a receiver. 

The First National Bank of Myton, opened January 8, 1919, existed, on paper only, a few months and contributed only trouble and was liquidated by a receiver with only liabilities. 

The following is a list of conditions and events against our city's growth:

Slow development of the New Hope District bonds and loss of water rights; the return of unsold town lots to Indian Tribal 

Relations Committee; sale and removal of the Upalco Flour Mill about 1926; many fires; and loss of liquor revenue due to the passage of the Dry Law in 1916, passed State Legislature in 1917. 1

MYTON 

311 It is almost unbelievable, the Herculean tasks accomplished when we recount the attempts made, converting the untamed wilds of sagebrush and greasewood flats, boulder strew hummocks of washes and gullies, to smooth out nature's attempts of leveling, correcting, and shortening these winding trails of passage; just the ground work for the great system of canals and highways. What required many days for a trip to Salt Lake City, can now be done in a few hours.

Myton is the rugged example of various groups of people of different teachings and modes of living, working together in a common undertaking, with a high degree of harmony and tolerance for each other's point of view.

The first settlers came from all vocations and trades; a few had some means but mostly betting youth against odds to make it on self-built success. They were thrown with Danite characters of all frontiers life, who go for excitement and change, also the Riff Raff of Boom Settlement, who expect a quick grub stake or sell out and go to greener pastures, while more stable ones carry on to build a home and future.

With all successes, or failures, from whatever source, Myton is the most favorable and natural site for development of industry or something big. We vision it best as a place for a beet sugar industry, with a Railway, and surrounded by immense tracts of fertile soil especially adapted to its culture; a sugar factory, byproducts for feeding and finishing livestock; many processing plants such as phosphate for fertilizer from the immense deposits of phosphate rock in our near by hills.

The History of Myton would be incomplete without the mention of its medical staff, in which the staff from Fort Duchesne was of great help, as was the government hospital where many were taken for treatment. In the early days only home remedies were available until a patient could be taken to Vernal. 

Dr. Bjorensen was probably the first practicing physician to locate at Myton, after which many came and went, in fact quite a few located here, including Dr. Brown, a dentist; Dr. Thomas, a man of medicine and surgery; Dr. E. R. Enoch, who was here for a considerable number of years and considered among the best of doctors; 

Dr. Nule, and a dentist, Dr. Watking. Special mention is made of 

Dr. Lurrine Miles and Dr. J. D. Whitmore, who served our citizens faithfully for many years.

NEOLA Contributed by Ruth B. Jensen NEOLA HE TOWN OF NECLA lies at the base of the Uintah Mountains at an elevation of 6,017 feet. Neola is ten miles north of 

Roosevelt, one of the Uintah Basin's largest settlements. 

Whiterocks and Hayden are east of Neola, with Monarch some four or five miles west. When this section of the country was opened for white settlers, the entire country was covered with tall sagebrush and junipers. Hundreds of unbranded wild horses roamed here, at will. There were so many of them that the first settlers had to fence their lands before they could raise crops. At present there are no wild bands of horses left.

The contour of this valley is sloped in a southernly direction. 

This section of the Basin was originally an old river bed where the Uintah river ran at that time. The rocky gravel soil formation gives evidence of having been covered with water at some time in the history of this country. Just north of Neola is the distinct outline of a prehistoric canal, that shows high understanding of surveying. 

There is other evidence of some civilization being here before the white man came.

The Ute Indians originally owned most of Neola. Much of the land is owned and controlled by the Utes and the Ute Government 314

NEOLA 

315 at the present time. A few Indian families, Jim Atwine, Patsy 

Bolliver, Clark Tanner, John Starr, Wash, Blackhawk, Martin Van and others, have continued to live here among the Whites and generally speaking are good "mind-your-own-business" Indians. The 

Indian owned land is a decided handicap to Neola's expansion. This land is non-taxable and where leased by Whites very little improvement has been made.

Several families took up homesteads in and around Neola when the Reservation was opened up for settlement in 1905-1906. Enoch and James F. Woodward, Frank Winn, Wm. Greenhall, Joseph 

Peterson, Nute Sheldon, Samuel Peterson, Clifford Bryant, Roger 

Horrocks, Luther Moore, Peter Duncan, John Houston, John Fuell, Charles Hutcheon, Dave Peterson, W. A. Kone, W. E. Glines, T. N. 

Dodd, George Yack, Nephi Rhoades, Geo. Averett, Sam Barnhurst, Judd Mitchell, Ben Wilkerson, Jesse Peterson, Jabez Taylor, Peter 

Peterson, J. O. Peterson, Barbara Smithson and family, Ed Sumner and many others took up homesteads. 

There was a small town started two miles east of the present Neola townsite by the name of Packer, in 1908. Here in Packer 

School House in the spring of 1911, a special meeting was held

Forty acres of land was purchased from Sam Ponowitz (Indian) through the U. S. Government, and was surveyed and plotted as a townsite by Nile Hugel, a civil engineer of Vernal, Utah. This survey was made on April 20 and 21, 1912. W. A. Kone chose the name Neola, derived from an old Indian word meaning "The Last 

Move" or "Move No More." During the fall of 1912 the Packer school house was moved to Neola in time to start school October 12, 1912. This was the first building in Neola. Miss Alice Clark and 

Mrs. Charles Bennett were Neola's first school teachers. Miss Clark had a log house built (on lot four, block three) by early spring of

1913. The following summer James Barnes was appointed Postmaster of Neola and built a small lumber building adjoining his amusement hall on the south. The first mail service was at Hayden where people went to send and receive mail. In 1912, mail was brought by Dell Searle from Cedarview to Whiterocks. As Mr.

Searle went through Neola enroute to Whiterocks, he left mail on the corner (where Neola Trading Co., stands at present) in small personal sacks tied to several posts which people had placed there for their mail boxes. One early settler, James Rhoades, told the writer of this history, that half of the townspeople gathered at this corner each day to receive their mail. Mr. Searle also carried dentist's equipment and pulled teeth for people. He also told of how the children grouped together to play games and visit. 

By 1915, there were 38 families in Neola with a population of 200 people. Mr. McNeil Duncan, the son of Mr. and Mrs. Peter 

Duncan, was the first white male child born in Neola, and Neola Wilkerson, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John Wilkerson, was the first white girl.

This same year Leslie Allen built and operated the first general store on the same block with the amusement hall. Prior to this time 

Whiterocks was the main trading post. With poor roads and heavy wagons as the only means of conveyance, it was a long cold ride to Whiterocks and back for a little coal oil, sugar, flour, bacon or tea. 

Fruit was a luxury to these early settlers. Wild berries, choke cherries, bull or buffalo berries, sarvis berries and rhubarb were the only fruits available, unless the settlers drove 50 miles to Vernal for plums, apricots and apples.

When the Wilford Oberhansley family moved to Neola in 1912, from Payson, Utah, they brought an abundance of bottled fruit with them. When the townspeople learned of the fruit, they brought flour, water buckets, tin milk pans and any other household effects they could spare to trade for the fruit. So many came to trade that Mrs. Oberhansley had to refuse to part with any more of their precious fruit.

Many of the sturdier men of the valley drove freight wagons to Price for household supplies. This trip usually took ten days or two weeks depending upon weather conditions. Sometimes the whole community was out of flour before some freighter returned. 

An amusement committee was chosen by the townspeople with 

Samuel T. Peterson (chairman), Eliza Averett, James D. Mecham and Sophia Hardy as helpers. The first public dance was given on 

July 24, 1913. Before this date all social gatherings were held in private homes or the school house. Peter Peterson played his "fiddle" for square dances and was the only music at that time. After the erection of the amusement hall by James Barnes, picture shows

NEOLA 

319 (silent), roller skating and regular weekly dances were the main recreation, together with baseball, foot and horse racing. The Indians had excellent horses and keen competition was exercised between
