the Whites and his Red Brothers. Home dramatics soon became popular with occasional theatres managed and directed by John 

Burgess and Ruth Blazzard (a school teacher). Some delightful and entertaining productions were produced and enjoyed by the surrounding townspeople who came from far and near to enjoy a drama. With usual admittance prices at 25c and 50c per ticket, some of these plays netted over $100.00 per night. 

The first orchestra of home talent was composed of German 

B. Workman (violin), Pearletta Taylor (violin), Janez Taylor (bass violin), Richard Beeler (accordian) and 

Beeler (accordian) and Sadie Burgess (piano). In 1916 a Brass Band was organized with Judd Mitchell (coronet leader), T. V. Kjelstrom (trombone), Rafael Winn (coronet) Lyle Morgan (snare drum), James Barnes (bass drum), S. T. Peterson (bass horn) and David Smithson at the piano.

July 4th and 24th were fittingly celebrated each year with parades (Indians included) patriotic programs, competitive sports, ball games, wild horse races, foot and horse races, children dances with pop corn, pink lemonade and occasionally ice cream. Public dances in the evenings to which everyone, old and young came. 

Rows of benches were covered with quilts and sleeping children too small to be left at home. But the spirit of good fellowship and neighborliness made these events still remembered by these early settlers.

In writing of industries of pioneer days of this locality we must bear in mind that we are still in pioneer conditions in many ways. For instance, home butchering of hogs and cattle, improvised machinery, rendering of fats, soap making, sewing, rug weaving and making the family clothing is prevalent in all homes and farms. 

In those first few years of Neola's settlement and struggle for existence, medicines and doctors were strangers to the settlers. The nearest doctor was the Indian Agency doctor at Whiterocks. Home concocted herbs and salves were the only remedies used. More people were cured through simple faith than through medicine or doctor's cure. Special tribute should be paid to one of our first settlers, Mrs. Wilmer Burgess (Jane Heath), a midwife, who gave of her service day and night untiringly to answer the call of those who were ill and in need, by horseback, over unmarked trails, in sunshine or snow until she was over 70 years of age. She helped at the birth of several hundred babies and helped nurse the mother and care for the families in each instance. Her regular fee for such service was $3.00 but too often the people were too poor financially to pay her at all. She often had to wash clothing before it could be used for the new born babies. “Grandma" Burgess lived to be 92 years old.

Communication with outside towns and cities was inadequate as were all other accommodations. However, mail routes together

NEOLA 

321 with telephone service to Price, Colton and Watson were established by 1915 and 1916. T. T. Wilkerson installed the first telephone in 

Neola. 

The highways (or roads) were in such pioneer condition that one of the first settlers (Peter Duncan) told of it taking him all day and until nearly morning to come from Roosevelt to Neola, a distance of ten miles, in March of 1907. When the townspeople went to Roosevelt for Church Conferences and special entertainments, someone hitched a team to a wagon taking as many as could ride. Sometimes lunch was prepared and eaten enroute as it would take all day to make the trip down and back. In 1917, the county hired some of the local men with their teams to turnpike 

1-First band-James Mecham, Lionel Jensen, Syril Fitt, John Yack, Samuel T. Peterson, G. B. 

Workman, Elmer Richmond, Mr. Morley;2-First July 4 celebration; 3-First July 24 celebration;

the road between Neola and Roosevelt. Then in 1935-1936, the road was graveled, making it passable the year around for cars, trucks and school busses. It was not until 1918 that W. E. Glines, George 

Averett, John Fuell, Ed Gardner, Thomas Smith and Chris Jensen had the first Ford cars in Neola. 

The first families of Neola had very trying hard times. When in need of money they had to seek employment from different irrigation projects. Mainly from the Indian department, which started construction of two large canals through this country in 1903. Each farmer raised enough of each kind of foodstuff for his family. There were no markets for farm products except cattle, which were driven to Heber City or Colton, the nearest railroad center. The homesteaders had to file on water from the Uintah 

River. They had to build their own separate canals and irrigation projects and accept secondary water rights, which proved to be very unsatisfactory in later years, when they found that the Indian Department would use all the water in dry seasons. The Independent 

Ditch Co., the T. N. Dodd Irrigation Co., and the Dry Gulch

Irrigation Co., enlarged one of the Indian Department canals which was owned and operated by the White settlers. 

About the only industries established in and near Neola were saw mills and lime kilns. T. T. Wilkerson had a shingle and saw mill in Pole Creek, north of town. The Indian Department owned a saw mill in the Uintah Canyon in 1912, but later moved it to the head of Dry Gulch and from there to Johnny Star Flat. Just recently this sawmill as been hauled to Fort Duchesne and sold for junk. I. N. Workman, in 1912, owned the lime kiln located fifteen miles north of Neola.

Leslie Allen started the first store in Neola in 1915. T. T. 

Wilkerson bought staple groceries and had a store in a tent on the Alice Clark lot. Henry Peterson, Mr. and Mrs. E. N. Bullard each built and operated stores during the years from 1916-1922. 

Hugh Barnes built a large general merchandise store in the summer and early fall of 1920, but was only in business a short time when fire completely destroyed his business. During this same year, Thomas 

Smith bought the Leslie Allen store and later sold it to L. D. 

Gardner in 1923. In 1925 Lionel Jensen and L. D. Gardner bought the T. T. Wilkerson store and combined it with the stock from the Leslie Allen store under the name of Neola Trading Co. Lionel 

Jensen bought out the Gardner interest and continued in business until April, 1946 when he sold to Lorin Mills. G. B. Workman, John Yack, Esther Powell, M. R. Mitchell and I. N. Workman have each at short intervals operated stores in Neola. Paul E. 

Nelson built a store and stocked it with an almost complete line of groceries, meats and hardware that he operated for several years, but later sold out to Lionel Jensen. In the past year, 1946, Ivan

NEOLA 

323 Bastian has built a store just 14-mile east of the Neola Trading Co. 

C. J. Neilson built the first creamery in Neola in 1921. Later the Mountain and Western States Creamery Co., built a modern, brick creamery which was destroyed by fire in 1940. Neola now boasts of a new up-to-date plant owned and operated by Calder Brothers of Vernal, Utah. This plant is one of the most modern 

CASE 

1-First steam thrasher; 2-First school with its first class.

in the West and is managed by Thurman Peterson. 

In 1921, electric facilities were installed in Neola by the Uintah Power and Light Co. At the present time nearly every home in Neola has electric lights and power. The ranches and farms are being furnished power and lights by the R. E. A. 

During 1934 and 1935, Neola with the aid of a grant under the W. P. A. projects, piped water from a spring 3/4 of a mile north of the town. The cost was eight thousand dollars in money and one thousand dollars in donation from the citizens of the community. It has proven very satisfactory, furnishing plenty of good culinary water and making it possible for people to modernize their homes. 

Mr. and Mrs. Charles Hutchean.

William A. Wilkerson built and maintained a blacksmith shop, just north of Neola Trading Co., for many years. He was considered a first class repair man and plow shear sharpener. He is credited with putting shoes on heavy work horses and half broke saddle ponies with skill equalled by none.

LeGrande Gardner has owned and operated a sawmill on the 

Pole Creek Mountain several miles north of Neola for many years. It is from this mill that most of the building material for local homes has been sawed. The townspeople have purchased permits for trees and hauled them to the Gardner sawmill for the natieve lumber. Glade Davis has a sawmill at his farm at the present time, where much lumber is surfaced and planed. For several years, between 1925-1930, Dick Spencer had a pool hall in one of the original school buildings, which he opened for recreation for the men of Neola. 

The Yack Brothers have a honey industry that has furnished choice honey all over the United States and is recognized as superior on any market. 

In 1946, Albin Peterson of Salt Lake City, installed a paint and wallpaper business in the Paul Nelson building. 

Turkey and chicken industries deserve considerable recognition in writing of businesses and industries in Neola. Jos. Duncan,

NEOLA

Arthur Hutcheon, Ira A. Pace, Arlando Pace, Alex Hutcheon, Lorin 

Mills, Jos. Yack, Mrs. Charles Lynn, Thurman Peterson and B. A.

Kimball are a few of the larger producers of eggs in Neola. 

Mention should be made of the thresher and harvester business here also. James O. Peterson told the writer of this history that he could remember when his father, Peter Peterson, cradled three acres of wheat and bound it because there were no grain binders at that time. The first thresher in Neola was brought here by Wilford 

Oberhansley in 1912. This was operated by steam. Other thresher outfits were horsepower operated. Several companies, comprised of the farmers of the locality, have large combines and threshing co-operations. 

Bishop Lionel Jensen who has been prominent in Neola's development. He has served two long periods as Bishop, owned and operated a general store, and has been on the County Board of Education. 

He is also a stock raiser and farmer. 

The dairy business in Neola is the main money-making concern.

Nearly every farmer has a herd of 

Holstein cows and pastures to keep them. Modern dairy barns are to be seen on the farms of the largest milk producers, with all modern equipment installed.

Schools and opportunities for higher learning have been a handicap to Neola. The nearest colleges are Price, Utah, and Provo, Utah. In 1918 a school house, built of red brick, with two class rooms, large hall and two coat rooms, was erected at a cost of four thousand dollars. School was taught in two other buildings just east of the new school house and four by 1919-1920 there were teachers with grades ranging from the 1st to the 9th inclusive. Mr. 

Hugh Barnes was our first school board member from this school precinct. In 1923, the Duchesne County School District established a school bus route from Monarch to Neola to Roosevelt. The students of high school age were taken to Roosevelt. By 1927, a larger four-roomed school house was erected 1/4 mile east of Neola Trading 

Co., store. This building cost nineteen thousand dollars. In 19361937 under a P. W. A. project, a spacious gymnasium was added to the school building with an added cost of ten thousand dollars, with showers, toilets, stage and athletic equipment, under William

Crozier as foreman. This building was perfected while Lewis E. 

Allred was a school board member of the Neola precinct in Duchesne County. There are beautiful trees, lawns and white picket fence together with swings, slippery slides, tennis court and other recreational facilities. Neola boasts of one of the best lunch rooms in the 

County and has served lunch to school children daily since the project was started in 1937. Mrs. Lovina Roberts has been head cook during these years. 

Neola School. 

In 1930, a community club was started by the Duchesne County Demonstrator, Miss Alice Peterson. This club was open to all women regardless of age and proved a valuable organiaztion to our town. Mrs. Clara M. Olsen acted as the first president. The women were taught better homemaking, better homes and gardens. 

This club has sponsored many worthwhile projects such as donating money to help fence the front of the Ward House grounds, under the leadership of Lillian Peterson. While Mrs. Sarah Crozier was president, a water system was installed in the cemetery grounds which the Ward had previously purchased from the Joseph Peterson estate. This has made it possible for the people to keep the cemetery beautiful with lawns and flowers. Under the presidency of Mazella 

Gardner, the club sponsored the purchase and erection of a picket fence for the school grounds. 

In case of death of one of our citizens, it has been customary, until just recently, to call in the Relief Society Presidency and Bishopric of the ward. They in turn would appoint someone to care

NEOLA 

327 for the body and sit up day and night putting ice packs, if necessary, around the body. The grave would be dug and prepared by volun-

teer labor. The clothes made by the Relief Society members, and the mourners fed and cared for by the community. The casket was made by local carpenters. T. T. Wilkerson and Dennis J. Mitchell made most of the caskets and they were very beautiful and substantial at a cost of $25 to $40 each.

One cold night a casket had to be made for a funeral the next day. T. T. Wilkerson, Mr. R. Mitchell, Walter Wimmer and 

Lionel Jensen volunteered to have it ready. After working nearly all night in weather about 20 below zero, they had it finished. An argument came up about it being too small for the corpse, so Lionel Jensen, being about the same size as the deceased person, got in the casket while the others tried on the lid to see if it would fit. Walter Wimmer sat on the lid and declared it a perfect fit. 

However, Lionel was very glad to get out, after some struggling, since it fit so tight.

In the year of 1939 and 1940, M. R. Mitchell built a large garage and service station across the street south of Neola Trading Co. At the present time, it is owned by the Chevron Oil Company and operated by Tal M. Wardle. Olaf Duncan owns and operates a Service Station and garage just east of the Neola Trading Co. 

In 1921, Gerilda J. Wilkerson took over the management of the Post Office and moved to the T. T. Wilkerson store. In 1925, the Post Office was moved back to the Wilkerson residence. Mrs. 

Wilkerson was postmistress for 15 or 20 years. When Mrs. Wilkerson retired, her daughter Rose Malnar moved the Post Office to its present site in 1936 and is postmistress at the present. 

Neola Church of Jesus Christ of Latter Day Saints was organized into a branch of the Hayden Ward, Duchesne Stake on 

July 11, 1915, with John A. Olsen as Presiding Elder. One year later the branch was made a Ward with J. A. Olsen as Bishop. At this meeting on September 17, 1916, there were forty members of the Church present. At the present time, the Latter Day Saints 

Church is the only one in the community with ward population of 506. There are several Catholic families who attend religious services in Roosevelt. In 1925, Under Lewis E. Alred as Bishop, the Ward purchased James Barnes' amusement hall for Ward purposes. It was torn down in 1927 and work began on a new Ward House at a cost of twenty-five thousand dollars. Through the untiring efforts of the women of the community, some of the names were: Emma 

Oberhansley, Palmera Peterson, Bernetta Duncan, Mary Crozier, and Mazella Gardner, were chosen to take charge of planting shrubs and flowers. Together with the men, under leadership of 

Chairman Lionel Jensen, the grounds were prepared by soil hauled from north of town 3 miles, leveled, landscaped, lawns planted, shrubs, trees and flowers were put around the Church grounds at

Public. 

A forty acre tract of land was purchased by the Latter Day Saint Church in 1927, just south of Neola townsite, part of it was surveyed into town lots, and the balance was retained for a public park. A half-mile race track was started under supervision of Bishop 

William K. Dye. Donation labor, amounting to several thousand dollars, was done by the men and boys of the community. Two large corrals, ball diamond, and a large grandstand were built in 1946 with William Crozier as foreman, at a cost of $1200 to $2500 with seating capacity of 300 or more. Water has been piped from the City water system to the park and two fountains built. The 

Daughters of Utah Pioneers have planted trees, black willow, Russian olive, and Siberian elm, together with rose bushes. The park is still in need of lawns and more shade trees before it will meet the needs of the community.

The Daughters of Utah Pioneers were organized at Neola 

September 26, 1933, with County Captain Ella Young of the Roosevelt camp presiding and organizing the new camp. Pearletta T. 

Workman was the first camp captain and Rose W. Malnar secretary and treasurer. The charter members besides the two named above were: Elmina Whiting, Amanda Duncan, Emma L. Wimmer, Mary 

Sweeney, Ruth Jensen, Ada Hutcheon, Lois Duncan, Lillian Peterson, Jennis Roberts, Mae Arbuckle, Zora Peterson, Leona Wilkerson, Girilda Johnson Wilkerson, Clara M. Olsen, Rilda W. Horrocks,

Rhia Duncan, Lecta Horrocks and Zina Mecham. 

In the past thirteen years the organization has grown to a membership of thirty-eight with twenty-eight paid up registered members with twenty-five members paying their memorial building fees. Clara M. Olsen is credited with being the first daughter in

Duchesne County to pay her Memorial dues. The Daughters of

NEOLA 

331 Neola Camp have sponsored many worthwhile projects in our community. On July 24, 1935 the daughters had charge of the celebration. A large parade with twenty-two floats was the first issue of the day. A "Utah" float took first prize with Girilda J. Wilkerson as 

Utah and Emma L. Wimmer, Mary Crozier, Amanda Duncan, Martena Jensen, Valina Keele and Mame Gardner as her maids.

These women were the oldest members of the D. U. P. at this time. 

The gate receipts and concessions were at a total of $233.93. At the present time the Daughters have two projects under way: an outdoor fireplace on the public park grounds, and the mounting of Neola's first bell on the Church property. 

In the past forty years Neola has grown from a small scattered town of half a dozen log cabins with dirt roofs to a thriving community of many beautiful modern homes, with spacious lawns, shrubs, flower, cement walks, lily ponds, out-door living rooms and fireplaces, ornamental fences, and cement paths. Most of our farms have modern dairy barns, chicken houses, and machine sheds. Neola is still growing.

November 6, 1923, the first organization of Daughters of Utah 

Pioneers was effected with Sophia May as captain; Olive Boberg, first vice-captain; Effie Jacobs, second vice-captain; and Lorina

Anderson, secretary-treasurer. June 1924, a delegation attended the 

State Centennial Convention. They were Donna Burgess, Martha J. 

Edwards and Alta Phillips. The slogan for this meeting was "Sell

yourself to Pioneer Work." Donna Burgess reported directions on filling membership blanks; Martha Edwards stated that in writing the histories of progenitors, the joyful as well as sorrowful events should be included; Alta Phillips reported on secretarial work. 

Since that time the organization has grown from eight members to forty. Meetings are held every fourth Friday monthly, at three o'clock in the members' homes. At these gatherings, outlined lessons are given and various business is discussed. Songs are part of the meetings, also; this Camp has 15 Pioneer song books for use at meetings, and to prepare special numbers. The group is actively participating in Centennial affairs, having staged two successful old-time dances, complete with costumes, and they plan for others during the year.

The Roosevelt Camp has placed the first school bell used in this locality in the City Park, as an exhibit. The bell was purchased for the school in 1907, and in December 1915, the town employed it as a curfew and fire alarm. Replaced by a modern fire siren in 

March, 1929, the old bell lay discarded until July 1945, when the 

D. U. P. placed it on exhibition.

The present officers of the Roosevelt Camp (1947): Lila 

Nickell, captain; Virgie Murphy, first vice-captain; Clara Timothy, second vice-captain; Olive Harrison, secretary and treasurer; Myrtle 

Labrum, historian; Alta Phillips, chaplain; Martha Edwards, registrar; Elsie Taylor, custodian of relics; Tina Wilkins, chorister; and Essie Alexander, organist.

ROOSEVELT Written by Donna Burgess and Mildred Dillman 

The material has been gleaned from a "myriad" of sources. 

Most of the old pioneers have been consulted on every issue. Newspapers from the files of Mildred Dillman have furnished much of the quoted material. Whenever a fact could be established by something written during the time the event transpired, it has been accepted as authentic.

Tina Wilkens, Sam Page, John Redmond, Pearl Redmond, 

Mrs. Halen, Theo Phillips, Eugene and Nick Harmston, Mrs. 

Searle, Lila Nickell, Olive Harrison, J. A. Pack, Mary Orser, Homer 

P. Edwards, Alta Phillips, Clarence L. Ashton, Myrtle Labrum, Gertrude Fredrickson and Elsie Taylor are a few of the people who have helped and we wish to acknowledge their kind contributions. 

Again we kindly remind our readers that this is a brief Early History, and only meager facts could be mentioned due to the space allotted.

ROOSEVELT 

Taken from the preface of the Revised Ordinances of the City of Roosevelt, Utah, which was compiled by Ernest H. Burgess, City Attorney.

OOSEVELT, UTAH IS SITUATED on all or parts of the following original homestead entries: Ed F. Harmston, Frank Orser, Henry Guckert, J. Garnet Holmes, Elroy Wilkins, Dan 

Llyberts, A Birch, and George M. Pickup. The first filing made was the Harmston entry in February, 1906. The first sign of life in the redemption of the arid land was a tent pitched by A. C. Harmston, January 1, 1906, near the present Harmston home, just south of the Occidental seed plant.

In April, 1906, the original townsite of the NW quarter was plotted and named Roosevelt by Mrs. Ed Harmston in honor of 

Theodore Roosevelt. On May 15, 1906, the Dry Gulch Irrigation 

Co. established its headquarters here. Mrs. J. G. Holmes was the first postmistress. The first store was the Roosevelt Mercantile Co., established by Messrs. H. J. Harding, Ward Pack, J. G. Holmes and William H. Smart. On December 24, 1910, the Roosevelt Hall was completed, giving the town the first commodious meeting place for general purposes. The work was done by popular subscription and donations. The Uintah Power and Light Company turned on the first lighting current, July 4th, 1914.

When the new town was established, it was in Wasatch County, but a new county was created out of the eastern part of Wasatch County in 1914. The town of Roosevelt incorporated at a mass 335

"The city of Roosevelt is situated on the lower extremity of a fertile mesa, which extends down from the Cedarview country in the northwest. The east and west branches of the Dry Gulch

Creek form a junction at the foot of this tableland and drains the valley surrounding the city. The town is seven miles due west of the 

Uintah River, and in the exact geographical center of the Uintah Basin. The elevation of the city is 5050 feet and the surrounding valley 5000 feet." 

"Roosevelt is the headquarters of the Dry Gulch Irrigation 

Company organized on December 1, 1905, and is in the center of the territory watered by this company." 

Before any houses were constructed on the site of Roosevelt, Woody Alexander built a cabin a short distance southwest of the town. Levison Hancock hauled materials from Vernal and did the

carpenter work. Indians passing by thought the house was to be a saloon and called it "Whoopee Kahn." John Galloway home in the Hancock Cove was the second cabin built, but it burned down the following spring. Theo Phillips' was the first house built south of Roosevelt. 

As has been stated, the town of Roosevelt occupies part of a 160 acre homestead originally filed on by Ed F. 

Harmston of Vernal, Utah. The Uintah 

Realty Company, composed of Mr. Harmston, Ward E. Pack, and J. G. Holmes were interested in establishing a town on this land. "Eager to see things started, they did not wait until the Harmston homestead was proven up on, but Mr. 

Harmston purchased outright with $750 worth of script, 40 acres of ground which he surveyed and planted during the winters of 1905 and 1906. This become the town of Roosevelt, named by his wife, Mary E. Harmston, who was a great ad mirer of President Theodore Roosevelt."-Roosevelt Standard. 

Ernest H. Burgess, attorney, first high school principal and Bishop. He made a fine contribution to Roosevelt.

In December, 1905, Craig Harmston, Floyd Harmston, known as Nick, and their father, Ed F. Harmston, pitched a tent on the site of the town of Roosevelt. The father returned to Vernal, but the boys stayed during the winter. This tent was the first domicile in Roosevelt.

However, around the townsite many houses were being built on homesteads: Levison Hancock, Ether Hancock, Daniel Hancock, and Ole Oleson-all had houses. When spring came J. B. Harris, Charles Iorg, John Redmond, Bert Henry, Frank Hamilton, Theo 

Phillips and his brother Jake, Pat Miller, John O'Connell, and

William H. Smart, some with their families, had settled in the neighborhood of the town. Later Ward Pack, J. Austin Pack, Pearl 

I. Pack, Joseph Hardy, S. H. Freston, Ben Wilson, Thomas Schisler, Samuel Page, Heber Timothy, Clarence I. Johnson, Andrew

Roberts, Ed Heller and Frank Orser lived near the townsite. 1906: 

Following is a list of the first homesteaders around Roosevelt,

Very few of the original homesteaders remained upon the land. 

Relatives and people who were interested in settling in a new country came. Many of these early timers became part of the civic life of Roosevelt. 

Captain Andrew Whitlock and his family were among those who helped keep order. He had been in the reservation some time before the opening.

Roosevelt-1910. 

townsite, pitched his camp on his property and then built the first house in Roosevelt. It still stands on the southeast corner of the town. George Pickup built the first nice home. "According to an article in the Vernal newspaper, the snow in the winter of 1905-1906 was the deepest it had been on the reservation for years, and those who were living in tents were suffering with the cold. Ed F. Harmston told them to do as the Indians did, 'go to the Cedar tree, break off the branches, rub them up fine, and spread them on the ground under the beds'. After doing so they were able to keep warm."

All the people in and around Roosevelt hauled their culinary water in barrels from Bert Henry's Spring southwest of town on the Frank Hoyt place. There was also a spring of good water west, and one north of town.

In the process of home building fruit trees and trees for ornament and shade were not overlooked. A nursery containing such trees was planted to the southwest of Roosevelt, and a large part of the shade trees came from this nursery stock originally planted by S. P. Dillman on the Elmer Dillman homestead. 

In May, 1906, Frank Orser, J. B. Harris and Nick Harmston sawed the logs, which came from the Colton place in Bennett, to build the Roosevelt Mercantile Company store, the first to be built in the town. The company was organized by J. G. Holmes, Ward 

Pack, Wm. H. Smart and H. J. Harding, who managed it for two years. In 1907, Joseph Hardy took over the management.

ROOSEVELT 

349 

Shurtliff Hotel-Now owned by Mary Beecroft. 

In the summer of 1906, J. U. Page, Wm. T. Page, and Dan 

Hancock hauled some of the logs from Vernal to help build the Mercantile. Willard Anderson hauled the first load of lumber from the mountains to Roosevelt. It was used for the shelves in the 

Mercantile. In the Summer of 1906, J. U. Page supplied the poles for the telephone line between Roosevelt and Neola except the last load which was hauled by L. D. Hamblin. 

During the winer of 1908-09, J. U. Page, Wm. Page, Joseph and Willard Anderson, Ed Heller, and Mr. Wood hauled 250,000 feet of lumber from the Lyberts mill on the Dry Gulch for the Dry Gulch Irrigation Co. They hauled it on bobsleds and delivered it to 

Roosevelt, Ioka, Myton, Bluebell, and Neola. The snow at the crossings was eight feet deep that winter. 

Mr. J. W. Anderson had been on the reservation many times before its opening. He came as early as 1890, as a freighter from 

Price to Vernal with his father. In 1894, he hauled freight from 

Price to Randlett and later via Indian Canyon and Myton brought elaterite from the Basin. In 1896, Willard brought by way of a light wagon, two Mormon missionaries by the names of John Kilpack and John Oliphant. In 1902, he drove one of the famous six teams of horses used to freight the gilsonite of the St. Louis Mine at the 

Strip. 

In the progress of the Mercantile's construction, the first dance was held in it July 24, 1906. The building was completed in the summer of 1906.

On the south side of the store, Wm. Reynold "switched" for a well which was dug, but no water was found. 

Farther south on the same side of the street, C. J. Johnson opened up a business for the Consolidated Wagon and Machine Company in a one-room, black, tar paper covered building. 

The Post Office, a boarded-up tent, stood on the north side of the Roosevelt Mercantile Company Store. The postmistress was 

Mrs. J. G. Holmes. Later it was moved into the Mercantile with 

Henry J. Harding as Postmaster. 

Beyond the Post Office, across the street north, Ed F. Harmston's "Farm Shop" or Realty Company house was built. It consisted of one room. Recently it was donated by the daughter of Mr. 

Harmston, Mildred H. Norlan, to the Roosevelt Camp of the 

Daughters of the Pioneers. Mr. Harmston and his son Craig, a surveyor, were prominent in early civic affairs of the town. 

North of the Farm Shop stood the boarded-up tent saloon called "Rough Riders Saloon," of Heber Campbell. He built a dance hall just north of his saloon, and it served as a recreation hall for the town. George Brown managed both places for Mr. Campbell. 

Those buildings were located where the Onyx Hotel now stands. 

Campbell afterwards leased his saloon to McCarty and Allred for a year, then he sold it. Finally, it served as a storehouse for 

Ashton's Hardware until the merchandise was moved into the Birch Building across the street. The dance hall became the Jin Mease 

Drug Store.

In 1907, Robert Marshall and Caleb Sprouse ran a meat market in their one-room brick building across the street east from the Mercantile Company store. Some distance to the north of this shop stood the Dry Gulch Company office, a small frame building.

Beyond the office north was the Shurtleff Hotel, a tent, boarded up. 

Later Mr. Shurtleff housed his hotel in a small wooden building to which he afterwards added rooms and improvements. At first, he called his hotel the Colorado House.

Mrs. Strang and Mrs. Shurtleff opened a restaurant on the west side of the street.

C. J. Blackhurst built a building to the south which was used as the first High School building.

From 1909 to 1914, several buildings of the business section changed owners, and many new buildings were constructed. C. C. 

Larsen bought the brick butcher shop from Robert Marshall and Caleb Sprouse, and turned it into a general merchandise store. In 

1909, Pat Mackay came from Vernal, purchased the building from C. C. Larsen, and opened it as a barber shop. He added a "lean to" for living quarters for his family. In 1917-18, the Mackey shop became the city jail and remained as such for years.

ROOSEVELT 

353 George O'Niel and Emmanual Swain worked with Pat for many years. They later operated their own shops. 

Leo Young, Carlyle Reynolds, Jack Gleaton, and Jack Harm ston have been the town's best known barbers. Recently Mr. Reynolds has taken his sons into partnership. 

Alta Erickson was the first professional beauty operator. Mrs.

Lester Spurrier, Reatha Harmston, Lorna Oaks, Venice Bascomb, Fay Roller, and Essie Alexander are a few who followed this profession. 

Emanuel Swain, son of Robert Henry and Julia Johnson 

Swain, born Feb. 1, 1884 in Salt Lake City. Moved to Vernal as a boy where he worked with his brothers in the manufacture of brick. Came to Roosevelt in 1908 where he worked with Pat 

Markey as a barber, a trade which he carried on for over fifteen years in Roosevelt. About 1917 he took over the picture show which had been operated by Ebb Simms. Later a new show house was built by a company of Roosevelt business men, which Emanuel and members of his family operated until 1927. Under their supervision the show was named the Utopia. He was interested in music and played in the early day orchestra and band. 

During the summer of 1909, Charles Rich started the first ice cream parlor. George Pierce and Charles Spaulding ran it for him, but it closed its doors in the fall. Then Bert Smith and his brother, Sam, opened up another ice cream shop, located about where Safeway Stores now stands. The parlor was operated until about 

1914, when Bruce Calder started a small business in the place now used as a printing office. Hazel Bircher is the present operator.

A Shoe Shop, ready for business in 1910, was that of Ed Newton, and about 1911, he and Mr. Tucker built a large show house, which he called the New Tuck after his partner. It stood on the east side of the street opposite the location of the Campbell Dance Hall. The building, constructed by Jim Prince, Shumway, S. A. Russell, and J. A. Kelly was used for roller skating, shows, and dancing. It was called the Elan Theatre.

Thilo Zufelt started another shoe repair shop in 1910. However, N. S. Nielson built the first harness and shoe store combined. 

Clare Labrum and Marion Penfield are the present shoe repairers. 

George Pearce and Sol Hancock operated a meat market here and Xavier Bellon bought out Mr. Pearce's interest. Later be built a nice brick building across east from the first Post Office. Menel 

Taylor bought Mr. Hancock's interest. In 1919, Elroy Wilkins and 

Sons purchased the building, added more room, and for years operated a combined grocery store, meat market, and bakery.

East of the N. S. Nielson place was a livery stable built by Joe 

Sims and Charley Elmer in 1910. W. J. Priddy ran it in 1914, after which Less Eldredge operated it.

1-Ephriam Lambert, who was the First Presiding Elder of Duchesne Ward of the Wasatch Stake. He was a councelor to Presidents Smart and Colton and served as Stake Patriarch until his death; 2-Jerimah Murray and Christine. They were original homesteaders and he helped to build the school. He also operated the first thresher: 2-Samuel H. Freston, a first homesteader. His numerous descendents are all prominent in the civic affairs of Roosevelt; 4-Ester and Mollie Smith. 

A deep appreciation is due Ester who contributed freely many of the pictures used in this book; 5-Ed and Joe Pierce; 6-Tom and Bessie Kelsey. Tom was an early freighter; 7-Joseph and Clara Campbell; 8-Racheal Miles; 9-Will Alexander who was here before the opening of the reservation.

Then she moved across the street east from the Roosevelt Mercantile store. Had it not been for the pictures Mr. Smith took of early

She started the first Chili Parlor, Lunch Counter and Bakery. 

At various times she ran a hat shop, a five-and-ten cent store and a laundry. She was always assisted by her daughters, especially Tina 

Wilkins and Ester Lambert. Her daughters and sons have been active in the development of Roosevelt.

A Mr. Pope had the first blacksmith shop, made of five gallon tin cans. In 1906, Gus Fredrickson built his shop and for many years operated it. His sons are still operating a garage and repair establishment. Charley Wallace came a little later but became a very prominent citizen.

Heber Timothy developed a vein of Gypsum and furnished the town for many years with brick burned at his home kiln. He made all of the inner lining blocks for the L. D. S. Church building. 

C. C. Larsen finally settled his department store, hotel and offices on the corner west from the M. E. Harmston Drug Store. 

He built the first and only flour mill in 1909. He and his sons managed it for years, and then he sold it to Mr. Peterson from Duchesne.

William Gagon opened his mercantile store across from the 

Dry Gulch office about this time. 

Leslie Ashton and sons, Clare and Rae housed their hardware store in Roosevelt on April 25, 1913, first in the old Campbell

Saloon building, and then, in the fall of the same year they moved across the street into the north side of the Birch building. Clarence 

Ashton, who later became manager, opened a law office in the upstairs of the same place. He later became mayor. The Ashton 

Hardware has gradually grown into a department store with an added grocery and meat market. Ashtons also run a service station. 

O. H. Bracken and family came from Vernal, Utah about 

1911, and lived on east Main Street by the Dry Gulch Office. This merchant established the Bracken Mercantile in a store across the street. Later, they moved it into their own new brick building on 

Lagoon Street. 

Mr. M. E. Harmston and his family moved into their twostory Drug Store building across the road south from the Shurtleff Hotel about 1910. The living quarters were in the back of the store. Mr. Harmston was a helpful and capable pharmacist during those pioneer times. 

A. Birch, one of the first settlers, opened a furniture store. He later built a brick building. Mrs. Birch was one of the first women of Roosevelt to make good food available at reasonable prices. She

Bracken Mercantile on Lagoon Street. still has the reputation of an excellent cook. Mr. Birch was Mayor of Roosevelt.

Not long after the opening several families moved into Roosevelt. Among them were William Bryce, James Sabey and Horace 

Allred, who operated cattle ranches. Mrs. Bryce and her daughter 

Hazel became outstanding leaders in community and church activities.

Another early merchant was Jake Baron. His store was named "The Golden Rule."

Roosevelt was fortunate from the early years in having good pharmacists. There were M. E. Harmston, Jim Mease, Herb Isham, James Jensen and Robert Delaney. The City Drug Company was established opposite the bank on Lagoon Street. James Jensen and 

Mattie Edwards were the clerks. It moved north of Ashton's with 

Charles and Afton Schwencke as owners. Dave Huish followed 

Mr. Delaney in the Birch Brick Building. Mr. and Mrs. Dave Huish, their son Bob, and Reva, are still doing a thriving business. Charles 

Albright and Mr. McClellan are remembered in later years as 

Pharmacists of Roosevelt.

Dillman had his office north of the Isham Drug store. George 

Stewart is a recent attorney.

It was known as the Roosevelt Banking Company, and was housed in a small building on the north side of the Mercantile Store. An outstanding person connected with the banks of Roosevelt is Mrs. Jessie Orser. She was an employee in the first bank and has been assistant cashier in the present one since its establishment.

Mrs. Will Labrum has contributed the following information about the early saw mills, flour mills and sorghum mills of Roosevelt: 

Before the coming of the settlers who were lucky in drawing a good number in the opening of the reservation, the U. S. Government had started saw mills in the mountains north of Roosevelt, in order to get material for the buildings which were erected at Fort Duchesne. A number of those old saw mills are still standing although many of them have been abandoned.

In 1904, a man by the name of Bowles put the first saw mill in Mosby Mountain. It furnished a great lot of the building material for the first homes here in Roosevelt. It was the first mill besides the government mills in these parts. 

In the right hand fork of the Dry Gulch about twelve miles north of Bluebell is located one of these original government mills, Another almost indistinguishable now, was located in the Uintah Canyon about twenty-one miles north of Roosevelt. In 1909, Al 

Hardinger was operating this mill. While this was being worked, the families of some of the men lived there and under some of the beautiful pine trees just south of the mill site are the graves of two children who died of pneumonia during one of the exceptionally hard winters.

As for the owned mills, the Anderson Brothers, William and John, operated a mill along the Lake Fork River about four miles below Moon Lake, during the years of 1907, 8, and 9. On the left hand fork of the Dry Gulch about ten miles north of Altonah, Robert Krebs, during the opening of the reservation, operated a sawmill. About twenty miles north of Bluebell, Ike Lybert ran a mill in 1906, and furnished materials for many houses, barns and out buildings in Roosevelt. Mr. Seegmiller also operated a mill in the Pole Creek section in 1914. Jim Hards was supervisor of the 

Dry Gulch about twenty-five years ago. This mill was thirty-one miles northwest of Roosevelt. About thirty years ago Jim Johnson worked the sawmill on Mosby Mountain, about thirty miles from Roosevelt. president of the Roosevelt Commercial Club for two terms. He remained active until his death, June 19th, 1941. Nellie M. Wilkins survived him by six years, she died December 30th, 1947. 6-Ed. Evans, who owned the Roosevelt Implement Company. 7-It wouldn't be early Roosevelt without Ross Bartlett; he helped many people in the 1918 influenza epidemic. 8-Art Jacobs, the hunter; 9-Jack McLea, a businessman; 

10-The D. O. Mackay family. Mr. Mackay was a postmaster. His children have held many important positions.

ROOSEVELT 

365 In 1910, C. C. Larsen built and opened for business the flour mill which is now standing in the north eastern part of Roosevelt. 

Since there was not electricity at that time, Mr. Larsen operated his mill with a steam engine, but as soon as the Uintah Power and Light Co. began operations in Roosevelt, he changed to electricity. 

Not long after the Uintah Power and Light Co. began, they opened a mill just a little way from Myton. This mill is known as the 

Upalco Mill. Shortly after this a mill was built at Duchesne. 

In 1915, George Babcock brought a sorgham mill to Roosevelt and a number of people raised sugar cane and had Mr. Babcock grind their cane. One of the heaviest growers at that time was 

Paul S. Hanson who had twenty gallons of sorghum made during this year. Just west of the old Powell Store in Cedarview is still standing one of the old sorghum mills once used by Thomas Roberts. 

He started this in 1913. J. P. Rudy also operated a sorghum mill on 

Indian Bench about four miles north of the highway. 

NEWSPAPERS 

In the early days of Roosevelt, President Smart invited Ezra T. Hyde of Logan to bring his little newspaper plant and join him in founding a newspaper and job printing company. They formed a joint company with Mr. Smart as president and general manager, and Mr. Hyde as vice-president and editing printer. 

Young William Haines Smart was to assist. However the financial side proved non-lucretive, and Mr. Hyde returned to Salt Lake, and Mr. Smart took over the plant bearing considerable loss. 

After this his son, who had been assistant, ran the newspaper 

First threshing machine. Jerry Murray was the first operator.

and conducted the business with Mrs. Rose Hardy. Most of the printing of the paper was done by two women, Anne Brown and Claribel Waite. It was called The Uintah Standard and was published in a little lean-to building joining the Roosevelt Mercantile Company. However, it was discontinued after a few years. In 1914 the newspaper was reorganized as The Roosevelt Standard, with J. P. May as publisher and Arnold Reef as editor. Roosevelt was proud of that paper. Mr. Reef later purchased the Standard. 

The following is taken from the Roosevelt Standard of February 

10, 1915: "Having disposed of the Roosevelt Standard and the business connected therewith to the Roosevelt Standard Company, all accounts owing to said business are payable to the Roosevelt 

Standard Company. 

"Also, having disposed of the Duchesne Record and all business connected therewith to the Duchesne Record Company, all accounts due to the Duchesne Record are made payable to the 

Duchesne Record Company. Any just claims against the former business will be settled by the writer. "In joining the citizens in founding the corporations that have just been inaugurated at Myton, Duchesne and Roosevelt for the purpose of publishing and conducting printing businesses and turn-

William Wallace and George Harrison in 1920 bought the

Roosevelt Standard and incorporated it as the Roosevelt Standard Publishing Company. It became a member of the National Editorial 

Association, and of the Utah State Press Asosciation. His staff were as follows: Violet Harrison, business manager; George H. Harrison, editor; R. H. Harrison, associate editor; and Afton Harrison, feature writer with Mrs. M. J. Edwards, society editor. Several times it received honorable mention by leading newspapers.

DOCTORS 

According to the records and memory of "old timers" Dr. 

Kendall was the first doctor in Roosevelt. However, his stay was short, and no explanation is needed for his departure or that of his successor, Dr. J. W. Padget, who also stood the strain of a pioneer country doctor until August 15, 1914, when he left and finally settled in Kemmerer, Wyoming. 

The following advertisement is taken from the Roosevelt

Standard, Volume 1 No. 2, dated September 5, 1914:

Dr. Morton later purchased the residence of Arch Larsen on

North Main Street, remodeled it into a hospital, and operated it for years. Finally, he moved to Salt Lake City.

Dr. W. J. Browning came from Ogden about 1912. Besides assisting Dr. Morton in the hospital, he opened an office and practiced medicine.

In those days, a messenger-anyone available-would come rushing to the doctor's office or home and ask him "to hurry, please." The messenger might be from Roosevelt or from any community within a radius of a hundred miles or more. He would help the doctor harness a horse to a buggy; they would jump into the rig and ride to the patient. Usually, the doctor returned home a day or two later, if the distance made it necessary, to find other urgent calls. 

Dr. F. A. Graham practiced dentistry until October 1, 1914. 

It is not known when he first opened his office in Roosevelt. Dr. 

E. W. Browning announced his arrival in the Roosevelt Standard of June 10, 1915. He intended to locate here permanently as a dentist, but he left July 19, 1916. Dr. W. F. Walker of Kansas

City succeded him. Dr. Campbell and Dr. R. Q. Warnock continued for many years to practice in Roosevelt. 

The following advertisement first appeared in the issue of the

Roosevelt Standard dated October 19, 1914: 

EYES TESTED J. O. KING, O.D. 

LENS DUPLICATED Affiliated with A. O. A. Association of Utah Optometrists 

Dr. King also moved away after years of active service in his profession and participation in civic affairs. 

A doctor's life is one of hardship and sacrifice even in an 

Early Relief Society in front of the "Brown Palace".

ROOSEVELT 

373 established city or community, where modern apparatus, trained help and hospitalization are at hand, but the conditions that doctors are forced to endure in a pioneer country where homes and farms are isolated, and the distances between. them are miles; where roads are often washed out by floods, or roads are only trails; where no account books can be kept; make their lives supreme sacrifices in service. Such doctors in the history of the Basin are 

Dr. David Paul Whitmore and Dr. Lurrine Miles. 

Dr. William Leonard Sutherland is beloved by the many patients he served during the brief period he practiced here. For a short time, Dr. Montgomery served the people of the Basin during the absence of Dr. Whitmore.

Dr. L. L. Saunders was a doctor that tarried only a brief period. 

He helped very much in the planning of the hospital. He left the 

Basin to enter partnership with Dr. Henderson of Salt Lake City.

These women served in homes of the pioneers as doctors, nurses, cook, and housekeeper. They brought many babies into the world under the most difficult circumstances. They nursed serious ailments with old-fashioned love and care. 

Recognizing the need of a good local hospital the Roosevelt 

Stake Presidency with Ray E. Dillman, President, appointed a committee with Kate Killian, Stake Relief Society President and Dr. 

L. Miles as members to investigate the possibility of getting cooperation from the community if Mr. Dillman could get Church aid; it was through the untiring efforts of these three that the present hospital was planned. William H. Rupple Jr. deserves much credit for obtaining financial aid from civic organizations and serving on the Board of Trustees. 

Our first hospital was run by Dr. Martin in the old Snow home, located near the district school. Our present Roosevelt Hospital was opened October 20, 1944. 1-Weathy Shaffer, born March 13, 1856. She taught school in Wayne county beforebefore going to Mexico where she lived 23 years. While there she nursed and brought babies as she did here in Duchesne county. Final cost equalled approximately $50,000.00.

In the construction and financing, substantial help was given by the Lions Club, Commercial Club and Junior Chamber of 

Commerce. With donations of work and cash from stake and ward members.

Nurses' Aid classes were carried on with the assistance of Dr. 

David P. Whitmore and Dr. L. Miles and supervising nurse, Martha Shanks.

During the 28 months of operation there have been registered for care 4652 patients. There have been 436 babies born. There have been 19 deaths. There has never been a death of an obstetrical case, or surgery case.

During the first year, a group hospital plan insurance was sponsored by the hospital board and under the able supervision of 

Ernest H. Morrison, 825 families were enrolled. On October 15, 1946 the group joined with the Intermountain Hospital Blue Cross Group, and now has a local membership of 945 contract families. 

December 1946, the hospital was presented with additional equipment in the amount of $4975.00 which with the equipment purchased at intervals from hospital funds, makes it an institution where the patient can be assured of excellent care. 

There are now practicing in Roosevelt three efficient doctors who are cooperating in giving our vicinity the best in medical care: 

Dr. Wyler, Dr. Larson and Dr. Bourne. There are now 17 regular

Red Cross Nurses Class organized through the efforts of Mrs. Agnes Ferron. Martha Shanks, head nurse, is beloved by many.

During the first year unselfish service was rendered by the ladies of Roosevelt and vicinity, both in Nurses' aid work and maintenance and keeping up of supplies. 

The Relief Society of Moon Lake Stake have also driven to Roosevelt on occasions to assist in this work. 

The first cemetery was an enclosed spot just below the Harmston Bench, and Jim Barker was the first to be buried there. As soon as the city was incorporated the plot south of town was purchased and Roosevelt has been proud of that little spot and the caretakers. 

Jim Sprouse was the sexton for many years. He was followed by a 

Mr. White, Bowden and Milt Nickell have taken care of it for many years. 

Ralph H. Labrum and Maggie Miller were married August 30, 1910. They were the first couple married in Roosevelt. They were married in the Dry Gulch office by John Strain, who was secretary of the Dry Gulch at that time.

When the town was well established, Earl Mullins started a grocery store on the west side of Main Street. He died shortly after. 

There were several unique industries in the early history. 

Brigham Christensen, Silas Mower, Heber Peterson and the Yaks made Roosevelt famous for honey. It was known as Vernal honey at first but now Uintah Basin Honey is sought by people all over the United States.

Raising of Alfalfa seed became a very profitable industry. The 

Peppard Seed Company erected a large cleaning plant. The Occidental Seed Company also opened an office in the Basin. 

As this is only a brief early history there has been no attempt to include the business concerns of recent date. Space allotted isn't sufficient to include all of them. 

Mayor George H. Harrison did much to improve Roosevelt.

A new administration building was built, roads were paved and sidewalks made. Many things too numerous to include all were added in his long years of service. 

There are a few things that should be recalled to the old timers: Bruce Calder with his mules and the string of cans that made them go; Arnold Reef and his pipe-as he went about the city's work for many long years as treasurer; the many houses that the Timothy boys built; Ren Johnson and the city's water troubles;

ROOSEVELT 

381 Duffy Sturgeon and his cigar; the vacancy of the whole town in deer hunting season; the Labrum boys and their service stations; gathering for mail each morning at the Post Office; Mullins family and their store; Sam Weiss and his hardward; the many creameries; 

Dan Mitchell and his road problems; the old golf course; Ren

Fenn and his marshall work; Dick Scholes and the fine way in which he kept the grade school; Marion Rasmussen and the high school; Wilard Day and Leo Hanson with their creameries; Melba and Leo's back yard; Amelia Daniels and her curio shop; Marion 

Martenson and her store; Costuros; the two nice motion picture show houses operated by the Harrisons; the bakeries; the Garden 

Gate Floral; there are so many more to be included it is difficult to remember that this is really an early history and hope that soon a complete one will be edited upon Roosevelt including its entire development in detail. Seth Wilson and his mail route should be remembered.

MUSIC 

Charles Milligan and wife, and Elisha Campbell with a piano, banjo and violin, formed the dance orchestra for the years 1906, ETIENNE PATRY One of the Old Settlers. 

1907 and 1908. Next came Will 

Taylor's Indian Orchestra, a very popular one. And about this time Pat Markey, a barber, moved into the town. Pat is still alive, and still runs his barber shop, but how he loves musicand he plays many instruments well. He organized the first band and also an orchestra, both of which furnished the town music for many years. Pat wanted very much to teach band music in the high school, and for one year he was employed. His success was assured, but his teaching certificate was lacking, for he had never had an opportunity to attend school. He received no contracte. 

Following Pat Markey, Mr.

Bigley became leader of the high school band, which served as a town band also. About 1911 a seven-piece orchestra was then organized under the direction of 

John Wiscombe, an excellent violinist. He required the members to

attend regular weekly practices, and sometimes they met oftener. The regular weekly practices, and sometimes they met oftener. The membership consisted of the following: John Wiscombe, violinist; 

Nick Swain, tuba; Pat Markey, clarinet and trumpet; Emanuel 

Swain, alto horn; Farrell Wilkins, trombone; Lila Smith, piano; and

Justice Snow, drummer. Later, Lila Smith moved away, and Donna 

Burgess replaced her. Justice Snow finally left also, and Arthur 

Smith became the dummer. The orchestra was called, "The Roosevelt Orchestra," and it drew large crowds, especially on holidays. 

Young people came from all over the Basin to attend the Roosevelt dances, which were held in the Amusement Hall. 

Roosevelt Standard, July 19, 1916: 

"We have some new musicians among us. They are known as the Jacob's Orchestra and composed of three pieces: John Jacobs, violin; Ernest Jacobs, violin; and Arthur Jacobs, cornet. They recently played for a dance at Cedarview, and those people claim it was fine music."

Roosevelt Standard, June 7, 1916:

WATER WORKS 

"The citizens of the community went into the matter cooperatively and stored the waters of the Uintah River in a large reservoir at the highest point on the Bench in the west part of town, from which point the water in conveyed through the streets 

Plant and site of Utah Power & Light Co.

in wooden pipes. While a part of the canal which is to bring water free from any seepage or drainage direct from the river will not be completed until fall, the water at present is pure and wholesome. 

A more complete analysis of the water from the reservoir and pipe line has been recently made by the State Chemist, and the water found to be absolutely free from disease."-The Roosevelt Standard 

Vol. 1, No. 8, October 19, 1914. 

November 17, 1915, the city councilmen decided to drill an artesian well on the reservoir site. They gave the contract to the 

Staley well-drilling outfit. Since then other wells have been dug west of the town and the water piped into the reservoir. 

TELEPHONE 

On April 19, 1916, Ernest H. Burgess, Dr. J. E. Morton and

George A. Pearce were appointed as a committee to get signers for the telephone exchange in Roosevelt. Seventy-six signed and agreed to take the telephone for one year with the understanding that the Uintah Telephone Company proceed without unusual delay to 

Essie Neal, one of the first telephone operaters. install a local telephone exchange at Roosevelt with a hundred and five capacity, and to erect a suitable building for that purpose, which was to cost aproximately $66,000.00.

April 11, 1917-"The Uintah 

Telephone Exchange has been in operation here since January, and they have been giving satisfactory service. The business now promptly answer their phones and you have no party on the wire if they are in. "They have about sixty-five phones in service, and that is not enough to justify the expense this company has gone to. They have installed new switch boards with a three hundred phone capacity: they have a fine new brick building, and there are three operators to run it. "This is the central for the whole Uintah Basin in sending messages to the outside, and is considered one of the biggest improvements in Roosevelt this past year."

ROOSEVELT 

385 

In April 22, 1917 is the following article: 

The Uintah Telephone is going to build their extension to Salt 

Lake City this winter. Manager Charles Neal left here Monday with a gang of ten men for Stockmore, from which point they will commence surveying the line westward. 

The line will go up the west fork of the Duchesne to Wolf Creek Pass, thence the south fork of Provo to Park City. 

Besides this extension, branch lines will be extended to Tabiona, Mt. Emmons and Bluebell, and also to Neola, and if it can be arranged, one to Leeton and Lapoint.

This company is also getting ready to build a brick warehouse on the rear of their lot. Roosevelt will be the central station. 

Mrs. Essie Neal, Myrtle King, Afton Birch, and Gladys Zimmerman are all remembered as efficient operators.

EARLY THRESHERS 

The first threshing machine to come into Roosevelt was a

Case Portable Engine Machine (pulled around by horses and was a Company machine). Owned by Joseph Hardy, Owen Bennion, Gilbert Bearsow, Charles Milligan and Jerry Murray. Jerry Murray was the manager and Charles Milligan was the engineer. They threshed the first grain that was raised in the vicinity of Roosevelt in 1907.

The next thresher to come in was a horse power machine owned by Ira Bryant. His brother Elisha Bryant, Jake Taylor and

Leo Wright, helped on the machine, these are all we can remember of the crew.

On all the jobs our neighbors would help one another, as extra help, grain pitchers, tending sacks and other help as needed. In our neighborhood Jack Sheean, Lafe Woods, John O'Conell and Charles

Milligan all worked together and we women folks helped each other to cook, as we really had large crews in those days to cook for. 

We would build a table of boards across the kitchen and it was always full; after threshing was done we would tear it down until next year.

About 1909, Will Taylor, a part Indian from Indian Bench, brought in the first Steam Tractor Machine. Helping Bill Taylor on this machine were Will Brown, George Brown and Will Taylor's boy. The tractor was so heavy it broke most of the bridges and was stuck a large part of the time, for bridges were not built for such a load, and roads were not too good; so it was rather difficult for this heavy tractor to get around.

The next machine to come in was one run by Herman Miller, Don Kolb and a Campbell.

This has been written from a diary and memory. Mr. Jerry 

Murray helped with the names of the men on the first machine. 

We did not look on the threshers in those days, when we had 12

to 15 men to cook for, as being as much of a job as we do now. 

We were all happy and the men always stayed with the machine at night and we would spend evenings in the house singing and playing cards until about bed time, with much the same humble sociable spirit that the first pioneers had, always willing to help our neighbor to get his threshing done. 

-Above "Early Threshers" contributed by Alta Philips. 

SCHOOLS 

In 1907, Mr. and Mrs. Ed F. Harmston donated a two-acre tract of land on which the citizens of Roosevelt were to erect a school house. A one-room frame building was built with Jerry 

Murray supervising, and school began with about fifteen pupils. 

N. C. Cable was their teacher, and they brought their own books from home.

In the Duchesne Record published at Myton and dated 

September 9, 1910, is the following item: "Prof. L. Claire Winslow passed through here Saturday and opened the Roosevelt school Monday." The first eighth grade graduating exercises were held under his direction in the elementary school building. The following graduates were Elmer Roberts, Marie 

Lambert, Eugene Harmston, Jennie Roberts, Lois Bennion, Hazel

Bennion, Fannie Gagon and Miss Riley. The picture of this class is in the possession of Mrs. Tina Wilkins, who was the photographer and Eugene Harston, a member of the class, also has a copy. 

Early High School in the Blackhurst Building.

ROOSEVELT 

387 In 1911, Ernest Burgess, principal of the Elementary School taught classes in Algebra and History to a group of six, first year high school students. Lurrine Miles, his assistant High School teacher taught Latin and English.

The next year, 1912, Wasatch High School, No. 2 opened its doors in the Blackhurst Building, on Main Street. J. Frank Hoyt was the principal with LeRoy Beagley and Donna Burgess as instructors. 

In 1913, under J. Frank Hoyt as principal, Lucile Young,

Florence Circle and LeRoy Beagley taught Civics, Mathematics, Home Economics, English and Agriculture. 

In 1914, High School started in the Blackhurst building but was continued in the New High School on the Hill. 

Dedication services were held February 17, 1915. The program was "of high order and very instructive." The program consisted of a basketball game between Roosevelt High School and Duchesne. 

The line-up was: Roosevelt-Rasmussen, Bracken, Wilkins, Lam bert, and Case vs. Jensen, Woods, Odekirk, Abplanalp, and Scott of Duchesne. The score was Roosevelt-42 and Duchesne-18. It was rather an initiation of the new gymnasium. The crowd then went to the auditorium and the folowing program was given: 

M. B. Pope, president of the High School Board of Education called the meeting to order and Reverend T. B. McClement gave the invocation. The High School Orchestra under the direction of 

Donna Burgess gave a selection.

President Pope was followed by Ed. F. Harmston of the County 

Board of Education who gave a detailed account of the building of the new High School. He said that the building had cost $36,817 and that the land had been donated by President Smart. 

Joe Pearce played a violin solo, "Oh My Father". He was followed by a few remarks by President Smart who then gave the dedicatory prayer for the opening of the New High School. He prayed for greater unity of the people of Duchesne County and for better co-operation of the people of the Basin. "Oh Genevieve," was rendered by the High School Male Quartette, followed by a vocal solo by Mrs. Hazel Frizell. Principal J. 

Frank Hoyt discussed the "growth of the high school in the Basin". 

Superintendent J. A. Washburn was the concluding speaker. The high school students sang, "The Fairy Waltz." and the services were closed by J. H. Hardy offering the benediction. 

A banquet was served to all of the guests. 

High school was continued in this new building and the first 

High School graduating class held their exercises, Friday, May 28.

The graduates were Feliz A. Irmen, of Randlett; Afton Sabey, F.

In 1915, a girl's basketball team gained Basin-wide reputation for their excellent efficiency. The team enrollment was Viola Harmston, Verna Bennion, Hazel Bennion, Afton Schwencke, Mary 

Case, Eva Stewart and a Miss Hamilton. 

The "Rustler" appeared shortly after the Thistle as the weekly school paper.

Walter Atwood left many memories for his students to cherish. 

Many activities were sponsored under the Toyack Chapter of the Future Farmers that was organized under his supervision. The chapter house was built by the Toyacks and an outstanding feature was the fireplace that was made of stone blocks sent from almost every state in the Union. Mr. Atwood organized a trip to the 

World's Fair in Chicago, and most of the chapter of the Toyack's went. It was a grand experience. 

Martha J. Edwards, affectionately known as "Aunt Mat," by most of her students, is still teaching and producing plays, training declamation participants, and driving her car to almost every sport event. How many thousand miles "Aunt Mat" has traveled with students over the long period of years is not known. She taught 

14 years in the Roosevelt High School. During the thirty-five years she has lived in Roosevelt, she has produced fifty-six plays for the school and community. 

Roosevelt High School in construction.

ROOSEVELT 

393 A. LaMont Neilson is one of the few teachers who still is instructing the Basin's youth. For years he has taught in the Roosevelt High School. Recently he received his Master's Degree on the 

History of Uintah Basin. 

James Hood came to the Basin very early as an instructor in music. During his teaching, President Smart called him on a mission to establish choirs in every community. He selected five assistants who were Dr. Morton, Jack Wiscombe, Dr. W. L. 

Sutherland and Mrs. Sutherland, and Donna Burgess. Mr. Hood recently stated that often a membership of over twenty enrolled in small communities such as Myton. He traveled to each community and gave private instructions to choir leaders in order to have efficient instructors.

An item of interest in the November 1914, issue of the Standard is the following: "Miss Nora Workman, Miss Florence Cirkle and 

Miss Mary Fisher, popular teachers in our schools, and Miss Hazel 

Neilson, a pupil attending High School, have rented the Capt.

Whitlock cottage on State Street and have set up housekeeping." 

Under the direction of Charles Schwencke debating became an outstanding event each year for many years at the High School. 

Classes in science were far advanced under his direction. His students remembered him as the "best teacher they ever had."

After his death a plaque was placed in the high school in his memory by many of his former students. 

R. Budelier was principal of the elementary school in 1912 and 

1913. He became well beloved by the community. Under his direction Scouting was first introduced into the Basin. He left 

Roosevelt to be principal of the Hayden school and J. A. Washburn was principal in 1914.

During Mr. Washburn's administration, Duchesne County was divided from Wasatch and Mr. Washburn became the County 

Superintendent of Schools. Roosevelt Standard, January 15, 1915 

issue reads: "Superintendent Washburn is visiting the schools in the county this week; Ray E. Dillman is acting as principal of the grade school in his place." When Mr. Dillman had to attend court, Mrs. Cre Evans substituted as principal.

The Elementary School was fortunate to have in its faculty a long list of efficient and excellent qualified teachers for many years. A few only can be mentioned but tribute should be paid to those who have taught for many years. Some of these are still on the teaching staff..

Mary Orser taught in the Basin before she came to Roosevelt. 

She was on the Uintah School Board in its early history. Mrs. 

Orser, gracious, firm and civic minded, instilled into her pupils many of the cardinal virtues of living as well as required subjects.

She was greatly missed when she retired. 

Nora D. Workman Mathews almost grew up with the school.

She came here as a very young woman and it is doubtful if she has missed a school year yet. Hundreds of boys and girls learned their three "R's" under her direction. A great lover of flowers, her room was almost a garden for the whole school year. In her spare time she took care of the Library, encouraging boys and girls who came, to read good literature. She is still teaching in the Roosevelt Ele mentary School. 

Eva J. Lewis taught for many years. A tribute is paid to her in the story of Fruitland, where she first started to teach in the Basin. Her first teaching was done in her own front room. Many parents have been thankful for her fine judgment and foresight in helping their children with the problems of elementary training. 

In 1909, in the Elementary School two more additional rooms were added, and within a short period another building of similar structure was added on the west part of the block. With the increase of students due to the consolidation of the schools, a new building was erected in 1926. Students came from Crescent, the 

Hancock Cove, Ioka and the Myton Bench.

To include all of the fine operas, programs and activities that were given at this school would make a detailed report that cannot be included in this brief history. 

In 1919, Marie Collett introduced the Smith Hughes work to the Basin. Her enthusiasm awakened the rural homemaker to better living and a program of progression. 

The county school census of 1919 was 1,574 boys and 1,497 girls. This was a gain of 118 pupils over the previous year. 

In the tower of the first school building hung a large school bell. It rang every school day, and of course many times every Halloween, until December 25, 1915, when the city bought it for a curfew and a fire warning. It was given to the Daughters of the 

Pioneers in July, 1945, and under the direction of Mrs. Tina 

Wilkins, it now rests on the City grounds. 

On April 2, 1920, the first Junior Prom was given in the High School Gymnasium. It was a gala affair. The Standard gives the Junior Class much praise for this fine effort.

During 1920, David Nickelson and Cora Collett were sent to the U.S.A.C. to a special training school for a few weeks. They brought back an appreciation of higher education and new methods. in agriculture and home sciences. 

Miss "Decie" Emert brought the world to the class room that she taught. During her long years of teaching her summer vacations were spent in foreign lands, including Europe, Alaska, the 

Hawaiian Islands, Mexico and most of the States. 

Other beloved teachers were: the three Collett girls, Elinor

ROOSEVELT 

395 

Atkins, Bee Jordon, Viola Harmston, Afton Sabey, Oral Goodrich,

Alvin Coombs, Hazel Manwaring, Blanche Murphy, Della Lambert, 

Carl Pryor, Ronald Wiscombe, Florence Pack, Paul Nicholson, Melba Gardner, Bell Angus, and Anne Warnock.

The Hot School Lunch was first organized with a cup of hot milk or coca heated on the stoves in the school rooms. This was served by Rella Feldstead and the teachers of the various rooms aided her. Sometimes stew or bread and butter were added. Mr. J. 

Watts was the principal. Eva Lewis, Nora Mathews and Josephine

Hunt were the teacher's committee. From this meager beginning parents were enlisted to help gather materials for the following year. Mrs. Ruby Allred worked very ardently and through her efforts the school lunch survived and grew during the hard years of the depression. Agnes Ferron and Hazel Frizzell assisted on the parents lunch committee.

The P.T.A. became an organization in 1919. Willis Johnson who had been elected the first president resigned and Dr. W. L. 

Sutherland was appointed to carry through that year. It became a 

National unit under the leadership of Mildred Dillman as president in 1938.

Due to the loss of the early minutes it has been impossible to give a correct list of the presidents. The following have been remembered as early leaders: Leo Young under whose direction most of the playground equipment was installed; Mrs. Felker, Mrs. 

Whitmore, Mrs. Mary Orser, Art Jacobs, Hazel Ashton, Viola 

Harmston, Elva McLea, Helen Sprouse, Luke Clegg, Willard Day,

Mrs. Albright, and Ruby Allred under whose constant support the 

P.T.A. has been kept an active organization. 

The High School staged a big Carnival, April 29, 1919. 

Rouvoe Christensen was queen with Pearl Edwards as crown bearer.

The first Teachers Convention met under the direction of Superintendent Washburn in 1914. It was well attended and held at Roosevelt. Many conventions have been held here since. 

SOCIAL LIFE 

Social life of the early pioneers consisted of house parties, dances, celebrations of holidays. Everyone was invited to these parties because even with all, the crowd would be small, especially of young folk. The first 4th of July, 1906, was the first big celebration and was held in the Lambert Grove, one mile south of town. Florence Shurtleff was crowned Queen during the program. 

Jigs and square dances were included. Lunch was sold by Mrs. 

Shurtleff and her sister, Mrs. Strang. The groceries were hauled from Vernal. Chicken, salads, pies, and ice cream were included

1-Parade in 1907; 2-Bowery in 1908; 3-Zina Hancock, one of the early queens; 

3-Clearing ground for celebration in 1908; 4-1911 No. 1 Parade; sham battle with the Indians. 

5-Famous

ROOSEVELT 

397 in the menu. Pink lemonade was served during the afternoon. It proved a bit on the protein side as a dead cow was found just above in the gulch where the water was taken. 

The mercantile building was not finished until just before the 24th of July celebration. The first big dance was held then. People sat on packing boxes for chairs. Children's beds were made in every corner. Bill Taylor and A. Reynolds played the violin and the dulcimere. It was an all night affair, and daylight found the wagons just pulling to their homes miles away. Theo Phillips furnished the kerosene for the light.

In 1907, the completion of the big room in the school house furnished a place to hold dances. The dance always included an impromptu program and people responded readily. Frank Hamilton was one of the first dance floor managers. Soldiers stationed at Fort 

Duchesne came often, bringing their good music.

Sam Page rented the dance hall in town and he called the old fashioned dances with enthusiasm. 

The second 4th of July was held in this big school room. Zina 

Hancock was the Goddess of Liberty and all of the girls of the town contributed to her costume by loaning some of their own clothes.

In 1907, the first masquerade ball was held in the school house. 

Sam Page, dressed as a clown, won first prize for the best gentleman's costume, and Pearl Redmond as a cowboy, took the first prize for the ladies. The prizes were sofa pillows.

In the Uintah Standard published in Roosevelt, 1909, the social calendar reported that C. C. Larsen and Mr. and Mrs. C. I. Johnson entertained at parties. Most of the population of the town went to both. 

In 1910, honoring Miss Pearl Redman, a wedding shower was given by Florence Shurtleff and Mrs. Kendall. It was the first shower given in the town. 

One of the biggest and best remembered 4th of July celebrations was held in 1911, under the Hill, just south of the town. Gene 

Reynolds was the Queen and escorted by girls on horseback representing every county in Utah. Banners were worn across their shoulders. This was the year of the big "Massacre." The covered wagons formed a circle. The Indians ambushed the camp and proceeded to scalp some of the men. Heber Timothy was bald but painted his head bright red over which he wore a wig. So real was the pretended scalping that several of the women fainted. 

During September 21, 22, 23, 1912, a huge Hay Palace was built for the first big fair of Duchesne County. It was made of fresh baled hay piled high and a canvass roof. Each town was represented by a booth, including Fort Duchesne Indian Service. Fruits were displayed that astounded the visitors. It was supervised by the new
