Fairs were held each year thereafter for many years. Fair committees were assigned the year before and many successful fairs are reported. In 1913, Fort Duchesne held an exhibit, but it was not until 1916 that their first Fair was organized under R. T. 

Bonnin as manager with a large committee of Indian people. 

In 1914, the County Fair was held at Myton with Roosevelt citizens taking an active part. J. M. Russell was selected as chairman, with D. C. Boyer, Ross Bartlett, S. R. Page, LeRoy Beagley, Frank Watkins, Xavier Bellom, James Johnson, Mrs. Homer P. 

Edwards, Mrs. R. S. Collett, as his sub-committee. Displays, horse races, ball games, trap shooting and auto races, programs and parades, in fact all that makes up a good County Fair were arranged and presented to a very large and grateful citizenship. 

Very early in Roosevelt, the Utah State Agricultural College held institutes. On October 27, 1914, a Farmer's Institute conducted by Dr. John L. Caine and assisted by Miss Gertrude McCheyne, and County Agent Martin L. Harris, lectured on sanitation, livestock, dairying, and human nutrition. Under Miss McCheyne's direction the Home Economics Club was organized with Mrs. 

W. A. Miles as president, Mrs. Charles Wallace, vice-president; and

Mrs. Fred Motter as secretary. The object was to aid in the betterment of the home and school. They met every week for a number of years and accomplished much good. The Library was started under their direction. A dance was given, with a book as the admission ticket provided a good start. Fly campaigns and school ground equipment programs were sponsored by them. 

On August 29, 1914 the Overland Tour of Automobiles from 

Colorado Springs arrived over the Pikes Peak Ocean to Ocean 

Highway. They were met at Vernal by George Pearce, Ed F.

Harmston, Mr. and Mrs. R. S. Collett, Mr. and Mrs. Sabey, Mrs. 

C. I. Johnson and Homer P. Edwards. "The hall was beautifully decorated with flags and bunting and at one end of the room a large sign of 'Welcome'.-The band rendered sweet strains of music. The menu consisted of fried chicken, dressing, trout, potatoes, corn, string beans, chicken nut salad, cheese, sliced tomatoes, sliced baked ham, beet pickles, baked apples with whipped cream, ice cream, cake, oranges, iced tea, bread, butter, honey and coffee."

The first speaker was President W. F. Jensen of the Salt Lake Commercial Club. R. S. Collett was the chairman from Roosevelt. 

The Salt Lake Commercial Club under George Steiner as president, visited the Basin September 16, 1915. The reception committee was Elroy Wilkins, chairman; R. S. Collett, Ed F. 

Harmston, sleeping quarters; entertainment: Clare Ashton, J. P.

May, Ed F. Harmston, Mrs. W. A. Miles and Mrs. Frank Orser. 

The 24th of July 1915, at the celebration Irma Shumway was

crowned as queen with Ella Roberts and Tina Wilkins as her aids. 

It was during this celebration that Roosevelt had a balloon ascension and a parachute jumper. This celebration was held under the direction of the Commercial Club and the committee consisted of 

O. H. Bracken as chairman with Ray E. Dillman, C. I. Johnson, Mrs. W. A. Worthen, Mrs. W. A. Miles, Mrs. Clarence Johnson,

J. M. Russell, Mrs. A. Birch and W. S. McKinley. 

In September 1918, Governor Simon Bamberger accompanied a party into the Basin seeking a route for the railroad from Salt Lake to Denver. His party was royally entertained by the citizens of Roosevelt. Ren Johnson, Bill King, Dan Allan and Ray Dillman went into Uintah Canyon and within a few hours caught enough trout for the entire banquet. The party continued on to Denver and in the Post an article appeared stating that the Governor had decided that the mountain passes were too steep to start the road at this time.

On April 11, 1917, the National Farmers Association held their meeting in the Commercial Club rooms and elected the following officers: Elroy Wilkins, president; J. P. Harris, vicepresident; Ed F. Harmston, secretary-treasurer. Fifteen applications were made amounting to $38,900. 

During 1919 fine programs were sponsored by the various clubs and high school. Mr. Hood planned several events to be brought from different places. The famous Salt Lake Quintette and the 

Ellison-White Chautauqua from Oregon gave a full week of fine entertainment.

High School and the Elementary schools produced operas and plays each year under the direction of the teachers. They were all well patronized. Morley Vernon assisted by Mrs. Mattie Edwards directed most of these operas.

In 1914, Devere-Foote Dramatic Club played, "Ten Nights in a Bar Room," and the Henderson-Bartlett Music Club gave an evening of fine entertainment.

In 1920, the Dry Gulch Cattle Association was a full grown organization continuing in operation for many years. It protected the rights of its members. 

January 18, 1915-"At an annual meeting the Uintah Forest

Grazer's Association met at the Cullen Hotel in Salt Lake City and organized the Uintah Sheep Growers Association. Mr. E. D. 

Clyde was elected president. "The sheep growers of Roosevelt attended and joined the organization which had a membership of 7150 men."

The productions of the Drama were among the chief achievements of Roosevelt. From the very early years, plays were produced by many of the various organizations. One of the first was produced by Sam Page. He took active part for many years in the gathering

ROOSEVELT 

401 together of people interested in the theater. The Y.M.M.I.A. and the Y.L.M.I.A. for many years took great pride in the selection of the best drama available.

Roosevelt was very fortunate in having as a director Mrs. 

Julia McLea, who had been on Broadway in many famous plays under the direction of Henry Miller. Under her guidance the plays, "Come Out of the Kitchen," and "Granddaddy Long Legs," thrilled several hundred people. Hazel Ashton was the leading lady for many years in plays. Later she directed several for different organizations. 

Mrs. M. J. Edwards has to her credit the production of over fifty plays. Myrtle Lambert also produced many plays. Roosevelt loves home dramatics.

CLUBS AND ORGANIZATIONS 

The Roosevelt Commercial Club held its meetings in the first few years of Roosevelt's history. Men gathered as early as 1909 to form this club. Officers were not elected until 1914. The club was not incorporated until January 19, 1928 under the following officers: Christy Anderson, president; George H. Harrison, vicepresident; O. J. Bracken, H. R. Sturgeon, Lynn Orser, Luke Clegg, C. I. Johnson, directors; and Harry Smith, treasurer; and D. O. 

Mackay, secretary. 

The club met first in the Campbell Saloon, then the Birch

Building, moving across the street into the Ashton Building. When their quarters on Lagoon Street, across from the Bank, burned on 

Christmas Day in 1924, they moved into the M. E. Harmston

Building until their present club rooms were ready. They have remained there.

The Roosevelt Commercial Club has been responsible for part of the growth of Roosevelt. They have aided nearly every project that has been undertaken. So numerous have been their charitable donations that they all cannot be included here. Mention should be made of Homer P. Edwards who rendered service almost continuously from its first meeting until the present. He has held almost every office in the organization. Jack Redmond has been the caretaker since 1925. Clarence Lambert has also served many years.

The Woman's Home Economics Club was organized under the direction of Mrs. Gertrude McCheyne and Dr. John Payne of the U.S.A.C. in October 1914. An account of the organization has been given previously. 

A Philharmonic Society under the direction of Mrs. Pearl

Christenson and Hazel Frizzell, brought much enjoyment to many of the early residents. They met weekly and their objective was to enjoy music and fine entertainment. Local talent was developed. 

Their influence has carried down through the years and Roosevelt

Baseball was one of the most popular sports. Most all of the young men organized teams. Myton, Vernal, and Fort Duchesne's famous Indian boys were the main opponents. Games were played on every holiday. A recreational field was just north east in the flat from Roosevelt-along about 1914. It was later moved south

A Tennis Club was organized by the young men as early as 

1913. They used the Fort Duchesne courts to practice and many a contest was played between Roosevelt teams and the Fort Duchesne

Officers Club. The enrollment about 1916, was Herbert Isham, Rae Ashton, Dr. Whitmore, Dr. Sutherland, Carl Bracken, Leland

Ashton and Axel Birch. The High School also had a court made under the direction of Charley Schwencke and organized the "Racket Club." The officers were Joseph Smart, president; Cora 

Collett, vice-president; and David Nicholson as secretary.

The Thimble Club held its first meeting at the home of Mrs. 

Ada Johnson in 1913. It was not until 1915 that officers were elected and the sewing club was changed into a Literary Club along with needlecraft. On September 8, 1915, Mrs. Bruce Calder was elected president, with Jessie Hyatt as vice-president and Mrs. 

Lynn Orser as secretary. In the 1920's it was changed at the suggestion of Mrs. Mary Orser, to the Altrus Club, under which name it still meets regularly. Throughout the years this club has been a congenial group. At one time it joined the National Federation of 

Women's Clubs but resigned a few years later. They donated books to the Library. 

On August 29, 1914, the Campfire Girls met at the home of 

Mrs. W. A. Miles and organized. Miss Elsie Collett and Miss Cre 

Miles were the leaders. This organization soon died after the Bee 

Hive Girls were created by the Y.L.M.I.A. under the direction of 

Mrs. Flora Collett. Mildred Miles was the first Bee Keeper.

From the Standard, August 29, 1914 "The Campfire Girls hold their meeting at the Hall every Wednesday evening. The Young 

Ladies are certainly enjoying good times, generally winding up the evening with fascinating dancing: Visitors are always welcome." 

The Rebecca Lodge was organized in January, 1925. “Sego 

Lily Rebekahs Lodge No. 36, installed the following officers Tuesday evening: Noble Grand, Olga Birch; Vice-Grand, Florence 

Shurtleff; Secretary, Maud Burton; Treasurer, Edna Shurtleff; 

Warden, Helen Kramer; Conductor, Florence Circle; Chaplain, Tina Smith; Outer and Inner Guard, A. Birch; Right Supporter 

Noble Grand, Pat Markey; Left Supporter Noble Grand, Mary

Fisher." This Lodge conducted fine entertainments for their members and the citizens of Roosevelt for years. Accounts of their activities are replete in the early issues of the Roosevelt Standard. 

The Trinity Guild was organized early in 1915. Mrs. Whitmore, Mrs. Arnold Reef, Mrs. Nora Mathews, Miss Florence Circle, were among the early officers. It was not until recently that this club was disorganized. They met at the home of the various members and much charity work was accomplished.

Mrs. Harrison received State and National recognition when she edited and organized the first 

State paper, "Forward Together," while she was serving on the

State Board. 

The Chapter of the National 

Red Cross was organized during 

World War I. It has carried on diligently ever since.

The Fish and Game Club for women, the only one in America of its kind, was incorporated by 

Mrs. Violet Harison who served as its president for three years.

The Men's Fish and Game 

Club was organized under the guidance of Mayor George Har rison. During the early 1920's many bridge clubs sprang into existence, the Friday Bridge, Just-a-Mere, The Dinner Bridge Club and many others. Most of these are still meeting.

There are also many 

Friendly Neighbor circles.

Outstanding in its service to the community, is the Christmas Celebration Committee that has been carried on for many years. 

The town has been lighted a month before Christmas and Santa Claus has come to town. A parade and nuts and candy have been given to all the children on Christmas Eve. Baskets of food taken to all in need, and programs rendered at the Church. Mrs. Peter 

Anderson, Homer P. Edwards, Mrs. Jessie Orser, Mr. Heber Hall,

Tina Wilkins, are among those who have served for many years to make the celebration a success. Bruce Calder and Art Wiscombe will long be remembered in the red velvet suits and white whiskers who helped to give out promises, along with the candy and nuts.

The 4-H club was organized under the direction of Miss Alice 

Pederson, County Home Demonstrator. Mrs. Pearl Redmond and her daughter, Mrs. Mary Burgess achieved National recognition for their efforts. Mary won the honor of going to Chicago for her fine work. Then Afton Clegg was selected under the direction of Mrs. Mary Burgess. Mrs. Pearl Redmond was awarded the certificate of merit and three gold seals, December 7, 1931, and a gold medal. Marva Anderson (a nurse), Effie Jacobs and Reva Owens aided in this work. 

From the files of the Standard, Oct. 1, 1919, is the following account of the organization of the American Legion: "Fifteen of our returned soldiers met in a meeting last night and formed and prepared an application for a post of the American Legion. Robert H. Wallis was appointed temporary chairman to preside over the meeting. It was decided and agreed upon that the name of our post at Roosevelt should be "Burns," in honor and in behalf of the distinguished services rendered and finally the giving up of one of the Basin's heroes, Hubert Burns of Randlett who died in the struggle at Chateau Tierry in France. Those constituting the charter members are the following: 

Ray Jordon, Robert Raymond Reef, Marion LeRoy Corbett, R. 

Sterling Collett, Rae Ashton, Newt Miller, Joseph Pearce, Daniel 

Lewis Brown, Merrel Wilkins, Weston Timothy, Robert Wallis,

Thomas Shisler, Lewis Devoe Shurtleff, Paul S. Nicholson and Guy Carne. 

As soon as the Charter is received from Washington, this organization will enthusiastically pull together for the purpose for which this association was established." 

The Auxiliary to this Legion was perfected in its organization a short time thereafter.

In 1920, April 28, a Ladies' Republican Club selected Mrs. 

Bryce as president; Mrs. Sabey was vice-president and Mrs. Burton,

Mrs. Bell Reef as secretary. Jessie Orser was voted in as treasurer. 

Other clubs of more recent origin are the Lions, the Junior 

Chamber of Commerce and the Junior Culture Club. Fred Ferron was first president of the Lions Club with Homer P. Edwards as secretary.

The Junior Chamber of Commerce was organized October 7, 1939 with Ronald Wiscombe as president; Ted Harmston, vicepresident; Frank Shaw as treasurer; George Stewart as secretary; the directors were Clifford Woodruff, Merrill Croft, Ross Larsen and Frank Mohlman. There were about sixty charter members. 

The Junior Culture Club was not organized until about 1942. 

In writing this brief account, a reminder is made not to forget the famous "Gutter Gang of girls." They know who they are and what good times they had for years.

The Jitney Club met in the early years in the top of Ashton's store. Games and dancing were the usual entertainment.

The Boy Scouts were organized under the direction of R. Eva Ashton and Bell Wilkins, leaders of the American Legion Auxiliary. 

Mrs. Ashton has been nationally recognized.

ROOSEVELT 

411 Budlier. Ray Dillman took over in 1914 and has been active ever since. He recently received his thirty year badge. Willard Day and 

Alfred Lublin were among those who served as Scout Masters for years. Willard received his Beaver award recently. Paul Wilkins, Homer Edwards and Paul Murphy are to be remembered by helping sponsor the Scout Program for a long period of time. 

WILLARD DAY 

Scout Leader and poet.

Reed Clegg and Miles Dill man were the first Eagle Scouts in the Uintah Basin. Kirk Wil kins won the highest rank of Sil ver Palm. The Scouting program in Roosevelt has been one of its crowning achievements.

Just the highlights of the recreation program have been briefly given. It is gratifying to know that so many varied activities were sponsored because by them the true character of a community can be judged. "By

Their leasure time a community can be estimated." 

INDIAN DAY The President of the United States designated Friday, Septem ber 26, 1941 as Indian Day. Governor Maw likewise proclaimed this day for Utah's Indian Day. The Mayor, George Harrison, having this called to his attention called a group of people together asking that Roosevelt honor the Indians this day. Committees were formed and Indian Day was born in Roosevelt. 

The Indians at first were very suspicious as to anyone honoring them and many stayed away from the celebration. However, a large number did respond. Vean Blair, Floyd Roberts, and Mildred Dillman worked day and night for a month to see that this celebration was a success. Many others joined the last two weeks. The Indian 

Department responded with enthusiasm this first year.

A parade of thirty floats, most with Indians and native decoration of pine trees and shrubbery was the theme. Six bands came from all over the Basin. Barbeque tended by George Poulas was the drawing card for the free lunch at 1 o'clock. Over three thousand sandwiches were served. Watermelon and soda water were given freely to all Indians by the Auxiliary American Legion. 

Governor and Mrs. Herbert B. Maw, Mr. and Mrs. Tracy

Welling, and Mrs. Betty Elgan were guests. The Indians honored

the governor by taking him into the tribe and giving him the title of 

"Chief White Feather." 

Games and prizes for best costumes occupied the afternoon. 

Free Picture Shows for the guests closed the first celebration of 

Governor Maw dancing the Bear Dance, Indian Day. 

Rae McDonald leaves his taxi business for this big deer hunt. Deer skins were sold mostly to Ben Miles, local buyer, or given to the Indians who tanned them and used them for clothing and mocassins; often hundreds were gathered in one hunting season.

ROOSEVELT 

413

9-Andrew Frank, an important leader; 10-Pageant was the concluding event, written and produced by Mildred Dillman; 11-Lillie Wash, who helped with every committee; 12-Laura Mapsock, who, with her husband Henry, worked valiantly for Indian Day.

Indian Day.

A pageant written and directed by Mildred Dillman in which over one hundred Indians participated has been one of the outstanding features of Indian Day, along with the Rodeo and barbecue. 

Since that time, each year has been a bigger and a better celebration. It is sincerely hoped that Indian Day may grow into the finest celebration in Utah. It is unique and very timely that we should honor the Indian.

ROOSEVELT STAKE PRIMARY Submitted by Lela Labrum 

Prior to a Stake organization this country was a part of Uintah 

Stake under President Wililam H. Smart.

1-Ephraim Lambert; 2-Paul H. Hanson, a Mayor of Roosevelt; 3-W. H. Gagon; 

4-Joseph H. Hardy; 5-Heber T. Hall, Mayor of Roosevelt under whose direction the 

L.D.S. Church was finished; 6-Joseph Lambert; 7-Alice Lambert; 8-Rose Hardy;

Group of Eagle Scouts, Bernell Swain, Merrill Croft, Bill Edwards, Ray Shisler, 

A. A. Anderson, Kirk Wilkins and Willard Day.

A Primary organization was effected at Roosevelt Town, April 

24, 1910. Officers were: Rose M. Hardy, superintendent; Olive 

Bingham, first counselor; Libby Sabey, second counselor; Marie 

Lambert, secretary. 

August 28, 1910, a branch primary of the Roosevelt Primary was organized at Hayden with Eliza Horrocks as superintendent; 

Pearl Taylor, first assistant; Arilla Bastian, second assistant and

Agnes Zabriskie, secretary. 

THE FIRST STAKE ORGANIZATION 

September 15, 1910, Duchesne Stake was organized and President William H. Smart was sustained as president of the new stake.

THE FIRST STAKE PRIMARY ORGANIZATION 

December 4, 1910 a Stake Primary was organized in the new 

Duchesne Stake. The first officers of the Stake organization were: 

Rose M. Hardy, president; Ada M. Johnson, first counselors; Verna 

M. Lusty, second counselor and Mary A. Gagon, secretary.

The year 1910 saw Primaries organized in Roosevelt Ward, Mural 

Branch, Cedarview Branch, Boneta Ward, (Theodore Ward).

In 1912 organizations were made at Cumeal Branch, Crescent, Taboina and Bluebell. 

1913 saw organizations at Mountain Home, Bennett, Ioka, and Myton, also some other wards were reorganized. 

Rose M. Hardy, the first president was born at Heber City, Utah, January 23, 1874. She moved with her parents to Ashley

Valley in December, 1879. She was a school teacher for many years. She married Joseph Hardy and they moved to Roosevelt in 1907. She is the mother of seven children. 

Mrs. Hardy was released as Primary President on June 30, 1918.

CARLY HISTORY OF DUCHESNE COUNTY 

1-The Wilson School located where the Ballard Ward Chapel now stands. It was built in the shape of an "L", on the homestead of Mr. Jerry Murray. When the present Ballard school house was built this building was cut in two sections and made into private dwelling houses. Number 1 is located one mile south of the Ballard school house on the homestead of Mr. Carmon. Number 2 is located three fourths of a mile east of the Ballard school on the homestead of Mr. Jerry Murray; 2-Early group of R. S.; 3-Morrill Labrum's mission farewell party; 4-(Wilson) Ballard school built 1919. It is located on the George Pickup homstead; 5-Mary Burgess' 

Ballard school group. -Ballard School picture contributed by Mary Burgess.

Arvilla Day 

Exact dates were not available from 1939-48. In the Stake, Ray E. Dillman became President on February 2, 1941. Joe Bentley,

Russell Todd, and Lee Angus have been his counselors. Jack Wiscombe and Paul Murphy have been the Stake Clerks.

Horace Ritchie, Marden Broadbent, Ezra Nixon were the Bishops until the wards were divided. Hollis Hullinger became 

Bishop of the Roosevelt Ward and Ezra J. Nixon was made Bishop of the Roosevelt Second Ward. 

Florence Nielson followed Margaret Wiscombe as Stake Relief 

Society President. Shelma Fredrickson and Blanche Murphy have been the Stake Mutual Presidents. Paul Nelson has been the 

Y.M.M.I.A. Superintendent. B. A. Childs, Ernest Morrison, Frank

Walker, Randall Stewart, and Dr. R. V. Larson were the Superin tendents of the Stake Sunday School Boards. 

Not being able to obtain an accurate list of the ward officers, it was deemed best not to include it here. 

Seminary for the Roosevelt Stake started very early under the direction of President Smart. Among the teachers at the Roosevelt 

Seminary were Dr. Farley G. Eskelson, Anna Smart, Albert Sells, 

Stanley Gunn, F. E. Berrett, B. A. Childs, Bartley Heiner, Frank

Walker, G. Belnap, and Woodrow Church. 

A Seminary for the Alterra High School was later organized. 

Bartley Heiner, Martha Edwards, Frank Walker, and Wendel Johnson have been among the teachers.

FIRES IN ROOSEVELT By Pearl Redmond 

Sunday morning, October 24, 1915, four buildings on upper

Main street between what is now Lagoon and First North were burned in less than two hours.

Jimmy Mease, who was the owner of the Roosevelt Drug Store, was sleeping in the back room, when he woke up he saw the room was full of smoke; he grabbed a pair of old trousers and ran out and that is all that he saved. His building and entire stock was burned. 

An estimated loss of $88,000 with $3,000 insurance. He gave the first alarm; then ran across the street and woke Clair and Ray 

Ashton, but the Drug Store was soon gone.

The Commercial Club was the next to go. They got the billiard table out by taking off the legs. The table and a lot of other things that were taken out were placed too near the fire and burned, except the table, it was scorched and minus one leg, as Mrs. Sarah Zimmerman, threw the leg into the fire in the excitement. (North of the 

Commercial Club was J. M. Russell's real estate office; it was the next to go; that building was a total loss.) 

A. Birch's furniture store was the next to go, they saved some of the furniture but all that was in the attic was a complete loss. 

At the rear of this building was a warehouse and it was here the bucket brigade did good work and saved the building. 

W. H. Gagon's store was the next that looked like it would go, they moved all the stock out and put it into wagons and moved it away. Hard work by all saved the rest of the block. 

In the meantime some valiant work was done on the south side of the burned district; about twenty feet south of the Commercial Club stood Ed. F. Harmston's office. Everything was moved out of this building. The wind was blowing from the west and it looked very dangerous for Ashton's block, Charles Wallis' Blacksmith Shop, and the Dry Gulch office. But water was poured on the buildings and they were saved. 

September, 1916, Raymond Reef was appointed first Fire 

Chief, with Ray Ashton, Jack Bracken, Ross Bartlett, and Tom

Chisler as firemen. O. H. Bracken at this time was mayor. Three hundred feet of hose was bought in 50-foot lengths. 

Roosevelt had another big fire. Tuesday night, January 23, 1917, shortly after 11 o'clock, the curfew and Trinity Church bells began ringing and the people were aroused from their slumbers by the fire alarm. It was found that the Elan Theater which faced west on the block between Lagoon and First street where the Bowling Alley now stands, was ablaze and the building was doomed. 

They were able to save the piano, some benches and a number of pairs of roller skates, but the fine picture machines were burned. In

ROOSEVELT 

441 the southwest room the Farmers Commercial Co. had about 5,000 worth of seed stored which was lost. The building was 50x90 feet and cost $7,500 with an insurance of $3,000. 

On the south side was Roberts and Todds planing mill, which was consumed, with all machinery. They saved the work tools and some planed lumber. The loss was $6,000 with no insurance. 

On the north of this building, about 50 feet, was the amusement hall, which was also used as the Church house. It was thought it would be next to go. Everything was moved out. Finally three young men got on the rafters and kept throwing snow while about 200 kept throwing snowballs at the south side of the hall. It was the first time in the history of Roosevelt that a fire was successfully fought with snowballs, which was the only means we had to extinguish the blaze. This amusement hall did eventually burn December 7, 1930. 

Louis Laris-Early stockman and real estate dealer. He supplied a market for the farmers in the depression years. 

May 1920, E. M. Swain building, which was a harness and shoe shop south of the Cash Meat Market, burned down. It was a total loss with very small insurance. 

Early Christmas morning, 1924, the 

Commercial Club burned down. J. H.

Harrison, former houseman of the club, turned in the alarm but it had such a start and it being so cold it burned to the ground. It was 24 degrees below zero and the hose was frozen so no headway could be made. 

Who could forget "Old" Kinniman, poundkeeper and horse trader, or "Dr." Schulyer and "Wass Kahl a ha" or Wm. Trinniman and his sheep, or Fred Jensen as the poundkeeper of the city; Silas

Mower was also well known for the excellent honey he raised. 

Others who have helped in the development of Roosevelt are: 

Jess Johnstun, who operated the first auto stage from Price to Vernal; Herbert Barlow, who was a stage driver on that line; Judge 

Doggett; Reva Owens and her school lunch program; Paul Watkins, and G. B. Kelley, who built many of the first homes, among which was the Huish Drug; Helene Harmston and Blanche Murphy, whose dress shop was an asset to the town; Christy Anderson and 

Clair Larson, who operated a Bottling Works and Clair is still going strong; the Garages at Crumbo's, Wogec's, Clyde Murray's, Lynn's,

and the Mitchell Bros.; Katie Schwenckie's Hotel; Vern, Parley, and 

Presly Timothy's Home Lumber Store; Marian Mortensen's 5 and 

10c Store: Bill and Steve's Cafe; Mrs. Atkins' Cafe; Hillard Broderick has done plumbing for many years; Valborg Lowes Millinery: the Sterling Transportation with Bus Ruppel as manager; Dr. and 

Mrs. Spurrier, Chiropractors; Bill Mann and Parley Rawlings, painters. It is sincerely hoped no one has been omitted.

Dedicated to the Memory of my loving and Courageous Mother

MRS. MARY ANN MAXWELL THE HISTORY OF TABIONA VALLEY 

T IS APRIL 1904, the sun comes creeping over the distant snow covered mountains and reflecting on the grandeur of the beautiful glistening snow drifts of the towering western mountains.

Slowly the sun rose higher and higher, until just a tip now was hidden behind the mountain and each side reflected the golden hue until it looked like the vast splendor of a king's crown. 

Far below lay a little sleepy valley tucked away in the foothills, and as if suddenly awakened by the sunrise, the song from a flock of meadow larks broke the steady roar of the crystal mountain stream, and the whole valley seemed to awaken by the happy song of spring.

Coyotes were howling in the distance, and a rabbit went bounding through the brush; somewhere nearby came the lonely cry of a wolf.

Suddenly in the distance a soldier's trumpet blast the air and there in the center of the valley by the mountain stream stood a government soldier's camp. 443

Soldiers were placed here to keep peace among the Indians; the two brick chimneys which extended from the Fort were used for many years as land marks, all locations for many miles around were surveyed from these chimneys. During their stay here, the soldiers went through many hardships as the Indians were extremely hard to handle. On one occasion when the Indians were on the warpath, the Fort was surrounded by yelling, painted Indians, days passed and no one dared venture outside the gate. At last the water supply was gone and the soldiers were forced to dig a well to obtain drinking water.

It was the fall of 1905, the government released the valley for homesteading, and one by one over the mountain passes the farmers came driving in seeking their homestead which they had drawn, not knowing exactly where it would be located, but it seemed that almost everyone was satisfied and began at once to build a home. The first fall just one-room cabins were erected which were enlarged or new ones built within a year. These were owned by Mr. Alma W. Wagstaff, Mr. T. A. White, Mr. Olie Hand, Mr. 

Bert White, Mr. A. W. Maxwell, Mr. Nephi Chatwin, and Mr. 

Robert Michie.

How happy they were that fall, preparing and planning for the day when they could bring their families to this new home, Home! what a grand old word! and to these hard-working God-fearing men it meant Heaven!

Each one with a large family of from four to ten children was anxious to get those children out in this grand mountain country and watch them thrive and grow as strong and happy as the beauti- 

Part of the members of the D.U.P. in Tabiona, Utah-Camp Sacagawea. Reading from left to right standing: Mrs. Thelma Nye, Mrs. Minnie Hamilton, Mrs. Deon 

Rhoades, Mrs. Marie Thomas, Mrs. ildred Webb, Mrs. Orpha Michie, Mrs. Irene

Thomas. Sitting: Ina Van Tassell, Mrs. Jessie Maxwell, Mrs. Elaine M. Webb, Mrs. Twila Rhoades, Mrs. Clara Nye, Mrs. Norma Wagstaff, Daphne Van Tassell.

TABIONA 

445 

1-Ephraim; 2-Wanroades; 3-Marcellas Lewis; 4_5-Granddaddy Lakes; 6-First camp trip into the valley.

ful little fawns that run unmolested at their mother's side. 

But the Indian summer soon came to an end; the mornings were crisp and cold, and misty snow clouds hung heavy in the air, so at last, much to the disappointment of the homesteader, they must lock the door of the lonely little cabin and, with team and wagon, drive back to spend the winter with their families. 

Mr. Wagstaff, who was a carpenter by trade, looking through rose colored glasses, was seeing his crude cabin as a castle which some day he would reign over with his adoring wife, the queen of his heart. After saying a lingering goodbye to the inside of the cabin he locked the door and on the outside he wrote these words: "God Bless Our Home," but on returning in the spring he found that someone had erased two words, and on the door was now the words, "God What a Home."

Now the winter months were drawing near and with no more chopping, no more hammering, silence once again fell in the little valley, only the lazy quiet life of the Indian was all that was left, so, during those long winter months those little cabins nestled peacefully under a soft blanket of snow.

Many miles away around a cozy fireplace, happy children were listening eagerly to thrilling stories told over and over again of the Indians who lived on the Uintah Reservation, of the grand old Chief Tabby whom everyone loved so much, his home had been located on the Maxwell homestead, a crude fence and an irrigation ditch showed signs that some farming had been started. 

Chief Tabby was head over a large tribe, he made many trips to Salt Lake City, where he met in conference with Col. 

Tourelotte, Supt. of the Indian Affairs for the territory, so it was they made friends with the white men and sent a delegation of men to invite the Navajos to come and trade, and stop stealing and be friends with the Mormons. Tabby (which means Sun), had a long and adventureous life. He died at the White Rocks Agency in 

November, 1903, and was buried in the Rock Creek valley.

At his home on the Maxwell homestead immediately behind his tepee rose the vast and beautiful mountain which they called Mt. Tabby named after their Lord and Master, Chief Tabby. The mountain stream which goes winding down the center of the valley was named by an old French trapper; it means chimney, or sturdy rock foundation, the head of the Duchesne river comes from a small box shaped canyon where on three sides are ragged rocky cliffs, and as from a fireplace comes gushing forth the North Fork of the 

Duchesne river.

On the eastern side of Tabiona we see a towering long range of mountains; here indeed did the Indians enjoy hunting wild game, and for miles chase large herds of wild horses. This they called Big

TABIONA 

447 

1-First Mrs. Nye. deceased; 2-Mr. and Mrs. Dave Nye; 3-Mr. and Mrs. Alma 

Wagstaff; 4-17th of March, 1924, Tabiona.

Mountain, here among the valleys and shady nooks on Big Mountain lie several Indian graves, and in these graves are also buried all of her or his personal belongings, and other things such as frying pans, or coffee pots and similar things that may be needed in the happy hunting ground. Some bodies were laid to rest in the boughs of the sturdy old pine tree. 

In a small valley at the foot of the Mountain there still remains a clearing that once was an Indian race track. Many happy hours were spent when the Indians all gathered, bringing their favorite race horse to try him out.

At that time there lived close to the tepee of Chief Tabby, an aged Indian; his name was Lehi John; he claimed to be more than 100 years old. He was small, totally blind, crippled and bent with rheumatism, but in spite of this he went wherever he pleased, feeling his way with his cane before him. He told many interesting stories, of seeing the first train of covered wagons slowly winding their way into the Salt Lake Valley, of himself and others sitting on their ponies staring and wondering what all this meant, and later making friends with Brigham Young and Heber C. Kimball.

Other interesting characters were Bridger Jim, who as the story goes was head of the small tribe after the death of Chief Tabby and had a great deal of money; some was gold found in the mountains near here. Others were Indian Dan, Sam Robinson, and younger than these are Ephraim and Joe Bush who still have farms here. Joe Bush is a son of Bridger Jim. 

It is spring in 1906, the air is full of excitement as the farmers make many trips with huge loads of farming implements, crops are planted and great preparations are made for the families who were soon to come.

The first family to move in was the family of A. W. Maxwell, Mrs. Mary Ann Maxwell arrived on August 5, 1906 with a family of ten children, the youngest only three months old. Awaiting her was a one-room cabin with a dirt floor which had been dampened and swept until it was as hard and clean as cement. A covered wagon and a tent were also used as bedrooms during this first summer and winter; in the meantime a large two-room log house was built; this also had a dirt roof and dirt floor. 

The first settlers had some trouble with the Indians, but only when they were drinking. Soon, with kindness, Mrs. Maxwell found many true friends among the Indian squaws; time after time it was her loving and kind hands who washed and dressed a sick baby or smoothed the pillow of an aged squaw, and a small kindness like this was never forgotten by an Indian. In many ways they returned her kindness, sometimes it was by a helping hand, and other times it was by making with their hands some beautiful gift; it was a kindly old squaw who made and gave the first pair of

TABIONA 

449 shoes to Alvin, the baby when he was four months old. A tiny pair of mocassins, made of buckskin and covered with beads. 

Mrs. Maxwell lived in a little world of her own for six months without seeing another white woman; but in spite of many hard- 

1-Home of Arthur W. Maxwell; 2-Mr. A. W. Maxwell; 3-Sarah Ann Maxwell; 

4-Early homestead. 5-Mrs. Robert Mitchie, church worker; 6-Robert Mitchie.

ships we were happy. 

Once again I am living in my childhood, running through the trees and climbing mountains, gathering beautiful wild flowers, and running home with a bucket full of wild cherries or berries from which Mother would make delicious jelly, or together we would clear the land of sagebrush and roast potatoes around a huge bonfire at night, or help my sisters wash stacks of dirty dishes after 

Mother had cooked dinner for a family of twelve.

Other days I remember were summer rains when there were days we had to amuse ourselves inside the house; the boys with buckskins and rawhide, would practice making ropes and tying knots, or playing checkers, and the girls would sometimes sew, or make candy, or sometimes parch wheat which we would eat like popcorn. If these storms lasted long, sometimes the dirt on the roof would become wet through, and then like a sieve the water would run through.

I remember one night very distinctly; it had been raining for many days; there was hardly a dry corner in the house, and for some reason perhaps from the dampness, one of the boys lay ill with a high fever and a sick headache. Mother was pale and worried, and because of her sadness we children were sitting quietly in a corner like frightened kittens. Suddenly, from outside where the boys were doing their chores came the strain of the beautiful old song, "Be it ever so humble there's no place like home." I watched my mother with frightened eyes, but to my surprise her eyes brightened, and a tiny smile played at the corner of her lips; she turned and spoke, "Come children, your brother is better, and you must hurry to bed." So once again we were happy, and in spite of our humble house, there was no place like home. 

Mr. and Mrs. Bert White arrived with the first farmers early in the spring of 1906; they stayed only a short time that summer, later making their permanent home here. 

Mrs. Pearl White was a healthy, strong, and very beautiful young woman, the mother of two children.

Their first home was a one-room cabin with a dirt floor, a dirt roof, and no door. At night a quilt was hung at the door to keep the draft from the children; many nights Mrs. White had to spend alone with the children, while Mr. White was out after supplies or camping in the woods logging.

One of these lonely nights, soon after she had fallen asleep, she was awakened; a strange sound reached her ears, as a dog lapping up the sour milk outside the door which was to have been the pigs breakfast. As the White's had no dog of their own, she thought perhaps it was a dog of the neighbors across the valley, but she was restless, and slept very little the rest of the night. The next morning

all around the house and the chicken house were the tracks of a bear. Soon after this the Whites had a strong door at their cabin. 

On November 6, 1906, twins were born to Mr. and Mrs.

Bert White in Cottonwood; so now when she came home to the little cabin, there were four children and soon a new and a larger home was completed. The twins were named Ruth and Ruby. In 

November 1907, just after the twins were a year old, Ruby suddenly took sick and in a few days the grim shadow of death crossed the door of the little log home; Ruby had been called away. 

Let us pause at the door-The Mother is crying, perhaps she is thinking: Why, Oh Why, when there are so many children in the world must it be her little girl, the child they loved so much. 

But she has no time to cry there is work to be done, the neighbors were all so far away, so through tear dimmed eyes, the Mother went about washing and preparing the little body for its last sleep. 

After this was completed, it was decided the body must be taken to the home cemetery in Cottonwood. So, one cold, stormy day in November, Mr. and Mrs. White set out on the long trip to 

Cottonwood. As they reached the mountain passes, a terrible blizzard overtook them, and for several hours they were lost, but moving slowly and fighting their way through sleet and ice to keep from giving up and freezing they wandered on. Finally, when they had almost given up hope, the wind died down and once again they were able to see where they were, and with little difficulty resume their journey. Soon they reached Cottonwood, where old friends welcomed them and they were comforted in their sorrow. 

About 1909 Mrs. White was again confined, and before help could reach her she entered into the deep shadows, all alone she faced the birth of her own baby, and from the shadows she fought for her child's life, and when help finally came, they found a healthy new born babe, and a Mother tired but happy. So through all circumstances this brave woman met both life and death alone. 

In February Mr. and Mrs. Alma W. Wagstaff and family moved in. Mr. Wagstaff was busy at once building a new room, for they found that one room in cold weather was not enough for their family, so clearing the frozen ground the best he could he built another room out of green cottonwood, but this was not so successful. Mrs. Wagstaff was sick with rheumatism for several weeks, due to the dampness of the bedroom, and when spring finally came the cottonwood bedroom, as if by magic, the walls sprang out in a cool spray of green cottonwood leaves. 

Mrs. Wagstaff before coming here was a first class midwife and had worked for several years in Wasatch County, and soon after moving to Tabiona she found she was needed, so although she went through unlimited hardships, and for very little money, she worked faithfully, until her recent death. Only when her health failed her would she be forced to give up the work she loved so

TABIONA 

453 well.

It was with kindest respect and deepest love we laid to rest a Mother we shall never forget; she was loved by everyone, young and old and has indeed been a Mother of Mercy, always sweet, loving and kind, with beautiful and strong gifted hands, always with faith and unfailing skill this courageous woman had worked in her profession for over fifty-four years. Her face was furrowed with lines of hardships, her hair was silver, but to us she grew more beautiful, as we looked we saw a halo around her silver head, we cannot put in words our feelings for this grand old lady, whom we loved and almost worshipped, the Mother of our Valley. 

In the spring of 1907 several new families moved into the valley. Among these were Mr. and Mrs. Wadley and family, Mr. and Mrs. Robert Giles and family, Mr. Sam Powell, and Mr. 

Warren Colton.

Mr. Colton was to be married in a few weeks; he came in high hopes and began at once to build a home for his beautiful bride-to-be. Soon with help a two-room cabin with an extension porch in front and a dirt roof was built. This was the first home of Dr. and Mrs. Warren Colton, who are well known in Utah. 

Dr. and Mrs. Colton now reside in New York City, but no doubt you should ask him, he would still hold fond memories of his honeymoon spent in a crude little cabin in Tabiona Valley. if

Mr. and Mrs. William Wadley were happy at the thought of making a home in this new country, and began at once planning and building. They had a family of five children, most of them big enough to help. Soon a two-room house was built and fixed into a very comfortable home.

It was early in October 1907, Mr. Wadley was returning from a trip to Pleasant Grove with his last load of furniture and supplies before the winter snows began; he was traveling alone and one of his horses was a newly broken colt, as he came to the bear camp his load was too heavy for his horses to pull to the top. Mr. Woodard, another traveler on the road, offered to hitch his team on also and with four horses pull the load to the top. As the strange horses were brought up, the colt became frightened and just as Mr. 

Wadley bent to unyoke the harness, the strange horse turned on the colt, this frightened the colt and he kicked with terrific force striking Mr. Wadley on the head. He fell unconscious to the ground, a large gash in the side of his head and the blood streaming down his face.

Mr. and Mrs. O'Driscol who camped nearby came running to the scene of the accident. Ot first they thought he was dead; then as they found a spark of life, Mr. Woodard decided to leave him with the O'Driscol's, and with the one thought that they must have a doctor, he set out with his team and wagon to bring Dr.

Daningburg, who at that time was doctor of Kamas Valley. 

For about six hours Mr. Wadley lay at death's door in the camp of Mr. and Mrs. O'Driscol. Hardly a spark of life could be seen and almost every minute they thought he would leave them. 

The hours they spent would be hard to imagine so far away from everyone and so hopelessly alone, no sound at all, only the breeze softly whispering among the tall lonely pine trees, so quiet it makes one feel like screaming or laughing, just to drive away the terrible feeling that one must run away from it all, but they stayed faithfully by, watching the patient for any change, but little change seemed to come as he lay quietly, hardly breathing. 

Little did they know that his thoughts too were drifting, from far off clouds he could see his own body lying on the ground, and then as he journeyed on he talked with several persons in his journey and was told he must return, that his mission on earth was not finished; so slowly through cloudy valleys and hazy dreams he once again awoke and to his surprise he was lying in a clean spotless room of a hospital, and as his vision slowly cleared, he could see the doctor and a nurse standing near. 

To everyone's surprise he had gone through successfully the operation of removing 14 ounces of cracked skull; even the doctor thought it hopeless but said that he would do the only thing possible to save his life. His condition improved rapidly and soon he was able to return to his family, but he was still far from well; there seemed to be a pressure on his head which caused terrible headache; for two years he suffered untold agonies. So he was taken to 

Salt Lake City to Dr. Robinson, a brain specialist, and an operation was performed to release the pressure of bone which was pressing on his brain. A gold plate was placed on his skull and 7 gold bolts were put in to hold the bone in place. This operation was one of the most successful operations in history at that time. A price of $10,000 was offered for his head at the time of his death. The operation is called the miracle of the world. 

So it was that Mr. Wadley returned to his home, a well and happy man. He was born in St. George, Utah in 1864. When yet a baby his parents moved to Pleasant Grove, and there he spent his boyhood, until moving to Tabiona in 1907. 

In about 1927 Mrs. Wadley died, and since then Mr. Wadley and his boys, until his death in 1939, lived in happy contentment on the ranch. Their home is one of the beauty spots in the valley, a grand old house set on a sloping lawn back among a grove of tall pine trees. Mr. Wadley was known throughout the country for his beautiful vegetable gardens and his success in transplanting pine trees. 

On April 13, 1908, a baby girl was born to Mr. and Mrs. 

Robert Giles; this was the first child born in Tabiona, a darling

She was born in a one-room cabin which had sheltered five other children all winter, and a large grain bin had been built inside the cabin to keep the seed grain dry. Most of the grain had been taken out of the bin and the Mother's bed was set up inside of the grain bin, over the remainder of the wheat. On July 3, 1908 the baby was christened

A small branch ward had been organized, a branch of what was at that time Theodore Ward, and which now is Duchesne. Mr. 

T. A. White was appointed Presiding Elder and meetings were held in different homes until about 1910 when the first church house

was completed.

The first Bishop of Tabiona Ward was Bishop 

James Jones. 

The first U. S. Post Office was placed in the home of Mr. 

Bruce Wilson. In order to receive mail the farmers took turns going to Duchesne once a week on horseback bringing the mail, this indeed was a great day when everyone gathered to receive letters from loved ones many miles away.

The first school was held in the winter of 1908 in the cabin home of Mr. Ernest Sadler, Mr. Sadler acting as teacher; most of the students had to ride for several miles in a sleigh to attend school. 

Other winters, school was held at the home of Mr. and Mrs. 

Bert White and Mr. High Jones until about 1910 when the church house was completed and was also used as a school room. 

Early in the season, before this school house was completed, a large tent was used for a school room; the tent was very crowded for now there were enough pupils for a good class in all grades. 

The tent proved to be very hot on warm days and very cold on chilly days.

I remember several school rooms in my youth, and many hardships on the youngsters, facing terrible blizzards, in zero weather, being so cold when we entered the school room we could hardly stand. I remember taking off my coat and stuffing it in the opening where the window pane had fallen out, and then sitting chilled all day in a drafty room. What a price we paid for the little knowledge gained! I remember my sister who was delicate in her childhood fainting several times from this cold and strenuous walk.

Mr. and Mrs. Robert Michie and family were among the first to settle in Tabiona. They, with their family of eleven children, lived in a one-room cabin; they were a happy, healthy family until tragedy darkened their door.

Of eleven children only three were boys, the baby, a boy, was loved and fussed over by the older children and others, but there came a day when he was about five years old that death robbed them of their darling baby brother. This child was the first to be buried in the Tabiona Cemetery, and the child's father chose the location. 

The mother was very sad to leave that lonely little grave but this grave was not alone long; other babies were soon to follow, and not long after the father of this little boy met with an accident at the local sawmill, hurting his back and breaking some ribs. Shortly after this he died, leaving a lonely widow and ten children who must have clothing and food. 

Although the winters were cold and the work was hard this courageous woman with her undaunted faith and prayers, raised her good family to beautiful womanhood and manhood.

TABIONA 

457 In about 1908 Mr. and Mrs. O. T. Hicken filed on a homestead and moved from Wasatch County to Tabiona Valley. They lived in a one-room cabin which also served as a grainery to store their winter wheat and oats. Mr. and Mrs. Hicken were both well educated and a jolly young couple, soon they had many friends. 

Mrs. Eunice Hicken was an ambitious and hard working woman; by lending a helping hand soon between them they had the land cleared of sagebrush and ready to start farming. She also made many friends with the Indian squaws; many times she has gone into their homes and taught them to bake their own light bread, and to darn and patch. She enjoyed the free life of the open country and spent a great deal of her time out of doors. 

One day as she watched the men at work in the field, the thought came to her, perhaps a good cool drink of fresh buttermilk would be a refreshing drink for them, so she started out with a large pitcher of buttermilk. She was crossing a swampy straight in the field not far from the men when suddenly, the ground beneath her feet began to suck her down. She tried to move but the suction drew her farther down; she called out and the men came running to her aid, she was almost up to her shoulders before they reached her. They worked with frantic speed, and at last pulled her loose from the swampy suckage.

Mrs. Hicken was loved by everyone for the many years of nursing she gave to the sick of Tabiona. Night after night she was called to sit with the sick; many babies she brought into the world without the aid of a doctor; other times she was called to dress the dead; always sweet and uncomplaining, she did what she could to relieve the pain and suffering of others and comfort the sorrowing.

She, herself is the mother of fifteen children, seven of whom are still living; she has known the many heartaches of seeing her children sick and dying; three she lost of pneumonia, and one beautiful little girl died of typhoid fever; this was the third grave in the Tabiona Cemetery.

With tear-dimmed eyes as she parted with one after another of these little ones, it seemed she had gone through more than her share of sorrow, and as she was growing old, if only we could say, "Well done, sweet one, sit down and rest," but no, we cannot say that or could we take over her burden for her, she sat for years a helpless invalid, or lay there, day after day helpless, suffering untold agonies. We can only whisper a little prayer, that sometime, somewhere, you will understand.

Now there was need for a townsite; so a meeting was called and a townsite was decided upon. The land was bought from the 

Indian Department at Fort Duchesne; the name of "Tabiona" was chosen, named after Chief Tabby. The lots of the new towns were

surveyed and soon several homes were built. 

The Post Office was moved from the Maxwell ranch, Mrs. 

Maxwell being at that time Postmistress. 

Mr. Robert Giles owned the first store in Tabiona.

In 1918, a large four room brick school house was erected by the County, which later has been remodeled. On May 11, 1939, work was begun on a new extension of this school house, nine rooms were built on, including a large gymnasium, a kitchen and shop. 

The building is modern in every way, the cost of this new building is about $30,000.00. About 200 students are registered in the 

Tabiona, Hanna consolidated school, consisting of full courses in twelve grades.

It is April 1940, and as the sun comes creeping over the distant eastern mountains, it seems to smile upon the beautiful fertile valley, for miles both north and south, stretch the vast alfalfa fields, and most contentedly, graze fat purebred milk cows. 

The population of Tabiona is about 400 people, the town boasts of two good stores, a good picture show, a cheese factory, and the best water in the Uintah Basin is piped into every home in town from an ice cold spring on the mountain side. 

The town of Tabiona is an incorporated town, the third incorporated town in the Basin. The first Mayor of Tabiona was 

Mayor Benjamin Turnbow; other members of the town board were J. Larmar Johnson, Ervan Clegg, Lawrence Maxwell, Jesse

H. LeFevre, and Hursel Jones. 

The main industry is dairying; over 5000 pounds of milk are taken in daily by the local cheese factory.

About 950 head of cattle are fed at the present time, about 12,000 head of sheep, about 2,730 tons of hay, 4,475 bushels of wheat, 8,390 bushels of oats, and about 1,300 bushels of barley.

This estimate covers only the district of Tabiona. 

The sun is setting in the Valley, the sunset of a day well spent; the sun is setting also for all the dear old men and women who have made this valley what it is today. 

It has been nice knowing them, and I wish them health and happiness for they too are in the sunset of a well spent day. 

A MOUNTAIN FOR AN ARTIST 

Ah, my good painter do you think, If I'd describe, that you could paint

A grand mountain, stretching vast adn high, For many miles across the sky.

Ah, yes it stands sir, as a statue stern, With many a peak and crook and turn.

But first dear painter, start quite low, Sloping gently upward as you go.

A tall and waving ridge comes next With cedars and pines all scattered and mixed. 

Ah, how can I describe, so you can see, This masterpiece you must paint for me? 

Next, a peak with grass and stalk, Where often a shepherd herds his flock, And remember, dear painter, this mountain's high,

It looks almost as if it reaches the sky. 

Now sir, the last little peak has a perfect form, But without any trees, looks all forlorn. 

Down from the top, good painter true, With vines, trees and skies so blue,

Another ridge which looks faint but wide, Why, it almost covers the whole mountain side. 

Ah, here, dear sir, how often I've gone to pick 

Such beautiful wild flowers in bloom so thick. 'Tis sunset, sir; how grand it looks; 

The sunset rays touch each little nook, With buzzing bees, and songs of birds, Off in a distance, coyotes can be heard.

The hoot of an owl, the rabbits full bound- 

Ah, sir, I forgot you cannot paint sound! 

Please paint for me this mountain high,

Standing as a statue against the sky. 

And now my I reveal the name, Of the mountain you must paint for frame? 

Ah, I wonder, sire, would it make you happy 

To hear it was named after Old Chief Tabby?

Written by Minnie Maxwell Hamilton JESSUP WATSON THOMAS 

Jessup Watson Thomas was born at Heber City, Wasatch 

County, Utah, on October 6, 1878, a son of Joseph and Margaret (Watson) Thomas, the former of whom was born in Council 

Bluffs, Iowa. Came to Utah, Sept. 9, 1852, in the Bryant Jolley

Company. The latter was born in Edinburgh, Scotland, pioneer settlers of the Heber City community of Utah, where the father gave his attention to the livestock industry. The subject of this review was a lad of about eight years when his parents died, the

death of the father having been followed by that of the mother a few days later, and nine of their eleven children having survived them.

Jessup W. Thomas received the advantages of the local schools in his boyhood and at the age of fifteen he left to herd sheep. 

His independent career was marked by his close association with the raising of sheep and cattle. 

In 1900, at the age of twenty-one he bought some sheep, some of the first sheep brought into Duchese County. He herded them along the slopes of the Duchesne River, above the Tabiona Valley, at this time there were no homes in Tabiona but there were Indian camps along the river. Prior to herding sheep on Tabby Mountain he herded for years in Red Creek and Current Creek. He purchased his present ranch in the period shortly after the opening of the 

Mrs. Sarah Chatwin former Indian reservation to setlement, and made high grade improvements on the place including a modern home and a private electric plant that provided both light and Frigidaire service. By the purchase of other ranches, Mr. Thomas acquired a large land area and developed his business to one of broad scope in the raising and handling of cattle and sheep, for which he provided an enormous range. He sold much of his land and holdings but still has retained a fine and well ordered ranch. 

October 26, 1899 he married 

Eunice Lindsay and they became the parents of five children, all of whom survived the mother, namely: Alta, Jessup, Ora, Darrel, and Velda. In 1921 Mr.

Thomas was united in marriage to Miss Irene Cope, and five children were born to them: Ina, Weston, Irene, Royal Cope, and

Linda Lee.

TALMAGE 

Contributed by Adaline Anderson

THE HISTORY OF TALMAGE 

OSEPH DRAPER and H. P. Ottosen were the first families to come to Talmage. This was in the spring of 1909. They went back to their homes in other settlements for the winter and returned the following spring. In 1910 the John W. Davies and Thomas Kenison families came. They pitched tents in Draper 

Hollow on the William Davis homestead and later built cedar log cabins. The next family to arrive was Ephraim McMillan. They built a cabin of cedar logs on the lower end of the homestead. 

They all left for the winter but spent the summers here until 1911, and the Thomas Kenison family stayed all winter which was the beginning of a permanent settlement. 

Joseph Draper and sons started a sawmill on the Indian grazing ground. This furnished employment for men of the surrounding towns and was a means of building better houses of sawed logs. 

Much of this timber was hauled to Myton, Duchesne, and Roosevelt. 

There was no water on the flat, and the men hauled all that was used for household and stock from the Lake Fork River, eight miles away. In 1912, the Farnsworth Canal was begun but was not much good. However, the water was run down the big hollow which made it closer for stock and household use. 

In 1911, the town was called Winn and with Mt. Home, Boneta and Winn formed the Boneta School District with C. A. 

Burton, Oscar Wilkins, and James Mickelson as trustees. In 1913 a school was started with 12 pupils. Mrs. Margaret Todd was the teacher. School was first held in the Sam Draper house (Donohue's) until Christmas, and they moved to a one-room building built for a meeting house.

In September of 1911, a Post Office was started at Winn. 

Miss Rachel Eva Davies-Rust was the Postmistress, and she served the first six months free of charge. The mail was brought from 

Boneta (separation office) on horseback. Prior to this the people had to go to Boneta to get their mail. After the Post Office was established, the mail route was changed and came directly from Duchesne and on to Boneta. It has made several changes since that time. 

John Kenison was the first white child born in Winn, February 1, 1912. 

Mrs. William Thompson started a small store at her cabin on the farm. Later they moved to the townsite and enlarged their stock to meet the needs of the people. 461

1-Early homsteaders Earnest Allen, Laurence Maxwell and Bob Manwell; 2-Johnny 

Allred's old home; 3-4-4th of July, 1913.

TALMAGE 

463 On September 21, 1913, 40 acres of land was bought from John Johnson's homestead and laid off in one-acre lots for the townsite. Four acres were left for a town park. 

About 1914 the name was changed to Talmage, named for 

James E. Talmage, an apostle of the L. D. S. Church. That fall the Post Office was moved to the townsite in the new home of Sarah E. Davies, who held the position of Postmistress until her death on Dec. 29, 1927. Ruby G. McDonald was made Postmistress February 13, 1928. She held this office until July 31, 1945, when Alice B. Sorenson was made Acting Postmistress.

In February 1921, Mrs. Thompson died and the store was sold to Mary L. Shelton. This store burned, and she began a store on the lot north of the meeting house. Later this was sold to Elray 

Larson who owned it for about 18 years, and it was sold to Glen Sorensen.

The first Fourth of July celebration was held in 1914. Every family attended. Since that time there has been a celebration nearly every year. The years that they have not had one in Talmage, it was held in connection with one of the other towns. 

Winn was made a branch of the Boneta Ward, with A. G. 

Burton as Bishop, and Lewis Allred, as Presiding Elder of the Winn 

Ward. Talmage was made a ward, September 17, 1916, with A. G. 

Burton as Bishop, and Ola J. Jensen and George B. Rust as coun-

selors. They held this position until March 28, 1937. 

The first Sunday School was organized the same year with 

D. M. Todd as Superintendent. The first Relief Society was organ ized with Esther H. Draper as president. Lauretta B. Sanders was chosen as the first Primary president. The first Y. L. and Y. M. 

M. I. A. was organized November 27, 1917 with Sarah M. Beck stead and John L. Allred, Jr., as President and Superintendent. The first community Christmas tree and program was held that year and has been held every year since that time.

UPALCO TPALCO was first called Lake Fork. In the summer of 1905, a few homesteaders came to build small one room houses on their homesteads. Among these were Ephraim Marrett, Ted Howell, Oscar Nelson, George Evans, John Horten, Robert

Marshall, Mell Pitt. Lorenzo Pitt was the first to build his cabin, he and his wife lived in it nearly all summer. They hauled water in barrels from the river and snaked cottonwood logs for their house up the hill. This summer two sisters by the name of Hill filed and moved on what is now the Eph Mecham farm. They hired someone from Myton to bring them on to their place. These girls did not even know how to set up their tent. Lorenzo and Katy 

Pitt helped them stretch their tent and built them a cabin. The girls soon left; it was too much for them.

After these first settlers built their cabins, they left for various occupations, some worked at sawmills and some on canals. Then 

Ted Howells moved in here in 1906. Mrs. Howells never saw a woman all winter. They could see one light in the distance in this flat. In April 1907, Marritts came with their family and T. B. 

Hallet started a small grocery store in a tent. Newell Knight and

John were next to settle in the country. In August 1904, Arthur Key brought his family and they lived on the Purdy place under the hill until November 13, 1906, when they moved into their own home. They lived in Myton and Nevada until January 1908; then they came back. The Jeff Mathews and Rob Marshall families had moved in March 8, 1907. 

May 1, 1908, Allegra Key, the first baby girl, was born. Mrs. 

Howells had buried a baby boy in 1907. In 1908 Nap Mitchell moved his family into a tent while he built them a rock one-room house. He also built T. B. Hallet a rock store. They stayed the summer then moved out until the fall of 1909. Dave Richardson built a cabin on his homestead. Lorenzo Pitt and wife, Dave 

Richardson and son Jody lived in it until Lorenzo moved his cabin down from his west forty acres. George Potts settled on his homestead in July, 1909. This same year a celebration was held in front of the Hallet store in a bowery. People from miles around came in their wagons. Free lemonade and prizes for the children's races were given. They chose sides from the crowd and played ball. 

In 1908 and 1909 some tragic deaths occurred. Newell Knight died of heart trouble, leaving his wife and eight children, another leaving nine. No one knows the suffering and sorrow of these families. Mr. Wolfe, who lived on the Lake Fork River, took a hemorrhage of the lungs and died. His wife was alone with him. when he died. Her hair turned snow white over night. There were no neighbors within miles. A twelve year old boy was dragged 465

to death by a team, his body being lacerated with rocks and prickly pears filling his wounds. His father was a Presbyterian minister; they left the country soon after the accident. A little Gines girl burned to death while her mother was after her cows. A

Woodard boy choked to death on a bean. Caskets for these first deaths were built by the men. Lumber was scarce and in some cases the side boards from wagon boxes were used. 

Up until 1909, the older children either rode horses or stayed at the Palmer home to go to school in the one-room shack called Palmer School. This was later remodeled by Wasatch County School 

District into a good building. In 1909 the Lake Fork people decided there were enough children for a school, there being 21 children of school age. They moved a shanty up on the main road, built plank benches, plank seats and hired Will Neal from Mt. Emmons for teacher. He rode horseback to school every morning. That fall 

Marshall Mathews, Geo. Potts, and Dave Richardson got out logs for a new school house. It was not finished until the next spring. 

By this time more people had moved in, Mechams, C. C. Synderguard, Abe Stevens, Woolseys, and Cases. When the building was finished there were children enough for two teachers. The building was hued logs; the floor had wide cracks, but good times were had in this place.

Up until this time house parties and an occasional dance were the social events. The first wedding dance was given in the new building by Oscar Nelson, the bride being the school teacher,

Naomi Mitchell. 

Pioneering has ever been a struggle for food, shelter and clothes, for culture and education. The earliest struggle was for water. Water had to be hauled. The first water used on this flat was taken out at the Sand Wash. This was not sufficient and could not be kept in the ditch for rolling sand. The farmers got together and started Class C. Canal coming out of the Lake Fork River at the Hunt place. They worked very hard, meeting many disappointments. When they had it nearly finished, the side hill kept sliding into the ditch as fast as they could dig it out. A few Lake 

Fork men got discouraged. Marshall, Mathews, Potts, Syndegard, Mitchell, Horton, Nelson, and John Angus went further up the river about 15 miles and got the water out. Mrs. Lilas Park came up to see her husband who was working on the Class C. Canal, while walking along the canal a loose rock fell from the cliff, hit her baby on the head, killing it instantly. After spending $90,000 on 

Class C., they finally gave it up, joined Lake Fork enlarging the 

Lake Fork and making the Class C. Canal. After the Lake Fork ditch was completed the Forkers had what they called Ditch Day, a celebration was held, program, dinner, and dance at night. The ladies sold ice-cream to help put some improvements on the school

UPALCO 469 house. At this dance the Krebbs brass band furnished the music. 

The Canal was completed in 1911. 

The first settlers had a hard time getting along. The only source of income some of them received was from the butter and eggs they sold. The women hitched their teams to the buggies or wagons and went to Altonah to the Miller store or to the Hallet store in Myton to do their shopping. They traded their eggs and butter for groceries and a few dry goods. When they went to the stores to try and get a few items the store keepers would say, "We are out; will have them in a few days." The goods were hauled in by freight teams from Price. It took from 10 days to three weeks to make the trip.

Pastures did not bother the first settlers for they could turn their cows out in the flat or in the cedars. In the evening one or two from a family went to round-up the town herd. One Sunday all the young folks got horses and went to round up the town herd. 

The children did not think it was a hardship; it was fun for them. They did not grumble when the winter hay had to be hauled from Vernal for 20 or 30 dollars a ton. It took time and hard work to rail the sagebrush and get the land ready to produce hay, corn, potatoes; in early years some sugar cane was grown for molasses. There was a sugar cane mill at Hayden. Some farmers hauled their cane there to be processed. Peanuts, watermelons, and good gardens were raised. Dairying was the chief industry. 

Around 1913 the power plant was built on the Lake Fork River, furnishing power to Roosevelt and Myton. The plant was called the Uintah Power and Light Company. Upalco was next to have lights. The Lake Fork people had to go to Hallet's store for their mail; then Mrs. Marett was Postmistress until Mrs. Frizell was appointed Postmistress at the power plant. The Post Office was named Upalco from the first letters in Uintah Power and Light Company. Then it was later established at the Upalco store with 

Edwin L. Murphy as Postmaster. Cream was hauled to Mt. Emmons for Mutual Creamery.

March 17, 1914 the corner stone was laid for the rock building on C. C. Syndeguard's place. When this was finished, the old log hall was abandoned. It was around this time quite a fight was put up to have the County Seat somewhere in this vicinity, as they argued that this was the central place. In the winter of 1919, they started to get out timber for the amusement and Church house that is still standing. In 1923, the Mitchell boys started to build a large garage; they later sold it to Nelson Brothers for a creamery. More cream was produced and people started to improve their herds and looked to their pastures.

When the amusement hall and church was completed, it was

found needful to permit the Duchesne School District to use it for school, Mr. Edwin Murphy and Miss Josephine Hunt being the first teachers. They taught for a number of years in this building without a curtain to separate the departments. Before going into the building Mr. Murphy, Miss Hunt, and Miss Marshall taught in tents just north of where the Church House now stands.

SHORT SKETCH OF DEEP CREEK 

By Lila Nickell and Olive Harrison SMALL SECTION of the Uintah and Ouray Indian reserva tion was opened for settlement when Agent Critchlow was in charge of the Indian affairs. As soon as it was known that this land, now known as Deep Creek, was open for homesteading the white people began moving in. 

They settled without schools or churches but one of the first jobs was to find a place to find school. The Ben Jones family had two cabins on their place so they lived in one and let one be used for a school house. Emmaline Bingham was the first teacher. 

Marion Thomas and his wife Sarah Ellen, were among the first settlers, arriving about 1886. He was a member of the "Mormon 

Battalion" and Mrs. Thomas was believed to be the last survivor connected with the Mormon Battalion. She was born November 28, 1846, at Pueblo, Colorado, daughter of Norman Sharp of the

Mormon Battalion, who died as the result of an accidental gunshot wound received before the stop in Pueblo. Her mother was Mrs. 

Martha Jane Sargent Sharp one of the few women who made the march with their husbands. Mrs. Thomas was a guest of honor at the unveiling of the Mormon Battalion Monument in Salt Lake 

City in 1927. 

Mr. and Mrs. Thomas were married in January, 1886. "Jux", as Mr. Thomas was called by everyone who knew him, was the first 

Sunday School Superintendent at Deep Creek. He died in 1914. 

Mrs. Thomas died March 1, 1937.

Among the first settlers were: The Daltons, Strangs, the John 

Allreds, Tom Dunnivon, the Tee Taylor families, Andrew Allcorns, the Wilsons, Tommie Caldwell family, the Francis Caldwells, Spragues, Bub and Jack Meeks, Eli Tracy, Lou Black and family, the Joe Hatch family, John Clark's family, the Thomas Labrum families, Henry White and two sons (Milton and Jimmie), James 

Blancett and sons (John and Jim), Charlie Smith and sons (Charlie and George), Ben West and family, Alf Johnson and family, Widow 

Susan Marler and her family.

The Labrum families came in the fall of 1887 and it took them ten days to make the trip from Salt Lake Valley. After arriving the men contracted with the government at Fort Duchesne to furnish the soldiers a certain amount of cord wood during the winter and by filling these contracts they earned enough to keep the families during the winter. Later they burned charcoal for the forges at the Fort. They took up two homesteads on what was called 

Crow Creek. Thomas Labrum, Sr. discovered a vein of coal near 471

Thomas G. Labrum died March 12,1923 

Susan G. Labrum died Sept. 14, 1927

The Jon Harrison family came from Mona about 1896 and bought Joe Hatch's homestead. Mr. Harrison was the second Sunday

School Superintendent, their daughter Alice acting as Sunday School 

Secretary. Mrs. Margaret Harrison, affectionately called Aunt Mag by most everyone in Mona and as Grandma Harrison, was always ready to help whenever there was sickness and acted as mid-wife whenever asked. There were two cabins on their homestead and one was used for a school house for years. Mr. Harrison was a school trustee and the school teacher nearly always boarded with the 

Harrison family.

Mrs. Susan Labrum was another school trustee and was quite active in community work, also serving as practical nurse in time of illness.

Governor Heber Wells was the first governor to visit this part of Utah, coming in July 

Joe Harrison of Deep Creek and Billy Muse, a friend of his, were asked to act as guards and they escorted the governor from the mail station of the Duchesne river (now the town of Myton) to Vernal. Mrs. Mary Orser was on the welcoming committee. She well remembers this day because she says they had barrels of lemonade made ready to serve the crowd and it snowed and was so cold they nearly froze.

BRIEF HISTORY OF THE BOARD OF EDUCATION OF DUCHESNE COUNTY 

Duchesne County School Districts were originally under the direction of Wasatch County. There were the following Districts at the time of separation from Wasatch County: Stockmore, Theodore, Packer, Antelope, Midview, Lakefork, Boneta, Hayden, Fruitland, Roosevelt, Cedarview, Myton, and Alexander.

Leonard Gardner formerly of Neola. He has been one of the Basin's leading cattle growers. He served as a member of the 

Board of Education several terms. 

After the consolidation of the various districts of Duchesne

County into eleven School Dis tricts, it may be of interest to many to be reminded of the old schools that exist no more.

They are as follows: Basin, Blue 

Bench, Riverside, Wardles, Palmer, Banner, Monarch, Alexander, Juanita, Hartford, Woodbine, Farm Creek, Harper,

Stockmore, Strawberry, Blue mesa, Utahn, Redcap, Midview, McAffee, Antelope, Fruitland, and Upalco.

The County Commissioners appointed a temporary organization, at first, and on May 6, 1915, they met and elected the following permanent officers: 

William O'Neil, President. 

H. O. Ottosen, Vice-President. 

Ed. F. Harmston, Clerk. 

A. F. Maxwell, Treasurer. 

L. R. Carey, member. From the various districts the following members were elected and served from January, 1916, until 1948, as their terms indicated.

The Superintendents that have served in Duchesne County are as follows: Jesse A. Washburn, J. F. Hoyte, W. A. Paxton, Charles M. Iverson, R. D. Law, LeRoy Bishop, and W. J. Bond. 

Florence B. Madsen and Anne E. Stark have been the only 

Primary supervisors. Miss Stark began in 1936 and is still employed. 

Mrs. Reva Owens has been supervisor of the lunch rooms. It is impossible to include all of the Principals and teachers in this brief volume; however, Duchesne County has been very fortunate in having so many well qualified and fine personnel in their schools.

The enrollment of the pupils in Duchesne County at the close of school year in 1915 was 1,669; in 1932, during the depression year, was 2,665; in 1948 it is 2,665.

PART III

PART III 

Appreciation is expressed to all who have helped with this supplementary material for Part III. It was requested by so many pioneers that their desires were to have preserved a record of their descendants in Wars, especially World War II, in this book. 

The Roosevelt Standard donated the use of all of their cuts. 

This accounts for the variation in size of the pictures. It was difficult to reproduce all pictures due to variation in size and textures and the added cost of engraving and printing of this added material. 

Many names will be omitted because no official record could be obtained. This is regrettable, but after many requests the response for records was very gratifying. Some material came too late to be included.
